# Roadmap

Full phase-by-phase detail — scope, rationale, what shipped, what's still open. See
[README.md](README.md) for the product overview, or [CHANGELOG.md](CHANGELOG.md) for
the version-by-version release history (a different axis — a phase groups a
coherent chunk of work across the project's lifetime, a release/tag carries its own
semantic version number). Same convention as
[`ansimax`](https://github.com/Brashkie/ansimax)'s own roadmap.

[English](./ROADMAP.md) · [Español](./ROADMAP.es.md)

**Status**: ✅ Complete · 🟡 Partial · 🔴 Planned

## Language philosophy

Sythrall isn't "built with six languages" — each language sits at the layer where it
actually earns its place, and Phases 13+ below are organized around that split
instead of a flat "add more languages" list:

| Layer | Language | Role | Evidence so far |
|---|---|---|---|
| Interaction | **TypeScript** | UI, Monaco integration, editor intelligence, diagrams — the only layer a user directly touches | Vite + Monaco + Mermaid + Chart.js + xterm, all shipped (Phases 1–9) |
| Intelligence & Science | **Python** | AI/ML detection, orchestration, scientific workloads — **not** the static-analysis engine long-term: that role is being retired to Rust (Phase 18's Native Analysis Core), one measured slice at a time | 23 library detections, ML/DL Inspector (Phase 2). `static_parser.py` itself is the legacy piece Phase 18 is migrating out of Python, not a permanent fixture |
| Native Analysis | **Rust** | The static-analysis engine, committed destination — parsing, AST, symbol resolution, complexity, security, quality, graphs. Each slice is still profiled/benchmarked before the swap (that's *how*, not *whether*) | `terminal-server` (PTY handling), `complexity-engine` (9–21× faster than `radon` — Phase 11), rich Python analysis ported (Phase 18) |
| Legacy ecosystem/interop | **C/C++** | Analysis target (shipped, Phase 18) — reading and fully understanding existing C/C++ systems for what they are: security, memory layout, complexity, architecture, dependencies. Since 2026-08-30, also the primary raw material Phase 25's Modernization Intelligence works from — not to replace C/C++ everywhere, but to identify which specific pieces of a legacy system are candidates for Rust/Zig/modern C++, backed by evidence the analysis above already computes, never a blind convert | Shipped (Phase 18) — `cparse.rs`; `memlayout.rs` (Phase 23) feeds the ownership/RAII candidates Phase 25 will use |
| Scientific/HPC | **Fortran** | Analysis target (shipped) **and**, since 2026-08-30, an open candidate for real implementation inside Sythrall's own engine — not just numpy/scipy's free LAPACK/BLAS speed, but Fortran Sythrall itself writes, wherever a measured numerical hot spot justifies it. `fortran_bench.rs`'s validation kernel is the first proof this is more than theory: Sythrall already compiles and runs real Fortran for its own purposes, not just to read someone else's — and as of 2026-08-30 that kernel is a real, standalone `.f90` file in the repo (`kernels/validate_matmul.f90`, pulled in via `include_str!`), not a string literal buried inside Rust source. The fuller shape this is heading toward (Phase 26): a prototype written in Python, a real numeric kernel in Fortran once the shape is proven, Rust orchestrating the call, Assembly only for the single hottest inner loop if profiling still justifies it after all of that | Shipped (Phase 20) — `fparse.rs`, `tree-sitter-fortran`, `fortran_bench.rs` (Phase 23), `kernels/validate_matmul.f90` |
| Machine-level | **Assembly** | Analysis target — pattern-matching on raw `.s`/`.asm` text (Phase 19's first bullet) plus a real executable-structure analyzer over PE/ELF/Mach-O/COFF/wasm (Phase 19's second bullet, via the `object` crate — not Capstone/LIEF/`objdump` by subprocess, the pure-Rust equivalent of the same idea, see the phase's own entry for why) — **and**, since 2026-08-30, an open candidate for hand-written hot paths inside Sythrall itself, but deliberately the *last* rung of a ladder, not a first resort: Rust first, then Zig/C if Rust's abstractions are themselves the bottleneck, and only Assembly once profiling shows a compiler genuinely can't close the gap — SIMD, CPU-specific instructions (AVX2/AVX-512/NEON), crypto primitives, a specific hot loop, never "rewritten in ASM because ASM is fast" | Shipped (Phase 19, all 3 bullets) — `asmparse.rs`/`binparse.rs`/`callingconv.rs`. **Actually profiled, not just left open** (2026-08-30): `cargo bench --bench complexity_bench` on 1000 functions runs in ~96ms (~96µs/function) — parser/tree-walk overhead, not a dense numeric loop SIMD would help with. No implementation-language use — honestly, no candidate found yet, not merely "none looked for" |
| Native tooling | **Zig** | Build/cross-compilation/standalone distribution (Phase 29, renumbered 2026-08-30 — see "Where this is going, next" below) **and**, since 2026-08-30, a real candidate for low-level components themselves — codecs, C interop, native tooling pieces that want maximum control with minimal abstraction, not only the toolchain wrapping Rust's own binaries. **The guardrail that keeps this from becoming decoration**: never "this is in Zig because Zig is fast" — only "this is in Zig because it needs memory/ABI/toolchain control or C interop that makes the component itself simpler here than in Rust," a real technical reason stated up front, not retrofitted | **Shipped (Phase 29, 2026-08-30)** — `scripts/cross-compile.sh`/`.ps1`, `cargo-zigbuild` cross-compiling `complexity-engine`/`terminal-server`; verified end to end on this dev machine (Windows → real Linux ELF binaries, confirmed with `file`, not just "it compiled"). Not yet used as the language for a component's own logic — only as the cross-compilation toolchain so far |

The rule for moving anything into Rust (or any native language) used to be a real
either/or: profile first, benchmark the replacement, keep it only if the numbers
justify it — Phase 10's large-project investigation found the real O(n²) cost was
three ordinary Python bugs, not the parser, and fixed it without a new language;
Phase 11's `complexity-engine` found a real, measured 9–21× win and adopted Rust.
Both outcomes came from the same process; neither was assumed going in.

For the static-analysis engine specifically, that question is now settled: it's
moving to Rust, fully, not just wherever a benchmark happens to favor it — see
Phase 18's Native Analysis Core below. What survives from the old rule is the
*method*, not the *decision*: each slice is still ported, parity-tested against
the Python it replaces, and benchmarked before the call sites switch over, so the
migration never trades correctness for the sake of hitting the target faster. The
either/or framing still applies to any *other* future native-language adoption
outside this specific migration — this is a one-time, explicit exception, not a
reversal of the general rule.

**Philosophy update (2026-08-30), confirmed explicitly with the user, not assumed**:
Fortran, Assembly, and Zig stop being purely "analysis target"/"separate toolchain
concern" and become real candidates for *implementing* pieces of Sythrall's own
engine — not a decision to actually rewrite anything yet, just removing the
blanket "never an implementation language" framing the table above used to state
for Fortran/Assembly. **The same either/or rule from Phase 10/11 governs this
exactly as it would a new Rust adoption**: profile first, find an actual
bottleneck or a genuine low-level-control need, benchmark the alternative,
adopt only if the numbers (or the capability gap) justify it — no speculative
rewrites "because the language exists." No concrete candidate has been profiled
or scoped yet for Assembly or Zig as implementation languages; Fortran already
has one small proof point (`fortran_bench.rs`'s validation kernel, Phase 23),
though that was written to validate a *static claim*, not because a Sythrall
hot path itself needed Fortran's numeric performance.

## Where this is going: Computer Science Intelligence, not a linter

The honest one-line description of Sythrall today is "reads code, computes Big-O."
Phases 13–23 below are the plan for growing that into something more specific:
*Sythrall analyzes software from the mathematical/algorithmic structures underneath
it, through to the compiler, the machine code, and the hardware it runs on.* Not by
becoming a math solver or a compiler — by connecting the CS theory that already
explains *why* Sythrall's existing heuristics work (Big-O, the Chomsky-hierarchy
classifiers from Phase 8/12, the Lambda-Calculus framing on tail recursion) to the
rest of the theory those ideas come from, and building detectors for it the same
heuristic, benchmark-first way everything else in this roadmap got built:

```
                    Computer Science Engine
                              │
        ┌─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┐
        │                     │                      │
   Algorithms             Mathematics             Languages
        │                     │                      │
    Big-O/Θ/Ω            Discrete Math          Formal Languages
    Data Structures       Logic                 Grammars
    Graphs                Recurrences           Parsing Theory
        │                     │                      │
        └─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘
                              │
                       Compiler Theory
                              │
                    Lexer → AST → IR → Codegen
                              │
                        Machine Code
                              │
                             CPU
```

Each layer below is still gated by the same rules already established in this
roadmap: heuristic and explicit about it (matching the WASM-hint/CS-Engine-classifier
style, not claiming semantic proof), and any native-language adoption still requires
its own before/after benchmark (Phase 18's rule, proven twice already).

## Phases

### ✅ Phase 1 — Foundation
- [x] Flask backend + pylint, flake8, radon
- [x] TypeScript + Vite frontend (zero frameworks)
- [x] Monaco Editor + Chart.js + Docker

### ✅ Phase 2 — ML/DL Inspector
- [x] 23 library detections, 23 pipeline patterns, 25 models
- [x] 20+ issue rules (data leakage, reproducibility, framework-specific)
- [x] Score 0–100 + Mermaid pipeline diagram

### ✅ Phase 3 — Zoom/Pan + Responsive
- [x] Diagram zoom + pan, responsive layout, mobile bottom navigation

### ✅ Phase 4 — FastAPI + Project Upload
- [x] Flask → FastAPI migration, file/folder/ZIP upload, 83 tests

### ✅ Phase 5 — Static Analysis + Editor Intelligence
- [x] Multi-language AST parser (Python · TypeScript · C/C++) — no AI
- [x] Big-O estimation per function, cyclomatic complexity, WASM/Cython hints
- [x] Real-time linting (fast ~1ms, heavy ~80ms), inline diagnostics
- [x] Hover: signature + Big-O + CC + docstring
- [x] Go to Definition · Find References · Semantic autocomplete · Rename Symbol
- [x] 162 automated tests

### ✅ Phase 6 — Code Graph + Project Explorer
- [x] Import Graph · Call Graph · Circular Dependencies
- [x] Sub-phase A: graphs from sidebar; Sub-phase B: graphs from uploaded ZIP projects
- [x] Cross-folder dependency resolution, NetworkX circular detection
- [x] Interactive Force Graph *and* Complexity Heatmap dir-tree (`renderForceGraph`/`renderDirTree` in `panels/graph.ts`, custom physics engine, no D3) *(both implemented and tested — a later audit found the whole graph module had zero callers anywhere in the app; Mermaid Tree View got wired in Phase 10 below, but `generateWholeProjectDiagram` in `app.ts` still passes both `onForce`/`onDirTree` as no-ops (documented in its own docstring) — neither has a UI trigger yet)*
- [x] Project Explorer: file tree + multi-file tabs + global search + outline
- [x] 316 automated tests

### ✅ Phase 7 — Problems Panel + Live Metrics *(shipped code, wiring found incomplete years later — see Phases 10 & 12)*
- [x] **Problems panel** (VSCode-style): errors · warnings · Big-O · complexity · security findings — *originally shared a DOM container with the file-analysis view and would have clobbered it; resolved by giving it its own right-panel sub-tab (`#rpp-problems`) instead of merging the two views — see Phase 12 below*
- [x] **Live metrics bar** in editor: LOC · functions · imports · complexity score · Big-O worst · parse time (ms) *(connected in Phase 10 below — the module existed since this phase but `editor.ts` never called it)*
- [x] Auto-recovery if parser fails (safe mode + regex fallback)
- [x] Corrupt file detection
- [x] Session restore *(connected in Phase 10 below, alongside persisting the active project — restoring "which file was open" only became meaningful once there was a real project to restore its content from)*

### ✅ Phase 8 — Computer Science Engine *(direct extension of the existing analysis engine, no new architecture)*

Not just "what" the code does — *why* it behaves that way. Built entirely on data `static_parser.py` and the Big-O engine already compute:

- [x] Full complexity picture per function: Θ (tight bound), Ω (best case), O (worst case) — not just worst-case O *(Python only for now; C/C++/JS/TS still show O only)*
- [x] "Why" explanation attached to every Big-O result (e.g. *"2 nested loops — inner loop runs n times per outer iteration"*)
- [x] Recursion detected → tail-call detection + "Lambda Calculus" framing *(recursion-depth estimate skipped — depends on runtime input, not reliably computable statically)*
- [x] Regex detected → classify as Finite Automaton / Chomsky Type-3 (Regular) *(direct `re.XXX(...)` calls only — doesn't trace a `re.Pattern` saved in a variable)*
- [x] Grammar/parser-shaped code detected → Context-Free Grammar / Pushdown Automaton / Chomsky Type-2 *(heuristic: name keyword + recursion/explicit-stack shape — both signals required to keep false positives down)*
- [x] Graph traversal detected → label as DFS/BFS/topological sort, O(V+E) *(heuristic: variable-name signals — `visited`/`seen`/`explored`, `in_degree`, a queue with `.popleft()` — not real data-flow analysis)*

### ✅ Phase 9 — Integrated Terminal + Folder Explorer + Theme

Not originally on this roadmap — came directly from user feedback mid-development, folded in because each piece was small and well-scoped on its own:

- [x] **Integrated terminal**, real interactive shell over WebSocket — first use of **Rust** in the project (`terminal-server` sidecar: `portable-pty` + `axum`), token-protected, zero-friction auto-connect for local use, panel switcher between the shell and a live Logs view
- [x] **Folder explorer** in the sidebar ("+ Folder") — VSCode-style expandable tree from a real disk folder, cross-browser via `webkitdirectory` (deliberately *not* the File System Access API, which is Chromium-only)
- [x] **Light/dark theme** toggle, persisted, dark by default
- [x] [`ansimax`](https://github.com/Brashkie/ansimax) (own library) for the `npm run dev` startup banner, later reused for the terminal panel's own welcome banner (see the "Terminal v2" bullet below)
- [x] **Terminal v2 — multi-session tabs, shell picker, branded prompt, real VSCode-parity chrome**: the original terminal was a single global shell with no tabs — each browser tab of Sythrall could only ever have one shell running. `terminal-server`'s `handle_socket` already spawned one independent `PtySession` per WebSocket connection, so multi-tab needed zero backend architecture change — the frontend just opens N sockets instead of reusing one (`components/terminal.ts`, full rewrite from a module-level singleton to a `TermSession[]` array).
  - **Virtual, workspace-relative prompt (`$sythrall>`, `$sythrall/proyecto>`)** — not just a `Sythrall` prefix in front of the real OS path. Sythrall is headed toward being a SaaS/cloud product, and the real filesystem path of whatever machine happens to be hosting it (`C:\Users\PC` today, some container path tomorrow) isn't information an end user should see. `pty_session.rs::workspace_root()` captures the directory `terminal-server` itself was launched from (the same for every session that process spawns) once; `build_command` re-derives, on every prompt render, the current directory *relative* to that root — `$sythrall` at the root, `$sythrall/apps` after `cd apps`, `$sythrall/apps/web` a level deeper — and falls back to showing the real absolute path prefixed `$sythrall:` only if the user navigates outside the root entirely (never silently lies about where they are). Passed as a startup **argument** to the shell (`powershell.exe -NoLogo -NoExit -Command "function prompt {...}"` recomputing `$rel` from `$PWD.Path` on each call; `bash` via `PROMPT_COMMAND`, re-evaluated before every prompt the same way; `cmd.exe`'s `prompt` mini-language has no conditionals or string ops at all, so it keeps a plain absolute-path prompt), never as bytes written to the PTY after spawn. The first version tried writing the prompt-setup command to the PTY right after spawning — verified by hand that it produces a visible "flash": the shell isn't reading stdin yet, so its line-editor (PowerShell's PSReadLine, specifically) echoes those bytes on screen as if the user had typed them, before the real prompt appears. Passing it as a launch argument instead means the shell never renders its default prompt at all.
  - **Second real bug found and fixed the same way**: the relative-path prompt initially always showed the `$sythrall:<absolute path>` fallback, even at the workspace root itself. Root cause — `portable-pty`'s ConPTY spawn does *not* reliably inherit `terminal-server`'s own working directory the way a plain Win32 `CreateProcess` call would (confirmed by hand: a trivial `cmd.exe` launched the same way inherited the cwd correctly, but the interactive `powershell.exe`/`cmd.exe` sessions spawned through the pty layer did not). Fixed by calling `CommandBuilder::cwd()` explicitly with the same root instead of relying on inheritance — now `$PWD.Path` on the very first prompt always matches `$root` exactly.
  - **Shell picker** — new `GET /terminal/shells` (unauthenticated, same reasoning as `/terminal/token`: exposes nothing sensitive, just which shell binaries exist for the sidecar's own OS) backs a dropdown next to the "+" button; `?shell=` on the WebSocket query string is checked against a strict allow-list server-side (`resolve_shell`) — the client's string is **never** passed straight to `CommandBuilder`, since that would be a remote-code-execution vector if the terminal token ever leaked.
  - **Tabs**: close (×) per tab, double-click to rename inline. Hiding the terminal *panel* no longer kills every session — matches real terminal apps (VSCode included): a build or long-running script left in a tab keeps running in the background while the panel is hidden, exactly like a minimized terminal window. Closing a specific tab's × is what actually kills that PTY session now.
  - **`@xterm/addon-search`** (Ctrl+F, prev/next/close) and **`@xterm/addon-web-links`** (URLs printed in the shell are clickable) — neither existed before, only `addon-fit` was wired.
  - **Copy/paste, VSCode/Windows Terminal convention**: `Ctrl+C` copies the selection instead of sending SIGINT when text is selected; without a selection it passes through as `^C` like always. `Ctrl+V` pastes the clipboard as input.
  - **Auto-reconnect** — a dropped connection (sidecar restarted, network blip) retries the same session up to 5 times with a short backoff instead of leaving a dead terminal.
  - **`ansimax`-powered welcome banner** (`utils/terminalBanner.ts`), same gradient/figlet-art wordmark and color palette as the `npm run dev` console banner, for one consistent visual identity. Real bug hit and fixed while building this: `ansimax` is written for Node CLIs — merely calling `gradient()`/`ascii.banner()` threw `process is not defined` in the browser (`vite build` doesn't catch this; it only surfaces at runtime), because its color-support detection reads `process.env`/`process.stdout` unconditionally. Fixed with a tiny shim (`utils/processShim.ts`, imported before `ansimax`) that defines a minimal `globalThis.process` — and sets `FORCE_COLOR=3` deliberately, since `ansimax`'s own TTY-detection would otherwise see no real `process.stdout.isTTY` and silently print plain, uncolored text, even though xterm.js renders truecolor ANSI perfectly.
  - Expanded the xterm theme from a bare `{background, foreground}` pair to a full 16-color ANSI palette matching Sythrall's own accent colors, plus cursor/selection colors.
  - Split-pane (side-by-side terminals) and scrollback surviving a full page reload were both considered and deliberately left out of this pass — neither was asked for beyond "feel like VSCode," and each is a sizable feature on its own.
- [x] **Terminal auth redesigned — static shared secret → session tokens (JWT), base for multi-user/cloud with no login yet**: the single-secret model above (256-bit CSPRNG, constant-time comparison, served only to loopback requests) was solid for "you and your own machine," but has no path to the multi-programmer, remote-server model Sythrall is headed toward. Rebuilt as a clean auth-server/resource-server split: `apps/api` (new `routers/auth.py`) becomes the sole token issuer; `services/terminal` stops generating its own secret and becomes a pure verifier — it doesn't care who issued a token, only that its signature and expiry are valid. That's exactly the shape the system keeps the day real login exists: `apps/api` signs tokens after a real login instead of for an implicit `"local"` user, and `services/terminal` doesn't change a line.
  - HS256 with a shared secret (not RS256) — no real identity authority exists yet to justify a keypair; the secret (`SYTHRALL_AUTH_SECRET`) is generated once by `scripts/dev-banner.mjs` (runs sequentially before `concurrently` starts the parallel dev processes, avoiding a generation race) and persisted to the root `.env`.
  - Claims: `sub` (`"local"` today), `scope` (`"terminal"` — defense in depth if this secret is ever reused for another token kind), `iat`/`exp` (1-hour lifetime).
  - **Real bug caught by the test suite, not code review**: `jsonwebtoken` v11 doesn't auto-select a crypto backend — without `features = ["rust_crypto"]` in `Cargo.toml`, every `encode`/`decode` call panics at runtime. A near-identical false start followed during end-to-end verification: re-running a stale pre-fix `target/debug/terminal-server.exe` reproduced the exact same panic, until it was rebuilt.
  - **Third real bug, only caught against the user's actual running dev server**: the frontend's token fetch was modeled on the *old* endpoint's relative-URL pattern (correct there, since Vite's `/terminal` proxy really does target the Rust sidecar's port). The new token is issued by `apps/api`, and every other fetch to that backend already bypasses Vite's proxy entirely via `getApiBase()` (which hits `:8420` directly in dev — Vite's own proxy entries for that backend all point at a dead `:8000`, invisible until now since nothing used a relative fetch against them). Fixed by switching to `getApiBase()`, matching the rest of the codebase.
  - Frontend token cache moved from a bare string in `sessionStorage` to `{token, expiresAt}`, read from the JWT's own `exp` claim, discarding it a few seconds before real expiry instead of reusing a stale token blindly.
  - Verified end-to-end against real `apps/api` + `terminal-server` processes on isolated ports with a raw WebSocket client: a valid token opens a real PTY session; a tampered signature, an expired token, a wrong `scope`, and plain garbage are all rejected with 401 — all 5 cases confirmed by hand, plus 5 new Rust tests and 3 new Python tests, all green.

### ✅ Phase 10 — Rebrand + `apps/` restructure + large-project scaling + enterprise-style shell

Not originally on this roadmap — triggered by wanting the project to scale to real large codebases and to look/behave like the reference tools used during development (Aikido, Datadog, DeepSource), not by a version-number plan:

- [x] Renamed **CodeWatch PRO → Sythrall** across the project (package name, Docker services, internal identifiers, git remote)
- [x] Restructured into `apps/api` · `apps/web` · `apps/terminal` — one directory per service, tool manifests stay at repo root (Turborepo/Nx-style layout) *(`apps/terminal` moved to `services/terminal` later, splitting user-facing products from independent processes — see [`CHANGELOG.md`](CHANGELOG.md))*
- [x] **Large-project benchmark**: built a reproducible synthetic-project harness (up to 4003 files, up to 1600 functions/file) instead of assuming a rewrite was needed. Found and fixed three real O(n²) bugs — two hidden inside one-line comprehensions, one a dead computation the frontend never read. Import Graph generation on the 4003-file case went from 3.9s to 0.128s (30×) with zero new languages. Details in [`CHANGELOG.md`](CHANGELOG.md#460). The parser itself (`static_parser.py`) was already linear and needed no changes — the earlier PyO3 finding below still holds.
- [x] **Nav rail replaces the horizontal tabbar** — persistent vertical icon nav (`apps/web/src/utils/icons.ts`, inline SVGs, `stroke="currentColor"` so they follow the active theme with zero extra code), same pattern the reference tools use. `switchTab()` didn't change — the new nav items kept the same `class="tab"`/`data-tab`/`id="t-*"` convention, so this was purely a markup/CSS change.
- [x] **One active project, not four disconnected upload paths.** Before: "+ Code"/"+ Folder"/"+ Log" in the sidebar were ephemeral (lost on refresh, never touched the backend) while Projects was the only persisted path — two mental models for the same idea. Now "+ Code"/"+ Folder" create or append to the **active project** (same backend endpoints Projects already used, `project_id` now optional on `/api/upload/{files,folder}` to support appending), and Editor · Issues · Diagram · Static · Metrics all read from whichever project is active — pick a project once, work across every panel.
- [x] **Audit-driven fixes**, found using the same "does this actually have a caller" method that caught the Force Graph gap above: reconnected the Live Metrics Bar and Session Restore (`panels/problems.ts`, written for Phase 7, never called from `editor.ts`); active project now persists to `localStorage` so both restore automatically on reload; fixed the APIs tab badge (never updated); Metrics panel gained an active-project mode matching Issues/Diagram/Static.
- [x] **Problems panel** placement — still needs a decision (see Phase 7 note above) before it can be wired in without clobbering the existing file-analysis view. *(resolved in Phase 12 below — own right-panel sub-tab instead of sharing a container)*
- [x] **Nav-first restructure, round two — sidebar goes from a 72px icon rail to a 200-220px labeled, grouped, collapsible sidebar; Projects becomes a card grid; the active project becomes the real source of truth for Static/Metrics/Architecture/Findings** — a deliberate architectural UX change, not a cosmetic one, modeled on Vercel's Workspace → Projects → Project mental model (not its visual design). Sidebar sections (Overview/Project/Analysis/Services/Intelligence) already existed as mute HTML-comment groupings — made real with visible section labels (same pattern the mobile "More" menu's `.bn-more-section-label` already used) instead of inventing a new taxonomy. Renamed "Diagram"→"Architecture" and "Issues"→"Findings" (label only, no id/TabId churn). New persistent active-project indicator under the logo, clickable from any tab.
  - **Projects grid** (`panels/upload.ts`) replaces the flat list: language badges, size/file-count/relative time from the already-cheap `ProjectSummary`, and health scores shown **only when already computed this session** (new `state.projectHealthCache`) rather than forcing an expensive `parse-project` per card just to populate a list — confirmed no cheap bulk-health endpoint exists server-side before deciding this.
  - **Explicit decision against a second per-project tab bar**: the original mockup showed a breadcrumb-style sub-nav ("Project X — Overview Static Security...") replacing the main nav once inside a project. Not built — no routing/breadcrumb mechanism exists anywhere in the app today (`switchTab` is pure CSS-class toggling over always-mounted panels), and building one would mean two navigation systems reaching the same views. Generalized the Dashboard's existing "Project: X · Switch" banner (`.dash-project-head`) into a shared `utils/projectHeader.ts` atom instead, reused by Static/Metrics/Architecture/Findings.
  - **Findings (formerly Issues) gains real project-awareness**, not just a rename — it used to show only flake8/pylint lint per file even with an active, fully-analyzed project. `apis.ts::currentIssueSource()` converts the project's real security findings + structural/naming/architecture smells (the same ones Static already renders) into the existing `Issue[]` shape, reusing `smellColorSeverity()`/the smell label maps already built for Static rather than inventing a parallel severity scale.
  - **3 real staleness bugs found verifying with Playwright** (invisible clicking through slowly by hand): Metrics checked `state.results.issues.length` (only ever populated by per-file lint, never by project analysis) to decide whether to show a project summary; Static's panel re-render unconditionally overwrote the analyzed view with the "click to analyze" prompt even when cached project data existed; neither Findings nor Metrics re-rendered on tab switch at all, so a fresh analysis elsewhere went unnoticed until something else forced a repaint. Fixed by keying off `state.results.projectDashboard` instead, re-hydrating Static from cache, and adding the same `switchTab` special-case Dashboard already had.
  - **Two follow-up fixes from user feedback on this same restructure**: (1) Projects still had its old upload form (tabs/dropzone/name/submit) permanently docked above the new grid, and the grid's own "+ New Project" fired a completely different mechanism (the sidebar's folder picker) instead of reusing that form — two redundant paths to the same action, same class of bug as the earlier Analyze/ZIP duplication. Fixed: the form is hidden by default (`st.showCreateForm`), opened on demand by the one real "+ New Project" button, dismissed via Cancel/"← Back to Projects". (2) The right panel's "Flow" widget listed "APIs" as the first stage of *code* analysis (a separate concern, not part of that pipeline) and tracked its 5 steps via hand-written `setStep()` calls scoped only to the old ad-hoc pipeline, with no notion of a project at all. Replaced with `Project/Files/Analysis/Findings/Report`, computed fresh on every render from real state (`activeProjectId`, `results.projectDashboard`, a new real `projectAnalysisRunning` flag) instead of manually toggled per step — same "only when those states are real" principle applied to this widget specifically.
  - **Modular per-tab layout — the Files sidebar and Flow panel stop being mandatory everywhere, third round of feedback on the same restructure**: the user flagged twice, with screenshots, that both panels sat empty and irrelevant on the Projects screen, unlike the clean Vercel reference they kept sending. Their own rule for the fix: *"if a panel doesn't contribute information to the current context, it doesn't take up space"* — not a collapsed 40px strip "just in case," zero width, center expands to fill it (`.center` was already `flex:1`, so `display:none` alone was enough — no other CSS needed). New `applyTabLayout(tab)` + a `TAB_LAYOUT: Record<TabId, ...>` lookup (same convention `HEALTH_TAB_TARGET` already used) decide per tab: Files shown on Editor/Findings/Architecture/Metrics/Diff/Static, hidden on Dashboard/APIs/ML/Logs/Projects; Flow shown on Dashboard/Editor/Findings/Architecture/Metrics/Static, hidden on APIs/ML/Diff/Logs/Projects. **Real finding, not assumed**: the "Logs" tab today is the app's own generic activity log (`appendLog()`, called from dozens of places, plus the backend's `GET /logs`) — not a viewer for uploaded `.log` files at all (`state.results.logErrors` is computed but never rendered anywhere) — confirmed before deciding Logs doesn't need the sidebar.
  - **Correction to the first pass, from the user's own reference table**: the first version made Files dynamic on Dashboard/Findings/Architecture/Metrics/Static (hidden once a project was active, since the analysis itself no longer reads from the sidebar then) — the user corrected this: that sidebar isn't just the ad-hoc dropzone, it also doubles as a **file-tree navigator for the active project**, letting you jump to a specific file's results (e.g. on Static) — hiding it once a project is active throws that navigation away, not just a dropzone that's no longer needed. With that correction the final table is **fully static** (no longer depends on `activeProjectId` at all), which also simplified the code — the hook that recomputed layout on project activate/deactivate was removed as dead weight; only tab switches trigger it now. **Explicitly left open**: with Files always shown on Static/Metrics/Findings/Architecture, that sidebar today still shows the empty ad-hoc dropzone with a project active, not the real project-tree navigation that justified keeping it visible — building that (the active project's tree, clickable to jump to a file's findings) is a real feature on its own, not just a visibility toggle.
  - **Fourth round of feedback on this same restructure — "Editor" comes out of the nav-rail as its own destination, becomes a contextual view of the active project instead**: reasoned by the user from Sythrall already being project-centric — *"editing code isn't really a category of Sythrall; it's an action within the project"* — same pattern as VS Code (the project is the context, the file opens within it), rather than `Projects`/`Editor` as two sibling destinations. Most of the mechanics were already built without anyone finishing the wiring: `openProjectFile()` → `selectFile()` → `switchTab('editor')` (`panels/upload.ts`) already worked end to end — the real blocker was an unconditional `_goto('dashboard')` right after opening a project, which jumped to another tab before the tree ever got a chance to show (removed from both places it lived). With just that gone, picking a project and exploring its tree become the same step. `renderResult()` swaps its now-redundant "Use in editor" button for "← Back to Projects"; the `_goto()` helper was removed entirely (zero remaining callers).
  - **Real regression found only while verifying with Playwright, not while writing the change**: `switchTab()` had always highlighted the matching nav-rail item inside its own loop, regardless of whether the switch came from a click — with `#t-editor` gone from the DOM, that same loop stopped highlighting anything at all when entering the Editor (no error, but no "you are here" signal either). Fixed by highlighting "Projects" in the nav-rail while viewing the Editor — consistent with Editor being nested under Projects rather than a sibling tab — and syncing the mobile bottom-nav's "Editor" item (which *is* a real destination there) directly from `switchTab()`, so opening a file without a direct `.bn-item` click still highlights it correctly on mobile too.
  - Verified with Playwright against the production build with `/api/upload/projects`/`.../tree`/`.../file` mocked: opening a grid card keeps you on the same tab, clicking a tree file activates the Editor panel and highlights "Projects"/"Editor" on desktop/mobile respectively, "← Back to Projects" returns to the clean grid, and the remaining 10 tabs still switch cleanly with no console errors.

- [x] **Deep cleanup / hardening / UX pass over the Phase 1-10 core (2026-09-02)** - a direct request ("limpieza profunda, optimizacion de fases 1 a 10, robustecer, mas limpio, mas ordenado, mejorar frontend/UX/UI"), and the first pass in a long time to *audit* this original surface (`apps/web/src` plus the older `apps/api/routers`) rather than build on top of it. What it found is why the pass was worth doing - none of these were cosmetic:
  - **`esc()` didn't escape quotes, so every attribute interpolation was injectable.** It covered `&`, `<` and `>` - safe for text content, unsafe for attributes, and this codebase uses it in attributes everywhere (`title="..."`, `data-file="..."`). With a user-chosen value (an uploaded filename) a quote closed the attribute and opened another, turning `mi" onmouseover="alert(1)` into a live handler. Now all 5 characters. On top of that, **8 sites interpolated filenames into HTML with no escaping at all** (the 6 file selectors, 4 spots in `explorer.ts`, 2 in `analysis.ts`), and `explorer.ts` carried **its own private copy of `esc`** with the identical incomplete escaping - deleted, it uses the shared one now. The duplicated `<option>` builder became one `helpers.fileOptionsHtml()`.
  - **`POST /metrics/live` blocked the event loop on every keystroke.** It is the most-called endpoint in the app (the editor fires it while typing) and it ran `ast.parse` plus whole-file regex synchronously inside an `async def`: while it parsed, the entire backend froze - terminal, uploads and any in-flight analysis waiting behind it. Extracted to `_compute_metrics_sync` and wrapped in `run_in_threadpool`, same for `/metrics/validate`'s own `ast.parse`. Fourth appearance of this bug class in this repo, and by frequency the worst place it could have been hiding.
  - **The log stream grew the DOM without bound.** The in-memory buffer had been capped at 300 forever, but rows were only ever appended: over a long session `#log-stream` accumulated thousands of nodes - memory that only goes up, and an increasingly expensive `scrollTop = scrollHeight` forcing layout over all of them. Now trimmed to the same cap; verified in the browser that 500 log lines leave exactly 300 rows instead of 502.
  - **The light theme was half-implemented in three places.** Toasts and log rows had the dark palette written as literal hex, so in light mode a toast was a dark box with neon green text and the log stream was neon on white. Monaco - the largest surface on screen - stayed black entirely, because it cannot read CSS variables. Fixed with tokens for the first two, a new `sythrall-light` Monaco theme for the third, switched live through a `THEME_EVENT` that `utils/theme.ts` dispatches (rather than making that module import Monaco, since it runs before the editor exists). The terminal stays dark on purpose - a dark terminal inside a light IDE is the convention, not an oversight. `initTheme` also stopped forcing dark on a first visit: it now respects `prefers-color-scheme`, with both `localStorage` accesses guarded for private mode.
  - **The 5 native `prompt`/`confirm` calls are gone**, replaced by `utils/dialog.ts` + `.app-dialog`. The native ones froze the main thread mid-drag-and-drop, ignored the theme (they are browser chrome), and on mobile were an OS modal. The replacements are themed, close on Escape, confirm on Enter, close on backdrop click, return focus to whatever opened them, and carry `role="dialog"`/`aria-modal`. Their copy also stopped being generic - the delete confirmation now says what gets deleted and that it cannot be undone.
  - Smaller order/cleanliness items: `.empty-icon` was dead CSS (its only reference was a comment) - removed; the log toolbar's "Scroll" label clipped against the right edge at 1400px (missing `nowrap`/`flex-shrink:0`, and its inline style became a class); inconsistent glyphs normalized to the set the rest of the app already uses; toasts gained `role="status"`/`aria-live="polite"`, so a screen reader announces action results that were previously silent.
  - `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check`/`ruff` clean, **328 backend tests with no regressions**. Verified end to end in a real browser by uploading a file named with an embedded quote and event handler: **0 injected handlers** with the name intact inside its `title` attribute, **0 native dialogs**, the app's own dialog opening and closing on Escape, the system theme honored from first load, Monaco following the toggle (white to `#0e1225`), the "Scroll" label fully visible, and zero console errors.

### ✅ Phase 11 — `radon` replaced by an own Rust sidecar (`complexity-engine`)

Not a performance rewrite — the trigger was not wanting to depend on a third-party library's internals for something the project can own outright, for a codebase one person maintains solo. Measured before claiming any speed win, following the same benchmark-first approach as the large-project scaling work above:

- [x] **New Rust sidecar `apps/complexity`** *(moved to `services/complexity` later — see [`CHANGELOG.md`](CHANGELOG.md))*, same architecture as `terminal-server` (persistent process, HTTP, not a subprocess-per-call and not a PyO3 native extension) — `rustpython-parser` for the AST, this project's own code for cyclomatic complexity (McCabe), Maintainability Index (Coleman-Oman formula), and raw line metrics. Both sidecars now share one root `Cargo.toml` (2 `[[bin]]`, 1 `[lib]`).
- [x] **Benchmarked with Criterion against real `radon`**, not assumed: 10 functions — 0.42ms vs 8.97ms; 100 functions — 4.7ms vs 89ms; 1000 functions — 102ms vs 899ms. 9–21× faster, measured on the same synthetic files both ways.
- [x] `radon==6.0.1` removed from `requirements.txt`; `services/complexity_client.py` calls the sidecar over HTTP and degrades gracefully (empty complexity/MI, no crash) if it isn't running — same optional-capability pattern as flake8/pylint.
- [x] Fixed a real startup-race bug found while wiring this in: the old design cached "is the tool available" once at backend startup, which meant a slow first `cargo build` could leave the capability stuck `false` for the rest of the session even after the sidecar came up. The actual analysis call sites no longer gate on that cached flag — they call the sidecar live and fall back gracefully per-request instead.
- [x] Rust unit tests (`cargo test`) for complexity/MI/raw-metrics against hand-computed values, run by the same CI job that already built `terminal-server`.

### ✅ Phase 12 — Closed the last 3 CS Engine classifiers + Problems Panel placement

Closes out the Phase 8 CS Engine roadmap items and the open Problems Panel decision from Phase 7/Phase 10:

- [x] **Regex → Chomsky Type-3 (Regular)**: detects direct `re.compile/match/search/findall/...` calls per function. Honest about its limit — doesn't trace a `re.Pattern` saved in a variable, only direct `re.XXX(...)` calls.
- [x] **Grammar/parser-shaped code → Chomsky Type-2 (Context-Free)**: heuristic requires *both* a name signal (`parse`/`grammar`/`tokenize`/`lexer`/...) *and* a shape signal (recursion or an explicit append/pop stack pattern) — either signal alone produced too many false positives in testing (a plain recursive `factorial` isn't a parser just because it's recursive).
- [x] **Graph traversal → BFS/DFS/Topological Sort, O(V+E)**: heuristic on variable names (`visited`/`seen`/`explored`, `in_degree`) plus a queue (`.popleft()`) vs. stack/recursion shape to tell BFS from DFS. Same explicitly-heuristic, not-semantic-analysis style as the existing WASM-hint detector (`_wasm_hints_python`) — the new code follows its exact conventions.
- [x] **Problems Panel got its own home**: a 4th right-panel sub-tab (`Flujo · Análisis · Servidor · Problems`, `#rpp-problems`/`#problems-content`) instead of trying to merge it into the existing file-analysis view — resolves the DOM-container conflict documented since Phase 7 without touching that view's richer content (Pylint score, MI, per-function table). Wired into `editor.ts::applyMarkers()`, exactly where `panels/problems.ts` had documented the intended integration point since it was written.
- [x] Removed 3 confirmed-dead exports with zero callers, re-verified twice (`editor.ts::copyEditorContent`, `explorer.ts::explorerMarkModified`/`explorerRefresh`). A longer list of exports with no *visible* caller was found in the same pass but deliberately left alone — not enough certainty they aren't API surface for a feature that hasn't landed yet (same situation Force Graph was in before it got wired).

---

The phases below are the 11 conceptual pillars of "Computer Science Intelligence" (see above) plus two closing phases that sit outside that theory — one architectural, one productization — grouped by how well-founded they are, not by how far in the future they sit. Phases 13–14 are the most direct continuation of what Phase 8/12 already shipped; 15–17 are genuinely new theoretical ground; 18–20 build out the language-philosophy table; 21–22 fill a gap the original 9-pillar pass missed entirely (security, code quality) — same heuristic, confidence-scored style as everything else here, not a new methodology; 23 is architecturally different from everything above it (needs a running process, not source text); 24–25 close the loop — 24 is the API boundary that would let Phases 13–23 grow as plugins instead of every analyzer landing directly in `apps/api`, 25 is how the product actually reaches people once there's something to ship.

### ✅ Phase 13 — Algorithmic Intelligence *(extends Phase 8's O/Θ/Ω engine, not a new architecture)*

Phase 8 already computes worst-case O, best-case Ω, and tight-bound Θ per function — a specific, standard application of asymptotic notation in algorithm analysis (matching the convention used by CLRS and most algorithms texts). Worth making explicit rather than implicit, since the general definitions are broader than that one use:

- [x] **Asymptotic notation reference**, surfaced wherever a Big-O result already appears: a collapsible `<details>` reference table above the Static panel's Big-O table (`panels/static.ts::_renderBigOTable`) covers all five symbols — O (upper bound), Ω (lower bound), Θ (tight bound), o (strict upper bound), ω (strict lower bound) — general definition first, honest that `o`/`ω` aren't computed (no reliable static heuristic distinguishes "strict" from the tight case). The Python hover tooltip (`_build_hover_python`) gets the same explanation as a one-line footnote under its O/Θ/Ω table. JS/TS hover intentionally untouched — it only computes O, not Θ/Ω, so the fuller legend doesn't apply there yet.
- [x] **Space complexity** alongside time complexity, built in Rust and Python from the same pass this time (`services/complexity/src/space.rs` + `static_parser.py::_infer_space_python`, both wired the same day) — same heuristic AST-based approach as the time engine (no execution), but the signal is *what auxiliary structure gets built*, not *how many times a loop runs*: a loop that only accumulates into a scalar (`total += x`) is O(1) space even though it's O(n) time. Detects growing collections (`.append`/`.add`/`.update`/`.insert`/`.extend`, or `d[k] = v` subscript-assignment) at loop-nesting depth (O(n) at depth 1, O(n²) at depth 2 — a matrix built in nested loops), nested comprehensions (`[[0 for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(n)]`), and recursion-stack depth (O(log n) for binary-split recursion reusing `bigo.rs`'s existing split detector, O(n) for linear recursion, since Python doesn't optimize tail calls). Byte-for-byte parity confirmed between the Rust and Python implementations on all 8 cases by hand. Wired into `/intel/hover` and `/intel/analyze` (Rust-first, Python fallback, same gate as Big-O) and a new **Space** column in the Static panel's Big-O table. Benchmarked with Criterion vs. real `_parse_python()` timing on the same synthetic files: 10 functions — 0.77ms vs 18.0ms (23.4×); 100 — 9.17ms vs 212.6ms (23.2×); 1000 — 254ms vs 2083ms (8.2×) — and reported honestly that adding this pass slowed `analyze_rich()` itself by 30–59% against its own prior baseline (two more full-body walks per function stack up), not just the win against Python.
- [x] **Recurrence relation recognition** for divide-and-conquer functions, **Rust-only** (`services/complexity/src/bigo.rs::resolve_master_theorem`) — deliberately not mirrored into the Python fallback (see below). Before this, a recursive function with no loop of its own (`merge_sort`, a recursive binary search) fell straight through every branch of the existing loop-depth heuristic to its generic default (`O(n)`, "base case"), because that heuristic only reasoned about loop nesting and a single `is_recursive` bool. Recognizes `T(n) = a·T(n/2) + f(n)` and resolves it via the Master Theorem's three cases, showing the recurrence itself (`T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Θ(n)`) alongside the resolved `Θ(n log n)` — not just the answer. `a` (subproblems per call) is its own small piece of reasoning, not a reused counter: it treats `if/elif/else` branches as mutually-exclusive alternatives (`max` across branches) and sequential statements as additive (`sum`), so `merge_sort`'s two unconditional recursive calls give `a=2` while a recursive binary search's two calls in mutually-exclusive `elif`/`else` branches correctly give `a=1` (`T(n)=T(n/2)+O(1)` → `Θ(log n)`, not `T(n)=2T(n/2)+O(1)` → `Θ(n)`). `f(n)`'s degree comes from whichever is larger: the function's own loop nesting, or — via a second pass over the file's already-analyzed functions — the Big-O of a helper it calls (`merge_sort` calling `merge()`, already resolved to `O(n)` on its own). 5 Rust tests (`rich::tests`) cover the real cases (`merge_sort`, recursive binary search, non-divide-and-conquer recursion). **Not ported to `static_parser.py`**: an initial pass did build full parity, but with the Rust sidecar as the primary path (running whenever available) and Python only a degraded fallback, maintaining two full implementations of this specific piece wasn't worth it — the pure-Python fallback now always reports `recurrence: None` for every function (confirmed by 4 Python tests) and keeps answering with the pre-existing generic loop/recursion heuristic, same as before this phase. Wired into `/intel/hover` (Rust-rich branch only — the row simply doesn't appear when the sidecar is down) and the Static panel's existing recursion badge tooltip — no new UI surface, reuses the hover that already existed for `recursion_note`.

### ✅ Phase 14 — Data Structures & Graph Intelligence *(same heuristic-pattern style as the existing WASM-hint/dead-code detectors)*

Direct continuation of the CS Engine (Phase 8/12) — same static-analysis approach, no new architecture. Not just naming the structure, explaining it:

- [x] **First slice: Heap, Trie, Fenwick Tree (BIT) — 3 of 10, chosen by detection confidence, not list order** — new Rust module `datastructures.rs`, same heuristic style as `classifiers.rs` (regex/grammar/graph_traversal), built Rust-first from scratch (genuinely new feature, not a Phase 18 port). **Heap** (function-level, joins `RichFunction`'s existing `regex_class`/`grammar_class`/`graph_traversal`): `heapq.heappush`/`heappop`/`heapify`/etc. usage, same "detect stdlib attribute calls" pattern as the regex classifier. **Trie** and **Fenwick Tree** (class-level — the first classifier fields `RichClass` has ever had): name-keyword matching, with Trie backed by a shape fallback (insert+search methods + a dict-valued `self.X = {}` attribute) and Fenwick backed by something stronger than naming — the `i & (-i)` low-bit isolation idiom, detected as raw AST shape (`BinOp(BitAnd)` over a value and its `UnaryOp(USub)` negation) regardless of what the class is named, the most literal instance of "detect from AST shape" this phase asks for. Wired into the Static panel: a new `.bigo-datastruct` badge next to functions (alongside Regex/CFG/graph-traversal badges) and, for the first time, next to classes too. Verified with 12 Rust tests (8 unit + 4 end-to-end via `analyze_rich`) and 6 new Python tests (`TestDataStructureDetection` in `test_static_analysis.py`) hitting `/static/parse` against the real sidecar, plus live Playwright verification of the actual badges/tooltips rendering in the Static panel.
- [x] **Second slice: AVL Tree, Red-Black Tree, Bloom Filter — 6 of 10** — pure extension of `data_structure_info`, zero new wiring anywhere else (the badge/field plumbing from the first slice already generalizes). **AVL**/**Red-Black**: same class shape (`left`/`right` children + a `rotate*` method — a self-balancing BST) distinguished by which extra bookkeeping attribute is present when the class name doesn't already say which — `height`/`bf`/`balance` for AVL, `color`/`red`/`black` for Red-Black; a balanced-BST shape with *neither* attribute triggers nothing (no way to tell which one it is, so it doesn't guess). **Bloom Filter**: unlike Trie/Fenwick, name is required here, not optional — `add`+`contains` alone is too generic a shape (any collection wrapper has it), so this one needs name AND shape together, same discipline `grammar_info` already established. Verified with 10 more Rust tests (7 unit + 3 end-to-end) and 6 more Python tests, including explicit negative cases (a balanced-BST-shaped class with no height/color attribute stays unclassified; `add`+`contains` without "bloom" in the name stays unclassified) proving the false-positive guards actually hold.
- [x] **Third slice: Segment Tree, B-Tree, Skip List, HashMap — 10 of 10, this bullet closed** — same pure extension pattern, still zero new wiring. All 4 require the class name (none has a shape distinctive enough alone — `build`+`update`+`query` also describes a Fenwick without that name; `insert`+`search` also describes a Trie or any BST). **HashMap** additionally requires a method with "hash" in its name — name alone isn't enough, because any `dict`-wrapping class could be called "HashMap" without implementing real hashing/bucketing, and flagging Python's own ubiquitous `dict` usage would be pure noise, not signal (the same reasoning that led to deleting the dead `circular_deps` field earlier in Phase 18, applied here proactively instead of after the fact). Verified with 10 more Rust tests (6 unit + 4 end-to-end) and 6 more Python tests, including the HashMap-name-without-hash-method negative case. **144 Rust tests total, 480 backend tests total, Clippy clean.** No frontend changes needed for slices 2 or 3 — the `.bigo-datastruct` badge and its wiring, verified live with Playwright in the first slice, already render whatever string the backend returns.
- [x] For each match: complexity (time *and* space), typical operations, use cases, tradeoffs vs. the alternatives — the same "why," not just "what," standard the Big-O engine already holds itself to — **done for all 10**, each badge's tooltip carries this
- [x] **Graph algorithms as first-class operations, first slice: centrality/hub detection** — new `centrality` graph type reusing the import graph already built for the other 4 (Import/Call/Circular/Heatmap). A file is flagged as a "hub" when it's in the top 5 by in-degree *and* has at least 2 dependents (avoids labeling a file with a single import as a hub). Wired into the Diagram panel's project-graph dropdown — reachable by a user, not just an API response, the same "does this have a UI trigger" discipline the Force Graph gap taught this project. Originally Python-only (`networkx`) with a note that it was blocked on Phase 18's Graph Engine porting the import graph *construction* to Rust first — **that dependency is now resolved**: `_build_centrality_graph` (`routers/graph.py`) calls `graph.rs::build_centrality_graph()` in the Rust sidecar (Phase 18's Graph Engine, second slice), see that phase's entry for detail.
- [x] **Nested-structure interaction detection — this bullet closed, Phase 14 complete** — scoped to the single concrete, highest-value, lowest-false-positive-risk case rather than the full "any structure interacting with any structure" scope: an accumulator initialized as an empty list literal (`seen = []`), checked with `in` (membership test) *and* grown with `.append(...)` inside the same loop — the textbook "should've been a `set()`" smell. Each membership check on a list is O(n); doing that once per loop iteration that also grows the same list is a hidden O(n²) traversal, which is exactly the "how structures combine" framing this bullet asked for, not per-function complexity in isolation. New `smells.rs::check_quadratic_list_membership` (Fase 22's `StructuralSmell` shape, kind `quadratic_list_membership`) — no type inference needed: requiring the empty-list literal at initialization is what rules out false positives on dicts/sets (where `in` is already O(1), nothing to flag). Wired into `function_smells` alongside the other 3 function-level smells; frontend reuses the existing generic `StructuralSmell` rendering (`static.ts`'s `SMELL_LABEL`/`SMELL_KIND_ORDER` extended with the new kind, `client.ts`'s `StructuralSmell['kind']` union extended) — no new UI surface needed, same badge/list the other smells already render into. Verified with 5 new Rust unit tests (positive case, 3 explicit negative cases: membership-without-append, append-without-membership, `set()` instead of a list) and 4 new Python tests against `/static/parse`. 149 Rust tests total, Clippy clean.

### ✅ Phase 15 — Mathematical Intelligence *(discrete math used to explain programs, not a math solver)*

Not "Sythrall proves theorems." The discrete-math concepts that already underpin the rest of this roadmap, made explicit where they're already implicit in what Sythrall detects:

- [x] **Sets & relations — the graph-traversal classifier's O(V+E) claim now verified against a real `set()`, not assumed from the variable name** — the honest gap this bullet names was real: `classifiers.rs::graph_info` flagged BFS/DFS purely by checking whether a variable was *named* `visited`/`seen`/`explored`, with zero check that it was actually a `set` — meaning the O(V+E) claim (which only holds if membership testing is O(1)) was resting entirely on a naming convention. `GraphInfo` gains `visited_is_confirmed_set`, backed by a new `assigned_as_set_literal_or_constructor()` that looks for an actual `set()`/`frozenset()` call or a non-empty `{...}` set literal assigned to the candidate name — the same kind of "fresh constructor" pattern `purity.rs` already established for a different purpose. When confirmed, `graph_note()` explicitly connects the dots to set theory: membership (`in`) is the set-theoretic ∈ relation, backed by a hash table (O(1)), which is *why* the traversal is O(V+E) and not O(V²) — explicitly naming the same reasoning that already flags a list-backed `visited` as a hidden O(n²) (`smells::check_quadratic_list_membership`). When it can't be confirmed (the tracker might be a list, or comes from an untraced source like a parameter), the note says so honestly instead of asserting a complexity bound it can't back up — the same "don't guess, say what you can't confirm" discipline the Functions/purity bullet established. Zero new UI: this only changes the *text* of a tooltip that already existed (`graph_traversal_note`), no new field, no new badge. 9 new Rust tests (`classifiers::tests` — this module also had zero direct tests before, same gap as `bigo.rs` last row, now closed) and 2 new Python tests (`TestSetsAndRelationsGraphTraversal` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar — all 531 backend tests pass unmodified, no frontend changes needed at all. **248 Rust tests total (239 previous + 9), 531 backend tests total (529 previous + 2), Clippy clean across `--all-targets`.**
- [x] **Functions — pure vs. side-effecting classification, intra-procedural and conservative by design** — new `services/complexity/src/purity.rs`, wired into `rich.rs::build_function()` (`RichFunction` gains `is_pure`/`purity_note`). Explicitly scoped to what a single function's own body can prove, not a whole-program analysis: it does not descend into functions it calls (that would be interprocedural, out of scope), so "pure" means "nothing in this function's own body proves it mutates external state or does I/O," not "this function and everything it calls are pure." Flags exactly 3 provable cases — a known-impure builtin called directly (`print`/`input`/`open`/`exec`/`eval`/etc.), a `global`/`nonlocal` declaration, or a parameter mutated via attribute/subscript assignment or a mutating method call (`.append`/`.update`/`.pop`/etc.) — while deliberately staying silent (not guessing) on the genuinely ambiguous case: a local variable of unknown provenance (`y = helper(x); y.append(1)`) could be a fresh object or an alias of something external, and there's no way to tell without following `helper`'s return value, so it's simply not flagged either way rather than guessed at. Reassigning a parameter's own name (`x = x + 1`) and mutating a freshly-constructed local (`result = []; result.append(...)`) are both correctly recognized as *not* impure — the same object-identity distinction that makes the feature useful instead of flagging nearly every function that touches a list. Same "aggregation over what's already computed, informational not a defect" treatment as the data-structure/graph-traversal classifiers: no new endpoint, a `.bigo-purity` badge shown only when a function IS proven pure (matching how `.bigo-datastruct` only shows when a structure is detected, not for the "no structure" case) — the `var(--info)` tier, since impurity is the normal/expected state for most functions and isn't worth flagging as a defect. Scoped to Python only, matching every other CS-Engine-style classifier to date (regex/grammar/graph-traversal/data-structure are all Python-only too — this isn't a new boundary, it's the existing one). No-sidecar skeleton path (`_skeleton_functions_python`) gets `is_pure: False` explicitly (never claims unproven purity) rather than omitting the field. 12 new Rust unit tests (`purity::tests`) + 2 end-to-end (`rich::tests`) and 4 new Python tests (`TestFunctionPurity` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar — `tsc --noEmit` clean, all 525 backend tests pass unmodified. **223 Rust tests total (209 previous + 14), 525 backend tests total (521 previous + 4), Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Combinatorics — nested-loop cardinality reframed as the rule of product, purely explanatory, zero new detection** — new `bigo::combinatorics_note()`, computed inside `bigo::full()` right where `loop_analysis()` already produces the loop-nesting `depth` that Big-O classification was already using — no new AST walk, no new signal, just an explanation attached to a number that was already being computed. For pure nested-loop cases (not recursion, not the binary-split/log-n case — those don't have a "rule of product over loops" reading), `depth` nested loops each ranging over `n` get a note connecting it to the actual combinatorics: "the rule of product (multiplication principle): n^depth total combinations, the same count as ordered depth-tuples with repetition from n elements" — 1 loop gets a shorter "single pass, no combinatorial interaction" note instead. Surfaced as a hover tooltip on the existing Big-O badge in the Static panel (`static.ts`) — deliberately zero new badge/column, matching the phase's "explanatory note, not new UI" framing for the second time running (same trick as De Morgan). 8 new Rust tests (`bigo::tests` — this module had zero direct tests before, only exercised indirectly through `rich.rs`; closes that gap too) and 4 new Python tests (`TestCombinatoricsNote` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar — `tsc --noEmit` clean, all 529 backend tests pass unmodified. **239 Rust tests total (231 previous + 8), 529 backend tests total (525 previous + 4), Clippy clean across `--all-targets`.**
- [x] **Boolean algebra — De Morgan simplification hints, shipped Rust-first, zero new UI surface** — new `smells.rs::check_de_morgan_simplifiable()`, wired into `rich.rs::function_smells()` alongside the other 5 structural smells. Detects `not (a and b)`/`not (a or b)` (exactly 2 operands — with 3+, "simplified" would stop being one clean application of the law and start describing something more confusing than the original) via `Expr::UnaryOp(Not)` wrapping `Expr::BoolOp`, reusing the existing `StructuralSmell` shape (`kind`/`name`/`line`/`message`) instead of inventing a new finding category, endpoint, or panel — same trick Phase 14's `quadratic_list_membership` used to ship with zero new plumbing. Deliberately a readability note, not a rewrite, per the phase's own framing: the message explains the equivalence, nothing rewrites the conditional. Frontend touch is the same 2 spots every new smell kind needs (`static.ts`'s `SMELL_LABEL`/`SMELL_KIND_ORDER`, `client.ts`'s `StructuralSmell['kind']` union) — given a color (`var(--info)`, the "informational" tier rather than a defect-severity color, since this isn't a Fowler/Martin code smell like the other 5) new to this map specifically because a math-explainer note isn't the same kind of finding as god-object/long-function. 6 new Rust tests (`smells::tests`, both connectives + the 3-operand non-trigger + plain `not` + nested-inside-`if` case) and 4 new Python tests (`TestDeMorganSimplifiable` in `test_structural_smells.py`) against the real sidecar — `tsc --noEmit` clean, all 521 backend tests pass unmodified. **209 Rust tests total (203 previous + 6), 521 backend tests total (517 previous + 4), Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Proof-by-induction framing — closes the phase, but first had to correct its own premise: the "base case" detection this bullet assumed already existed, didn't** — `recursion.rs` before this row only ever detected the recursive call itself (the inductive step); there was no code anywhere checking for a base case, so this bullet's real first task was building that primitive, not just attaching a note to something already there. New `has_base_case()`: walks the function's own returns (same scope-inclusion as `analyze()`'s existing `is_recursive` count, for consistency) looking for at least one `return` whose value contains no self-call anywhere in its expression tree — not just in tail position, so `return n * factorial(n-1)` correctly counts as "this return recurses" even though the self-call is wrapped in a `BinOp`, not the return's top-level expression. That last part needed a small new primitive of its own: `walk::walk_expr_tree()`, a public single-expression entry point into the same depth-guarded walker the whole engine already shares, so `recursion.rs` didn't need to fabricate a synthetic `Stmt::Expr` just to reuse `walk_stmts`. `induction_note()` fires only when *both* halves are confirmed (`is_recursive && has_base_case`) — silence, not a guess, when a function recurses with no return found that avoids it (the same shape a genuine infinite-recursion bug would have, which this heuristic correctly declines to bless with an induction framing). Explicitly a shape observation, not a generated proof: doesn't check the base case is reachable or that the recursion is well-founded, matching the phase's own stated boundary from the start. Surfaced by appending to the recursion badge's existing tooltip (`static.ts`) — zero new badge, same trick as every other row this phase. `recursion.rs` (like `bigo.rs`/`classifiers.rs` in the two rows above) had zero direct tests before this — 9 new ones close that gap while covering the real feature. 3 new Python tests (`TestProofByInduction` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar — `tsc --noEmit`/`vite build` clean, all 534 backend tests pass unmodified. **257 Rust tests total (248 previous + 9), 534 backend tests total (531 previous + 3), Clippy clean across `--all-targets`. This closes Phase 15 — all 5 bullets shipped.**

- [x] **Optimal-control preconditions — linear algebra checked in the CODE, not a control-theory engine** (2026-09-02). Came out of a conversation about a Moving Target Defense paper for flight control (LQR + Riccati + entropy-weighted softmax switching). The conclusion of that conversation is what defines the scope: **Sythrall analyzes source code, not plant models.** An engine that receives A/B matrices and computes controllers would be a different product wearing this one's name. What *is* Sythrall's job is reading the code that calls a control solver and checking the mathematical preconditions that code silently assumes.
  - New `services/complexity/src/control.rs`. `scipy.linalg.solve_continuous_are(A, B, Q, R)`, its discrete twin, and `control.lqr`/`dlqr` all require, for the result to mean anything: compatible dimensions (`A` is n×n, `B` is n×m, `Q` is n×n, `R` is m×m), `Q`/`R` symmetric, `R` positive definite (it appears inverted in `R⁻¹BᵀP`), `Q` positive semi-definite. **Neither library validates all of that** — a singular or misshapen `R` returns garbage or a cryptic exception, and the bug is found flying, not compiling.
  - **What is provable and what isn't, which is the whole design.** With matrices written as literals in the same scope, dimension mismatch and asymmetry are *visible*, not estimated — confidence `High`. Positive definiteness is **not** decidable from literals without computing eigenvalues, but its **necessary** condition is: a positive-definite matrix cannot have an entry ≤ 0 on its diagonal, nor a PSD one an entry < 0. Violating a necessary condition **proves** the matrix isn't PD/PSD, so that finding is `High` too — and the message says outright that satisfying it proves nothing. There is deliberately no "R looks fine" finding: this module reports proven violations, it never certifies correctness.
  - Reads the matrix forms people actually write: nested list literals (with or without an `np.array`/`asarray`/`matrix` wrapper), `np.eye`/`np.identity`, `np.diag([...])` and `np.zeros((n,m))` — a zeroed `R` is never positive definite, so being able to read it is worth the few lines. Unary minus is handled explicitly, without which no matrix containing a negative value would parse at all, which is precisely the case that matters most for catching a non-PSD `Q`.
  - Scope-aware (`walk_stmts_own_scope`, same as taint tracking): an `R` defined inside another function is not used to resolve a call at module level. And it runs both **per function and once over the module**, because in control code the normal case *is* module level — a script that defines A/B/Q/R and calls the solver — so without the module pass this analysis would see nothing in the typical file.
  - If the matrices aren't readable (loaded from disk, passed as parameters, built by another function), there is no finding. Nothing is guessed.
  - 18 new Rust tests. **735 Rust tests total (717 previous + 18)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **328 backend tests** with no regressions (the Python side is one relay key; notebooks get it for free since they run the same engine). Verified live in a real browser against a script with one correct LQR call and four broken ones: exactly **4 findings** — `R` 2×2 against a single-column `B`, `R = zeros` on the diagonal, `Q = diag([1, -4])`, and a non-symmetric `Q` — with the correct call and a function loading its matrices from `.npy` both correctly silent.
  - Explicitly not attempted, and named rather than left implicit: **stabilizability of `(A,B)` and detectability of `(A, Q^{1/2})`** — the actual minimal conditions for a unique stabilizing solution — need rank/eigenvalue computation, so they are not statically provable from literals. Worth noting for whoever picks this up: those are the *correct* conditions, weaker than the "full controllability and observability" that control papers often state, and the difference matters for the MTD case that started this, where degraded actuator configurations typically lose full controllability while remaining perfectly stabilizable.

- [x] **The other half of the same paper: the Gibbs selection — and the claim about it that the code cannot support** (2026-09-05). The MTD row above stopped at the LQR/Riccati side. ADMIRE doesn't stop there: after solving for the controller it picks the next configuration with `p_i ∝ exp(-V_i/ε)` — the exact minimizer of `Σp_iV_i - εH(p)` — and raises ε to buy unpredictability. In code that's a hand-written softmax over a cost vector, and it has silent preconditions of its own. Three more checks in `control.rs`, riding the same `ControlFinding` stream, so **zero new plumbing: no new endpoint, no Python change, no new frontend section** — three labels in `CONTROL_FINDING_LABEL` and nothing else.
  - **`gibbs_nonpositive_temperature`** — ε literal, resolved directly or through a variable in scope. At ε = 0 the two runtimes disagree in a way worth spelling out: Python floats raise `ZeroDivisionError`, NumPy arrays don't raise at all — they give `inf`/`nan` with a `RuntimeWarning`, the distribution goes all-`nan`, and the selection silently becomes arbitrary. At ε < 0 nothing fails *at all*: the exponent flips sign, the Gibbs distribution concentrates on the **maximum** cost instead of the minimum, and the code keeps returning valid probabilities that sum to 1 while systematically choosing the worst option.
  - **`softmax_unstable`** — a hand-written softmax with no max-subtraction. The finding is only raised when the `exp` is actually **normalized** (divided by a sum), because an `exp` on its own is a decay curve, not a distribution — without that gate every `np.exp` in every codebase would be a finding. Three normalization shapes are recognized: inline (`exp(x)/sum(exp(x))`), two-step (`w = exp(x)` … `p = w/w.sum()`), and in-place (`p /= p.sum()`). Stabilization counts whether it's inside the `exp` or one line above it (`z = v - v.max()` then `exp(z/eps)`), and `logsumexp` counts as well as `max` — both are correct idioms, and flagging them would mean reporting exactly the code that did the right thing.
  - **`random_choice_bad_probabilities`** — the randomized-switching step itself. `numpy.random.choice(a, p=probs)` rejects a negative entry and rejects a vector that doesn't sum to 1; when `probs` is a literal, that `ValueError` is visible without running anything. `random.choice` from the stdlib can't collide with this: it has no probability parameter, so requiring `p=` (or a 4th positional, a signature only NumPy has) is enough to tell them apart.
  - **The honest limits, each one pinned by a test.** The **sign of the exponent** is deliberately not checked: `exp(+V/ε)` is wrong if `V` is a cost and right if it's a reward, and the code doesn't say which — guessing would be inventing. `exp(-β·V)` uses the **inverse** temperature, where `β = 0` is the uniform limit and perfectly legitimate, so the multiplicative form is left alone. A name that is assigned a literal *and* later something unreadable (`eps = 0.0` … `eps = schedule(t)`) is dropped from the map entirely rather than reported against its stale value. And `scipy.special.softmax` triggers nothing, because it already does the shift internally.
  - **What the module says about ε and resilience, written into the module doc.** Raising ε raises the entropy of the distribution — that is arithmetic. That more entropy implies more resilience against a real attacker is a hypothesis *about the attacker*, not something readable in the code nor a consequence of the formula. Sythrall reports violated conditions; it does not grade a policy.
  - 18 new Rust tests. **817 Rust tests total (799 previous + 18)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** unchanged — and unchanged is the point: the Python side already relays `control_findings` by key, so a new kind costs nothing there.

### ✅ Phase 16 — Formal Language Intelligence *(completes the Chomsky hierarchy Phase 8/12 already started)*

Phase 8/12 already ships two tiers of this: regex → Chomsky Type-3 (Regular), grammar/parser-shaped code → Chomsky Type-2 (Context-Free). This phase completes the hierarchy as an educational and classification reference:

- [x] **Type-1 (Context-Sensitive) / Linear-Bounded Automaton, added cautiously — Type-0 deliberately left undetected, and that's the honest answer, not a gap** — this bullet's premise (find a concrete AST signal, document honestly where there isn't one) forced a real decision before writing any code: unlike Type-3/Type-2 (grounded in AST shapes with real theory behind them — regex literally IS a regular-language engine, a stack+recursion parser literally IS pushdown-automaton-shaped), there is no honest way to claim "this function processes a context-sensitive language" from AST shape alone — that classification is a property of the whole language something recognizes, not one function's shape. Confirmed with the user before committing to an approach (a genuine judgment call about product honesty, not an implementation detail) rather than either inventing something speculative or skipping the bullet outright. What shipped: new `classifiers.rs::semantic_analysis_info()`, recognizing the classic pattern real compilers add on top of a context-free parse — a symbol table that *grows* (assignments into an initially-empty dict) and *gates* a decision on whether something is already/not-yet declared, *rejecting* (raising) on violation. The critical design point, tested explicitly: this is one AST-shape away from memoization (`if key not in cache: cache[key] = compute(); return cache[key]`), which never raises on a cache miss — a real correctness/context check does. Labeled `"Type-1 (informal)"` everywhere (the badge itself reads `Type-1?`, not `Type-1`) and every note explicitly says "resembles... not a formal classification" — the hedge is load-bearing, not decoration. Type-0 (Turing Machine) gets **no per-function detector at all**, by design: general-purpose recursion/control-flow with unbounded state is the *default* computational model for any Turing-complete language (which Python already is) — forcing a per-function "Type-0 detected" badge would be either meaningless (it'd fire on almost everything) or arbitrary (picking some subset to call out with no principled line), so the honest move is explaining this in the reference panel below instead of manufacturing a detector.
- [x] **Chomsky Hierarchy reference panel** — a new collapsible `<details>` block in the Static panel (`static.ts`, next to the existing Big-O notation reference, same interaction pattern) pairing each of the 4 types with its automaton and Sythrall's actual detection status: Type-3 → Finite Automaton → the `Regex` badge; Type-2 → Pushdown Automaton → the `CFG` badge; Type-1 → Linear-Bounded Automaton → the new `Type-1?` badge, captioned with the same hedge as its tooltip; Type-0 → Turing Machine → explicitly "no per-function badge," with the one-sentence reasoning from the row above. New generic `.ref-details`/`.ref-table` CSS (not `.bigo-*`-namespaced despite living next to the Big-O reference) — deliberately reusable by any future reference block, not a one-off.
- 7 new Rust unit tests in `classifiers::tests` (including the memoization-vs-semantic-analysis distinction as its own explicit case, a pre-populated dict literal correctly NOT counting as a candidate, and a dict-only-read-never-grown case also correctly excluded) + 2 end-to-end in `rich::tests`, plus 2 new Python tests (`TestSemanticAnalysisPattern` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar — `tsc --noEmit`/`vite build` clean, all 536 backend tests pass unmodified. **266 Rust tests total (257 previous + 9), 536 backend tests total (534 previous + 2), Clippy clean across `--all-targets`. This closes Phase 16.**

### ✅ Phase 17 — Compiler Intelligence *(integrate mature tools, don't rebuild them — both bullets shipped)*
