Lambda-Calculus framing already exists on tail-call recursion (Phase 8) — this phase is where that theory connects to an actual compiler pipeline, not a Sythrall-authored compiler:

- [x] **Compiler pipeline visualization — embedded Compiler Explorer** (2026-09-02) — embeds [Compiler Explorer](https://godbolt.org) (open source) instead of building a teaching compiler from scratch, exactly as this bullet named. Deliberately limited to `c`/`cpp`/`fortran` — the 3 languages this engine already really compiles elsewhere (Phase 23/26's own kernels) — not `assembly` (already the end result) or Python/JS/TS (no comparable native codegen). Compiler IDs pinned to each language's "snapshot"/"nightly" alias (always resolves to the latest available build) rather than a numbered version, so the link never points at a compiler the service has since removed.
  - **A real dead-end found and corrected before shipping, not after**: Compiler Explorer's chrome-less embed route (`/e#clientstate:<base64>`) was the first choice — no header/sponsors/privacy modal — but a second verification pass using a deliberately distinctive function name (`sythrall_marker_fn_zzz`, after the first pass's check for the word "square" turned out to be a false positive — "square" is also the name of Compiler Explorer's own default example) revealed that route silently **ignores** the supplied source and always shows its own default snippet instead. Caught and fixed before shipping: switched to the documented `/clientstate/<base64>` route, confirmed live to actually load the real analyzed file's source — at the cost of showing Compiler Explorer's full site chrome (including a one-time privacy-policy modal on a browser profile's first visit), an accepted, honestly-documented tradeoff over silently showing the wrong code.
  - **Explicit, per-click consent, not automatic** — the first case in this entire engine where analyzed source code leaves the machine to a third-party service (everything before this is either 100% local Rust computation or a Sythrall-authored kernel, never the user's own code). The iframe is never built into the initial render; a disclosure note plus a "Cargar Compiler Explorer" button carries the pre-computed URL in a `data-*` attribute, and only a click injects the `<iframe>`.
  - A 20,000-character source-size cap shows a plain message instead of attempting the embed for unusually large files, rather than building an oversized URL.
  - Verified live end-to-end in a real browser (Playwright): button renders only for c/cpp/fortran files, click injects the iframe, and the real analyzed file's exact source (confirmed via a distinctive marker string, not a coincidental substring match) loads and compiles inside it — zero console errors. `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean. Pure frontend — no Rust/Python changes, since Compiler Explorer does its own compiling externally.
- [x] **IR-level view of the tail-call rewrite — proof via the Compiler Explorer embed above, not a new visualization** (2026-09-02) — closes the exact gap this bullet named: Phase 8's Python `recursion_note` could only ever *assert* "this could become a loop" in prose, since CPython never actually performs tail-call optimization, giving nothing real to show. New `cparse.rs::is_tail_recursive()` ports the identical shape check `recursion.rs::calls_self` already uses for Python (`return f(...)` where the value IS the self-call, nothing pending after it — confirmed against the real tree-sitter-c grammar before writing anything: `return_statement`'s named child is the `call_expression` directly) to C/C++, the two languages this engine actually sends to a real compiler. Unlike Python, gcc/clang **can** rewrite a direct self-tail-call into a loop (sibling call optimization) starting at `-O1` — so for C/C++ this bullet has something real to point at.
  - New `CFunction.is_tail_recursive`/`recursion_note` fields (mirroring Python's `RichFunction` shape) reuse the exact same frontend badge Python's tail-call detection already renders (`f.is_recursive || f.is_tail_recursive` — a one-line OR, since C never sets `is_recursive`, only the more specific `is_tail_recursive`), rather than inventing new markup for the same concept in a different language.
  - **The actual "instead of asserting" part**: when a file has any tail-recursive function, the Compiler Explorer button from the bullet above switches from default compiler options to `-O2` and its label changes to name the reason — clicking it lets the user check the REAL generated assembly for whether a `call` back to the function's own name is present or not, rather than trusting a claim. Without pinning `-O2` specifically, the embed would default to no optimization (where TCO never happens), which would make the note look wrong rather than the flag being missing.
  - 5 new Rust tests (`cparse::tests` — accumulator-style tail factorial detected, classic non-tail factorial correctly NOT detected since its self-call is wrapped in a multiplication, a tail-call nested inside an `if` still detected, and the same detection confirmed working in C++ too) — **568 Rust tests total (564 previous + 4, one existing test extended rather than duplicated)**, Clippy clean.
  - Verified live end-to-end in a real browser: the "tail-call" badge renders on a hand-written accumulator-style C function, the Compiler Explorer button's label switches to mention `-O2`, and the constructed URL was decoded and inspected directly (bypassing a third-party first-visit privacy modal that made scraping the live iframe's rendered text unreliable for automated verification) to confirm the exact analyzed source, the correct compiler id, and `options: "-O2"` all round-trip correctly. No Python changes — pure Rust computation relayed as-is, same policy as the rest of this engine's C/C++ analysis.
  - **A real false-positive bug found by a fresh audit and fixed the same day (2026-09-02)**: `is_tail_recursive()` walked the function's body with the file's generic, scope-blind `walk()` helper — a self-call appearing inside a nested C++ **lambda** (`auto helper = []() { return total(5); };`), a local scope of its own, was misattributed to the *containing* function, which never actually called itself. Confirmed live: a `total()` whose only `return` was `return 0;` came back `is_tail_recursive: true` purely because of the lambda inside it. Fixed with a new `walk_own_scope()` that stops descending at `lambda_expression`/`function_definition`/`class_specifier`/`struct_specifier`/`union_specifier` boundaries — each is its own name scope, so a self-call found inside one is never evidence about the function around it. 1 new regression test (`cparse::tests`) confirming the exact repro case no longer misfires.

### ✅ Phase 18 — Native Analysis Core *(committed migration: `static_parser.py`'s role moves to Rust, fully — not just wherever a benchmark happens to favor it)*

- [x] **Project Health moved to Rust: the last authority formulas still living in Python** (2026-10-10). It closes the gap this phase's own rule left open — *Rust holds authority, Python relays* — exactly where it showed most: the four numbers the product presents as its headline result were computed in the relay layer (245 lines, 42 arithmetic operations, measured).
  - **Same formulas, byte for byte**, with `scoring_version` untouched: porting and recalibrating at once would make it impossible to attribute a changed number.
  - **Differential test over 4000 random inputs against the Python implementation: 0 differences.** Plus 12 tests pinning each formula decision.
  - **The detail that would have broken parity silently**: Python rounds half-to-even, Rust rounds half-up. Replicated deliberately, with a test.
  - With no sidecar there are no scores and it says so (`null` + error), rather than reintroducing the second formula this migration removes.
  - Result: **42 scoring arithmetic operations in Python → 0**. 1505 Rust tests (+12), 345 backend tests untouched.

- [x] **Python's relay cost 40× more than the analysis — a throwaway HTTP client per call** (2026-09-29). The honest close of this phase: the migration to Rust was done, but the path to reach Rust cancelled it out.
  - **Measured in isolation**: creating an `httpx.AsyncClient` per call, **166.1 ms**; reusing it, **1.3 ms** — 123×. Rust's analysis of one file takes **4.5 ms**, so **98% of a request's time went into opening and closing a localhost connection**.
  - **End to end**: `parse-project` over 200 files **34.22 s → 0.99 s** (34.7×). That number settles it: 200 × 4.5 ms = 0.9 s, so the new path is now bounded by the analysis itself.
  - The client is keyed **per event loop** (pytest and `uvicorn --reload` each create a new one; a bare global would raise `Event loop is closed`), dead ones are pruned, and `lifespan` closes the live ones.
  - **Found by measuring an outside critique** the user pasted, claiming *"Python will become a bottleneck"*: the conclusion was right, the cause was not. Python does not orchestrate slowly — it already uses `asyncio.gather` — it was the throwaway client.
  - 338 backend tests (+4), and the full suite dropped from 350 s to 329 s as a side effect.

`complexity-engine` (Phase 11) was the proof of concept; this phase is the decision it justified. **The static-analysis engine is moving to Rust, completely** — parsing, AST construction, symbol resolution, complexity, security, graphs, quality metrics, all of it, progressively consolidated into a modular native core. Python's role narrows to what Phase 2's ML/DL Inspector already does well: AI, ML, and scientific workloads — not source-code parsing. The end state has **no permanent compatibility layer and no dual implementation**: once a piece is ported and proven correct, the Python version it replaced is deleted, not kept around "just in case." `static_parser.py` itself is the migration's target, not a fixture that survives it.

What doesn't change: *how* each slice moves is still disciplined — ported, parity-tested against the Python it replaces on the same input, benchmarked with Criterion, and only then swapped into the live call sites, exactly the process the first slice below already used. The commitment is to the destination; the rigor is in how each step gets there, so migrating fast never means migrating carelessly.

- [x] **Rich Python analysis, ported to `complexity-engine`, Python duplicate deleted (not just wired alongside)** — `_parse_python()`'s per-function/class/import work (Big-O, Θ/Ω, cyclomatic complexity, tail-call recursion, space complexity, security/taint, structural + naming smells, the 3 CS Engine classifiers) runs in Rust, exposed as `POST /parse/python`. `_parse_python` itself became `async` and now calls the sidecar first for all of it — **the Python heuristic implementations were deleted, not kept as a parity-tested fallback**: ~30 functions removed from `static_parser.py` (all of `_infer_big_o_python`/`_theta_omega_python`/`_loop_analysis_python`/`_has_binary_split_python`, `_recursion_info_python`, the regex/grammar/graph-traversal classifiers, `_infer_space_python` + helpers, `_security_findings_python` + its taint-tracking helpers, every `_check_*` structural/naming smell function). Without the sidecar, `.py` analysis degrades to a lightweight AST-only skeleton (`_skeleton_functions_python`/`_skeleton_classes_python`: name/line/args/docstring/calls/complexity, real — but `big_o`/`space_complexity` as `"?"`, `security_findings`/`structural_smells`/`naming_smells` as `[]`) instead of a second full implementation — the same call this phase already made for Halstead/MI (Phase 11) and Recurrence Relations (Phase 13), generalized to the whole engine. **Now wired into `/static/parse` and `/static/parse-project` too** (the Static panel's main table and Project Health, previously 100% pure-Python, never even checking the sidecar) — `parse_project`'s per-file loop uses `asyncio.gather` instead of sequential awaits, so N files means N concurrent sidecar round-trips, not N serial ones. `pytest` now starts the real sidecar for the whole session (`tests/conftest.py`, `cargo build --bin complexity-engine` if no debug binary exists yet) instead of exercising a Python fallback that no longer exists — the ~130 tests across `test_security_findings.py`/`test_structural_smells.py`/`test_naming_smells.py`/`test_static_analysis.py` now verify the real Rust engine end-to-end via HTTP, with a handful of assertions updated where Rust's (English) wording differs from the deleted Python (Spanish) heuristic's.
- [x] **Graph Engine, first slice ported: Import Graph** — `graph.rs::build_import_graph()` (nodes/edges/mermaid/entry_points/summary), exposed as `POST /graph/import`. `_build_import_graph()` in `graph.py` now `await`s the sidecar and returns its result directly; the only Python it deleted is `_import_graph_to_mermaid()` (Import-Graph-specific). `_build_project_edges`/`_short_name`/`_safe_id`/`_module_to_candidates` stay in Python for now — Call Graph and Circular Deps (the other 2 variants `graph.py` still serves in Python) share them, and porting those is the next slice of this same row. Rust-only, same degradation criterion as Halstead/security/smells above: if the sidecar is down, the endpoint returns an explicit error shape instead of recomputing in Python. Verified against the 11 pre-existing `TestImportGraph` tests (`apps/api/tests/test_graph.py`) passing unmodified — same input, same output shape, now computed in Rust — plus 8 new Rust unit tests for `module_to_candidates`/edge-building and a `graph_bench.rs` Criterion benchmark. Found and fixed a real bug in the same pass: `/graph/project` (the project-upload graph endpoint) called `_build_import_graph()` without `await`, which would have silently returned a coroutine instead of a dict once the function turned `async` — caught by `test_graph_phase2.py` before it shipped.
- [x] **Graph Engine, second slice ported: Centrality** — `graph.rs::build_centrality_graph()` (in/out-degree, `nx.degree_centrality`'s `(in+out)/(n-1)` formula reimplemented as plain arithmetic over the edges `build_import_graph` already built — no `petgraph`/graph-library dependency needed for this one), exposed as `POST /graph/centrality`. `_build_centrality_graph()` in `graph.py` now `await`s the sidecar; the shared `_files_summary_for_sidecar()` helper (factored out of the Import Graph slice) builds the payload for both. Closes the note Phase 14 left on this same feature ("deliberately still Python... Phase 18's Graph Engine needs the import/call graph construction itself in Rust first, which doesn't exist yet") — that construction now exists. Verified against the 7 pre-existing `TestCentralityGraph` tests passing unmodified, plus 7 new Rust unit tests (degree counting, hub threshold, stable-sort tie-breaking, empty/no-edges cases) and a second `graph_bench.rs` benchmark. **Coverage gap found and closed in the same pass**: `test_graph_phase2.py::test_all_4_types_work` predated centrality as a 5th graph type and never exercised `/graph/project`'s centrality branch — the exact kind of missing-`await` bug the Import Graph slice hit would have shipped silently here. Renamed to `test_all_5_types_work` with `"centrality"` added to the list.
- [x] **Graph Engine, third slice ported: Call Graph** — `graph.rs::build_call_graph()`, exposed as `POST /graph/call`. Pure aggregation, no new graph algorithm: each file already arrives with its own precomputed `call_graph` (a list of `{from, to}` function names, built by a *different*, per-file helper in `static_parser.py` that this slice does not touch) — Rust just merges those across files, resolves each function's owning file, and colors by Big-O. `_build_call_graph()` in `graph.py` now `await`s the sidecar via a new `_call_graph_payload()` helper (a different shape from `_files_summary_for_sidecar` — Call Graph needs per-function detail and the precomputed call edges, not imports/dead_code). Reimplemented `_bigo_color`/`_bigo_level` as their own small Rust functions rather than sharing the Python ones — those stay in Python because `_build_heatmap` (still Python, out of scope here) also depends on them. Faithfully preserves an existing, non-obvious behavior: when two files define a same-named function, the later one (in file iteration order) wins — not a bug to fix, a design quirk to replicate exactly. Verified against the 8 pre-existing `TestCallGraph` tests passing unmodified, plus 5 new Rust unit tests (duplicate-name override, to-file resolution, no-edges fallback showing all functions, hot-paths filtering, empty case) and a third `graph_bench.rs` benchmark. No coverage gap this time — `test_all_5_types_work` (added in the Centrality slice) already exercised `/graph/project`'s call branch.
- [x] **Graph Engine, fourth and final slice ported: Circular Deps — row complete** — `graph.rs::build_circular_graph()`/`find_cycles_capped()`, exposed as `POST /graph/circular`. The real algorithmic decision this row deferred three times: rather than porting `nx.simple_cycles()` (Johnson's algorithm) or adding `petgraph` (which doesn't ship simple-cycle enumeration anyway), a capped DFS cycle-finder was written — each simple cycle is discovered exactly once, via its unique minimum-ordered node (the same core trick behind Johnson's/Tiernan's algorithms, minus the "blocking set" optimizations that only matter for performance on dense graphs, unneeded here since the whole search stops the moment the cap is hit). This was the correct call, not a shortcut: none of the 15 existing `TestCircularGraph`/`TestProjectCircular` tests compare exact cycles or NetworkX's enumeration order — every assertion is loose (count/membership only) — so exact Johnson's-algorithm parity was never actually required. `find_cycles_capped`/`_build_project_digraph`/`HAS_NX`/`nx` stayed in Python at the time (`_build_architecture_smells`, Phase 22, still used them) — **since ported to Rust too, see the "Dependency Engine" row below**. Verified against all 15 pre-existing tests passing unmodified, plus 6 new Rust unit tests (3-node cycle detection, acyclic case, cap enforcement on a dense 6-node complete graph, a node participating in 2 distinct cycles, isolated files never in a cycle, cycle-description closure string) and a 4th `graph_bench.rs` benchmark (acyclic and with-a-cycle variants). **A third call site, in a different file, was found this time**: `routers/static_analysis.py`'s Project Health endpoint (`parse_project`) imports and calls `_build_circular_graph` directly for its `total_cycles` score and to feed `_build_architecture_smells` — missed by the first grep pass (which only searched `graph.py`) and caught only because `test_static_analysis.py`'s `TestProjectHealth` class exercises it end-to-end. Fixed with the same missing-`await` pattern as the prior two slices. **This closes the Graph Engine row entirely** — all 4 graph types (Import, Centrality, Call, Circular) now run in Rust.
- [x] **Per-file `call_graph` (`/static/parse`) ported** — `rich.rs::build_call_graph()`, pure aggregation over `RichFunction::calls` (already computed), included in `RichAnalysisResult`/`POST /parse/python`. Deliberately **not** a full Rust-only cutover like the rows above: `_build_call_graph`/`_extract_calls_python` (Python) stay as the no-sidecar fallback, since `_skeleton_functions_python` already carries `calls` by design specifically so call graph keeps working degraded — deleting that fallback would be a real regression, not something this phase asks for.
- [x] **`circular_deps` deleted, not ported** — the investigation above also flagged this field (`_parse_python`'s per-file "circular imports between dotted module parts" heuristic) as dead code: zero references anywhere in `apps/web/src`, not even declared in `client.ts`'s `StaticResult` type — only a name in `/static/languages`' feature list. Deleted outright (`_detect_circular_imports` and the `circular_deps` key removed from `static_parser.py`, the feature-list entry removed from `/static/languages`) rather than ported — porting dead code would have been wasted Rust effort in the same vein Fase 14's "does this have a UI trigger?" discipline already warns against. `find_cycles_capped` itself stays (still imported by `routers/graph.py` for `_build_architecture_smells`) — only its one now-orphaned local caller went away. No tests referenced either the function or the field, confirmed before deleting.
- [x] **WASM/hot-path hints (`/static/parse`) ported** — new `services/complexity/src/wasm.rs`, pure post-computation over `RichFunction` (`big_o`/`complexity`/`loc`/`name` already computed) exactly like `build_call_graph` — no new algorithm, just the heuristic (Big-O-hot / cyclomatic-complexity / LOC thresholds, numeric-sounding-name keyword match, `.wasm` module usage in the file) reimplemented over data Rust already has. Included in `RichAnalysisResult`/`POST /parse/python`. Same deliberate-fallback treatment as `call_graph`, not a full cutover: `_wasm_hints_python` stays in `static_parser.py` as the no-sidecar path — `_skeleton_functions_python`'s own docstring already documented it was keeping `big_o`/`complexity`/`loc` real specifically so WASM hints (and call graph) keep working degraded, so this was always the intended fallback, not a second implementation kept "just in case." 7 new Rust tests (`wasm::tests`) and 6 new Python tests (`TestWasmHints` in `test_static_analysis.py`) against the real sidecar via `/static/parse`. **159 Rust tests total, Clippy clean.** No frontend changes needed — `WasmHint`'s field names (`function`/`line`/`priority`/`reasons`/`recommendation`/`estimated_speedup`) matched the existing TS interface exactly.
- [x] **C/C++ parser fully ported, Python duplicate deleted** — new `services/complexity/src/cparse.rs`, same tree-sitter grammars as the Python it replaces (`tree-sitter-c`/`tree-sitter-cpp`, now real Rust dependencies instead of Python ones — added `tree-sitter`/`tree-sitter-c`/`tree-sitter-cpp` to `Cargo.toml`, removed the same 3 from `requirements.txt`), same node-type walk (`function_definition`/`preproc_include`/`struct_specifier`/`class_specifier`/`preproc_def`/`call_expression`), same Big-O/cyclomatic-complexity heuristics. Exposed as `POST /parse/c`/`POST /parse/cpp`. Unlike `call_graph`/`wasm_hints` above, this is a **full cutover, not a deliberate-fallback port**: neither `_parse_c` nor `_parse_cpp` ever had a degraded Python fallback to begin with (no tree-sitter installed just meant `_unsupported()`), so there was nothing to preserve — `_ts_extract_functions`/`_ts_func_name`/`_ts_extract_includes`/`_ts_extract_structs`/`_ts_extract_classes_cpp`/`_ts_extract_macros`/`_extract_calls_c`/`_infer_big_o_c`/`_wasm_hints_c` all deleted from `static_parser.py`, not kept. 8 new Rust tests (`cparse::tests`).
- [x] **JS/TS parser fully ported, Python duplicate deleted** — new `services/complexity/src/jsts.rs`, same regex+heuristic approach as the Python it replaces (JS/TS never had a real AST parser here, just regex — the port's fidelity target is behavioral parity with that heuristic, not new precision), same regexes translated to the `regex` crate, same LOC-estimation/cyclomatic/Big-O/dead-import logic. Exposed as `POST /parse/js`/`POST /parse/ts`. Same full-cutover reasoning as C/C++ above — no prior fallback existed to preserve: `_JS_FUNC_RE`/`_JS_CLASS_RE`/`_JS_IMPORT_RE`/`_JS_EXPORT_RE`/`_TS_IFACE_RE`/`_TS_TYPE_RE`/`_parse_js_ts`/`_estimate_js_func_loc`/`_extract_calls_js`/`_dead_js_imports`/`_infer_big_o_js`/`_cyclomatic_js`/`_wasm_hints_js` all deleted. 9 new Rust tests (`jsts::tests`). **A real "missing `await`" bug was caught the same way the Import Graph slice caught one earlier in this phase**: `routers/intelligence.py` calls `_parse_js`/`_parse_ts` directly (bypassing `parse_file()`) from 4 helper functions across hover/go-to-definition/find-references/completions/rename — all 4 were plain `def`, calling the newly-`async def` parse functions without `await`, silently returning an unawaited coroutine instead of a result. Caught by the existing `TestHoverTypeScript`/`TestGoToDefinitionTS`/`TestFindReferencesTS`/`TestCompletionsTS` test classes (13 failures) — fixed by making all 4 helpers `async def` and adding `await` at their 5 call sites across the router. **176 Rust tests total (159 previous + 8 C/C++ + 9 JS/TS), 502 backend tests total, Clippy clean.** `/static/languages`'s `available`/`capabilities` flags updated to reflect the real dependency now: `HAS_TREESITTER` (whether the Python `tree_sitter` package was importable — irrelevant now, nothing in Python imports it anymore) replaced by `LIB_FLAGS["HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE"]` (whether the sidecar actually responds) for all 4 non-Python languages; `metrics_live.py`'s own independent `tree_sitter` capability probe removed for the same reason (duplicated `caps["complexity"]`, misleadingly, once the Python package stopped being used for anything).
- [x] **Dependency Engine: Architecture Smells ported, Python duplicate deleted — closes the Graph Engine's last Python-only piece** — new `graph.rs::build_architecture_smells()`, exposed as `POST /graph/architecture`. Turned out to need **zero new primitives**: `build_project_edges`/`module_to_candidates` (Import Graph slice) and `find_cycles_capped` (Circular Deps slice) already existed in `graph.rs` — this is pure orchestration over what the 3 earlier Graph Engine slices already built, the same kind of "aggregation, not new analysis" `build_call_graph` was. Same 3 smells, same thresholds (`HIGH_EFFERENT_COUPLING=15`, `UNSTABLE_MIN_CA=3`, `UNSTABLE_INSTABILITY=0.5`) as the Python it replaces. **Full cutover, not a deliberate-fallback port**, same reasoning as C/C++/JS/TS: `_build_project_edges`/`_build_project_digraph`/`_module_to_candidates` (Python) and `find_cycles_capped` (`static_parser.py`) all deleted outright — their only caller was `_build_architecture_smells`, dead the moment it cut over. `networkx` removed from `requirements.txt` entirely — nothing in Python imports it anymore (`routers/graph.py`'s own `HAS_NX`/`nx` import, `static_parser.py`'s independent one, and `metrics_live.py`'s separate capability-probe check on it all went with it, the last one for the same "duplicated, misleadingly, once nothing used it" reason `HAS_TREESITTER` did). `_short_name`/`_safe_id` (also in `graph.py`) were **not** touched — `_build_heatmap` (still Python at the time, out of scope here — **since ported too, see the bullet right below**) still needed them. 4 new Rust tests (`graph::tests`, including the same efferent-coupling/unstable-dependency/circular-dependency cases the Python version covered) and 3 new Python tests (`TestBuildArchitectureSmellsRustUnavailable` in `test_complexity_client.py`) — the 5 pre-existing `TestProjectHealth` architecture-smell tests in `test_static_analysis.py` (`test_architecture_*`) pass unmodified, same shape, now computed in Rust. **181 Rust tests total (176 previous + 4 architecture + 1 `LineIndex`), Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Graph Engine, truly last piece ported: Complexity Heatmap — the row is now closed for real** — new `graph.rs::build_heatmap()`, exposed as `POST /graph/heatmap`, with its own payload shape (`HeatmapFileInput`: per-function `name`/`big_o`/`complexity`/`line`/`loc` — closer to Call Graph's per-function detail than Import/Centrality's file-level summary, since coloring/sorting needs both CC and Big-O per function). Reused rather than re-invented: `bigo_color`/`bigo_level`/`safe_id`/`short_name` already existed in `graph.rs` from the Call Graph slice — only `cc_color`/`cc_level` were new (no earlier slice needed to classify by cyclomatic complexity, only by Big-O). `_build_heatmap`/`_heatmap_to_mermaid` in `graph.py` deleted outright, along with their 6 now-fully-orphaned helpers (`_cc_color`/`_cc_level`/`_bigo_color`/`_bigo_level`/`_short_name`/`_safe_id` — confirmed via grep that Heatmap was their only remaining caller anywhere in the file). Same Rust-only cutover discipline as Import/Call/Circular/Architecture Smells: no Python fallback, explicit degraded shape (`functions: []` alongside the shared `nodes`/`edges`/`mermaid`/`error` shape) if the sidecar doesn't respond. 7 new Rust unit tests (`graph::tests`, prefixed `heatmap_*` — per-function CC/Big-O classification, worst-first sort order, full-path-vs-basename distinction, `by_level` counts summing to the total, Mermaid subgraph grouping, the empty-input edge case) verified against all 21 pre-existing heatmap tests (13 in `test_graph.py::TestHeatmap`, 8 more across `test_graph_phase2.py`) passing completely unmodified — same input, same output shape, now computed in Rust. **This is genuinely the end of the row**: all 5 graph types Sythrall ships (Import, Call, Circular, Centrality, Heatmap) plus Architecture Smells now run in `graph.rs`; nothing in `apps/api/routers/graph.py` computes graph data itself anymore, only parses/orchestrates/degrades. **297 Rust tests total (290 previous + 7), 552 backend tests total, unchanged, Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Symbol Engine ported — go-to-definition/find-references now Rust, per-file (scope confirmed, not project-wide)** — new `services/complexity/src/symbols.rs`, exposed as 6 endpoints (`POST /symbols/{definition,references}/{python,js,ts}`). Ported the exact logic `routers/intelligence.py` had (`_find_definitions_python`'s `ast.walk` over `FunctionDef`/`AsyncFunctionDef`/`ClassDef`/`Assign`/`AnnAssign`, `_find_references_python`'s walk over `Name`/`Attribute` occurrences with dedup-by-position, `_find_references_js_ts`'s word-boundary regex scan) 1:1 to Rust — same scope as before (one file at a time, not an index across the whole project; that's a separate, larger item, deliberately not attempted here since the frontend doesn't even thread a `project_id` into these calls today). JS/TS's definitions reuse `jsts::parse_js_ts` directly (already computed for `/parse/js`/`/parse/ts`, not a second pass) via a thin filter-by-name function; Python reuses the same `parser::parse_module`/`walk::walk_stmts` (the `ast.walk` equivalent) and the small leaf helpers `structure::arg_names`/`docstring_of`/`node_name` already built for `rich.rs`, rather than a third independent implementation. Two new `parser.rs` primitives needed adding: `column_of_offset` (byte column within a line — nothing in the engine had needed a column before, only line numbers). Deliberate difference, not an oversight: `signature` strings are built from a literal source-text slice (`slice_of`) instead of reconstructing something like `ast.unparse()`'s normalized output — simpler, more faithful to the actual code, and no existing test asserts an exact signature string (only that it contains the symbol name). **Not a full cutover, matching the `call_graph`/`wasm_hints` precedent, not the C/C++/JS/TS one**: Python's go-to-definition/find-references had zero sidecar dependency before this port (pure `ast.walk`, no prior fallback needed) — deleting that entirely would be a real regression if the sidecar is down, not a cleanup, so `_find_definitions_python_fallback`/`_find_references_python_fallback` (the exact old implementations, renamed) stay as the explicit no-sidecar path for `.py` files specifically. JS/TS keeps its existing no-fallback behavior (empty result if the sidecar is down) — unchanged, since it was already 100% sidecar-dependent once its parser was ported earlier in this phase. **A fourth "missing `await`" bug, same class caught 3 times earlier in this phase, found in the same pass**: `rename_symbol` (a 5th caller of the Python reference-finder, not `go_to_definition`/`find_references`) called `_find_references_python(...)` without `await` after it became `async def` — caught immediately by `TestRenameSymbolPython` before it shipped, not discovered later. 20 new Rust tests (`symbols::tests`, 15 unit covering both languages' definition/reference/fallback paths + parity fixtures) and 8 new Python tests (`TestSymbolEngineRustUnavailable`/`TestSymbolEngineRustAgainstRealSidecar` in `test_complexity_client.py`) — all 121 pre-existing `test_intelligence.py` tests (`TestGoToDefinitionPython/TS`, `TestFindReferencesPython/TS`, rename) pass unmodified, exercising the new Rust path end-to-end via the real sidecar `conftest.py` already starts. **203 Rust tests total (188 previous + 15), 517 backend tests total (509 previous + 8), Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Project Scanner ported — Rust gets filesystem access for the first time** — new `services/complexity/src/scanner.rs`, exposed as `POST /scan/project`. Previously, whole-project analysis meant Python reading every file off disk itself (`project_service.py::read_project_files`) and then making N HTTP round-trips to the sidecar, one per file (`asyncio.gather(parse_file(...) for f in files)`). Now Python resolves/validates the project directory (still 100% its job — no path-traversal logic duplicated in Rust) and sends the sidecar one already-validated absolute path plus the same `PARSEABLE_EXTENSIONS`/`IGNORED_DIRS` it already used (passed as request parameters, not hardcoded twice, so the two sides can't drift); Rust walks the tree, reads each file, and dispatches to the exact same per-language engines this phase already built (`rich::analyze_rich` for `.py`, `cparse::parse_c`/`parse_cpp`, `jsts::parse_js_ts`) — one call in, one call out, full parsed result per file. This is the first time this sidecar touches the filesystem at all (previously 100% pure-compute, content always arriving over HTTP) — justified specifically here because it collapses N round-trips into 1; the security boundary stays exactly where it was, Rust does zero new validation, just trusted-input file I/O. Closing this properly required also finally porting `dead_code` (unused-import detection) to Rust (`structure::unused_imports`, walks `Name`/`Attribute` usage same as `_parse_python`'s old `ast.walk` heuristic) — previously deliberately left in Python as "too trivial to bother porting," but a project-wide scan-and-parse-in-one-call can't leave one field for Python to recompute without shipping the file content back over the wire a second time, which would have quietly cancelled out the whole point of collapsing the round-trips. `_parse_python` still computes it locally in the no-sidecar skeleton path, unchanged. Each per-file result also carries a `loc` (physical line count) field now, so `static_analysis.py::parse_project`'s `language_distribution` aggregation doesn't need raw content back either. `static_analysis.py::parse_project` and `graph.py::generate_project_graph` both switched to the new single-call path (`static_parser.py::parse_project_files`, with the old N-round-trip behavior kept as the sidecar-down fallback, same graceful-degradation discipline as every other row here); `analysis.py::analyze_project` was deliberately left untouched — it never calls the sidecar per file at all (it runs flake8/pylint/AST directly on raw content), so there was no N-round-trip inefficiency there to fix, and moving its disk reads to Rust would only add a hard sidecar dependency for zero benefit. Found and removed a genuinely dead field in the same pass: `graph.py::_parse_all` was setting `parsed["_content"]` on every result — grepped for any reader across both the backend and the frontend, found none, removed it. **187 Rust tests total (182 previous + 5 scanner), 509 backend tests total (505 previous + 4 new dead-code-via-sidecar tests), Clippy clean.**
- [x] **Security, CWE catalog v1 complete in both languages** — `security.rs`: taint tracking + all 5 CWEs (SQL/Command Injection, Path Traversal, Insecure Deserialization, hardcoded credentials), ported the same day the Python version shipped for every one of them (Phase 21), same benchmark-then-swap discipline as every other row here
- [x] **Code Quality, four slices ported, now all Rust-only** — `maintainability.rs::halstead_metrics()`: the 5 Halstead components (Phase 22), Rust-only by design since Phase 11 replaced `radon` (no Python fallback ever existed); `smells.rs`: 5 structural smell checks, and `naming.rs`: 3 naming smell checks — both were briefly built in both languages, then the Python side was deleted in the same pass that retired Big-O/space/security to Rust-only above, so these three now share one boundary instead of two different ones; Project Health scores (Phase 22) aggregate `security_findings`/`structural_smells`/`naming_smells` to project level, orchestration/arithmetic over results the Rust-first engine already computed, not new analysis logic. Architecture smells (Phase 22) also shipped, but deliberately Python-only in `routers/graph.py` — the cross-file import graph they're built on was never ported to Rust (this phase's own unaddressed "Graph Engine" row above), so there was no Rust side to port them into yet, the same kind of boundary Halstead's Rust-only exception documents in reverse. Per-project trend history remains queued
- [x] **`static_parser.py`'s role closed out — every endpoint's primary path is Rust; the file itself stays as thin dispatch + deliberate fallback, not deleted** — this bullet's original wording ("the Python file is removed") assumed every last fallback would eventually go too; that's not what actually happened, and forcing it now would be a real regression, not a cleanup. What's true today: `/static/parse`, `/static/parse-project`, `/static/bigO`, every `/intel/*` endpoint, and `graph.py` all get their real analysis from Rust first — Python only still computes anything itself in the two places a genuine no-sidecar fallback was deliberately kept (`_parse_python`'s AST skeleton, `_find_definitions_python_fallback`/`_find_references_python_fallback` from the Symbol Engine row above), both **for the same reason**: unlike C/C++/JS/TS (which never worked without the sidecar to begin with, so cutting over lost nothing), Python analysis and Python go-to-definition/find-references both worked with zero sidecar dependency before this phase touched them — deleting those fallbacks would make Python's own language support strictly *more* fragile than the day this phase started, the opposite of what "Rust is principal" is supposed to buy. `static_parser.py` today is `parse_file()`/`parse_project_files()` (dispatch), 5 thin per-language wrappers (C/C++/JS/TS have no fallback logic left in them at all, just reshape a Rust response), and the 2 explicitly-kept fallback paths — not a second analysis engine living alongside Rust's, just the disciplined exception every other Rust-only row already documents. Confirmed with the user directly before closing this bullet this way, rather than assuming.
- [x] **A second consumer of `parse_python_rich` found outside `static_parser.py` itself: `routers/diagram.py`'s 4 Mermaid builders, migrated the same way** — a follow-up audit (outside this phase's own scope, but the same "Rust is principal" mandate) found `_py_flowchart`/`_py_callgraph`/`_py_classes`/`_py_sequence` each doing their own `ast.parse()` and re-deriving functions/classes/call-relationships that `parse_python_rich` already computes — real duplicated work, not a relay, just never noticed because `diagram.py` predates this phase's audits. Renamed to `_py_*_fallback` (bodies **completely unchanged**) and kept as the explicit no-sidecar path — same reasoning as `_parse_python`'s skeleton and the Symbol Engine's fallbacks above: these 4 builders worked with zero sidecar dependency before, so deleting that would regress, not clean up. New `_py_*_from_rich` siblings consume `rich["functions"]`/`rich["classes"]`/`rich["call_graph"]` directly when the sidecar responds. Needed exactly 2 small additions to Rust to close the gap: `RichFunction.returns_value` (does the body contain a `return` with a value anywhere, including nested functions — `structure.rs`'s new `has_return_with_value`, using the general non-scope-limited walker to match `ast.walk`'s exact reach) for the flowchart's node coloring, and `RichClass.attributes` (the actual attribute *names*, not just `attribute_count`'s number — `smells::count_self_attributes` renamed to `self_attribute_names` and changed to return `Vec<String>` instead of `usize`, switched from a `HashSet` to an order-preserving `Vec` since the names are now user-visible in a diagram, not just counted). **One deliberate, documented behavior difference, not a bug**: the reused Rust attribute walk collects `self.x =` from *any* method, while the Python fallback it complements only ever looked at `__init__` specifically (plus stray class-level assignments Rust's version doesn't capture) — accepted because the consumer is an informational diagram, not a correctness or security check, and writing a third distinct attribute-collection routine just to match a quirk exactly wasn't worth it here. The one other subtlety worth recording: each Mermaid type keeps its own distinctly-worded syntax-error text (`"SyntaxError línea N"` for flowcharts, `"Error de sintaxis"` for call graphs, etc.) — preserved by keeping a local `ast.parse()` gate *before* deciding rich-vs-fallback, mirroring the exact same "parse locally first, then maybe also consult the sidecar" order `_parse_python` itself already uses, not redundant work. 6 new Rust tests (`returns_value`/`attributes`, including one confirming the nested-function reach and one confirming insertion-order is preserved) and 4 new Python tests (attribute-listing from multiple methods, full degraded-fallback parity across all 4 diagram types, and the syntax-error path surviving the fallback switch) — the 14 pre-existing `TestAnalyzeDiagram` tests pass unmodified. **303 Rust tests total (297 previous + 6), 556 backend tests total (552 previous + 4), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc` clean.** `ml.py` (~500 lines of AST/regex heuristics with no Rust equivalent at all) is the next candidate this same audit flagged — genuinely large, not attempted here.
- [x] **`ml.py`, first slice ported: the 4 detection functions (libraries/pipeline/models/metrics) — the heuristic issue-finder, diagram builder, and scorer stay in Python, deliberately** — unlike every other row in this phase, `ml.py` never had a Rust consumer to cut over from; this is the first time this specific ML-stack detection runs in Rust at all, not a relay. New `services/complexity/src/ml.rs`, genuinely new (no existing Rust module to borrow from, same as `datastructures.rs` back in Phase 14): `ML_LIB_MAP` (23 entries), `PIPELINE_PATTERNS` (23), `MODEL_PATTERNS` (25), and `METRIC_PATTERNS` (14) all transcribed literally from the Python tables they replace, including `detect_metrics`'s per-alternative numeric-value extraction (`pattern.split('|')`, each alternative tried against its own `\s*[=:]\s*([\d]+\.?[\d]*)` suffix) — a mechanical port, not a rewrite, because the exact "which alternative wins the value" behavior has to match. `detect_ml_libraries` deliberately replicates a bug-fix already baked into the Python it replaces, not the naive version: dedup keys off the *canonical* library name (`info["name"]`, e.g. "NumPy"), not the alias string that matched (`np`/`numpy` both map to the same info) — indexing by the alias instead double-counted `import numpy as np` before that fix, and the Rust port has an explicit test (`alias_no_duplica_la_libreria`) guarding against reintroducing it. Exposed as `POST /ml/detect`. **One piece can never move to Rust, by design**: `version` (which locally-installed copy of a library is in the backend's own Python environment) requires `importlib.import_module` against that environment — Rust has no such environment to introspect. So the Rust detector returns everything *except* `version`, plus a `module` field (the base import name to look version up by); Python does the `_get_lib_version(lib["module"])` lookup as a post-processing enrichment step after the sidecar responds — same "some fields are inherently Python-side" pattern `available` in the plugin manifest (Phase 24) already established. **Deliberately partial migration, scoped down explicitly rather than attempted whole**: `_detect_ml_issues` (20+ heuristic rules, the single most valuable piece, and the one most worth porting) and `_ml_diagram`/`_ml_score` (pure string/scoring presentation over already-structured data, same reasoning `_py_flowchart_from_rich` used to leave Mermaid assembly in Python) are **not touched in this slice** — `_detect_ml_issues` depends on `libraries` already being computed either way, so it can keep working unmodified regardless of which side computed that; a real future slice, not a "done" claim. Same deliberate-fallback discipline as diagram.py/Symbol Engine: `_detect_ml_libraries`/`_detect_pipeline`/`_detect_models`/`_detect_metrics` renamed to their `_fallback` siblings (bodies **completely unchanged**), used when the sidecar doesn't respond — `ml.py` worked with zero sidecar dependency before this, so cutting that off would regress, not clean up. Same local-parse-gate order as diagram.py: `analyze_ml` does its own `ast.parse(req.content)` first, skipping the sidecar round-trip entirely for content that's already known-invalid (letting `_analyze_ml_sync`'s own fallback path re-raise and report the identical `SyntaxError` shape it always has, rather than duplicating that dict construction in two places). 13 new Rust tests (`ml::tests` — one per pattern table's positive/negative case, plus the alias-dedup regression test and an end-to-end test joining all 4 detectors) and 2 new Python tests (`test_ml_degrades_to_fallback_when_sidecar_unreachable`, `test_ml_fallback_still_reports_syntax_error_correctly`) — the 19 pre-existing `TestAnalyzeML` tests pass unmodified. **316 Rust tests total (303 previous + 13), 558 backend tests total (556 previous + 2), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc` clean.** `_detect_ml_issues`/`_ml_diagram`/`_ml_score` remain the real next candidate this slice explicitly deferred — not closed out the way Heatmap/diagram.py were.
- [x] **`ml.py`, second slice: `_detect_ml_issues` ported — detection is now 100% Rust, only presentation (`_ml_diagram`/`_ml_score`) stays Python** — the piece the first slice explicitly deferred as "the most valuable, the real next candidate." Turned out to be a clean fit for the same table-driven regex-port pattern as `detect_pipeline`/`detect_models`/`detect_metrics`: `_detect_ml_issues(content, libraries)` never touched the AST at all, only `content` text and the already-detected `libraries` list (reading only `name`/`import`, never `version`) — 21 heuristic checks (data leakage via `fit_transform`-before-`train_test_split` ordering, missing `random_state`/manual-seed reproducibility, missing validation/normalization, PyTorch `zero_grad`/`.to(device)`, Keras `EarlyStopping`, LightGBM/spaCy/OpenCV/Plotly/SciPy/Polars/IceCream-specific checks) producing 23 distinct emission points end to end. **No new endpoint needed**: since Rust already builds `Vec<MlLibrary>` inside `detect()` before Python's `version` enrichment step, `detect_ml_issues` runs in the same `POST /ml/detect` call using that same in-memory list — zero extra network round-trips, `issues` just rides along in the existing response shape. Ported literally, not "cleaned up" — same order, same regex, same thresholds (`to_pandas()` > 3 calls, IceCream > 5 calls, the exact spaCy loop-detection regex spanning a literal `\n`) as the Python it replaces; the 2 checks with dynamic message text (`to_pandas() llamado {n} veces`, the IceCream count) are the only `format!`-built strings, everything else stays `&'static str`. Same deliberate-fallback discipline as the first slice: `_detect_ml_issues` renamed to `_detect_ml_issues_fallback` (body **completely unchanged**) — used exactly when the sidecar doesn't respond, same as its 4 siblings. `_ml_diagram`/`_ml_score` remain untouched, unaware of which side computed `issues`/`libraries`/`pipeline`/`models`. 34 new Rust tests (`ml::tests` — a positive-trigger and, where meaningfully distinct, a negation case for every one of the 21 checks, plus one confirming zero libraries/zero patterns produces zero issues) and zero new Python tests needed — the 19 pre-existing `TestAnalyzeML` tests already assert on `category`/`severity`/`message` for most of these checks end-to-end against the real sidecar, and the 2 fallback-degradation tests from the first slice already exercise `_detect_ml_issues_fallback` through the same path, so both slices' correctness rides on tests that predate this one. **This closes `ml.py`'s detection migration for real**: every `_detect_*` function that used to be Python's own analysis is now a Rust computation with a preserved fallback; the only Python left in this router that isn't relay/orchestration is `_ml_diagram` (Mermaid string assembly) and `_ml_score` (arithmetic over an already-computed `result` dict) — pure presentation, same category as `_py_flowchart_from_rich`'s Mermaid building in `diagram.py`, not analysis left unmigrated. **350 Rust tests total (316 previous + 34), 558 backend tests total unchanged, Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc` clean.**

```
                    Sythrall
                       │
                 Native Analysis Core (Rust)
                       │
       ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
       │               │                │
    Parsing          Analysis         Graph
   AST/symbols    Complexity/Security  CFG/DFG
                       │
              Deep Analysis
       ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐
       │               │               │
    Security        Quality       Performance
```

One thing this is deliberately *not*: `static_parser.py` → one giant `static_parser.rs`. The modular split above — separate Rust modules per concern, the same pattern `bigo.rs`/`classifiers.rs`/`recursion.rs`/`structure.rs` already use inside `rich.rs` today — is what each bullet above ports into, not a single monolithic port. And once Phase 24 (Extensibility Platform) exists, this core doesn't have to hold everything forever either — CWE catalogs, additional rules, new language support, and AI-explanation models are exactly the kind of thing that belongs as a plugin *on top* of these primitives instead of hardcoded permanently into the Rust core itself.

### ✅ Phase 19 — Machine Intelligence *(Assembly side of the language-philosophy table — integrate mature tools, don't rebuild them)*

- [x] **The bridge from source to machine code (`compilar.rs`)** (2026-10-02). It closes the gap this phase left unnamed: the assembly module existed and nobody used it, because it required someone to paste assembly by hand.
  - `gcc -S` / `rustc --emit=asm` / `zig build-obj -femit-asm` for **C, C++, Rust, Fortran and Zig**, with the result fed straight into `asmparse.rs`. It executes nothing: it stops before assembling and before linking.
  - **The measured comparison that justifies it**: the same array sum, C at `-O0` gives 19 instructions and 9 data moves, at `-O2` it gives 13 and 3. Fortran, 29/16 against 18/4. Rust, 66/35 against 39/5.
  - **And it exposed a Phase 19 bug**: `asmparse.rs` treated every label as a procedure, so real `gcc -S` output split **one** function into five (COFF and Mach-O do not use ELF's `.L`). Fixed by going to the directives, which are authoritative; with no directives the old heuristic stands and the module's 30 prior tests pass untouched.
  - Three more findings along the way, each with a test: `.globl`'s separator is a TAB; MASM opens with `name PROC`, not `label:`; `symbol = value` is not an instruction.
  - **A plain Rust `pub fn` disappears under optimization** in a library crate (169 lines at `-O0`, 7 at `-O2`). That is not an error, and the module explains it instead of returning an empty panel: `#[inline(never)]` or `#[no_mangle]` force it out.
  - Zig in `Debug` emits **312,286 lines** for a ten-line sum, so the text is capped at 4000 — the analysis and the counts still cover the whole file.
  - 1449 Rust tests (+17), Clippy clean, 338 backend tests. `POST /static/compile-asm` hangs off an already-proxied prefix, deliberately.
  - **SIMD, 2026-10-02**: `asmparse.rs` knew `xmm` and nothing else. It now reports the extension (`sse`/`avx`/`avx2`/`avx512`), the width (128/256/512) and **vectorization**, with the distinction that keeps it honest — `addps` is packed, `addss` is scalar, and touching a vector register is not enough to call something vectorized. Verified against real gcc: the same loop at `-O3` emits no SIMD, with `-mavx2` it gives AVX2/256 and with `-mavx512f` AVX-512/512. `compilar.rs`'s bridge gains CPU target features (a closed table) so they can be requested.
  - **And it is finally visible (2026-10-02)**: a "Código máquina" section in the Static panel — a comparison table per optimization level (asm lines, instructions, per-category breakdown, SIMD state), level and CPU-feature chips, and the assembly behind a button. Verified in a real browser, 18 checks. Three real bugs surfaced along the way, one of them pre-existing: `renderStaticPanel()` accumulated one click listener per render, invisible until a handler that *toggles* exposed it.

Analysis of what code *becomes*, not just what it says. Each of these is its own serious project already solved well by dedicated open-source tools — the honest move is to embed those, not reinvent them:

- [x] **Assembly (x86-64) analysis-target support, first bullet shipped** — instruction/register/control-flow breakdown from raw `.s`/`.asm` snippets, exactly as scoped: pattern-matching on text, not a disassembler (that's the 2nd bullet below, explicitly deferred). Assembly never had any support in Sythrall before this — like Fortran in Phase 20, it's genuinely new in Rust, nothing to port. Treated as a **7th language plugin**, the same generalized mechanism Fortran already proved (Phase 24): `plugin.rs::builtin_manifests()` → `plugin_registry.py::extension_map()` → `static_parser.py::parse_file()` → `_LANGUAGE_HANDLERS["assembly"]`. Zero new dispatch infrastructure — paste/upload a `.s`/`.asm` file and it renders through the same Static panel as Python/C/C++/JS/TS/Fortran.
  - **Syntax auto-detected, confirmed with the user rather than picking one**: AT&T (`%eax`, `$5`, src→dst order — what `gcc -S` emits on Linux) vs. Intel (`eax`, `5`, dst→src — NASM/MASM/Godbolt), detected from the presence of `%`/`$` sigils in the text. Mnemonics (`mov`/`jmp`/`call`/...) are identical in both, so one classification table serves both syntaxes — only operand extraction and register-name matching differ (AT&T's `%` prefix is unambiguous; Intel has no sigil, so operands are checked against a fixed ~60-name x86-64 register list instead).
  - New `services/complexity/src/asmparse.rs`: procedures are delimited by labels — a label *without* a leading `.` and not purely numeric opens a new procedure (matches real GCC output: `my_function:` is a real symbol, `.L2:`/`1:` are local jump targets within one procedure, not separate functions). A comma-aware operand splitter handles addressing modes like `(%rbp,%rax,4)`, where the inner comma is not an operand separator — a real bug caught by its own test before it shipped, from an early version that skipped dot-prefixed local labels as if they were directives (`.text`, `.globl`) and silently lost every loop-back-edge target inside a procedure.
  - **Big-O deliberately narrow**: a jump (conditional or not) whose target label was already seen earlier in the same procedure is a backward edge — evidence of a loop → `O(n)`. Zero backward edges → `O(1)`. Does **not** attempt to detect nesting depth (`O(n²)` etc.) — that needs a real CFG, a jump in complexity this first slice doesn't take, same "don't claim more than the heuristic can prove" discipline `numerical_algorithm_note`/Type-1 already established elsewhere in this engine.
  - Each instruction carries a category (`data_movement`/`arithmetic`/`logic`/`comparison`/`control_flow`/`stack`/`other`) and a one-line "why" explanation (`je`: "jumps if the zero flag is set — equal, from a prior cmp/test"), same pedagogical convention as the rest of the engine. AT&T's single-letter size suffixes (`movl`/`addl`/`cmpq`) are stripped before classification, but only when what remains is a real sizable base — `jl` ("jump if less") is never mistaken for `j` + a size suffix, an explicit test guards this.
  - Registered as the 7th `plugin.rs` manifest (`id: "assembly"`, extensions `.s`/`.asm`, `needs: [Source]` only — no AST capability, same as JS/TS, since this is pattern-matching not a real parse tree) — `plugin_registry.py::_FALLBACK_MANIFESTS` and `project_service.py`'s `PARSEABLE_EXTENSIONS`/`CODE_EXTENSIONS` updated in parallel.
  - Frontend: new `.lang-asm` badge color, a new `_renderAsmBreakdown()` in `static.ts` — one `.metric-section` per procedure listing its instructions (category-colored, explanation in a tooltip) and its registers as chips, same list convention as `_renderMemoryLayout`/`_renderCallGraph`, deliberately not a new animated diagram. The existing Big-O table renders Assembly's procedures for free, same as any other language, since `AsmProcedure` shares the same `name`/`line`/`complexity`/`big_o`/`big_o_reason` shape as `CFunction`/`FortranFunction`.
  - 18 new Rust tests (`asmparse::tests`) and 5 new Python tests (`TestAssemblyIntelligence` in `test_static_analysis.py`) — 2 pre-existing tests updated for the new plugin count (6→7 languages, `plugin::tests`/`test_plugin_registry.py`/`test_complexity_client.py`). **383 Rust tests total (365 previous + 18), 568 backend tests total (563 previous + 5), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build` clean.**
- [x] **Executable analyzer (PE/ELF/Mach-O/COFF/wasm), second bullet shipped — closes the phase** (2026-08-31). The bullet as originally written named Capstone/LIEF/`objdump` as the tools to wrap; the actual choice made was the `object` crate instead — the pure-Rust equivalent of the same idea, already the library `rustc`/`lld`/`wasmtime` themselves use for exactly this (binary format structure, not disassembly), so it needs no subprocess, no C/C++ FFI, and no parsing of another tool's text output. Same "integrate a mature tool, don't reinvent the format spec" principle the bullet asked for, just the Rust-native version of it — deliberately **not** a disassembler: instruction decoding stays explicitly out of scope, exactly as the bullet's own wording drew that line.
  - New `services/complexity/src/binparse.rs`: `object::File::parse(bytes)` on raw binary content, returning format (PE/ELF/Mach-O/COFF/wasm)/architecture/64-bit-ness/entry point, plus sections, symbols, and imports/exports — the same 3 things the bullet named. A tiny `MAX_ITEMS = 500` cap on each list (`truncated: true` when hit, stated in the response, never silently cut) keeps a DLL with tens of thousands of debug symbols from bloating the response for no pedagogical gain. Malformed/unrecognized bytes degrade to `available: false` with a plain-language note — never a panic, verified with empty input, random garbage bytes, and plain text.
  - New endpoint `POST /parse/executable` (`main.rs`) — the first endpoint in this sidecar whose body is raw bytes (`axum::body::Bytes`, `Content-Type: application/octet-stream`), not JSON, since the input is inherently binary. Given its own `DefaultBodyLimit::max(64MB)` layer, since axum's global default (2MB) is fine for source text but far too small for a real `.exe`/`.dll`.
  - New Python router `apps/api/routers/binary_analysis.py` (`POST /binary/parse`, multipart `UploadFile`) — deliberately its own router, not folded into `static_analysis.py`: that router dispatches by extension to a TEXT parser (`_LANGUAGE_HANDLERS` reads content as UTF-8), and a compiled binary isn't source text, so it doesn't fit that "language plugin" model even though both end up rendered in the same Static panel. New `complexity_client.py::parse_executable_rust()` posts the raw bytes directly (doesn't reuse `post_to_sidecar`, which always sends JSON). Pure Rust-relay endpoint — zero Python-side logic beyond reading the upload and forwarding, so per the established policy it has **no Python test file of its own**; `cargo test` on `binparse.rs` is the sole verification, same as every Rust-only kernel in Phase 26.
  - **Real bug found and fixed while verifying this in a browser, not just via `curl`**: the root `vite.config.ts`'s dev-server proxy (`BACKEND_PROXY`) is an explicit per-prefix allowlist — and `/execution` (Phase 23/26's own router, added earlier this same session) was **never added to it**, meaning every "Validar empíricamente"/"Comparar con numpy/pandas" button in the Static panel had been silently 404ing in a real `vite dev` browser session this whole time, invisible because every prior verification of that feature was done via direct `curl` to the Python backend, never through the actual frontend dev proxy a user would hit. Fixed by adding both `/execution` and the new `/binary` prefix to `BACKEND_PROXY` in the same pass — caught specifically because this bullet's own verification insisted on a real browser upload through the real dev server, not another curl-only check.
  - Frontend: `client.ts` gains `ExecutableParseResult` and `api.parseExecutable(file)` (via the existing `xhrPost` multipart helper, same mechanism as `uploadZip`). `static.ts` gains an "Analizar ejecutable" button + hidden file input in the Static panel's toolbar (no extension filter — format is detected by binary signature on the Rust side, not by filename) and a dedicated `_renderExeResult()`/`#st-exe-result` — independent of the source-file pipeline (`_result`/`_renderResult`), since analyzing a binary has no "selected source file" to hang off of. Imports are grouped by DLL/library for readability instead of a single flat list of hundreds of rows. Verified live in a real Chromium session via Playwright against this project's own freshly-built `complexity-engine.exe` (26.7MB, real debug PE): format `Pe`/`X86_64`/64-bit detected correctly, 5 real sections, 166 real imports across 17 DLLs (`kernel32.dll`, `ws2_32.dll`, `ntdll.dll`, `VCRUNTIME140.dll`, several `api-ms-win-crt-*.dll`, etc.), 3 real exports (`tree_sitter_c`/`_cpp`/`_fortran`, the statically-linked tree-sitter grammars), zero symbols (expected — release/debug MinGW builds without a preserved COFF symbol table), zero JS console errors.
  - 4 new Rust tests (`binparse::tests` — empty bytes, random garbage bytes, plain text, and a real end-to-end test that compiles a trivial `.c` file with `gcc` on this machine and parses the resulting real PE, confirming a `.text` section and the `suma` symbol both survive). **451 Rust tests total (447 previous + 4)**, Clippy clean. No Python test file for the relay router, per the established Rust-only-kernel policy — the module's correctness is `cargo test`'s job.
- [x] **Calling-convention and stack-frame explainers, third bullet shipped** (2026-08-31) — new `services/complexity/src/callingconv.rs`, pure reinterpretation of the `AsmInstruction`s `asmparse.rs` already extracted per procedure, zero new parsing, same principle as `modernization.rs` reading `memlayout.rs::allocations`. Recognizes the textual *shape* of the standard x86 prologue (`push <bp>` then `mov <sp>, <bp>` — or `<bp>, <sp>` in Intel's reversed operand order — at the start of a procedure) and epilogue (`leave` or `pop <bp>` immediately before the final `ret`), by position only, not a real CFG: a procedure with an unusual shape (multiple `ret`s, computed jumps into the epilogue) can be misclassified — an accepted, stated limit, not hidden. Also extracts `local_stack_bytes` from an immediate `sub` on the stack pointer right after the prologue, and flags `is_leaf_function` (reusing `calls.is_empty()`, already computed). **Frame-pointer omission is treated as a valid, expected outcome, not a detector failure**: modern GCC/Clang default to `-fomit-frame-pointer` on x86-64, especially for leaf functions that call nothing and so have nothing to unwind — the explanation text says this outright instead of reporting "broken prologue." This is exactly the bullet's own framing: connecting the theory (why a prologue looks the way it does) to the actual bytes. New `stack_frame` field on `AsmProcedure`, riding along in the existing `/parse/asm` response the same way `memory`/`modernization` ride along on `/parse/c`/`/parse/cpp` — no new endpoint. Frontend: a one-line `Frame: estándar` / `Frame: omitido (leaf)` / `Frame: no estándar` badge next to each procedure's name in `_renderAsmBreakdown()`, full explanation in the tooltip, same pedagogical convention as the "Matrix?"/"Type-1?" badges elsewhere in this engine. 10 new Rust tests (`callingconv::tests`, plus 1 end-to-end test in `asmparse::tests` confirming the field actually reaches `parse()`'s output, not just that the isolated function works) and 2 new Python tests — verified for real against a freshly built sidecar via direct `curl` before wiring the frontend, not just typechecked: a hand-written AT&T snippet with `push %rbp; mov %rsp, %rbp; sub $32, %rsp; ...; pop %rbp; ret` came back with `has_standard_prologue`/`has_standard_epilogue: true` and `local_stack_bytes: 32`. **422 Rust tests total (412 previous + 10), 585 backend tests total (583 previous + 2), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.**

This closes Phase 19: all 3 bullets are shipped — Assembly text analysis (pattern-matching, first bullet), the executable-structure analyzer (PE/ELF/Mach-O via the `object` crate, second bullet), and calling-convention/stack-frame explainers (third bullet, built on the first).

### ✅ Phase 20 — Scientific Intelligence *(Fortran, deepened past a single bullet point)*

Fortran as an analysis target, connected to the numeric stack Sythrall already ships with (numpy/scipy's compiled LAPACK/BLAS backends) — not a language Sythrall's own engine needs to be written in. Unlike C/C++/JS/TS (Phase 18), this is the first language port that doesn't replace a pre-existing Python implementation: Sythrall had zero Fortran infrastructure before this phase, so it was built directly in Rust (`services/complexity/src/fparse.rs`) from the start, using [`tree-sitter-fortran`](https://crates.io/crates/tree-sitter-fortran) v0.6.0. Same wiring pattern as `cparse.rs`: a new sidecar endpoint (`POST /parse/fortran`), a Python client wrapper (`parse_fortran_rust`), and a `static_parser.py` dispatch branch — no Python-side fallback, same as C/C++/JS/TS (degrades to `_unsupported()` without the sidecar).

Bumping in `tree-sitter-fortran` surfaced a real ABI incompatibility: the crate's compiled grammar targets language ABI version 15, which the `tree-sitter = "0.24"` Rust binding (already a dependency, shared by the C/C++ parsers) does not support (`LanguageError { version: 15 }` at runtime, not a compile error). Fixed by bumping `tree-sitter` to `"0.25"` — its runtime supports ABI 15 while remaining backward-compatible with the older grammars `tree-sitter-c`/`tree-sitter-cpp` already ship, confirmed by the full existing C/C++ test suite staying green after the bump.

- [x] **DO-loop / array-operation detection, vectorization & SIMD candidates** — `do_loop_depth` computed by walking nested `do_loop` AST nodes (real tree-sitter nodes, not a text heuristic like C's `infer_big_o_c`). A `vectorization_note` fires only for depth-1/2 loops where an array element on the assignment's left-hand side is subscripted by the loop's own control variable (`A(I) = ...` inside `DO I = ...`) — extracted via a `DO\s+(?:\d+\s+)?(\w+)\s*=` regex over the loop's source text, the one place this module falls back to text matching (the grammar's `loop_control_expression` internals aren't documented stably enough to navigate by field). Deliberately hedged, not a proof: the note text says outright that it doesn't verify iterations are actually independent — `A(I) = A(I-1) + 1` (a real sequential dependency) still matches the same shape, and the engine doesn't do cross-iteration data-dependency analysis for any language yet.
- [x] **Numerical algorithm recognition with domain-specific framing** — a triple-nested (`do_loop_depth >= 3`) loop containing an assignment to a doubly-subscripted array (`C(I,J) = ...`) whose right-hand side multiplies two other doubly-subscripted array references fires `numerical_algorithm_note`: *"Triple-nested DO loop ... shape matches classic matrix multiplication, O(n³). Candidates: SIMD, loop blocking/tiling, parallelization (OpenMP/coarrays). Domain: HPC/Numerical Computing."* — the exact framing this bullet asked for, not a bare Big-O label. Explicitly a shape signal: it does not confirm the indices actually form `C(i,j) += A(i,k)*B(k,j)`.
- [x] **BLAS/LAPACK usage detection** — every `CALL`/function-call target inside a procedure is checked against a curated (not exhaustive — the ask was detection, not a full ~1500-routine LAPACK catalog) list of Level 1–3 BLAS and common LAPACK routine stems (`GEMM`, `GEMV`, `AXPY`, `GETRF`, `POTRF`, `SYEV`, `GESVD`, etc.), matched with or without the S/D/C/Z precision prefix (`DGEMM`/`SGEMM`/`ZGEMM`/`GEMM` all match). Surfaced per-function as `blas_lapack_calls`, flagging where a project already leans on compiled numeric backends instead of reimplementing something those backends provide.
- [x] **Full stack wiring, not just the parser**: `PARSEABLE_EXTENSIONS`/`CODE_EXTENSIONS` (`.f`/`.f90`/`.f95`/`.f03`/`.f08`/`.for`), the `/static/languages` capability-discovery endpoint, the live-editor metrics bar (`metrics_live.py::_metrics_fortran`, a regex-only quick calculator — same tier as its C/C++ counterpart, not the real tree-sitter parser), and the frontend (`lang-fortran` badge color, `LANG_DISPLAY_NAME`, three new hedged badges in the Static panel's Big-O table — "SIMD?", "Matrix?", "BLAS/LAPACK" — each with the full note as its tooltip, same convention as Phase 16's "Type-1?" badge). Monaco has no built-in Fortran syntax-highlighting mode, so the editor's `EXT_LANG` map was left untouched for Fortran files (cosmetic gap, out of scope for this phase — same as the pre-existing untouched gap for `.c`/`.cpp`).
- [x] **A one-line grammar gotcha caught by tests, not shipped silently**: `tree-sitter-fortran`'s grammar reuses the exact string `"function"`/`"subroutine"` both for the named container node (with `_statements` children) *and* the anonymous keyword token (`SUBROUTINE`/`END SUBROUTINE`) — `node.kind()` alone can't tell them apart. Without an `is_named()` guard, every procedure was counted 3 times (the real container plus its own opening and closing keyword tokens). Caught immediately by the first extraction test (`detecta_subrutina_simple_sin_loops` expected 1 function, got 3), fixed with one added condition, documented in the same commit.
- [x] **Test coverage**: 12 new Rust unit tests (`fparse.rs`) covering plain-function/subroutine detection, DO-loop nesting → Big-O (0/1/2/3+ → O(1)/O(n)/O(n²)/O(n³)), the vectorization-candidate shape (positive and negative), the matrix-multiplication shape (positive and negative), BLAS/LAPACK name matching (with and without precision prefix), `USE` statement extraction, and call-graph edges between known subroutines. 7 new Python tests: 2 in `test_complexity_client.py` (sidecar-down → `None`, sidecar-up → finds the subroutine) and 5 in `test_static_analysis.py` (end-to-end through `/static/parse`, one per bullet plus a sweep confirming all 6 recognized extensions dispatch to the Fortran path) — run against the real sidecar `conftest.py` already rebuilds fresh, not mocked.

### ✅ Phase 21 — Security & Taint Intelligence *(pattern-based data-flow analysis, confidence-scored — not a SAST replacement)*

A gap the original 9-pillar pass missed entirely: nothing in Phases 13–20 looks at security. Direct continuation of the CS Engine's existing style — Phase 8/12's regex/grammar classifiers already prove "heuristic pattern + honestly-labeled confidence" works — applied to source→sink data flow instead of algorithmic shape. Nothing here should ever be presented as "this IS a vulnerability," only as a pattern worth a human's attention, with the evidence shown so the claim is auditable. CWE catalog v1 complete:

- [x] **Taint tracking within a single function** — a recursive, provenance-carrying walk resolves a value back to an untrusted source (`request.args`/`form`/`values`/`json`/`GET`/`POST`/`COOKIES`/`headers`, `input()`, `sys.argv`, `os.environ`/`os.getenv`) through assignments, string concatenation, f-strings, and `.format()`, tracking *whether* the taint was built via string construction (the actual SQLi/command-injection signal) vs. passed through raw. **Deliberately not cross-function** — interprocedural taint needs the call graph the Dependency Engine below doesn't build yet. **Ported to Rust the same day it shipped in Python** (`services/complexity/src/security.rs`), not left as a someday-item. Both covering the vulnerable and the safe/parameterized form of every check, byte-for-byte parity between the two confirmed by hand on every case.
- [x] **CWE catalog v1, 5 of 5 shipped**: SQL Injection (CWE-89) — fires only when the query is *built* via concatenation/f-string/`.format()`, so `cursor.execute("...%s...", (val,))` correctly produces no finding; Command Injection (CWE-78) — `os.system`/`os.popen` (always shell) or `subprocess.*(..., shell=True)`, `subprocess.run([...])` without `shell=True` correctly produces no finding; hardcoded credentials (CWE-798) — name+shape heuristic, file-level rather than per-function since that's genuinely where these live; **Path Traversal (CWE-22)** — `open(path)` needs the same "built via concatenation" signal as SQLi (`open(f"uploads/{name}")` fires, `open(config_path)` doesn't), `os.path.join(base, seg)` fires on any tainted non-first segment even unconcatenated, since joining a raw `"../etc/passwd"` is already the traversal; **Insecure Deserialization (CWE-502)** — `pickle.loads`/`pickle.load`/`marshal.loads` are unsafe by construction (no "safe form" exists, unlike SQLi/command injection), so they fire at Medium confidence even *without* provable taint and escalate to High when the argument does trace to a taint source; `yaml.load` fires unless called with an explicit `Loader=yaml.SafeLoader`/`CSafeLoader`.
- [x] **Confidence-scored findings** (High/Medium — Medium introduced by CWE-502's untainted-but-still-risky case, the first real use of that level), never a bare yes/no — mirrors the "both signals required" precedent from Phase 12's grammar classifier: a source alone isn't a finding, source *and* the concatenation/sink signal both firing is
- [x] **Evidence-tree finding schema** — category → CWE → severity → confidence → source → sink → line → recommendation, one shared shape for every finding. Surfaced in the **Static panel** and in the Python **hover tooltip**, Rust-only (`security.rs`, see Phase 18 — the Python taint-tracking implementation was deleted, not kept as a fallback; without the sidecar, findings degrade to an empty list) — **not yet the Problems Panel**: that tab is fed by per-keystroke lint markers, and security findings currently run on the same heavier `/static/parse` pass as WASM hints, which live in Static too, not Problems
- [x] **Two real bugs found and fixed the same week this shipped**, both via an independent audit, not self-caught: (1) reassigning a tainted variable to a safe literal (`cmd = request.args.get("x"); cmd = "ls -la"`) didn't clear its taint, producing a High-confidence false positive on ordinary defensive code; (2) a nested function reusing an outer variable's name leaked taint across scopes. Both fixed by making the taint walk scope-aware (`_own_scope_nodes` in Python, `walk_stmts_own_scope` in Rust — a generic AST walker variant that stops at nested `FunctionDef`/`Lambda` boundaries instead of descending into them), with regression tests in both languages. The Rust port built *after* both fixes never had either bug.
- [x] **Project-level Findings, aggregated** (Phase 22 slice) — `security_findings` across every file in a project, not just one at a time, surfaced in the Static panel's project view with the originating file shown per item, sorted by severity
- [x] **CWE catalog v2, 3 more shipped: SSRF, Code Injection, XXE** (2026-09-01) — none of these ever existed in the old Python taint tracker (already deleted by the time these shipped, see the bullet above), so all 3 are 100% Rust-native from the start, reusing v1's exact taint tracker/sink catalog with zero new infrastructure.
  - **Server-Side Request Forgery (CWE-918)** — `requests.get/post/put/delete/head/patch/request` and `urllib.request.urlopen`. Unlike SQL Injection/Path Traversal, there's no "parameterized" safe form for an HTTP request's target URL, so this fires on ANY taint reaching the URL argument, built via concatenation or not — same "no safe form exists" precedent `os.path.join` already established for Path Traversal. Accepted, stated limit: doesn't distinguish taint landing in the host (the real SSRF risk) from taint in a path segment of an otherwise-fixed URL.
  - **Code Injection (CWE-94)** — `eval`/`exec` with a tainted argument. Same "no safe form, fires on any taint" logic as Command Injection's `os.system`/`os.popen` case — there's no list-of-arguments equivalent that makes `eval`/`exec` safe the way `subprocess.run([...])` (no `shell=True`) makes command execution safe.
  - **XML External Entity (CWE-611)** — `lxml.etree.parse`/`fromstring`/`XML`, unsafe by default (external entity resolution enabled) unless called with an inline `parser=etree.XMLParser(resolve_entities=False)` — same "unsafe by default unless an explicit safe flag is passed" shape as CWE-502's `yaml.load`/`Loader=yaml.SafeLoader`. Medium confidence on the call alone, High when the input argument traces to a known taint source. Accepted, stated limit: only recognizes an `XMLParser(...)` built inline in the `parser=` keyword itself — a safe parser instance held in a variable isn't recognized and would produce a false positive.
  - 10 new Rust tests (`security::tests`, covering the positive/negative/confidence-tier case for each of the 3) — verified live in a real browser via Playwright against a hand-written Python file exercising all 3 at once: all 3 findings rendered correctly with their own CWE/severity/confidence/source→sink/recommendation, identical visual shape to the 5 pre-existing CWEs, zero JS console errors. No Python changes needed — the finding schema and its rendering were already fully generic (no CWE number hardcoded anywhere outside `security.rs` itself). **515 Rust tests total (505 previous + 10)**, Clippy clean. 8 CWEs in the catalog now.
- [x] **CWE catalog v3, 1 more shipped: Open Redirect** (2026-09-01, same day) — `redirect(url)`/`flask.redirect(url)` (Flask; `django.shortcuts.redirect` deliberately excluded, since it accepts either a URL or a view name and disambiguating those without real type information would introduce ambiguity Flask's `redirect` doesn't have).
