Same "no safe form via concatenation, fires on any taint reaching the sink" shape SSRF/Code Injection already established. **First Medium-severity + High-confidence combination in this catalog** — a phishing enabler, not direct system compromise like SQLi/RCE/SSRF/XXE, but when the taint+sink pattern fires the certainty is the same as any other High-confidence finding. 3 new Rust tests (bare `redirect` name, dotted `flask.redirect`, and a fixed literal path correctly not firing). **518 Rust tests total (515 previous + 3)**, Clippy clean, verified live in a real browser — the `Medium` severity badge renders in a visually distinct color from the `High` findings around it, confirming severity differentiation actually reaches the UI, not just the API response. 9 CWEs in the catalog now.
- [x] **CWE catalog v4, 1 more shipped: Server-Side Template Injection** (2026-09-01, same day) — `render_template_string(s)`/`flask.render_template_string(s)` with `s` tainted, same "no safe form, fires on any taint reaching the sink" family as SSRF/Code Injection/Open Redirect. Deliberately distinguished from `render_template` (which loads a template from a fixed FILE and only ever receives user data as context *variables*, never as the template source itself — the safe form that never shares this sink) and from a bare `jinja2.Template(...)` constructor call bypassing Flask (out of scope for this first slice). Severity `High` — Jinja2 SSTI compiles the received string as template syntax, so attacker-controlled input reaching it can escalate all the way to full remote code execution (`{{ config.__class__.__init__.__globals__... }}`), not just XSS, putting it in the same impact tier as Code Injection rather than Open Redirect's phishing-only tier. 4 new Rust tests (bare `render_template_string` name, dotted `flask.render_template_string`, a fixed template string with data passed correctly as a context kwarg not firing, and `render_template` confirmed to never share this sink regardless of how tainted its own context kwargs are). No Python/frontend changes needed — same generic finding schema every CWE in this catalog already shares, confirmed by grepping for any hardcoded CWE number outside `security.rs` itself (found none). **564 Rust tests total (560 previous + 4)**, Clippy clean, verified live in a real browser: the finding renders with the identical evidence-tree shape (CWE/severity/confidence/source→sink/recommendation) as the other 9 CWEs. 10 CWEs in the catalog now.
- [x] **CWE catalog v5, 2 more shipped — and the first family in this catalog that doesn't use the taint tracker at all** (2026-09-02): every one of the 10 CWEs above asks the same question — *does external data reach this sink?* — which is why all of them take the taint map. These 2 ask a different one: *which primitive was chosen?* `hashlib.md5(...)` is exactly as broken on internal data as on user data, and `requests.get(url, verify=False)` accepts anyone's certificate no matter where the URL came from. Their check functions therefore don't receive `tainted` at all — not an oversight, the actual difference between the two families, written into the module doc so nobody "fixes" it later by threading the map through.
  - **Broken Cryptographic Algorithm (CWE-327)** — `hashlib.md5`/`hashlib.sha1` (dotted or bare, covering `from hashlib import md5`), plus `DES.new`/`ARC4.new` from pycryptodome/PyCrypto (56-bit keys and RFC-7465-banned RC4 respectively). **Not fired when `usedforsecurity=False` is passed** — Python 3.9+'s own documented way to say "this is a checksum, not security," the same "unsafe by default unless an explicit flag says otherwise" shape `yaml.load`/`Loader=SafeLoader` (CWE-502) and `XMLParser(resolve_entities=False)` (CWE-611) already use in this file. Confidence `Medium`, deliberately not `High`: MD5 remains a legitimate choice for non-security work (file dedup, cache keys, partition hashing) and not all of that code bothers with the flag — the finding names the broken algorithm, it does not claim to have proven the hash is used for security. SHA-256+ never fires; a `hasher.md5(...)` method on some unrelated object never fires either (only the `hashlib.` prefix or the bare name count).
  - **Improper Certificate Validation (CWE-295)** — `verify=False` on any `requests.`/`httpx.` call, plus `ssl._create_unverified_context()`. Confidence `High`, unlike CWE-327: `verify=False` has no benign reading — disabling validation is exactly and only what it does, so the connection stays encrypted against a passive observer while being wide open to the man-in-the-middle the certificate exists to stop. Anchored to those two library prefixes on purpose — `verify` is a generic keyword name any function could use for something else, so an unanchored match would fire on unrelated code (pinned by a test using `jwt_decode(token, verify=False)`, which correctly produces nothing).
  - 15 new Rust tests (`security::tests` — each CWE's positive case, the `usedforsecurity=False` and `verify=True`/absent negatives, SHA-256 and `ssl.create_default_context()` staying silent, the foreign-`.md5`-method and non-HTTP-`verify` false-positive guards, both firing independently in one function, and one confirming they work at module scope too — the same coverage gap `rich.rs` closed for the taint-based checks). No Python/frontend changes: confirmed by grepping the whole repo for a hardcoded `CWE-` string outside `security.rs` (none exists — the finding schema really is generic). **616 Rust tests total (601 previous + 15)**, Clippy clean, the 87 backend tests that exercise the rebuilt sidecar end-to-end still pass, verified live in a real browser against a 5-function Python file: exactly 3 findings — the `md5` one, the two certificate ones — with the `usedforsecurity=False` call and the `sha256` call correctly producing nothing, and the `Medium`/`High` badges visually distinct. **12 CWEs in the catalog now.**
- [x] **Security for JavaScript/TypeScript — closing a gap that was *actively misleading*, not merely missing** (2026-09-02). Every CWE above is Python-only: `security.rs` walks `rustpython_parser`'s AST, which JS/TS doesn't have here. Until now `static_parser.py::_parse_js_ts_via_rust` returned a hardcoded `security_findings: []`, so **any JS/TS project scored Security 100/100 in Project Health regardless of its contents** — a perfect score from absence of analysis reads exactly like a perfect score from clean code, which is worse than showing nothing.
  - **Said up front, in the module doc and in this bullet: there is no taint tracking for JS/TS.** The JS/TS parser is regex over text (`jsts.rs` has said so since its first line — there's no Rust-native equivalent as direct as `rustpython_parser`), and without an AST there's no data-flow to propagate. So these checks report the **presence of a dangerous sink**, not that external data reaches it. Three of the four are therefore `Medium` confidence — the same tier `check_insecure_deserialization` already uses when its argument doesn't trace to a known source ("using this at all is a real risk; tainted input reaching it is worse"). The fourth (TLS verification off) is `High` for the same reason its Python twin is: `rejectUnauthorized: false` has no benign reading.
  - New `services/complexity/src/jsts_security.rs`, 4 checks: **CWE-95** (`eval(...)`, `new Function(...)` — `obj.eval(...)` deliberately excluded, it's almost always something else), **CWE-79** (`innerHTML`/`outerHTML` assignment, `document.write`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` — `textContent` never fires, it's the safe alternative), **CWE-78** (`exec`/`execSync`, gated on the file actually containing `child_process`, since `exec` is an ordinary function name otherwise — `execFile`/`spawn` never fire, they're the recommended form), and **CWE-295** (`rejectUnauthorized: false`, `NODE_TLS_REJECT_UNAUTHORIZED=0`).
  - Findings are attributed to the enclosing function by line range — `jsts.rs` already computes `line`/`end_line` per function, so this is a lookup, not new analysis, and the **most nested** function wins when ranges overlap.
  - Known limits, not hidden: lines starting with `//`, `*` or `/*` are skipped, but a sink inside a string literal, or after real code on a line that then opens a block comment, can still produce a false positive — the same imprecision class the JS/TS parser beside it already documents. `structural_smells`/`naming_smells` stay empty for JS/TS on purpose: those modules work on the Python AST.
  - **A real bug caught by its own test before shipping**: the first TLS regex matched `process.env.NODE_TLS_REJECT_UNAUTHORIZED = '0'` but not the bracket form `process.env['NODE_TLS_REJECT_UNAUTHORIZED'] = 0` — the closing quote sits between the name and the `]`. Fixed by allowing the optional quote and bracket.
  - 17 new Rust tests. **672 Rust tests total (655 previous + 17)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, all 328 backend tests pass (the Python change is one line — the hardcoded `[]` becomes the sidecar's list — so no new Python tests, per this repo's Rust-only-relay test policy). Verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `.js` file: exactly 4 findings rendering in the same evidence-tree shape as Python's, each attributed to its own function (`render()`/`correr()`/`calcular()`), with `textContent`, `execFile`/`spawn` and an `eval` mentioned inside a comment all correctly producing nothing.
- [ ] Explicitly out of scope, permanently: full exploit analysis (ROP, heap spray, use-after-free) — already correctly fenced off under Execution Intelligence (Phase 23 below) as "competes directly with mature SAST tools"; this phase stays inside that same boundary, just static and narrower

### ✅ Phase 22 — Code Quality Intelligence *(the Maintainability Index Phase 11 already ships, broken into its auditable parts, plus the smells one MI number can't express)*

`complexity-engine`'s Maintainability Index (Phase 11) already computed a Halstead Volume internally but only exposed it pre-baked into one formula. This phase surfaces the components that formula is built from, and adds the structural/naming/architecture smells a single MI number can't catch on its own:

- [x] **Halstead metrics, broken out** (`maintainability.rs::HalsteadMetrics`/`halstead_metrics()`) — Vocabulary (η1+η2), Length (N1+N2), Volume, Difficulty, Effort, their own struct next to the MI score instead of staying an opaque input to one formula. Refactoring `compute()` to take the pre-computed metrics instead of re-walking the suite itself actually **removed** a duplicate AST walk that existed before this phase — a real measured win (4.6–7.1% faster at 10/100 functions), not just new surface. **Rust-only, no Python fallback** — same boundary MI/CC/raw metrics have had since Phase 11 replaced `radon` outright; the degraded shape is `halstead: null`, not a slower Python recomputation.
- [x] **Structural smells** (`smells.rs`, Rust-only — see Phase 18): long function (LOC>50), excessive parameters (>5), deep nesting (any block type, depth>4), large class (methods>15 or LOC>300), god object (methods≥20 AND self-attributes≥10) — conventional Fowler/Martin thresholds, each smell carries its threshold and reasoning in the message, never a bare label. `duplicated logic` (AST-shape hashing) is explicitly deferred, not silently dropped — needs a normalization/hashing scheme that doesn't exist yet.
- [x] **Project Health dashboard**: 4 scores (Security/Quality/Complexity/Architecture) aggregated from `security_findings`/`structural_smells`/complexity/the circular-dependency detector at project scale — each score carries its formula and raw counts alongside it, never a bare number. Surfaced on the Dashboard and in the Static panel's project view, sharing one renderer between both.
- [x] **Naming smells** (`naming.rs`, Rust-only — see Phase 18): single-letter variable outside a loop/comprehension (parameters deliberately exempt — `def add(a, b)` is idiomatic and the name sits next to the signature, so flagging it would be noise, not signal), inconsistent snake_case/camelCase mix within one file (up to 3 examples of each style quoted in the message), a nested function binding a name already used in an enclosing scope (module global or an outer function's parameter/local) — intentionally conservative, flags only mechanically-checkable cases rather than "unclear name" judgment calls that would need an LLM to arbitrate. Counted separately from structural smells in the Quality score (`health.quality.naming`) since naming issues are lower-severity per occurrence than a god object or deep nesting.
- [x] **Architecture smells** (`routers/graph.py::_build_architecture_smells`, Python-only): coupling/cohesion per module, built on the import graph that already exists (Phase 6/14) — high efferent coupling (>15 internal imports, calibrated above `main.py`'s own 11 legitimate composition-root imports), unstable dependency (afferent ≥3 and instability Ce/(Ca+Ce) >0.5, Martin's metric), and the already-shipped circular-dependency detector reframed as one instance of a general layer-violation check (each cycle now also surfaces as a `circular_dependency` smell entry, not just its own separate graph). Python-only by design — the cross-file import graph these smells need only exists in `graph.py`, never ported to Rust (that's Phase 18's own separate "Graph Engine" item, deliberately not tackled here) — the mirror-image case of Halstead being Rust-only because *its* data only exists in Rust.
- [x] **Eighth structural smell: mutable default argument (`mutable_default_argument`)** (2026-09-02) — `def f(items=[])` / `def f(opts={})`, plus mutable stdlib constructors (`list()`, `dict()`, `set()`, `bytearray()`, `defaultdict()`, `deque()`, dotted or bare). The **first smell in this file that reads the function's signature rather than its body**, so `function_smells` gained an `args` parameter. It's also the only one with no threshold behind it: the shape alone determines the behavior — the default is evaluated once, at `def` time, so every call that omits the argument shares the same object, and there is no "small enough to be safe" version of that.
  - **Not called a bug, because sometimes it isn't one.** `def fib(n, memo={})` is the classic hand-rolled memoization idiom, and the shared-across-calls behavior is exactly what makes it work. So the smell takes the memoization cache's name (from the detector shipped earlier the same day, Phase 26) and, when the flagged argument *is* that cache, says so: the message calls it deliberate, then still notes it stays fragile against a caller that passes its own dict, and points at `@functools.lru_cache` as the explicit form. When the cache has a different name than the flagged argument, the softened wording correctly does **not** apply — pinned by its own test.
  - The coupling lives at the call site (`rich.rs`), not inside `smells.rs`: that module keeps taking only plain values, staying a pure function of its arguments like every other check in it. The cost is recomputing `memoization_info` once per function — one extra AST walk, accepted deliberately in exchange for not making `smells.rs` depend on `classifiers.rs`.
  - Immutable defaults (`None`, numbers, strings, tuples, `frozenset()`) never fire; `kwonlyargs` and `posonlyargs` are covered too, not just the common `args` list (easy to miss — it's a separate vector in the AST, and it has its own test).
  - 10 new Rust tests. **655 Rust tests total (645 previous + 10)**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean after adding the kind to the frontend's union type, label map (orange, not yellow — this changes *behavior* between calls, closer to the hidden-O(n²) smell than to "this function is long") and sort order. The 87 backend tests that exercise the rebuilt sidecar end-to-end still pass. Verified live in a real browser against a 4-function file: exactly 2 findings — `acumular(x, items=[])` with the standard message and `fib(n, memo={})` with the deliberate-memoization wording — while `items=None` and the all-immutable-defaults function produced nothing, and `fib`'s row simultaneously carried the "memoizado" badge, the two signals agreeing on the same evidence.

- [x] **Structural smells for JavaScript/TypeScript — the other half of the same misleading-perfect-score problem** (2026-09-02). The Security bullet in Phase 21 above fixed one half: `security_findings` was a hardcoded `[]` for JS/TS, so any JS/TS project scored Security 100/100 by absence of analysis. `structural_smells` had exactly the same shape, so **Quality also read 100/100 for the same reason** — `quality_score` is computed from smell counts, and for JS/TS those counts were always zero.
  - **No new smell logic was written.** `smells::check_long_function` and `check_excessive_parameters` are pure functions of plain values (name, line, LOC / parameter count) and never depended on `rustpython_parser`'s AST — they simply had no caller on the JS/TS path. Now `jsts.rs` calls them with the data it already computes. The thresholds are literally the same constants, which is the point: "long function" means the same thing in both languages instead of two criteria sharing a name.
  - `JsFunction` gained a `params` field. `JS_FUNC_RE` had been capturing the parameter list in groups 2/4/6 since it was written, and the result was thrown away — that discarded capture is exactly what `check_excessive_parameters` needs. Splitting it is not a plain `split(',')`: a destructured default (`{ a, b } = {}`), an array default (`[x, y] = []`) or a TypeScript generic (`Map<string, number>`) all contain commas that do **not** separate parameters, so `split_params` tracks bracket depth. Without it a 2-parameter function would have been reported as having 4+ and fired the smell — verified live against exactly that case.
  - **The other three checks stay out, for one shared reason**: `check_deep_nesting`, `check_quadratic_list_membership` and `check_de_morgan_simplifiable` need a tree, not text. In JS you cannot even count block depth by braces — `{` is a block, an object literal *and* destructuring, so a brace counter would report a 5-deep `if` pyramid and a 5-deep config object as the same number. Not reporting beats reporting something that doesn't mean what it says.
  - `naming_smells` also stays empty for JS/TS on purpose: `naming.rs` genuinely does need the Python AST.
  - 10 new Rust tests (parameters from a declared function and from an arrow function, no-parameter case, the comma-inside-a-default/generic case, 6 parameters firing and 5 not, a 60-line function firing, a short clean function producing nothing, ordering by line, and TypeScript taking the same path). **692 Rust tests total (682 previous + 10)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`biome check` clean, `test_static_analysis.py` unchanged and passing. Verified live in a real browser against a 4-function `.js` file: exactly 2 findings — `muchosParametros` (6 > 5) and `funcionLarga` (63 > 50) — with the 2-parameter function and the destructured-defaults function both correctly silent.

### 🟡 Phase 23 — Execution Intelligence *(runtime instrumentation — a different kind of tool than everything above)*

Everything in Phases 1–22 is static analysis: source text in, facts out, no execution required. This phase is architecturally different — it needs a running process, ptrace/eBPF, or live packet capture, which is why it stayed an uncommitted "long-term" idea bucket for a long time. Numbered here to be honest that it's a real destination, not to claim it's close:

- [x] **Empirical Big-O validation for Fortran's matrix-multiply shape** — the first real slice of this phase, and much narrower than the other four bullets below (no ptrace/eBPF/packet capture needed, just `gfortran` and `std::process::Command`). Born from a direct ask: rather than adding Fortran to Sythrall's own engine as decoration ("Fortran matters" with no concrete task), this makes Fortran do real, functional work inside the engine for the first time — compiling and running actual Fortran to check Sythrall's own static claim, not just detecting Fortran in someone else's project.
  - New `services/complexity/src/fortran_bench.rs` — `complexity-engine`'s first-ever process execution (confirmed via full-workspace grep: zero `std::process::Command`/`Command::new` anywhere in this binary before this). Compiles a **fixed ~25-line Fortran kernel that Sythrall itself writes** — never the user's code, never anything uploaded — a generic `n×n` matrix multiply parameterized by `n` as a command-line argument (`GET_COMMAND_ARGUMENT`, compiled once with `gfortran -O2`, run 4 times at calibrated sizes). This is the scope boundary that keeps the execution surface tiny: there is no code path anywhere in this module that compiles or runs anything other than this one fixed template, so it never inherits the arbitrary-code-execution risk a general sandbox would need to solve.
  - Sizes (`300, 450, 600, 800`) were calibrated by hand against this dev machine's `gfortran`/Windows `CPU_TIME` resolution (~15.6ms granularity) so even the smallest measurement clears the clock's resolution instead of reporting a false zero — confirmed live before committing to the constants, not guessed. Each size is run 3 times and the **minimum** is kept (standard micro-benchmarking practice: OS/scheduling noise can only ever add time, never subtract it, so the minimum tracks the kernel's real cost more closely than a mean would).
  - `fit_exponent()` — a plain least-squares fit on `(ln n, ln seconds)`, no new dependency, `None` below 2 valid points. The response's `note` field states explicitly, same hedging discipline as `purity.rs`/Type-1 (Phase 16): this confirms the general shape scales as O(n³), it does **not** validate any specific user function's performance.
  - Deliberately no external timeout crate, no manual process-polling: the only thing this module ever executes is 100% Sythrall-authored, with sizes it chooses itself — there is no untrusted input on the execution path for a hang to come from. Documented explicitly in the module as a boundary that would need its own design if this ever runs anything less trusted.
  - `TempWorkDir` — a `Drop`-guarded temp directory (`fs::remove_dir_all` best-effort) so a failed compile/parse never leaves an orphaned `.f90`/binary in the system temp dir, same "no silent leaks" discipline already applied to the Force Graph memory leak earlier this session.
  - New endpoint `POST /execution/validate-matmul` (no body — kernel and sizes are fixed for this first version), a new Python router `apps/api/routers/execution.py` (deliberately **not** added to `static_analysis.py`, whose own docstring says "análisis estático... SIN IA" — this endpoint executes code, a different category), and `complexity_client.py::validate_matmul_bigo_rust()` (30s timeout override, matching the tiered-timeout convention already used for whole-project scans).
  - Frontend: a "Validar empíricamente" button appears next to any function's `Matrix?` badge (`numerical_algorithm_note`, Phase 20) in the Static panel. Since the kernel is generic rather than per-function, one click validates for the whole session — the result renders as a shared `n^X.XX` badge (green within ±0.3 of 3.0, amber otherwise) on every matching row, with the full hedge in its tooltip.
  - 7 new Rust tests (`fortran_bench::tests`) — 4 pure-math tests for `fit_exponent` (no `gfortran` needed), plus 3 that check `gfortran` availability and either run the real end-to-end compile+execute (confirmed working on this dev machine, exponent measured ≈2.9–3.6 across runs, comfortably inside the `2.0..4.0` sanity band chosen to catch a broken implementation without demanding lab-grade precision on a shared, noisy dev machine) or exercise the graceful-degradation path if `gfortran` isn't present — same "runs for real when available, degrades honestly when not" discipline as every other optional dependency in this codebase. 2 new Python tests (`test_complexity_client.py`) mirroring the same available/unavailable split.
  - New `examples/fortran/scientific_intelligence_demo.f90` (added alongside Phase 20, reused here) — `MATMULT` in that file is exactly the shape that triggers this validation, so it doubles as the manual end-to-end test fixture.
- [x] **Memory visualizer (stack/heap/data/bss) — shipped as static analysis, NOT the live-process inspection this bullet's own wording originally implied.** Confirmed explicitly with the user before building anything, not assumed: attaching a debugger to an arbitrary running process would be a genuinely new, unprecedented risk surface for this engine (inspecting/executing third-party code under a debugger) — `fortran_bench.rs`'s own module doc already draws this exact line ("running third-party code needs its own sandbox design, don't silently extend this"). What actually shipped: `services/complexity/src/memlayout.rs`, a new module that classifies every C/C++ variable into stack/data/bss **by its declaration in the AST** — correct by construction from C/C++ storage-class rules (never ambiguous, not a guess): a local variable without `static` is stack; a `static` local or a file-scope variable is data (has an initializer) or bss (doesn't); `extern` declarations are skipped (no storage in this translation unit); function prototypes aren't variables at all.
  - **`"heap"` is deliberately not a variable region — it's a separate allocation-site list**, a pedagogically more correct model than tagging the pointer itself: `int *p` is a stack variable regardless of what it points to; what actually lives on the heap is the memory `malloc`/`new` hand back. `allocations` records every `malloc`/`calloc`/`realloc`/`free` call (C) and `new_expression`/`delete_expression` (C++, real tree-sitter-cpp node kinds, not a regex guess), with the target variable resolved only in the two common, unambiguous patterns (`T *x = malloc(...)`, `x = malloc(...)`, `free(x)`) — no data-flow tracking beyond that, `variable: null` rather than a guessed value when the pattern doesn't match.
  - Shares the tree `cparse.rs` already parses for functions/classes/etc. — `memlayout::build(root, source)` takes the same `Node`, never parses the same text twice. Wired into `CParseResult` as a new `memory` field, riding along in the existing `/parse/c`/`/parse/cpp` responses exactly like `call_graph`/`wasm_hints` already do — no new endpoint.
  - Every result carries an explicit `note` hedge, same honesty discipline as `fortran_bench.rs`'s own: *"static declaration classification, not a measurement of a running process."*
  - 15 new Rust tests (`memlayout::tests` — one per region-classification case, `extern`/prototype exclusion, `malloc`/`free`/`new`/`delete` variable resolution including the unresolvable case, and an end-to-end region-count check) and 5 new Python tests (`TestMemoryLayout` in `test_static_analysis.py`, exercising the real sidecar end-to-end) — all 89 pre-existing `test_static_analysis.py` tests pass unmodified.
  - Frontend: `StaticParseResult` gains an optional `memory_layout` field (`client.ts`); `static.ts` gets a new `_renderMemoryLayout()` — a color-coded list grouped by region plus an allocations list, same `.metric-section` convention as `_renderCallGraph`/`_renderWasmHints`, deliberately **not** a box-and-arrow animated diagram (that level of visualization is already named separately as Phase 29's "Execution Path Simulator" — not reinvented here). New CSS in `static-addon.css` (`.st-mem-*`), reusing existing `--info`/`--ok`/`--muted`/`--orange` tokens rather than inventing a new palette. **365 Rust tests total (350 previous + 15), 563 backend tests total (558 previous + 5), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build` clean.**
- [ ] Concurrency analyzer (races, deadlocks, mutex/atomic misuse) — needs real execution or tools like ThreadSanitizer, not AST inspection
- [ ] OS engine (threads, paging, scheduling, IPC) — needs kernel-level tracing
- [ ] Network analyzer (TCP/TLS/QUIC/WebSocket) — needs packet capture; this is a Wireshark-shaped tool, not a static analyzer
- [ ] Security analyzer beyond pattern detection (ROP, heap spray, use-after-free exploitation) — competes directly with mature SAST tools (Semgrep, CodeQL, Bandit); the realistic version folds into Phase 21 above as "detect the pattern + explain the CWE," not a full exploit-analysis engine

## Where this is going, next: a platform, not just a core

Phases 13–23 above are one arc — Sythrall's own core learning to read code from the algorithmic structure underneath it down to the machine. Phases 24–30 below are a second, different arc, added 2026-08-30 from a direct, detailed ask: **the core doesn't have to know everything itself.** A real enterprise stack is rarely one language or one system — it's an ERP, a SQL Server instance, spreadsheets exported as CSV, and a C/C++ system that's been running since before most of the team was hired. The honest answer isn't teaching the Rust core every format and system that could ever show up; it's a typed extension boundary the core exposes, so the user activates only what their own stack actually needs:

```
                    Sythrall
                        │
                Extension System
                        │
        ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐
        ↓               ↓               ↓
    Connectors    Format Extensions   Language /
   (SQL Server,      (.xlsx, .csv,    Modernization
    Postgres,         .parquet,        Extensions
    GitHub, S3...)     .xml...)      (C/C++, COBOL,
                                       Fortran...)
        │               │               │
        └───────────────┼───────────────┘
                         ↓
             The user activates only
              what their stack needs
```

This second arc changes what Sythrall is honestly describable as. "Reads code, computes Big-O" (the framing the Phase 13–23 section above opens with) is still accurate for that arc alone, but Phases 24–30 point somewhere bigger: a **Software Intelligence & Modernization Engine**, not a linter and not a static-analysis tool that happens to have plugins bolted on. The shape, end to end — source code and its dependencies in; parsed and analyzed through the same Computer Science Intelligence Phases 13–23 already built (mathematics, security, architecture); a Modernization Graph (Phase 25) turning those findings into per-piece Keep/Refactor/Replace/Rewrite/Migrate candidates; a migration validated (Phase 26) before it's ever proposed as done, not just generated and handed over; the result benchmarked and reported, never asserted:

```
                    PROJECT
                       │
             ┌─────────┴─────────┐
             ↓                   ↓
        SOURCE CODE          DEPENDENCIES
             │                   │
             └─────────┬─────────┘
                       ↓
              COMPUTER SCIENCE
                 INTELLIGENCE (Phases 13–23)
                       │
       ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
       ↓               ↓                ↓
   Mathematics      Security       Architecture
   (Big-O/Θ/Ω)      (CWE/taint)    (graphs/smells)
       │               │                │
       └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                       ↓
              MODERNIZATION (Phase 25)
                       │
          ┌────────────┼────────────┐
          ↓            ↓            ↓
        Rust          Zig       Modern C++
          │            │            │
          └────────────┼────────────┘
                       ↓
              VALIDATE (Phase 26) → BENCHMARK → REPORT
```

Every language in the table above earns a slot in this pipeline because of what it specifically contributes, never because it exists: Rust is where the deterministic analysis lives; C/C++ is both an analysis target and the raw material Modernization reads; Zig only enters a component when Rust's own abstractions are themselves the wrong fit (ABI/memory control, C interop — never "because it's fast"); Assembly is the last rung, reached only after Rust and Zig/C have both been tried and profiling still shows a real gap; Fortran is the numerical-kernel step in a pipeline that starts as a Python prototype and ends orchestrated by Rust; Python stays the home for AI/ML/scientific work, not a legacy the migration is trying to erase. Every bullet from here to Phase 30 is 🔴 — vision, confirmed directly with the user, not a commitment or a timeline. Phase 24 already shipped a real plugin interface (3 bullets below) before this broader framing existed; everything else here is genuinely new ground, sequenced so each phase's own honesty note says exactly why it isn't built yet, the same discipline this document has held everywhere else.

### ✅ Phase 24 — Extensibility Platform *(the plugin/capability interface Phases 25–30 all build on top of)*

Every phase above adds directly to Sythrall's own codebase — reasonable while one person maintains it, but each of Phases 13–23 is realistically a plugin's worth of scope on its own. This phase is the boundary that lets that work happen outside the core without every analyzer becoming a `routers/` file Sythrall itself has to own forever. What shipped here (3 bullets below) was scoped deliberately small — no marketplace, no sandboxing, no third-party trust model, because none of that had a reason to exist before a second real plugin. **That's no longer the ceiling**: the vision below organizes future plugin work into 5 typed categories instead of one flat "plugin" bucket, because a SQL Server connector, a `.xlsx` parser, and a CWE detector are genuinely different shapes of thing, even though `plugin.rs`'s manifest interface (shipped) is generic enough to describe all of them without changing:

- 🔌 **Connectors** — connect Sythrall to an external system: SQL Server, PostgreSQL, Oracle, MySQL, GitHub, GitLab, S3, Azure. None shipped yet.
- 📄 **Format Extensions** — understand a file format Sythrall's own parsers don't: `.xlsx`, `.csv`, `.parquet`, `.json`, `.xml`, `.pdf`, `.avro`, `.protobuf`. None shipped yet.
- 🧠 **Analysis Extensions** — add intelligence over data a Connector/Format Extension already exposed, or over code the core already parses. Named retroactively: everything Phases 13–23 already shipped (CWE/Security, Graph, Dependency, Data-Structure, Numerical) already *is* this category, just not filed under the name until now.
- ⚙️ **Language Extensions** — add specialized language support. Also named retroactively: the 6 languages `plugin.rs` already serves (Python/C/C++/JS/TS/Fortran) are this category; Fortran is the proof case the 2nd bullet below already cites.
- 🔄 **Modernization Extensions** — understand a legacy format/language, detect what's incompatible with a modern target, then propose or execute a *validated* migration — never a blind convert. This is the category Phase 25 below develops in full; nothing here duplicates that phase, this bullet only names where it fits in the taxonomy.
- [x] **Plugin manifest + capability interface** — new `services/complexity/src/plugin.rs`: a purely declarative `PluginManifest` (`id`, `category`, `needs: &[Capability]`, `extensions`, `parser`, `features`, `builtin`) with zero dependency on the parser modules it describes — a manifest declares what a plugin does, it doesn't live inside the implementation. `builtin_manifests()` returns the 6 built-in language plugins (python/c/cpp/javascript/typescript/fortran), served over a new `GET /plugins/manifests`. Caught and fixed a real staleness bug while porting the content: the Python entry's `parser` field still said `"Python ast (stdlib)"`, a leftover from before Phase 18 — the real parser has been `rustpython-parser` in the Rust sidecar since then, with the stdlib `ast` module only running in the no-sidecar fallback skeleton. Corrected while rewriting, not left stale.
- [x] **One plugin type shipped end-to-end** — Fortran (Phase 20) is the proof case, exactly as this bullet suggested. `apps/api/services/static_parser.py::parse_file` no longer dispatches through a literal `if ext == ".py": ... elif ext in (".f", ...): ...` chain: extension → language-id resolution now goes through `services/plugin_registry.py::extension_map()`, itself derived from the same manifests `plugin.rs` serves. Fortran is no longer "another branch hardcoded into `static_parser.py`" — it resolves through the identical mechanism as the other 5 languages, not a special case. The actual `_parse_python`/`_parse_c`/`_parse_cpp`/`_parse_js`/`_parse_ts`/`_parse_fortran` functions are untouched — only *how* one gets selected changed, which is exactly why all 552 pre-existing backend tests kept passing unmodified: strong evidence the new dispatch is behaviorally identical to the old one, not just superficially similar.
  - **Manifests are fetched once at Python startup**, same timing and the same accepted degradation profile as `HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE` (`check_complexity_engine_sync()`, already computed once at import time in `main.py`) — if the sidecar isn't up in that exact instant, `plugin_registry.py` falls back to a local `_FALLBACK_MANIFESTS` constant (the identical 6-language content, frozen as a floor, not a second hand-maintained source of truth competing with `plugin.rs`) for the rest of that process's life. This is a pre-existing risk class in this codebase (the same race already exists for `HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE`), not a new one introduced here.
  - `routers/static_analysis.py::/languages` is rebuilt from the same manifests instead of its own separately-hardcoded dict — **zero frontend changes required**: `StaticLanguagesResult` in `client.ts` and `dashboard.ts::_renderLanguages` consume the identical JSON shape as before (confirmed via exploration that `features` was already unrendered dead weight in the UI, so nothing there was at risk either). `available` stays a Python-computed concern (sidecar-up-ness, not a manifest property): `True` for `"python"` (has its own fallback skeleton), `HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE` for the other 5 (no fallback of their own).
  - 5 new Rust tests (`plugin::tests` — 6 manifests present, no empty extensions/features, Fortran's 6 extensions all present, no extension claimed by two plugins at once, JSON serializes to the expected snake_case shape), 7 new Python tests (2 in `test_complexity_client.py` against the real rebuilt sidecar, 5 in a new `test_plugin_registry.py` covering both the manifest-driven and the fallback path via explicit monkeypatching). **290 Rust tests total (285 previous + 5), 552 backend tests total (545 previous + 7), Clippy/`tsc`/`ruff` clean.**
- [x] **Extension vs. plugin split** — named where the docs live now: `plugin.rs`'s module doc carries the formal definition (a *plugin* implements the manifest/capability interface above and adds analysis capability; an *extension* adds UI, consuming a plugin's output over the same JSON shape every panel already reads, no new interface of its own), and the two files the ROADMAP itself already pointed to as examples (`apps/web/src/panels/problems.ts`, the Phase 12 Problems tab; `static.ts`, which consumes all 6 language plugins directly) now cross-reference it in their own header comments. No new TypeScript type, no new architecture — exactly "a documented name for a seam that already exists," nothing more claimed.
- [x] **Permission model, schema only — no review UI, no enforcement yet** (2026-09-01, explicit user request to advance this ahead of a real consumer, against the default "wait for one" recommendation). New `Permission` (`read_project`/`analyze_files`/`network`/`filesystem`/`execute_code`/`secrets`) and `GrantLevel` (`Allowed`/`Denied`/`Limited`/`Sandbox` — `Sandbox` its own level, not an `Allowed` variant, since running third-party code under sandbox is a completely different infrastructure decision, still undesigned, from simply permitting it unrestricted) in `plugin.rs`, plus a new `PluginManifest::permissions` field. Distinct from the pre-existing `Capability` enum, which describes what a plugin needs from the analysis pipeline itself (AST? call graph?) — `Permission` describes what it needs from the outside system/world, the real security question a future Connector will need to answer. A manifest only lists the permissions it actually requests (`Allowed`/`Limited`/`Sandbox`) — absence means "not requested," not an explicit `Denied` entry for the other 5, same convention `needs: &[Capability]` already established. All 7 built-in plugins declare only `read_project`+`analyze_files` (`Allowed`), mirrored in `plugin_registry.py`'s Python-side fallback manifest. **Still explicitly not built**: any review UI, any real enforcement, and any plugin that actually needs `network`/`execute_code`/`secrets` — this is pure declarative infrastructure, same "don't build ahead of a real consumer" trap `AIProvider` (Phase 27) already documents avoiding, advanced here only because the user asked for it directly, not because a real need appeared. 3 new Rust tests, no Python tests (static data mirrored from Rust, not new logic).
- [ ] **`AIProvider` — moved to Phase 27 (AI Intelligence)**, where it now lives alongside Sythrall's own ML-orchestration future instead of sitting alone in this phase's list; not duplicated here, see that phase for the full bullet.
- [ ] **Real marketplace/SDK/sandbox — developed in full in Phase 30 (Sythrall Ecosystem)**, not this phase; this bullet used to say "deferred, no timeline" and still means exactly that, just with a fuller vision of what it'd look like once it's not deferred anymore.

The one rule that survives contact with everything above: `apps/api` keeps working with zero plugins installed — always has, this phase only adds a documented way to build on it, never a requirement to.

### 🟡 Phase 25 — Modernization Intelligence *(understand legacy code, propose or execute a validated migration — never a blind convert)*

The Modernization Extension category Phase 24 names, developed as its own phase because it's genuinely more than "one more plugin type" — it's the piece that makes Sythrall useful to a company whose stack didn't start yesterday, which the user's own scenario made concrete: an ERP feeding SQL Server, exporting to Excel/CSV, sitting next to a C/C++ system nobody's touched a spec for in years. Sythrall's job here is never a transpiler — it's *"study a legacy system and determine which parts are candidates for modernization,"* the user's own framing, kept verbatim because it's the right scope boundary:

```
Legacy Code
     │
     ↓
Language Analysis → Architecture Analysis → Security Analysis → Performance Analysis → Dependency Analysis
     │
     ↓
Modernization Graph — per piece, not one verdict for the whole project
     │
     ├── Keep
     ├── Refactor
     ├── Replace
     ├── Rewrite
     └── Migrate
     │
     ↓
Modernization Report
```

- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, first slice shipped: manual memory management** — new `services/complexity/src/modernization.rs`, genuinely the first real engine in this phase, not another vision bullet. Builds directly on Phase 23's `memlayout.rs`: zero new AST work, pure reinterpretation of the `allocations` it already tracks. Pairs each acquisition (`malloc`/`calloc`/`realloc`/`new`) with a release (`free`/`delete`) of the *same variable name* in the same file — two patterns, both with evidence attached, never a bare label: **`manual_memory_raii`** (both sides found — medium confidence, suggests a smart pointer in modern C++ or Rust's ownership model, either would make a forgotten/duplicated `free` a compile error instead of a runtime bug) and **`unmatched_allocation`** (acquisition with no release found anywhere in the file — high confidence, either a real leak or ownership transferred somewhere this heuristic can't follow, suggests Rust ownership specifically since that model forces the transfer to be explicit). **Honest about its own limit, not silent**: matching is by variable name within the whole file, not real per-scope tracking — the same simplification `memlayout.rs::resolve_target_variable` already accepted, documented again here rather than pretending this is more precise than it is. Wired into `CParseResult` as a new `modernization` field, riding along in the existing `/parse/c`/`/parse/cpp` responses exactly like `memory`/`call_graph` already do — no new endpoint, no new network round-trip. Rust pointer-ownership analysis across function boundaries, raw-pointer-without-malloc patterns, and unsafe-cast detection are explicitly **not** attempted in this slice — real future work, not silently folded in.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, second pattern shipped: double-free detection** (2026-08-31) — extends `modernization.rs` with **`double_release`**: any variable released (`free`/`delete`) more than once in the same file. Unlike the first slice's 2 patterns, this isn't "could be modernized" — it's a real correctness bug already present (undefined behavior in C/C++), reported at maximum confidence since counting repeated releases has none of the cross-scope ambiguity that name-based acquire↔release pairing carries (documented as an accepted limit in the first slice). Points at the *repeated* release's own line, not the first release or the original allocation, since that's where the double-free actually happens. Suggests `rust_ownership` specifically — Rust's compiler rejects moving or freeing an already-moved value, making this class of bug unwritable, not just easier to avoid. 3 new Rust tests, 1 new Python test — all pre-existing tests for the first slice's 2 patterns pass unmodified. **404 Rust tests total (401 previous + 3), 578 backend tests total (577 previous + 1), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build` clean.**
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, third pattern shipped: unsafe-realloc-reassignment detection** (2026-08-31) — extends `memlayout.rs`'s `AllocationSite` with a new `reassigns_same_pointer: bool` field, computed in `extract_allocations` by comparing `realloc`'s first argument against the variable it's assigned back to, then `modernization.rs` reads it to flag **`unsafe_realloc_reassignment`**: the classic `p = realloc(p, size)` bug — if `realloc` fails it returns `NULL`, and reassigning that directly to `p` overwrites the only reference the program had to the original allocation, an instant leak with no way back. Like `double_release`, this is a real bug already present, not a stylistic suggestion, so it's reported at high confidence; unlike the acquire/release patterns, no cross-scope ambiguity is even possible here since it's a single-call check on `realloc`'s own arguments. Suggests `rust_ownership` — `Vec`'s internal reallocation can't be told to overwrite its own only handle, so this bug class has no Rust equivalent to write in the first place. 2 new Rust tests in `memlayout.rs`, 2 new Rust tests in `modernization.rs`, 2 new Python tests — all pre-existing tests for the first two slices' 3 patterns pass unmodified. **408 Rust tests total (404 previous + 4), 580 backend tests total (578 previous + 2), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build` clean.**
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, fourth pattern shipped: use-after-free detection** (2026-08-31) — the first pattern in this file that walks the AST directly instead of only reinterpreting `allocations`, because "a use" isn't an allocation site. New `use_after_free_candidates()` in `modernization.rs`: for each release (`free`/`delete`) with a resolved variable, it searches for the first later occurrence of that same identifier inside the window *[release line, next acquisition of the same variable)* — reassigning to fresh memory closes the window, since the pointer becomes valid again. An occurrence only counts as a "use" if it isn't the left-hand side of an assignment (`p = NULL` is exactly the safe idiom this pattern must not punish, not a use) and doesn't coincide with another known allocation-site line for the same variable (so a second `free` isn't double-reported here *and* as `double_release`). Reported at `medium` confidence, not `high` like the other bug patterns — this is a genuine textual/line-order heuristic with no real control-flow analysis, so a `free` inside one branch followed by an unconditional use can be a false positive if the branches were actually mutually exclusive; that limit is stated plainly in the module doc, not hidden. Required exposing `memlayout::walk_flat`/`text_of` as `pub(crate)` (reused rather than duplicated, since `modernization.rs` already depends on `memlayout`'s types) and changing `analyze_c_cpp`'s signature to take the parsed `root`/`source` that `cparse.rs` already had on hand. 4 new Rust tests (real parsed C source, not synthetic `AllocationSite` literals, since this pattern needs actual AST structure), 3 new Python tests — all pre-existing tests for the first three slices' 3 patterns pass unmodified (13 existing Rust test call sites updated via a thin `analyze()` test wrapper that feeds them an empty AST, since none of them exercise this new pattern). **412 Rust tests total (408 previous + 4), 583 backend tests total (580 previous + 3), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build` clean.**
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, sixth pattern shipped: dangling local reference** (2026-08-31) — `return &x;` where `x` is a stack-region variable (including parameters) whose `scope` (already computed by `memlayout.rs`) matches the enclosing function. Unlike the 5 patterns above (which match by variable name across the *whole file* — the accepted limitation documented since the first slice), this one uses per-function `scope` directly, so it carries none of that cross-function-collision risk: when it fires, it's a real bug with no exception in standard C/C++ semantics, not a "could be" — automatic storage for `x` ends the instant the function returns, so the pointer handed back is dangling immediately. Reported at `high` confidence, same tier as `double_release`/`unsafe_realloc_reassignment`, not `medium` like `use_after_free` (which has genuine control-flow ambiguity this one doesn't). A `static` local is correctly never flagged — `memlayout.rs` already classifies those as `data`/`bss`, not `stack`, since static storage duration outlives the function call. Confirmed against the actual tree-sitter-c/cpp grammar before writing anything (not guessed): address-of (`&x`) is a `pointer_expression` node with an `operator` field, not `unary_expression` (that grammar node covers only `!`/`+`/`-`/`~`). Deliberately narrow — `return &arr[i]`/`return &s.field` (address of an array element or struct member) aren't detected, same "don't claim more than the pattern can prove" discipline as the rest of this engine. Required exposing `memlayout::function_definition_name` as `pub(crate)` (reused, not duplicated). 6 new Rust tests (real parsed C source per test, including one confirming per-function scoping actually isolates two same-shaped functions from each other — the exact ambiguity the other 5 patterns can't rule out). Rides along in the existing `modernization` field on `/parse/c`/`/parse/cpp` — no new endpoint, no Python test (pure Rust computation, same policy as every other Phase 25 pattern — this data was already Rust-relay-only before this bullet). **462 Rust tests total (456 previous + 6)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser (Playwright) against a hand-written 2-function C file: `local_value` flagged `dangling_local_reference`/high confidence at the exact `return` line, alongside the pre-existing `unmatched_allocation` finding for the file's unrelated unfreed `malloc`.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, seventh pattern shipped: `sizeof(pointer)` allocation size bug** (2026-08-31) — `malloc(sizeof(x))` where `x` is itself declared as a pointer (`T *x`), not the struct/type it's meant to point to. One of the most classic mechanical C mistakes: `sizeof(x)` measures the POINTER's size (4 or 8 bytes), not whatever `x` is supposed to point at, so the resulting buffer is far too small — writing past those few bytes corrupts adjacent memory with no visible error at the time. Purely mechanical, no forward-scanning needed unlike `use_after_free`/`dangling_local_reference`: the bug is fully contained in the `malloc` call itself, independent of anything before or after it in the function — reported at `high` confidence for that reason. Determines "is this a pointer" by walking declarations/parameters directly in Rust (`declarator_name_and_is_pointer()`), since `memlayout.rs::MemoryVariable::type_hint` only keeps the base type (`int` for `int *p`, no star) — done without touching `memlayout.rs`'s public schema at all, keeping this pattern fully self-contained in `modernization.rs`. **A real grammar surprise caught by the first test, not guessed**: `sizeof(p)` parses as `sizeof_expression value: (parenthesized_expression (identifier))` — the parentheses around the argument produce their own `parenthesized_expression` wrapper node, so the `value` field points at that wrapper, not directly at the identifier; unwrapping it once was needed before the pattern worked at all. Deliberately narrow: only the literal `malloc(sizeof(identifier))` shape is checked — `calloc`/`realloc` share the identical idiom in their own size argument but are explicitly out of scope for this first slice, and `sizeof(*x)`/`sizeof(x[0])`/`sizeof(SomeStruct)` (the *correct* forms) are exactly what this pattern must never flag, confirmed by its own negative tests. 8 new Rust tests (real parsed C source per test — detects the bug for a plain local, a parameter, a pointer-to-pointer, and an inline-initialized declaration; confirms no false positive for a non-pointer `sizeof` target, `sizeof(*p)`, `sizeof(int)`, or the same idiom via `calloc`). Rides along in the existing `modernization` field — no new endpoint, no Python test (pure Rust computation, same policy as every other pattern in this phase). **475 Rust tests total (467 previous + 8)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `struct Node *n; n = malloc(sizeof(n));` example: both `sizeof_pointer_allocation` (this pattern) and the pre-existing `unmatched_allocation` (the same `malloc` has no matching `free` either) correctly appeared side by side as two independent, genuinely different findings about the same line — not a duplicate of the same evidence under two labels.
- [x] **`sizeof_pointer_allocation` extended to `calloc`/`realloc` — closes the gap the seventh pattern named as explicitly out of scope** (2026-08-31). The bug shape is identical across all 3 allocator functions (`sizeof(pointer)` measures 4/8 bytes instead of the pointed-to type's real size), and the existing search already scanned *every* argument of the call for a `sizeof_expression`, not just the first — so covering `calloc(n, sizeof(p))`/`realloc(p, sizeof(p))` alongside `malloc(sizeof(p))` was a one-condition change (`alloc_fn` now matches `"malloc" | "calloc" | "realloc"` instead of only `"malloc"`), not a new pattern or a new `pattern` string. `realloc`'s own first argument (the pointer being resized, a bare identifier never wrapped in `sizeof`) is a natural near-miss for this search — confirmed by a dedicated test that it's correctly skipped in favor of the second argument's `sizeof_expression`. Also confirmed `sizeof(*p)` (the correct form) still never fires through `calloc`, and an unrelated function sharing the same `f(sizeof(p))` shape by coincidence (not one of the 3 real names) doesn't trigger either. 4 test changes in `modernization.rs`: the existing negative test asserting `calloc` was *not* detected was inverted to assert it now is, plus 3 new tests (`realloc` positive, `calloc` with the correct `sizeof(*p)` form, and the unrecognized-function-name guard). **500 Rust tests total (497 previous + 3)**, Clippy clean. No Python/frontend changes — same `ModernizationSummary`/`ModernizationCandidate` shape as before, so nothing downstream needed to change.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, eighth pattern shipped: unchecked malloc dereference** (2026-09-01) — `p = malloc(...)` (or `calloc`/`realloc`) dereferenced (`*p`), indexed (`p[i]`), or field-accessed (`p->field`) with no null/truthiness check (`if (p)`/`if (!p)`/`if (p == NULL)`/`if (p != NULL)`, or the same 4 shapes against `0`) found earlier in the function. Deliberately `malloc`/`calloc`/`realloc` only, not `new` — C++'s `new` throws `std::bad_alloc` by default instead of returning null, so it doesn't share this bug class (barring `nothrow`, out of scope here). Needs forward-scanning like `use_after_free` — directly reuses its `use_after_free_window_end()` helper for the `[allocation line, next allocation of the same variable)` window, rather than duplicating that logic. Deliberately narrow on what counts as a "dangerous use": passing the pointer as a function argument (`free(p)`, or any function that might itself handle `NULL`) does **not** count — only a direct dereference/index/field-access does, to avoid flagging the single most common and genuinely safe case (handing the pointer to another function) as a false positive. Confidence `medium`, not `high`, same tier and same reason as `use_after_free`: this is a textual/line-order heuristic, not real control-flow analysis, so a check that exists in the text but doesn't actually gate that specific use (e.g. no `return` in its body) still counts as "checked." Confirmed against the real tree-sitter-c grammar before writing anything (not guessed): `p == NULL` parses as `binary_expression left: (identifier) right: (null)` — `NULL` gets its own dedicated `null` grammar node, not a generic identifier — and `!p` is `unary_expression operator: "!" argument: (identifier)`. 10 new Rust tests (real parsed C source per test: plain dereference, index, field access, all 4 check shapes correctly suppressing it, function-argument-passing correctly NOT counting as a dangerous use, `calloc` sharing the same detection, and `new` correctly never triggering this pattern at all). Rides along in the existing `modernization` field — no new endpoint, no Python test (pure Rust computation, same policy as every other pattern in this phase). **528 Rust tests total (518 previous + 10)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `int *buf = malloc(...); buf[0] = 42;` (no check) example: both `unmatched_allocation` (the same `malloc` also has no matching `free`) and the new `unchecked_malloc_dereference` correctly appeared side by side as two independent findings about the same allocation.
- [x] **Deprecated Python API/library usage — closes the half of the "Legacy-format modernization candidates" bullet that can be done today, and fixes an empty report nobody was reporting** (2026-09-05). Until now `modernization.rs` was this phase's only engine and it only understood C/C++. The side effect wasn't "a missing feature": the project rollup (`modernization_by_file`) aggregates over *every* file, so **a pure-Python project showed an empty Modernization report** — indistinguishable from a project with nothing to modernize. That is the same misleading-emptiness shape this repo has now hit four times. The other half of the bullet (old SQL dialects, legacy data formats) still waits on Phase 24's Connectors/Format Extensions to be able to read those artifacts at all, and is left explicitly open.
  - New `services/complexity/src/pymodern.rs`, emitting the **same candidate shape** as the C/C++ engine (`variable`/`pattern`/`line`/`current`/`suggested_target`/`reasoning`/`confidence`). That is the whole integration story: `static_analysis.py::parse_project` already aggregates `r["modernization"]["candidates"]` generically and the panel renderer already draws them, so **the project rollup and the file view both work untouched** — the only frontend change is four `MODERNIZATION_TARGET_LABEL` entries.
  - **Six patterns.** `removed_stdlib_module` (24 modules: PEP 594's "dead batteries" plus the 3.12 removals — `imp`, `distutils`, `asyncore`, `telnetlib`, `cgi`, `lib2to3`…), `deprecated_library_module` (`pkg_resources`), `removed_stdlib_api` (`time.clock`, `inspect.getargspec`, `base64.encodestring`, `ssl.wrap_socket`, `asyncio.coroutine`, plus the 15 `unittest` aliases removed in 3.12), `deprecated_stdlib_api` (`datetime.utcnow`, `utcfromtimestamp`, `locale.getdefaultlocale`, `asyncio.get_event_loop`), `moved_to_collections_abc` (the 25 ABCs whose `collections` aliases died in 3.10), and `legacy_typing_generic` (PEP 585/604).
  - **The confidence split is a real distinction, not a mood.** `high` = the API **no longer exists** in some published version, so on that version it's a `ModuleNotFoundError`/`AttributeError`, not a warning. `medium` = still present, just deprecated — the cost is future or readability.
  - **The central limit, and it's in the `note` the user sees: this module does not know which Python version the project targets.** A project pinned to 3.8 is entitled to `typing.List`. So every finding *names the version* the API left or was deprecated in, rather than asserting the code is broken — the user knows their target, this analysis doesn't. Pinned by its own test.
  - **Three matching decisions each defended by a test.** Module matching requires a dot boundary, or `import importlib` would be a false positive against the `imp` rule — flagging the very module this engine recommends. `ssl.wrap_socket` is matched by full dotted suffix and never by last segment, because `context.wrap_socket()` on an `SSLContext` *is the fix*, and a tail match would report the correct code as the bug. `utcnow`/`utcfromtimestamp` *are* matched by last segment, deliberately: they exist on nothing else in the stdlib, so this covers `from datetime import datetime as dt` → `dt.utcnow()`, which a dotted match would miss.
  - **Legacy `typing` generics are one finding per import, not one per annotation** — a file with 40 `List[int]` annotations gets one candidate, at the import line, which is where the fix is. Without that, this pattern alone would drown every other finding in the report.
  - **A gap in the shared walker, found and worked around rather than papered over**: `walk.rs` never descends into `decorator_list`, so `@asyncio.coroutine` — an attribute with no call at all — was invisible. (That's also why `classifiers::memoization_info` takes decorators as a separate parameter instead of finding them itself.) Handled locally inside this module rather than changing a walker eight other modules depend on, with the reason written at the call site.
  - Explicitly not attempted: **third-party library deprecations** (`numpy.float`, `DataFrame.append`) — those need to know which version of each package the project uses; `pkg_resources` is the one exception, deprecated by setuptools itself for every version. And module matching is by name, not resolved path: a project with its own `pipes.py` gets reported anyway, stated in the note rather than hidden.
  - **The browser run failed on its first try, and what it exposed was worse than this feature.** The project rollup came back empty even though the file view was perfect — because a project scan doesn't go through `static_parser.py` at all, it goes through `scanner.rs`, whose Python arm hand-builds its own JSON. That arm was missing `modernization`… **and `control_findings`, which had been missing since Phase 15.** So the optimal-control and Gibbs findings shipped in the two rows above worked file-by-file and **silently never reached a project-level scan** — nothing reported it, because `static_analysis.py` aggregates with `.get(..., [])` and a missing key just looks like "no findings." The previous increment's browser verification used the single-file path and passed while the project path was blind. Both keys added to the Python arm and `modernization` to the notebook arm, plus a test — `el_escaneo_de_un_python_trae_todas_las_claves_que_el_proyecto_agrega` — that scans a Python file with a bad `R` matrix and an `import imp` and asserts *both* keys arrive. The existing coverage test checks LANGUAGES; this one checks KEYS, which is the failure mode that hid for weeks.
  - 23 new Rust tests. **840 Rust tests total (817 previous + 23)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions (the Python side is one relay key, same as `control_findings`). Notebooks get it for free — they run the same engine. Verified live in a real browser on a Python-only 2-file project: `legacy.py` produced 8 candidates with the correct confidence tiers, `moderno.py` produced none at all, and the project rollup — the thing that was empty before this change — listed `legacy.py` as **5 high / 3 medium** with its full per-pattern breakdown.

- [x] **Auditing the scanner arm-by-arm after the last bug found two more of the same, and one of them means a shipped fix never actually worked** (2026-09-05). The previous row found `scanner.rs`'s Python arm silently dropping `control_findings` and `modernization`. That looked like an oversight; it was a *pattern*. Every arm of `parse_one_inner` hand-builds its own JSON object, and the project aggregator reads with `.get(key, [])` — so a dropped key is indistinguishable from "this file has no findings." Nothing anywhere reports it. So instead of fixing one arm, this pass extracted the exact list of keys `compute_project_analysis` reads and diffed it against what every arm actually emits.
  - **JS/TS was the bad one.** `JsTsResult.security_findings` (Phase 21) and `.structural_smells` (Phase 22) exist for one reason, stated in their own doc-comments: a JS/TS project scored **Security 100/100 and Quality 100/100 by absence of analysis**, and those two fields were the fix. The fix landed in `static_parser.py` — the loose-files path — and never in `scanner.rs`, which is the path a *project* analysis takes. The project view is the only place those scores are computed at all. **So the bug those two bullets claim to have fixed was still fully intact exactly where it mattered**, and both bullets' browser verifications passed because they analyzed single files.
  - **C/C++ was the other.** `cparse` computes the full `ModernizationReport` (12 patterns) in the same call, and the scanner threw it away. `modernization_by_file` — the project-scope rollup written *specifically* for the C/C++ engine — had never received a single candidate through a real project scan.
  - **Fortran and Assembly are clean, and that was checked rather than assumed**: their parsers genuinely compute no security or smell data, so their `[]` is honest, not an omission. That distinction is now written at each site, so the next reader can tell an honest empty from a dropped key without going to the parser to find out.
  - 2 new Rust tests, and both were **confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code before the fix was applied** — `el_escaneo_de_un_js_trae_los_hallazgos_de_seguridad_y_los_smells` (a 3-line JS file with `innerHTML`/`eval` sinks) and `el_escaneo_de_un_c_trae_los_candidatos_de_modernizacion` (a `malloc` with no `free`). Together with the Python one from the previous row, all three check KEYS rather than languages, which is the failure mode that hid for weeks behind a green suite.
  - **A behaviour change worth stating loudly: Project Health scores for JS/TS projects will now drop.** They were inflated by missing analysis, not by good code. A previously "perfect" JS/TS project comparing against an old snapshot will see a real change in Security and Quality — that is the correction landing, not a regression.
  - **842 Rust tests total (840 previous + 2)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions. No Python or frontend changes — the aggregator and the panel were always right; they were being fed empty lists.

- [x] **Structural smells for C/C++ — the third and last language family scoring Quality 100/100 by absence of analysis** (2026-09-05). The audit in the row above fixed JS/TS's findings never reaching the project view. Following the same thread one step further: `structural_smells` was a hardcoded `[]` for C/C++ in *both* paths, so a pure-C project's Quality score was 100 for the same reason a JS/TS project's was — nothing was looking. Phases 21 and 22 named this failure mode for JS/TS; this closes it for the last family that still had it.
  - New `cparse.rs::structural_smells_c`, reusing the **same three `kind`s** Python and JS/TS already emit (`long_function`, `excessive_parameters`, `deep_nesting`) and the same `smells.rs` thresholds — so `SMELL_LABEL`, the colours and the ordering all already exist: **zero frontend changes**. `large_class`/`god_object` are deliberately left out: `CStruct` stores only name/line/kind, with no member or method count, and estimating one would be inventing a number.
  - **`else if` chains are not nesting, and that is a deliberate divergence from the Python engine.** Confirmed against the real tree-sitter tree before writing anything (dumped, not guessed): `else if` parses as `else_clause > if_statement`, genuinely nested. But a 6-branch dispatch written with `else if` — completely ordinary C — would then be reported as "6 levels of nested blocks", which is a **false statement**, not a style opinion. So the walker follows an `else`-chain at the same depth as its `if`. Pinned by its own test.
  - `void f(void)` counts as **0 parameters**, not 1 — `void` as the sole declaration is C's way of writing "no parameters", and counting it would trip the threshold one parameter early in every older C file. And the declarator is searched through the subtree rather than among direct children, because `int *f(...)` wraps the `function_declarator` in a `pointer_declarator` — looking only at direct children would silently skip every pointer-returning function. Both have their own test.
  - Nested scopes don't leak: a lambda's own nesting is not the containing function's, the same scope rule `is_tail_recursive` already applies after its own audit bug.
  - **Same behaviour change as the row above, in the other direction too**: C/C++ projects will now show a real Quality score instead of a perfect one. And a Python file whose only "deep nesting" was a long `elif` chain keeps its Python-side finding — the two engines now disagree on that case, and fixing Python's is a separate call because it moves an already-shipped number.
  - Verified live in a real browser on both paths: a hand-written `.c` with five functions produced exactly **3 smells** — `deep_nesting` on the genuinely nested one, `excessive_parameters` on the 6-argument one, `long_function` on the 62-line one — while the 6-branch `else if` dispatch and the 2-line `suma` produced nothing, in both the single-file view and the project scan.
  - **A calibration cliff this made visible, flagged rather than silently retuned**: `quality_penalty = smells / (functions + classes) * 300` means **any project where 1 in 3 functions carries a smell scores 0**, with no gradient below that. The test file (4 of 5 functions smelly, on purpose) scored 0, which is arguably right for it — but that ratio is now far easier to reach for real projects, since C/C++ and JS/TS have only just started contributing smells at all. Whether the multiplier should be softened is a product decision that moves an already-shipped number across every language, so it is left to the user rather than changed as a side effect of this pass.
  - 8 new Rust tests. **850 Rust tests total (842 previous + 8)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **CWE catalog for C/C++ — the fourth and last instance of "100/100 by absence of analysis", and the one in the Security dimension** (2026-09-05). The row above fixed Quality for C/C++. `security_findings` had exactly the same shape: a hardcoded `[]` in both paths, so a C project with a real command-injection bug scored **Security 100/100**. With this, all four language families now feed both of the score dimensions that depend on per-file analysis — the pattern that showed up four times in this repo is closed.
  - New `services/complexity/src/csecurity.rs`, with **3 CWEs chosen specifically because `modernization.rs` does not already cover them**. That engine has reported `gets`/`strcpy`/`strcat` (CWE-120), non-literal format strings (CWE-134) and width-less `scanf` (CWE-120) since Phase 25 — emitting those here too would put the same line under two different labels, which reads as two problems when it is one. A test pins that: a file full of `strcpy`/`gets`/`printf(fmt)` produces **zero** security findings here and still produces its modernization candidates, so nothing was lost and nothing is doubled.
  - **CWE-78 (OS Command Injection)** — `system`/`popen`/the `exec*` family whose command argument is not a string literal. Same criterion `format_string_vulnerability` already uses and the same one gcc's `-Wformat-nonliteral` uses. `High` severity, `Medium` confidence, and the recommendation says why: a non-literal argument can be a fixed internal string, so what is proven is the shape, not that external input reaches it.
  - **CWE-337 (Predictable Seed)** — `srand`/`srandom` whose argument contains `time`/`clock`/`getpid`. `High` confidence, because this one *is* visible in the call rather than estimated: the seed is the clock, so the whole sequence reproduces from knowing roughly when the process started. The recommendation states the honest scope — fine for a simulation or a game, not for a token, a key or a nonce.
  - **CWE-732 (Incorrect Permission Assignment)** — an octal mode literal with the other-write bit set (`0666`, `0777`) passed to `chmod`/`fchmod`/`mkdir`/`creat`/`open`/`openat`. `High` confidence, the mode is read directly. **`umask` is deliberately excluded**: its semantics are inverted (the bits passed are the ones removed), so applying the same check would report the opposite of what happens. The mode's argument index differs per function — `open` carries it third, `chmod` second — and a test pins that, because using one index for all of them would break both.
  - `0` alone is not treated as an octal mode (it is decimal zero), and `0644`/`0660`/`0755` correctly never fire — each with its own test, since the false-positive side is what makes a security list worth reading.
  - 13 new Rust tests, plus the scanner's C key-test extended to assert CWE-78 arrives through a project scan too — the same KEYS check that caught the last three of these. **863 Rust tests total (850 previous + 13)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Structural smells for Fortran and Assembly — and a correction to what an earlier row in this phase claimed** (2026-09-05). The scanner audit two rows above said Fortran's and Assembly's empty `structural_smells` were "honest, not an oversight, because their parsers genuinely compute none of this". **That was circular.** They computed no smells because nobody had written any — the exact excuse that had been true of C/C++ one row earlier. Both parsers already produce `loc`, and Fortran already produces `do_loop_depth`, so the smells were derivable the whole time. Every language family that has functions now feeds the Quality dimension.
  - `fparse.rs::structural_smells_fortran` — `long_function` from `loc`, and `deep_nesting` from `do_loop_depth`, both fields the parser already computed in the same call. Zero new AST work, the same "pure reinterpretation" `modernization.rs` was built on.
  - **The nesting message says what was actually counted.** `do_loop_depth` counts `DO` loops only, not `IF`/`SELECT` blocks, so it is a *lower bound* on real nesting. Using the standard threshold against it is deliberately conservative — when it fires there are genuinely 5+ nested loops, no ambiguity — and the message reads "counted over DO loops only […] so the real nesting is at least this deep" rather than claiming a depth it did not measure. Pinned by its own test.
  - `asmparse.rs::structural_smells_asm` — **only `long_function`, and the reason matters more than the smell.** An assembly procedure has no declared parameters (they travel in registers or on the stack per the calling convention — that is `callingconv.rs`'s job, not a count), and no nested blocks to count: control flow is jumps, and reconstructing nesting from jumps means building a CFG, which is a jump in complexity rather than a reinterpretation. Said in the code instead of leaving a silent gap.
  - `excessive_parameters` is absent for Fortran too, and for a concrete reason rather than an oversight: `FortranFunction` does not store parameters, so it would need new parsing.
  - Both manifests now declare `structural_smells`, and the manifest-truth test from the row above was **extended from 3 languages to 5** — with generated 60-line fixtures, since sixty literal lines in a test hide the intent rather than showing it. That test is what stops this declaration from going stale like the last ones did.
  - 5 new Rust tests. **869 Rust tests total (864 previous + 5)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions. No frontend changes — same three `kind`s, so the labels already existed.

- [x] **Optimization and cleanup over shipped phases — measured, not guessed** (2026-09-05). A pass with no new feature: profile what already ships, fix what the numbers point at, and remove duplication that had accumulated. Every figure below was measured before and after on this machine, in release mode.
  - **`parse_c`: 276 ms → 41 ms per call (6.8×) on a 481-line C file.** Profiling by stage showed `modernization` taking **261 of those 276 ms** — every other stage was single-digit milliseconds. The cause: `use_after_free` and `unchecked_malloc_dereference` each did a **full `walk_flat(root)` per allocation site**. A file with 80 `malloc` and 80 `free` walked the entire tree 160 times — O(allocations × nodes). Fixed by inverting the question: instead of asking "does this node use variable X?" once per variable, ask "which variable does this node use?" once and index by it. The two predicates went from `(node, name) -> bool` to `(node) -> Option<name>`, and one `TreeIndex` built in a single pass serves both patterns. **`modernization` alone: 261 ms → 21 ms (12.7×)**, with all 78 of its tests passing unmodified.
  - **`ml::detect`: 11.1 ms → 1.0 ms per call (10.7×).** `detect_ml_issues` had 35 loose `Regex::new(...)` calls and the three pattern tables compiled theirs *inside the loop* — **62+ regex compilations per analysed file**, and this module runs per file. Compiling a regex parses the pattern and builds an automaton; it dominated the cost, far above the matching itself. Fixed with a local `re!` macro backed by a `OnceLock` per call site, which keeps the pattern written where it is used — a global "name → pattern" table is exactly the kind of parallel structure that goes stale in silence, and this repo has already fixed that failure mode several times. The metric table was the worst case: it compiled a second regex *per alternative, per metric, per call*; both are precompiled now.
  - `fparse.rs` had three constant regexes rebuilt per node/function; hoisted the same way. The fourth is built from the file's own variable names, so it stays where it is, and that is said in the code rather than left as an apparent oversight.
