  - **`is_string_literal` existed twice** — as itself in `csecurity.rs` and as `is_literal_format_arg` in `modernization.rs`, byte-identical bodies. That is the dangerous kind of duplicate: the `modernization.rs` copy carries a doc-comment about a real false positive that took effort to find (a missing `raw_string_literal` made `printf(R"(...)", user)` report as a non-literal format). The second copy was born correct only because it was written by copying the first — the next fix to one would not have reached the other.
  - **`text_of` existed three times**, identical, in `cparse.rs`, `fparse.rs` and `memlayout.rs`. And `walk_flat`/`function_definition_name` lived in `memlayout.rs` as `pub(crate)` — working, but a module about memory layout is not the home of traversal helpers four others depend on.
  - New `services/complexity/src/tsnode.rs` collects them, marking explicitly which are language-agnostic (`text_of`, `walk_flat`) and which name **C/C++** grammar nodes specifically (`is_string_literal`, `function_definition_name`). Same treatment for `last_seg`/`tail_of`, written four times under two names: now `structure::last_segment`, next to the `node_name` that produces the dotted string in the first place.
  - **Checked and found clean, rather than "fixed" for the sake of it**: the frontend has **zero** exported symbols unused elsewhere, and the remaining non-regex `unwrap()`s are all `m.get(0).unwrap()` on a regex match — group 0 always exists when there is a match, so there is no panic path there. Reported as verified instead of invented.
  - **869 Rust tests total, unchanged** — that is the point of this row: no behaviour moved. Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Second optimization pass — the C/C++ path was 8× slower than Python or JS, and the reason was structural** (2026-09-05). Same method as the previous pass: measure first. Profiling a project scan by language on the same amount of code gave **C 93 ms vs. Python 11 ms vs. JS 12 ms for 10 files each**. Python's own path was measured too and came back healthy (`analyze_rich`: 4 ms on a 604-line file), so the gap was C-specific, not a general problem.
  - **`tsnode::walk_flat` allocated a `TreeCursor` per visited node.** It was recursive and called `node.walk()` on every node. The C/C++ pipeline runs ~15 full traversals of the same tree across `cparse`, `memlayout`, `modernization` and `csecurity`, so that cost multiplied. Rewritten to use **one cursor for the whole tree** (`goto_first_child`/`goto_next_sibling`/`goto_parent`), same visit order, same coverage. This alone sped up every stage: `structural_smells_c` 1.7×, `csecurity` 1.8×, `memlayout::build` 1.6×, `extract_includes` 3.4×.
  - **And there were TWO more byte-identical copies**, both named `walk`: one in `cparse.rs` and one in `fparse.rs` — the fourth and fifth duplicate of the same traversal, found while fixing the first and while auditing after fixing it. Deleted; their 17 call sites now use the shared one, so Fortran benefited from the single cursor too. None remain today: verified with a grep for the exact pattern.
  - **The bigger cause: `modernization` did 8 full traversals of the same tree**, four of them filtering for the very same `call_expression`. The index built for the previous pass now also collects nodes by kind in that same walk, and each pattern iterates its own list. A `visitar()` helper keeps `walk_flat`'s exact signature on purpose: switching a pattern from "walk everything and filter" to "iterate your list" is then a one-line change, and the body — with its `return`s meaning "skip this node" — stays untouched. Converting them to `for` loops would have turned those `return`s into "exit the function", a silent bug waiting to happen.
  - **A real mistake, caught by the tests, worth recording.** The mechanical rewrite assigned `sizeof_pointer_allocation` to the `call_expression` list, but that pattern's outer walk iterates **functions**, not calls — it needs to know which of a function's variables are pointers before looking at the `malloc`s inside. Six tests failed immediately. That is what they are for, and it is why this kind of rewrite gets verified rather than eyeballed.
  - **Measured on the same 20-iteration stage profiler, before and after** — `parse_c` on a 481-line file: `modernization` **20.6 ms → 5.4 ms (3.8×)**, and the whole pipeline **37.6 ms → 16.4 ms (2.3×)**. Combined with the previous pass, `parse_c` has gone from 276 ms to roughly 20 ms end-to-end this session.
  - **882 Rust tests, unchanged** — that is the point again: no behaviour moved. Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Third cleanup pass — duplication found by scanning for it, not by noticing it** (2026-09-05). The previous pass found the same traversal copied five times by accident. This one looked on purpose: a small script extracts every function body in the crate, normalises whitespace, and reports bodies that are byte-identical across modules. Three groups came back, and the largest was the one that mattered.
  - **`fit_exponent` existed in 9 modules, identical.** It is the least-squares fit over `(ln n, ln t)` that produces the *measured exponent* — the number Phase 26 uses to say whether a kernel really scales as O(n) or O(n²). **That is not a convenience helper, it is the measurement.** Nine copies mean a correction to the fit (handling a degenerate point set, moving to weighted least squares, changing the discard criterion) would apply to one kernel and not the other eight — and the entire point of the phase is that the numbers are comparable *between* kernels.
  - **`TempWorkDir` + its `Drop` existed in 10 modules, and `supports_dash_m32` in 3.** New `benchsupport.rs` holds all three. The one thing that genuinely differed between the `TempWorkDir` copies — the temp-directory prefix, which keeps concurrent validations from colliding and makes leftovers identifiable — is preserved as a parameter rather than lost.
  - **This reverses an explicit earlier decision, and the reason is written in the new module.** Each copy carried a doc-comment saying the duplication was deliberate: "every Phase 26 validator is self-contained, the same pattern `cparse.rs`/`jsts.rs` already follow with their own small helpers instead of a cross-dependency". **That precedent no longer exists** — those small helpers were the 4-5 copies of `text_of`/`walk` consolidated into `tsnode.rs` two passes ago, precisely because one had been fixed and the others had not. The justification was pointing at something that had been retired.
  - **`CallEdge` was declared five times** — `asmparse`, `cparse`, `fparse`, `jsts`, and `rich` (as `RichCallEdge`) — with the same two fields and none importing another. A function-body scanner cannot see that: the copy is of a *type*, not a body. Unified in `graph.rs` along with `call_edges()`, which also carries the three rules that were copied four times and are decisions rather than details: only emit an edge when the callee is a function *of this file*, exclude self-calls (recursion is `recursion.rs`'s job, and a one-node loop pollutes every cycle metric), and deduplicate by `(from, to)` because calling something three times is one relationship.
  - **425 lines of duplicated code removed**, and `fib_bench::fit_exponential_base` was correctly left alone: it fits the *base* of an exponential (`t ≈ a·bⁿ`, using raw `n` against `ln t`), which is a different measurement — an exponential shape has no power-law exponent to fit.
  - 6 new Rust tests for the now-shared code, including that the exponent of a perfect line is 1 and of a perfect quadratic is 2, that a degenerate point set returns `None` instead of an invented exponent, and that the directory prefix still reaches the path. **888 Rust tests total (882 previous + 6)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [ ] **Legacy-format modernization candidates** — old SQL dialect/schema → modern SQL; deprecated library/API usage → its current replacement; a legacy data format → a modern one — same "detect the gap, propose, don't silently convert" shape as the C/C++ case above, generalized to non-code legacy artifacts once Phase 24's Connectors/Format Extensions exist to read them in the first place.
- [ ] **Modernization Report as a first-class output, richer than today's flat candidate list** — a real per-piece graph (Keep/Refactor/Replace/Rewrite/Migrate) spanning more than memory-management patterns, same "evidence, not a verdict" discipline Phase 21's security findings already established and this slice's own `reasoning`/`confidence` fields already start.
- [x] **Modernization Report, per-file rollup at project scope — deliberately NOT the 5-category graph above** (2026-09-01). Explicit design decision, confirmed with the user before writing anything: the Keep/Refactor/Replace/Rewrite/Migrate graph would need pattern→category mapping rules that today would be invented, not backed by what any individual pattern actually proves — a real risk of violating the "evidence, never a verdict" principle Phase 21 already established and this same phase's own `confidence` field already practices. What shipped instead: `routers/static_analysis.py::parse_project` now aggregates `modernization` candidates across files (`modernization_candidates`, same `{**c, "file": ...}` pattern `security_findings`/`structural_smells` already use) and groups them per file (`modernization_by_file`: total/high/medium counts + a pattern breakdown) — a map of "where to look first," not a replacement for opening a file's own full candidate list. Surfaced in the Static panel's project view via a new `_renderModernizationByFile()`. 2 new Python tests (`TestProjectHealth`, genuine Python-side aggregation logic, same policy as the rest of that class) — verified live in a real browser. No Rust changes.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, ninth pattern shipped: C-style cast (`c_style_cast`, C++ only) — closes "unsafe-cast detection," the gap this phase's own summary paragraph had named as unattempted** (2026-09-01). Any `(Type)expr` — tree-sitter already classifies this as its own `cast_expression` node (confirmed against the real tree by a throwaway dump before writing anything: `cast_expression type: (type_descriptor ...) value: (expression)`, distinct from a function-style call `Type(expr)`), so no custom heuristic was needed to tell a real cast apart from a parenthesized grouping expression — the grammar already does that. **The first pattern in this file restricted to one language**: only fires when `is_cpp` (a parameter `cparse.rs::parse_with` already had on hand, threaded through `analyze_c_cpp`'s new 4th argument), because plain C has no `static_cast`/`reinterpret_cast`/`const_cast` to migrate to — flagging every C-style cast in a `.c` file would be pure noise with no real suggestion behind it. Not a bug like `double_release`/`dangling_local_reference` — a C-style cast isn't wrong by itself, it's a style/intent suggestion — so confidence `medium`, same tier as `manual_memory_raii`. Displays the target type as the finding's `variable` field (e.g. `Derived*`) since this pattern has no actual variable/scope concept, just a syntactic match. 14 test-call-site signature updates (the existing `analyze_c_cpp(...)` calls across the file's whole test suite, all passing the new `is_cpp: false` to preserve their existing C-only behavior unchanged) plus 5 new Rust tests (a primitive-type cast, a pointer-type cast, the language gate confirmed off for `.c` files, a parenthesized non-cast expression confirmed to never match, and the reasoning text). **560 Rust tests total (555 previous + 5)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written 2-class `.cpp` file with both an `(int)` cast and a `(Derived*)` cast — both appeared as independent findings with the correct target type and the `static_cast / reinterpret_cast / const_cast` suggestion.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, tenth pattern shipped: format string vulnerability (`format_string_vulnerability`, CWE-134)** (2026-09-02) — a call to `printf`/`fprintf`/`sprintf`/`snprintf`/`syslog` whose format-string argument (the position varies per function — 1st for `printf`, 2nd for `fprintf`/`sprintf`/`syslog`, 3rd for `snprintf`, confirmed against the real tree-sitter-c grammar before writing anything, including that `"a" "b"` string concatenation gets its own `concatenated_string` node that should count as a literal too) is **not** a string literal — the same criterion gcc/clang's own `-Wformat-nonliteral` already uses. If that argument can carry external data, whoever controls it controls format directives (`%x` leaks stack memory, `%n` writes it) without needing any other argument — one of the most classic, best-documented C vulnerability classes there is. Unlike `c_style_cast`, applies to C **and** C++ equally (the printf family isn't C++-specific). Purely mechanical, no forward-scanning, same self-contained shape as `sizeof_pointer_allocation` — but confidence `medium`, not `high`: unlike a pointer-size bug (always wrong regardless of context), a non-literal format argument could legitimately never carry external input (e.g. a fixed localized string chosen at runtime), so this flags the risky *shape*, not a proven exploit, same honesty already applied to `use_after_free`/`unchecked_malloc_dereference`. Suggests `rust_format_macro` (a new suggested-target, not reused from an existing one) — Rust's `format!`/`println!` macros require a compile-time string literal, so this bug class can't be written at all, not even by accident. 8 new Rust tests (`modernization::tests` — non-literal `printf`, a literal correctly not firing, concatenated-literal correctly not firing, `sprintf`'s 2nd-argument position, its literal form not firing, `snprintf`'s 3rd-argument position, an unrelated function name never matching, and the reasoning text). **576 Rust tests total (568 previous + 8)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `printf(user)` example — rendered with `confianza: media` and the `Rust format! / println!` suggestion.
  - **A real false-positive bug found by a fresh audit and fixed the same day (2026-09-02)**: `is_literal_format_arg()` only recognized `string_literal`/`concatenated_string`, missing C++11's `raw_string_literal` (`R"(...)"`) — its own distinct grammar node in tree-sitter-cpp, a sibling of `string_literal`, not a subtype. `printf(R"(Hello %s)", user)` is a perfectly fixed, compile-time literal — exactly the case `-Wformat-nonliteral` does *not* warn on — but this check flagged it as a finding anyway. Fixed by adding `raw_string_literal` to the match; harmless for C, where that node kind never appears (raw strings are C++-only syntax). 1 new regression test confirming the exact repro no longer fires.
- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, eleventh pattern shipped: unsafe C string function (`unsafe_string_function`)** (2026-09-02, same day) — a call to `gets`/`strcpy`/`strcat`. **The first pattern in this file that doesn't need to look at the call's arguments at all** — every other pattern inspects what's passed to a function; this one flags the function NAME itself, since none of these 3 accept a destination-buffer-size parameter in the first place, so the function can never protect itself from overflowing. `gets` gets `high` confidence — it has no safe calling convention whatsoever, which is why C11 removed it from the standard outright rather than merely discouraging it. `strcpy`/`strcat` get `medium` — a caller can use them safely if it already guaranteed the sizes some other way this purely textual heuristic can't see, same honesty tier as `format_string_vulnerability`. Suggests `rust_ownership` (reused, not a new target) — Rust's `String`/`&str` always bounds-check and grow on their own, so this overflow class doesn't exist. 6 new Rust tests (`modernization::tests` — `gets` at high confidence, `strcpy` at medium, `strcat` also detected, the bounded `strncpy` variant correctly not firing since it's the very alternative this pattern suggests, two unsafe calls in one function reported independently, and the reasoning text mentioning CWE-120 and the suggested replacement). **582 Rust tests total (576 previous + 6)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written function calling both `gets` and `strcpy` — both appeared side by side with their own confidence tier. 11 patterns in the catalog now.

- [x] **C/C++ modernization candidates, twelfth pattern shipped: unbounded `scanf` (`unbounded_scanf`)** (2026-09-02) — `scanf`/`fscanf`/`sscanf` whose literal format contains a `%s` or `%[...]` conversion with **no field width**. The same buffer overflow `gets` represents, but from the opposite direction: here the function isn't the problem, the *call* is — `scanf("%19s", buf)` is perfectly safe, `scanf("%s", buf)` can never be. That distinction is why this gets `high` confidence while `strcpy`/`strcat` sit at `medium`: for those, a caller could have guaranteed the sizes somewhere this textual heuristic can't see, whereas a width-less `%s` literally means "write until the input ends" regardless of anything else in the program — the evidence is complete inside the call itself.
  - A hand-written directive parser rather than a new regex dependency (same minimal-dependency discipline `security.rs` already applies for the same reason), handling the four cases that actually matter: `%%` (a literal percent, not a directive — `"100%%s"` correctly produces nothing), `*` (assignment suppression: `%*s` reads and discards, writing to no buffer at all), width digits, and length modifiers between width and conversion (`%ls` without a width is just as unbounded). Scansets skip to the closing `]` while honoring C's real rule that `%[]abc]` and `%[^]abc]` contain a *literal* `]` right after the `[`/`^` that doesn't close the set.
  - Only inspects **literal** formats: with the format in a variable there are no directives to read, and this pattern doesn't guess. That case belongs to `format_string_vulnerability`, which today covers only the `printf` family — extending it to `scanf` is separate work, deliberately not blended in here.
  - 14 new Rust tests (positive `%s`, explicit width, `%*s`, numeric-only formats, `%%`, correct format-argument index for `fscanf`/`sscanf` — which would silently inspect the `FILE*`/input buffer if the index were wrong —, scanset with and without width, the literal-`]` scanset edge case, `%ls`, a non-literal format producing nothing, C++ parity, coexistence with a `gets` finding in the same function, and the "first *unbounded* directive is the one reported, not the first directive" case). **630 Rust tests total (616 previous + 14)**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean after adding the pattern to the frontend's union type and summary shape (the only frontend change needed — rendering reads `c.pattern` directly, there's no per-pattern label map to extend). Verified live in a real browser against a 4-function C file: exactly 2 candidates — `scanf("%s")` at line 4 and `fscanf("%[^\n]")` at line 12, both `high` → Rust ownership — with the bounded `%19s` and the numeric-only `%d %lf` correctly producing nothing. 12 patterns in the catalog now.

The C/C++ work above is real (12 patterns now: manual-memory-RAII, unmatched-allocation, double-release, unsafe-realloc-reassignment, use-after-free, dangling-local-reference, sizeof-pointer-allocation — the last one now covering `malloc`/`calloc`/`realloc` alike —, unchecked-malloc-dereference, c-style-cast, format-string-vulnerability, unsafe-string-function, and unbounded-scanf; use-after-free, unchecked-malloc-dereference, format-string-vulnerability, and `strcpy`/`strcat`'s medium tier within unsafe-string-function all remain admitted heuristics, not certainties, given none has real control-flow/data-flow analysis behind it), but this phase is still far from done: no concrete architecture decision has been made about how a proposed migration gets *validated* (matching Phase 26 below) before Sythrall would ever suggest applying it — that's real design work for whenever a later slice tackles it, not assumed now. Real control-flow/branch analysis and anything beyond memory-management/cast/format-string/unsafe-function patterns remain unattempted.

### 🟡 Phase 26 — Algorithm Validation Engine *(generalizes Phase 23's Fortran matmul validation beyond one language, one algorithm shape)*

Phase 23 already shipped a narrow, real proof of this idea: `fortran_bench.rs` compiles and runs a fixed Fortran kernel to check whether a statically-predicted O(n³) shape actually measures as O(n³). This phase is that same pattern, generalized — a **static claim** (from the Big-O engine, Phase 13) compared against an **empirical measurement** from a controlled, Sythrall-authored experiment, not the user's own code:

```
Static claim (e.g. "O(n²)")
        ↓
Generate a controlled synthetic experiment
        ↓
Execute an isolated benchmark — same safety boundary fortran_bench.rs
already documents: never the user's code, only a template Sythrall itself
authors and controls end to end
        ↓
Fit a mathematical model to the measurements
        ↓
Compare: static ≈ empirical?
        ↓
"Predicted O(n²), measured n^1.94 — High confidence"  or
"Predicted O(n³), measured n^2.01 — ⚠ discrepancy detected"
```

- [x] **Beyond Fortran, beyond matrix multiply — 2 more kernels shipped, in Zig and in Assembly** (2026-08-31, direct response to the user pointing out that despite all the language-philosophy discussion, zero real Fortran/Assembly/Zig files existed in the repo). Same design work `fortran_bench.rs` already did once, repeated deliberately for 2 more languages, each validating a genuinely different algorithm shape rather than the same one twice:
  - **`services/complexity/src/zig_bench.rs`** — compiles and runs `kernels/validate_bubble_sort.zig` (a real bubble sort Sythrall itself wrote, `zig build-exe -O ReleaseFast`) at 4 sizes, validates O(n²). This is the first Zig code Sythrall's own engine has ever executed — not just used as a cross-compilation toolchain (Phase 29's bullet). Zig's `std.time.Timer` is high-resolution (unlike Fortran's coarse `CPU_TIME` on Windows), so the calibrated sizes (1000–8000) are far smaller than Fortran's. **A real bug caught by the first end-to-end test, not guessed**: `-femit-bin` needs its output path glued on with `=` as a single argument (`-femit-bin=path`) — passed as two separate `Command::arg()` calls, Zig parses it as a bare flag followed by a stray source-file argument and fails with a confusing "unrecognized file extension" error.
  - **`services/complexity/src/asm_bench.rs`** — assembles and runs `kernels/validate_sum_squares.s` (hand-written x86 GNU-as, AT&T syntax — the same syntax `asmparse.rs` already reads) plus a minimal C driver (`kernels/validate_sum_squares_driver.c`, needed only because a raw `.s` object has no entry point of its own) via `gcc`, validates O(n) for a single-loop sum-of-squares. Written for the 32-bit cdecl calling convention this dev machine's MinGW `as`/`gcc` actually target (confirmed via `gcc -dumpmachine` → `mingw32`, not assumed) — the argument arrives on the stack at `8(%ebp)`, the result in `%eax`, `%ebx` explicitly saved/restored since it's callee-saved in cdecl. Correctness spot-checked by hand before wiring into Rust at all: `sum_squares(10) == 385`, matching the closed form `n(n+1)(2n+1)/6`. Sizes calibrated in the hundreds of millions specifically because `clock()`'s resolution on Windows is coarse — confirmed live (200M/400M/800M/1600M iterations measured at 78/151/311/628ms, a clean ~2× per doubling) rather than guessed.
  - Both wired the identical way `fortran_bench.rs` already established: `POST /execution/validate-bubble-sort` / `POST /execution/validate-sum-squares` (`main.rs`), `validate_bubble_sort_rust()`/`validate_sum_squares_rust()` (`complexity_client.py`), `/validate-bubble-sort`/`/validate-sum-squares` (`routers/execution.py`, sharing a new `_degraded()` helper instead of copy-pasting the fallback shape 3 times). 10 new Rust tests (`zig_bench::tests`/`asm_bench::tests`, both with a real end-to-end test confirmed against the actual `zig`/`gcc` installed on this dev machine — not skipped, not mocked) and 4 new Python tests against the real sidecar. **401 Rust tests total (391 previous + 10), 577 backend tests total (573 previous + 4), Clippy clean.**
  - **The kernel that validates the pattern being trustworthy in the first place**: sizes/loop shapes chosen and calibrated live against this machine's actual toolchains before being committed to code — the same discipline `fortran_bench.rs`'s own sizes already documented, not relaxed for the 2 new languages.
- [x] **Zig and Assembly kernels wired into the frontend** (2026-08-31) — the backend half of the bullet above (`zig_bench.rs`/`asm_bench.rs`, their endpoints, their Rust/Python tests) had shipped fully tested, but nothing in the UI could reach either one: `client.ts` had no function for `/execution/validate-bubble-sort`/`/execution/validate-sum-squares`, and `static.ts` had zero buttons — an honest gap caught while auditing this phase's own completion state, not new work invented from scratch. Unlike matmul's control (which only appears inline next to a detected `numerical_algorithm_note` "Matrix?" badge — there's no equivalent "this looks like bubble sort" detector for either new kernel), both get a new always-visible **Algorithm Validation Engine** section in the Static panel, shown for `c`/`cpp`/`fortran`/`assembly` files: one row per kernel, each an independent button → spinner → measured-exponent badge, reusing the exact visual language `_renderMatmulValidationControl` established (a new shared `_renderKernelValidationRow()` helper parameterizes label, predicted exponent, and the `data-*` attribute so the 3 controls don't diverge in look or behavior). `client.ts` gains `validateBubbleSortBigO()`/`validateSumSquaresBigO()`, both returning the identical `EmpiricalValidationResult` shape `validateMatmulBigO()` already used — confirmed by curling all 3 `/execution/*` endpoints directly against a freshly built sidecar before wiring anything: bubble sort measured `n^2.14` (predicted `O(n²)`), sum-of-squares measured `n^1.00` (predicted `O(n)`), matmul unaffected at `n^3.89`. No backend changes; `tsc --noEmit`/`vite build`/`biome check` all clean.
- [x] **Two more algorithm shapes shipped: graph traversal and recursion depth** (2026-08-31) — direct response to the user asking to see real Zig/Assembly/Fortran files change, not just Rust/TS glue.
  - **`services/complexity/src/bfs_bench.rs`** — second Zig kernel, but a genuinely different algorithmic shape than `zig_bench.rs`'s bubble sort: `kernels/validate_graph_bfs.zig` builds a deterministic sparse graph (fixed out-degree 4 per vertex, with a ring edge guaranteeing every vertex is reachable from vertex 0 — without that guarantee a run could terminate early on a disconnected vertex, silently corrupting the measurement) and runs a queue-based BFS visiting all of it. With degree fixed, `E = O(V)`, so `O(V+E) = O(V)` — the *shape* being demonstrated is graph traversal with a queue, not a new exponent (this is exactly what the ROADMAP's own wording asked for: "graph traversal" as a shape, independent of whether its exponent happens to match an existing kernel's). Sizes (500k–4M vertices) calibrated live: below ~200k, Zig's `GeneralPurposeAllocator` overhead dominated and skewed the fit. Renamed away from the obvious `graph_bench.rs` after noticing it would collide in spirit (not in compilation — different Cargo target) with the pre-existing `benches/graph_bench.rs` Criterion benchmark for `graph.rs`'s call-graph performance, an unrelated thing.
  - **`services/complexity/src/fib_bench.rs`** — the first kernel in this phase to validate a **non-polynomial** shape: naive recursive Fibonacci (`kernels/validate_fib.f90`, deliberately *not* memoized — memoizing would make it O(n), which isn't what this kernel exists to prove), whose true complexity is **Θ(φⁿ)** where φ = (1+√5)/2 ≈ 1.618 (root of the recurrence's characteristic equation) — the tight bound, not the "O(2ⁿ)" upper bound commonly cited as a simplification. This needed genuinely new statistics, not a reuse of `fit_exponent`: polynomial growth (`n^k`) is linear on a log-log plot (`ln time` vs `ln n`), which is what `fit_exponent` already fits; exponential growth (`bⁿ`) is linear on a semi-log plot (`ln time` vs `n` untransformed) instead — applying `fit_exponent` to exponential data would produce a meaningless number that keeps drifting upward as the chosen N range grows, since exponential growth eventually outpaces any fixed polynomial. New `fit_exponential_base()` does the semi-log regression and recovers the base via `e^slope`. Verified live before committing to sizes: n=30/32 both landed in the same `CPU_TIME` quantization bucket (Windows' ~15.6ms clock resolution), so the final range (34–40) was chosen specifically to clear that; measured base landed at ≈1.58, within 2.3% of the theoretical φ. `EmpiricalValidationResult.estimated_exponent` is reused as the *measured base* here rather than adding a parallel response type — documented explicitly in both the Rust doc comment and the API response's own `note` field so the overloaded meaning is never silently assumed.
  - Both wired the same way as the other 3 kernels: `POST /execution/validate-graph-bfs` / `POST /execution/validate-fibonacci` (`main.rs`), `validate_graph_bfs_rust()`/`validate_fibonacci_rust()` (`complexity_client.py`), `/validate-graph-bfs`/`/validate-fibonacci` (`routers/execution.py`, reusing the existing `_degraded()` helper), and a frontend row each in the same **Algorithm Validation Engine** section Phase 26's frontend-wiring bullet above already built — the shared `_renderKernelValidationRow()` helper gained an optional `formatMeasured` callback so Fibonacci's row can honestly say `base≈1.58 (φ≈1.618)` instead of the `n^X.XX` label that would misdescribe what the number means for every other kernel. 12 new Rust tests (5 for `bfs_bench`, 7 for `fib_bench`, both with real end-to-end compiles against the actual `zig`/`gfortran` on this machine) and 4 new Python tests against the real sidecar — verified for real via direct `curl` against a freshly built sidecar before touching the frontend: BFS measured exponent ≈1.23 (predicted ≈1.0), Fibonacci measured base ≈1.58 (predicted φ≈1.618). **434 Rust tests total (422 previous + 12), 589 backend tests total (585 previous + 4), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.**
- [x] **Same governing rule as any native-language adoption** (Phase 10/11's benchmark-first discipline) — all 4 new-language kernels exist because the user explicitly asked for real Zig/Assembly/Fortran artifacts, not because a profiled bottleneck demanded them; that distinction is kept honest in the language-philosophy table above (Assembly's *own* implementation-language row still says "no profiled candidate" — these validation kernels are Phase 26's own controlled experiments, not evidence of a production hot path needing hand-written Assembly).
- [x] **Sixth kernel: O(n log n), a hand-written Assembly mergesort — and a real, serious stack-frame bug caught by exhaustive verification, not shipped by luck** (2026-08-31). `services/complexity/src/mergesort_bench.rs` compiles and runs `kernels/validate_mergesort.s`, an **iterative bottom-up mergesort** — deliberately not recursive (recursion is already `fib_bench.rs`'s job above; this kernel exists specifically to demonstrate O(n log n) via log₂(n) merge passes, each O(n), a much simpler thing to hand-write correctly than a recursive quicksort). `temp`, an auxiliary buffer the same size as `arr`, comes from a minimal C driver (`kernels/validate_mergesort_driver.c`, same reason as `validate_sum_squares_driver.c`: a raw `.s` object can't `malloc`) which also **verifies the array actually ended up sorted before reporting a time**, printing `-1` instead if not — a broken kernel must never silently feed a plausible-looking number into the exponent fit.
  - **The bug, found the hard way**: the first version wrote its locals (`width`, `i`, `left`, `mid`, ...) starting at `-4(%ebp)`. That's wrong — `push %ebx`/`push %esi`/`push %edi` happen *after* `%ebp` is set, so `-4`/`-8`/`-12(%ebp)` are where those three saved registers actually live, not free space. Every local write was silently clobbering a saved register; the epilogue's `pop` then restored garbage into it before `ret`. This was invisible at `-O0` (the caller reloads everything from its own stack, never trusting a callee-saved register to survive a call) but caused a real, reproducible segfault at `-O1` and above, where GCC *does* cache values like the `arr` pointer in a callee-saved register across the call, correctly trusting the cdecl ABI to preserve it — exactly what this bug violated. A first non-guarded reproduction attempt (heap padding, GDB watchpoints on an old Windows GDB 7.6.1) produced contradictory, unreliable signals; the bug was only pinned down precisely by allocating `arr`/`temp` via `VirtualAlloc`+`VirtualProtect(PAGE_NOACCESS)` so the buffer's end is a hard guard page — turning "sometimes corrupts something eventually" into an immediate, deterministic fault at the exact offending write. Fix: locals now start at `-16(%ebp)`, leaving `-4`/`-8`/`-12` alone. Re-verified for correctness across every size from 0 to 1,000,000 at `-O0` through `-O3` before trusting the kernel again — not just at the one size that happened to crash first.
  - **O(n log n) is not a clean power law**, unlike every other kernel's shape so far: the log-log exponent `fit_exponent` (reused as-is, no new statistics needed) measures for `n·log(n)` data isn't a fixed number — it lands a bit above 1.0 (measured ≈1.07–1.11 on this machine) and drifts slightly with the chosen size range, since `n log n` isn't literally `n^k` for any fixed `k`. The response's `note` says this plainly rather than implying false precision; the frontend still anchors the "close enough" check at exponent 1.0 (same ±0.3 band as the other roughly-linear kernels) because that's still enough to catch a badly broken merge (e.g. one that degenerates to O(n²)), just without claiming lab-grade precision on the number itself.
  - Wired the same way as the other 5 kernels: `POST /execution/validate-mergesort` (`main.rs`), `validate_mergesort_rust()` (`complexity_client.py`), `/validate-mergesort` (`routers/execution.py`), and a new row in the Static panel's **Algorithm Validation Engine** section. 5 new Rust tests (`mergesort_bench::tests`, including a real end-to-end compile+run against `gcc`) and 2 new Python tests against the real sidecar — verified via direct `curl` against a freshly built sidecar before wiring anything: measured exponent ≈1.07 against the O(n log n) prediction. **439 Rust tests total (434 previous + 5), 591 backend tests total (589 previous + 2), Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.**

The pattern is proven across 10 kernels now (3 languages, 7 distinct algorithmic shapes: cubic, quadratic, linear via four different algorithms — sum of squares, BFS-adjacent traversal, category aggregation, and closed-form linear regression — exponential, log-linear via two different techniques — bottom-up iterative merging and recursive divide-and-conquer partitioning — and, as of the bullet below, sub-linear via binary search), but this phase is still not "done": no architecture exists yet for picking a shape/language pair automatically from a static claim, or for validating anything beyond these 10 fixed, hand-authored kernels — that broader generalization is real future design work, not assumed now.

- [x] **Ninth kernel: recursive quicksort in Zig — the "recursive sorting shape" this phase had named as open** (2026-08-31). Fourth use of Zig in this engine (`zig_bench.rs`: bubble sort; `bfs_bench.rs`: BFS; `agg_bench.rs`: aggregation), and the first to demonstrate recursion in Zig within this phase (Fibonacci's recursion, Phase 26's 5th kernel, was in Fortran). New `kernels/validate_quicksort.zig` + `services/complexity/src/quicksort_bench.rs` — recursive quicksort with Lomuto partitioning, validating the same O(n log n) shape `mergesort_bench.rs` (Assembly, bottom-up iterative merging) already validates, but by a genuinely different technique: recursive divide-and-conquer partitioning, not iterative merging.
  - **A real correctness pitfall identified and designed around before it could bite, not discovered by accident**: pseudo-random test data bounded to a small range (like `validate_bubble_sort.zig`'s `% 1000`) would produce massive numbers of duplicate values once `n` reaches the hundreds of thousands this kernel needs — and a Lomuto partition degrades toward O(n²) on heavily-duplicated data (the classic "Dutch national flag" problem). Fixed by widening the value range to `% 1_000_000_000`, making duplicates statistically negligible at the sizes this kernel actually uses, so the plain last-element-pivot partition scheme reliably hits its average-case O(n log n) instead of accidentally validating a degenerate O(n²) case without anyone noticing.
  - **Self-verification before trusting any measurement**: the kernel checks the array is actually sorted after the recursive call returns and prints `-1` if not — same "a broken kernel must never silently feed a plausible number into the exponent fit" discipline `validate_mergesort_driver.c` already established, this time inline in Zig itself (no separate driver needed, unlike the Assembly kernels, since Zig has its own program entry point).
  - Sizes (250k/500k/1M/2M) calibrated live: measured exponent ≈1.08, consistent with the ≈1.07-1.11 range `mergesort_bench.rs` already measured for the identical shape via a different technique — direct evidence the two techniques converge on the same real complexity class, not just the same predicted label.
  - Wired the same way as every other kernel this phase: `POST /execution/validate-quicksort` (`main.rs`), `validate_quicksort_rust()` (`complexity_client.py`, pure relay), `/validate-quicksort` (`routers/execution.py`, reusing `_degraded()`), and a new row in the Static panel's Algorithm Validation Engine section (shared `_renderKernelValidationRow()`). 5 new Rust tests (`quicksort_bench::tests`, including a real end-to-end compile+run against `zig` on this machine) — no Python test, pure Rust-only relay per the established policy. **467 Rust tests total (462 previous + 5)**, Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser: badge rendered green (`n^1.07`, within the ±0.3 band), zero JS console errors.
- [x] **"Migrate away from numpy/pandas/scikit-learn", piece 1 of 3: matmul vs. real numpy, measured honestly — not assumed** (2026-08-31, user's own explicit framing: he wants Sythrall's numeric stack to *not depend on those libraries*, ported to Zig/Fortran/Assembly instead — matmul first, pandas aggregation and scikit-learn linear regression are the next 2 pieces, deliberately not attempted in this same slice). New `POST /execution/validate-matmul-vs-numpy` (`routers/execution.py`) runs the **existing, already-validated** `fortran_bench.rs` matmul kernel *and* real `numpy` (same 4 sizes, same values — `A[i,j]=(i+j) mod 7`, `B[i,j]=(i*j) mod 5`, `float32` on both sides, so the comparison is genuinely the same problem, not two problems that happen to share a name) side by side, timed with `time.perf_counter()`, minimum of 3 runs each — same microbenchmarking discipline as every Rust kernel above, just in Python since that's where `numpy` lives.
  - **The honest result, measured on this machine, not guessed**: numpy wins by roughly 170× at every size (e.g. n=800: Fortran triple-loop 0.734s vs. numpy 0.0043s). The response's `comparison_note` says this plainly — the point of this piece was never "prove Fortran beats numpy," it's **"measure before proposing a migration"**, the same principle the whole Algorithm Validation Engine already runs on. `fortran_bench.rs`'s kernel was written to validate an O(n³) *shape*, not to compete on speed; numpy delegates to BLAS/LAPACK, which is typically vectorized and multi-threaded. A native replacement that actually competes would need real cache-blocking/tiling, SIMD, or parallelism — named here as the real next step this piece does **not** claim to have done, not silently skipped.

Frontend: a new "Migración numpy/pandas/scikit-learn" sub-section under the Algorithm Validation Engine, a dedicated `_renderMatmulVsNumpyComparison()` (not the shared `_renderKernelValidationRow()` — this shows a 2-column timing table plus the honest comparison text, not a single measured exponent).   - New `POST /execution/validate-aggregation-vs-pandas` (`routers/execution.py`) runs the Zig kernel *and* real `pandas.DataFrame.groupby("category")["value"].agg(["sum","mean"])` on identical data (same 4 sizes, same `category = i % 10` / `value = (i*37+11) % 1000` formula), same timing discipline as piece 1.

Both pieces together make the actual lesson concrete: sometimes the library wins (piece 1), sometimes the native kernel wins (piece 2) — the discipline is measuring each case for real, never assuming either direction.

- [x] **Piece 3 of 3 (and last): scikit-learn linear regression vs. an 8th kernel — closes the migration goal** (2026-08-31). Fixed by adding a second `PRINT` line for the two values (ignored by the Rust-side parser, which only reads stdout's first line) — existing purely so the optimizer can no longer prove the computation is unobservable. Sizes (40M/80M/120M/160M) calibrated live: a wider range (240M) failed outright with a memory-allocation error on this 32-bit MinGW toolchain (2 REAL arrays at that size ≈1.9GB) — measured exponent ≈0.97 in the chosen range.

  - **The honest result — same direction as piece 2, still not assumed beforehand**: the Fortran kernel wins by roughly 25× (n=160M: Fortran ≈0.19s vs. scikit-learn ≈5.0s). `comparison_note` names the reason instead of concluding "native beats scikit-learn" in general: `LinearRegression.fit()` pays real general-purpose overhead (input validation, a solver that supports multiple predictors) that this kernel skips entirely by assuming a single predictor with a closed-form solution known ahead of time — an assumption a real `LinearRegression` call usually can't make. Final tally across all 3 pieces: numpy wins piece 1 by ≈170×, native wins pieces 2 and 3 by ≈20-25× — the discipline was measuring each case for real, with no assumption about which side would win holding across all three.
  - **A second real bug found and fixed while verifying this piece in a real browser** (this comparison is the slowest of the 3 — real wall-clock cost ≈40-55s at the largest size): `_numpy_matmul_timings`/`_pandas_aggregation_timings`/`_sklearn_linreg_timings` were all being called *synchronously* inside `async def` endpoint handlers in `routers/execution.py` — blocking FastAPI's single event-loop thread for the full duration of each comparison, freezing the entire app (confirmed live: the frontend's own "Todo operativo" status badge flipped to "Servicio no disponible" for the whole ~50s the sklearn comparison ran). This bug had been present since piece 1 shipped, invisible because every prior verification was `curl`/`pytest`-only — a single synchronous test client never notices a blocked event loop the way a live browser polling a status endpoint does. Fixed by wrapping all 3 calls in `starlette.concurrency.run_in_threadpool`, the same pattern this codebase already established for `upload.py`/`ml.py`/`diagram.py`'s own CPU-bound work earlier this session — re-verified live afterward: the status badge stayed "Todo operativo" throughout the same comparison.
  - 5 new Rust tests (`linreg_bench::tests`, including a real end-to-end compile+run against `gfortran` on this machine) and 3 new Python tests (`TestValidateLinregVsSklearn` in `test_execution.py`, verified against a live sidecar and real scikit-learn). **456 Rust tests total (451 previous + 5), 297 backend tests total (294 previous + 3)** — full suite re-verified green after the `run_in_threadpool` fix, Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.

New rule: a new Rust-only module gets `cargo test` as its *sole* correctness verification; Python only gets a test when Python itself contains real logic (e.g. the numpy comparison bullet directly above — that computation genuinely happens in Python, so it's tested in Python). A second pass removed `TestValidateMatmulBigoRustUnavailable`/`AgainstRealSidecar` too (2 more tests). **582 backend tests total (594 previous − 12), Rust test count unaffected (439) since none of this touched `services/complexity/src/*.rs`.**

- [x] **Tenth kernel: binary search in Assembly — the first SUB-LINEAR shape, closing the "searching...shapes beyond BFS" gap this phase had left open** (2026-09-01). Third use of Assembly in this engine (`asm_bench.rs`: sum of squares, O(n); `mergesort_bench.rs`: bottom-up merging, O(n log n)), and the first kernel whose growth is *smaller* than linear rather than a power of `n`. New `kernels/validate_binary_search.s` + `services/complexity/src/binsearch_bench.rs` — an iterative binary search over a sorted array where `arr[k] = k`, so the correct result for any search is always known in advance without a second data structure.
  - **The exponent-fit axis for this kernel is `log2(array size)`, not the array size itself** — genuinely new interpretation, not a new statistics function: `fit_exponent` (the same log-log regression every other kernel already uses, reused verbatim) is fed `(log2(n), seconds)` pairs instead of `(n, seconds)`. For true O(log n) growth, time scales *linearly* with log2(n), so an exponent measured against THAT axis lands near 1.0 — same anchor, same ±0.3-ish interpretation as any O(n) kernel, just a different x-axis. Documented explicitly in the response's `note` (same "don't silently overload a field" discipline `fib_bench.rs` established when it reused `estimated_exponent` as a measured exponential base instead of an `n^k` exponent).
  - **A single binary search is too fast to time at all** (8–27 comparisons even at this kernel's largest size) — solved the same way `asm_bench.rs` solved an analogous problem: a fixed, large repeat count (`SEARCHES = 4,000,000`, same for every array size) so total time scales as `SEARCHES × log2(n)`, comfortably measurable.
  - **Two real calibration problems found and fixed live, not guessed**: the first version cycled search targets sequentially (0, 1, 2, ..., n−1, 0, ...) and used `i mod n` (`idiv`) to compute them — measured exponent landed at ≈0.69, far from 1.0. Root cause, diagnosed by direct measurement, not assumption: (1) a perfectly sequential access pattern lets the CPU's branch predictor learn each search's left/right decisions, understating real binary-search cost against arbitrary targets; (2) `idiv` is a fixed per-search cost that doesn't scale with `log2(n)`, diluting exactly the signal this kernel measures. Fixed by generating targets with an LCG (`state = state*1103515245+12345`, the same generator `validate_mergesort_driver.c` already uses for data) and an AND-mask (`target = (state >> 16) & (n-1)`, requiring `n` to be a power of two — already true by construction) instead of `idiv` — exponent rose to ≈0.95–0.97. Separately, a narrow size range (log2(n) = 14/18/22/26) measured a notably *worse* exponent (≈0.60) than a much wider one (log2(n) = 8/16/24/27, ≈0.97) at otherwise-identical settings — confirmed live that this specific fit is far more sensitive to range width than any prior kernel's, so `KERNEL_SIZES` deliberately spans a wider ratio (256 to 134,217,728 — 512MB, the largest single allocation any kernel in this phase has made, confirmed safe on this 32-bit process before committing to it) than any other kernel in this phase.
  - Wired the same way as every other kernel this phase: `POST /execution/validate-binary-search` (`main.rs`), `validate_binary_search_rust()` (`complexity_client.py`, pure relay), `/validate-binary-search` (`routers/execution.py`, reusing `_degraded()`), and a new row in the Static panel's Algorithm Validation Engine section — the shared `_renderKernelValidationRow()`'s existing `formatMeasured` override (first used by `fib_bench.rs`) renders `log2(n)^X.XX` instead of the default `n^X.XX`, so the badge never implies the wrong axis. 5 new Rust tests (`binsearch_bench::tests`, including a real end-to-end compile+run against `gcc` on this machine) — no Python test, pure Rust-only relay per the established policy. **505 Rust tests total (500 previous + 5)**, Clippy/`ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.
- [x] **Light auto-match: highlight a kernel row when a function in the analyzed file shares its predicted Big-O — pure frontend, zero new backend or shape detection** (2026-09-01). The "pick a shape/language pair automatically from a static claim" architecture the note below names is real future design work, but a much smaller, immediately achievable slice of it needed nothing new: `apps/web/src/panels/static.ts` already renders each function's `big_o` in the Big-O table (Phase 13) using the exact same string values (`"O(n)"`, `"O(n²)"`, etc.) each kernel's own label already claims to predict. A new `KERNEL_BIGO_MATCH` lookup table plus `_matchedFunctionNames()` compares the two, and `_renderKernelValidationRow()` grows an optional "↳ coincide con `functionName`" badge when they agree — same honest hedge every kernel's own tooltip/note already carries: this confirms the SHAPE claim exists elsewhere in the file, not that the two are actually the same algorithm. **A real dead-end found and deliberately not shipped**: `graph_bfs` has no honest match this way — the Big-O engine never emits the literal `"O(V+E)"` string for a user function. Phase 14's `classifiers.rs::graph_info` *does* mark a real `graph_traversal: "BFS"` field for Python — but that field is Python-only, and this whole section only renders for `c`/`cpp`/`fortran`/`assembly` files, so the two signals structurally never coexist on the same analyzed file; wiring that match anyway would have been unreachable code masquerading as a feature, caught and reverted before shipping rather than left in. No Rust/Python changes, `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `O(n²)` C function — the bubble-sort row's badge appeared correctly next to it.
- [x] **First real shape detector: bubble sort — proof of the "match by algorithm shape, not just Big-O" pattern the light auto-match above approximates** (2026-09-01). Same precedent `numerical_algorithm_note` (Phase 20, Fortran matrix-multiply shape) already established, generalized to Python: new `classifiers::sorting_info()`/`sorting_note()` walk a function's AST looking for 2+ nested loops whose innermost body compares two ADJACENT elements of the same sequence (`seq[j] > seq[j+1]`) and swaps them via the Pythonic one-line idiom (`seq[j], seq[j+1] = seq[j+1], seq[j]`) — a shape signal, not a correctness check, same "this has the FORM of X" hedge as the Fortran precedent (doesn't confirm the outer loop actually shrinks the inner range each pass, or that the result ends up sorted). Deliberately narrow for this first slice: only the tuple-swap idiom is recognized, not the 3-line temp-variable form (`tmp = seq[j]; seq[j] = seq[j+1]; seq[j+1] = tmp`) — same "narrow first porción" discipline other patterns in this engine already accepted. New `RichFunction.sorting_algorithm`/`sorting_algorithm_note` fields, surfaced as a new "Bubble sort?" badge in the Static panel's Big-O table (same visual family as the existing "Matrix?" badge). **A second real dead-end found and deliberately NOT wired further**: extending the Phase 26 kernel auto-match to use this much more precise signal for the `bubble_sort` kernel was considered and rejected for the exact same structural reason as the BFS case above — `sorting_info` is Python-only (built on `rustpython_parser`'s AST, not portable to `cparse.rs`'s tree-sitter grammar without a separate implementation), and the kernel section never renders for Python files; the badge stands on its own as real shape intelligence for Python, not connected to the Assembly/Zig/Fortran kernel buttons. 8 new Rust tests (`classifiers::tests` — the classic form, a `while`-loop variant, and 5 negative cases: single loop, non-adjacent comparison, comparison without a swap, and a swap of the wrong indices) — **539 Rust tests total (531 previous + 8)**, Clippy clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `bubble_sort` function.
- [x] **Second real shape detector: binary search — extends the "match by algorithm shape" pattern beyond sorting** (2026-09-01). New `classifiers::search_info()`/`search_note()` (same file, same Python-only AST approach as `sorting_info`) recognize the classic shape: a `while lo <= hi:` loop that computes a midpoint (`mid = (lo + hi) // 2`, either summation order) and, guarded by a comparison that indexes the sequence by that same `mid`, narrows the range on both sides (`lo = mid + 1` on one branch, `hi = mid - 1` on the other — either a 2-way `if`/`else` or a 3-way `if`/`elif`/`else` chain, both recognized). Same "shape, not correctness" hedge as `sorting_info`: doesn't confirm the sequence is actually sorted or that both narrowing branches are reachable. Deliberately narrow for this slice: only the exact `(lo + hi) // 2` midpoint form is recognized — the overflow-safe `lo + (hi - lo) // 2` variant (which matters in fixed-width-integer languages, not in Python's arbitrary-precision ints) is out of scope. New `RichFunction.search_algorithm`/`search_algorithm_note` fields, surfaced as a new "Binary search?" badge in the Static panel's Big-O table (same visual family as "Bubble sort?"/"Matrix?"). Same deliberate non-wiring into the Phase 26 kernel auto-match as `sorting_algorithm`, for the identical structural reason (Python-only signal, kernel section is c/cpp/fortran/assembly-only). Also fixed a stale doc-comment on `RichFunction.sorting_algorithm` that claimed it "enables" the kernel auto-match — it never did; corrected to state the non-wiring explicitly. 8 new Rust tests (`classifiers::tests` — 3-branch and 2-branch classic forms, and 4 negative cases: one-sided narrowing, a strict `<` loop condition, a non-standard midpoint, and narrowing not guarded by a `mid`-based comparison) — **547 Rust tests total (539 previous + 8)**, Clippy clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `binary_search` function.
- [x] **Third real shape detector: naive Fibonacci — closes the "3-4 shape detectors" batch this vein was scoped for** (2026-09-01). New `classifiers::recursion_shape_info()`/`recursion_shape_note()` recognize the classic double-recursion step: a self-call with `n - 1` added to another self-call with `n - 2`, both using the same parameter, anywhere in the function body (not just directly under `return` — also matches `result = fib(n-1) + fib(n-2)` followed by a separate `return result`, and either summation order). Reuses `recursion::analyze`'s existing self-call detection idea but needs its own AST match since that module only counts self-calls, it doesn't check argument shape. Same "shape, not a complexity proof" hedge as the two detectors above, with an explicit added caveat this one needed that the others didn't: it does **not** check whether a memoization cache elsewhere in the function guards this expression — a memoized Fibonacci literally contains the same `fib(n-1) + fib(n-2)` text and would still match, even though its real complexity is linear, not exponential; the note says so directly rather than implying a guarantee the shape alone can't back. New `RichFunction.recursion_shape_algorithm`/`recursion_shape_algorithm_note` fields, surfaced as a new "Fibonacci?" badge (same visual family as "Bubble sort?"/"Binary search?"/"Matrix?"). Same deliberate non-wiring into the Phase 26 kernel auto-match as the other two, for the identical structural reason. 8 new Rust tests (`classifiers::tests` — direct order, swapped summation order, assigned-to-a-variable form, and 3 negative cases: a single self-call (factorial), wrong offsets (`n-1`/`n-3`), and mismatched parameters across the two calls) — **555 Rust tests total (547 previous + 8)**, Clippy clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `fib` function — the independently-computed Big-O engine's own `O(2^n)` for that function lines up with the badge, cross-confirming both signals without either depending on the other.
- [x] **Memoization detection — closes the caveat the Fibonacci detector above had been carrying in writing since the day it shipped** (2026-09-02). That bullet's own hedge said it out loud: *"it does not check whether a memoization cache elsewhere in the function guards this expression."* That was a real hole, not a cosmetic one — an `@lru_cache`-decorated Fibonacci and a naive one have **identical AST shape at the recursive step**, so until now the engine called them the same thing. New `classifiers::memoization_info()`/`memoization_note()` recognize two forms, each on its own evidence:
  - **Decorator** — `@lru_cache`/`@cache` and their dotted `@functools.` forms, including the called variant `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`: `structure::decorator_name` already normalizes all three syntaxes to the same string, so this is a name comparison, not a new AST walk. Strongest possible signal — the decorator *is* the memoization.
  - **Hand-rolled cache** — a dict that is both membership-tested (`if k in cache`) and subscript-written (`cache[k] = ...`) in the same function.
  - **Two deliberate exclusions, each with its own test.** A dict created *empty inside the body* (`cache = {}`) is **not** memoization: it resets on every call, so it caches nothing between invocations — precisely the mistake a hurried reader makes, and the engine doesn't make it. And a dict whose membership check `raise`s is the symbol-table pattern `semantic_analysis_info` already classifies as Type-1, not a cache — the exact distinction that module had documented in reverse ("memoization never raises"), reused here verbatim via its existing `has_membership_gated_raise`.
  - `recursion_shape_info` now takes the memoization signal and reports `memoized_fibonacci` instead of `naive_fibonacci` when both are present, each with its own note. **The memoized note names a genuine disagreement instead of hiding it**: the Big-O column still reads `O(2ⁿ)` for a memoized Fibonacci, because `bigo.rs` measures the body's own recursive structure and does not subtract a cache. Making the Big-O engine cache-aware is real design work with broad regression surface across every recursive function it scores — deliberately not attempted in this slice; the note tells the reader which number to trust and why the two differ.
  - New `RichFunction.memoization`/`memoization_note` fields and a "memoizado" badge, useful on any cached function, not just Fibonacci. 15 new Rust tests (`classifiers::tests` — all three decorator syntaxes, an unrelated decorator not matching, both hand-rolled idioms (`in` and `not in`), the fresh-dict and raising-dict exclusions, read-only and write-only dicts not matching, `naive` vs `memoized` classification, memoization standing alone without any Fibonacci shape, and both notes naming their concrete evidence). **645 Rust tests total (630 previous + 15)**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a 4-function file: `fib_naive` → "Fibonacci?", `fib_cached` (`@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`) → "Fibonacci memoizado?" + "memoizado", `fib_manual` (dict passed as a parameter) → "memoizado", and the fresh-dict function → neither.
  - **An honest limit observed during that same live run, not papered over**: `fib_manual` got the "memoizado" badge but *not* "Fibonacci memoizado?", because the shape matcher requires a single-argument self-call and that function's calls carry the cache as a second argument (`fib_manual(n-1, memo)`). Pre-existing detector narrowness, unchanged by this work — named here rather than left for someone to discover as a surprise.
  - **Also observed and worth recording**: running the full suite under load, `fortran_bench`/`agg_bench`'s real-toolchain end-to-end tests failed intermittently (2 failures, then 1, then 0 across three consecutive full runs; all pass in isolation). They compile and time real `gfortran`/`zig` programs and check the fitted exponent against a sanity band, so a loaded machine can push a measurement outside it. Nothing to do with this change — flagged as a known flake, with tightening it (serializing the toolchain benchmarks, or widening the bands) left as an explicit open question rather than silently absorbed.

### 🟡 Phase 27 — AI Intelligence *(AI as an optional explanation layer over deterministic findings, never the detector itself)*

Consolidates `AIProvider` (moved from Phase 24, quoted there in full) with the natural next step for `ml.py`/`ml.rs`'s already-shipped ML-stack detection (Phase 18) — model orchestration and notebook analysis, rather than only detecting that a project *uses* ML/DL:

- [ ] **`AIProvider` interface** *(the bullet moved verbatim from Phase 24)* — local ONNX/GGUF, remote API, or none configured — that a plugin can call to turn `Evidence` (Phase 21's data-flow path, Phase 13's Big-O reasoning) into prose, with the deterministic finding produced and fully usable with zero AI configured, same shape the Big-O `reason` field already has today. Deliberately still not started: no real model-loading infrastructure exists yet, and no plugin today has a concrete "turn this evidence into prose" requirement driving it — building the interface before a real consumer exists would be speculative infrastructure, the trap this project avoids everywhere else.
- [x] **Jupyter Notebook analysis (`.ipynb`)** (2026-09-02) — the one bullet of this phase actually buildable today, since it needs nothing from `AIProvider` or any model infrastructure: a notebook is JSON with code/markdown cells (`nbformat`), and a code cell's Python text can run through the exact same engine `.py` files already use, unchanged. New `services/complexity/src/notebook.rs` — `extract_python_source()` parses the JSON (`serde_json`, already a dependency), concatenates every `code` cell's text in order (markdown cells contribute nothing), and returns `(source, cell_ranges)`; `analyze_notebook()` feeds that concatenated text straight into `rich::analyze_rich()`, the same function `/parse/python` calls — zero new detection logic, one new front door. `.ipynb` becomes the platform's 8th language plugin (`plugin.rs::builtin_manifests()`, mirrored in `plugin_registry.py`'s fallback), so dispatch, the `/languages` endpoint, and the frontend's language badge all pick it up through the existing manifest machinery with no hardcoded extension list to update by hand. New `POST /parse/notebook` (Rust) / `parse_notebook_rust` (Python relay) / `_parse_notebook` (`static_parser.py`) — **Rust-only, no Python fallback**, same policy as Fortran/Assembly: a `.ipynb`'s content is JSON, not Python source, so the existing no-sidecar AST-skeleton path for `.py` files doesn't apply and isn't faked. Frontend: new `.ipynb`/`notebook` badge (`lang-notebook`, Jupyter's own orange), and a new "Celdas de código" info block listing each code cell's `(start, end)` line range in the concatenated pseudo-file — since every other finding's `line` is relative to that concatenation, not the original notebook, this is the one new UI piece needed to let a user manually cross-reference a reported line back to its actual cell, rather than silently mislabeling lines as if they were the notebook's own. 8 new Rust tests (`notebook::tests` — single cell, markdown cells correctly ignored, multiple cells producing non-overlapping ranges, malformed JSON degrading to an error instead of a panic, a `cells`-less-but-valid-JSON object correctly treated as an empty notebook rather than an error, the real `analyze_rich` engine actually running end-to-end on notebook-extracted code confirming a real Big-O result, and both `source` formats `nbformat` allows — an array of lines and a single string). **590 Rust tests total (582 previous + 8)**, Clippy/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser against a hand-written 3-cell notebook (1 markdown, 2 code) — the markdown cell's text correctly never leaked into the analysis, `bubble_sort` in the first code cell was detected with its real `O(n²)` Big-O and "Bubble sort?" badge, and the cell-range block correctly showed `celda 1: L1-9` / `celda 2: L11-12`.
  - **A real, pre-existing engine limitation surfaced by this work, not caused by it — fixed the same day, in its own follow-up pass**: the second code cell in that same test notebook was a classic top-level SQL Injection (`query = "..." + input() + "..."; cursor.execute(query)`) and it produced **zero** finding — confirmed, by testing the identical 2 lines as a plain standalone `.py` file, that this had nothing to do with notebooks: `rich::analyze_rich()`'s taint-tracking security engine (`security_findings`, inside `collect_functions`) only ever walked the bodies of actual function definitions — module-level/top-level statements outside any `def` were invisible to SQLi/command-injection/SSRF/every taint-based CWE check in the whole catalog (naming smells and hardcoded-credential detection *did* separately walk the whole module already, so those weren't affected). A real, already-existing blind spot in the engine's entire security analysis, not something specific to `.ipynb` — but one that lands disproportionately hard on notebooks specifically, since top-level (non-function) code is the norm there, not the exception it usually is in a `.py` file. **Fixed, at the user's explicit request to attack it as the very next step, not left as a documented gap**: `security::security_findings()` was already generic over any `&[Stmt]` and already used `walk_stmts_own_scope` internally — it never assumed `body` had to literally be a function's body. `rich::analyze_rich()` now also calls it once directly on the module's own top-level statement list (`security_findings("<module>", &suite, ...)`), the exact same "own scope" semantics as any function, correctly skipping the bodies of functions already covered elsewhere (confirmed with a dedicated test asserting no duplicate finding) while catching taint that flows directly at module scope. `"<module>"` as the scope name — Python's own traceback convention (`File "x.py", line N, in <module>`), not invented. 5 new Rust tests (`rich::tests` — top-level SQLi now detected, the finding correctly labeled `<module>`, no duplicate when the same pattern also exists inside an already-covered function, module-scope and function-scope taint confirmed not to leak into each other in either direction, and clean top-level code producing zero false positives). **595 Rust tests total (590 previous + 5)**, Clippy clean, all 301 Python backend tests still pass unmodified (confirming zero regressions across the whole existing suite), verified live in a real browser for both the original notebook (now showing `SQL Injection CWE-89, High, confianza Alta, <module>(), línea 12`) and the standalone top-level `.py` file.
  - **The same day, the user asked to attack the analogous gap in structural smells too, once the security one was closed**: `smells::check_deep_nesting`/`check_quadratic_list_membership`/`check_de_morgan_simplifiable` had the identical shape as `security_findings` before its fix — already generic over any `&[Stmt]`, already using `walk_stmts_own_scope` (or, for `check_deep_nesting`'s own `max_nesting_depth`, a manual descent that already never enters `FunctionDef`/`ClassDef` bodies either) — but `rich.rs::collect_functions` only ever called them once per function, so a deeply-nested `if` pyramid, an `O(n²)` list-membership loop, or a simplifiable `not (a and b)` written directly at module scope (again, the norm in a notebook cell, not the exception) went undetected. `rich::analyze_rich` now also calls all 3 once on the module's own `suite`, same `"<module>"` scope name as the security fix. Deliberately left out of this fix: `check_long_function`/`check_excessive_parameters` — neither has a conceptual equivalent at module scope (no LOC-of-a-callable boundary, no parameter list to measure). 4 new Rust tests (`rich::tests` — each of the 3 checks confirmed firing at module scope, plus a dedicated no-duplication test: the same 5-level nesting pattern written *inside* a function still produces exactly one finding, attributed to that function, not two). **599 Rust tests total (595 previous + 4)**, Clippy clean, all 301 Python backend tests still pass unmodified, verified live in a real browser against a file combining all 3 patterns at module scope — all 3 findings rendered correctly, each labeled `<module>`.
- [x] **Notebook reproducibility — the first signal in this phase that comes from the notebook FORMAT, not from running the Python engine over its code** (2026-09-02). The bullet above deliberately treated a `.ipynb` as "a container of Python text": extract the code cells, hand them to `analyze_rich`, done. That left `execution_count` — the `[N]` Jupyter writes beside each cell as it runs — parsed away and thrown out, even though it answers the single most common question about someone else's notebook: *does this reproduce if I run it top to bottom?*
  - Two signals, both read straight off the saved file. **Out-of-order execution**: walking code cells in *file* order, a cell whose `execution_count` is lower than one above it ran *before* that earlier cell — so the saved state can't be rebuilt with "Restart & Run All", because something read a variable that, reading the file downward, didn't exist yet. **Unexecuted cells**: `execution_count: null`, a cell that never ran in the saved session (or had its output cleared).
  - Compared against the **maximum** count seen so far, not the previous one — a design decision with its own test. After one out-of-order cell, comparing against *it* would let the cells below slip through as "fine" even though they too ran before the earlier cell. Forward gaps (`[1, 7, 9]`) are explicitly **not** flagged: they mean deleted cells or re-runs, and the relative order is still correct.
  - The note states what this does and doesn't prove, same discipline as `numerical_algorithm_note`/Type-1: it describes the **saved state**, not the code. A notebook developed out of order but re-run end to end before saving shows nothing here, and a flagged notebook isn't proven to hold wrong results — only that its saved order doesn't match its file order.
  - Frontend: the existing "Celdas de código" block now shows each cell's real `[N]` next to its line range — red for an out-of-order cell, grey `[ ]` for an unexecuted one — plus a one-line summary. The counts were already per-cell data; this makes them locatable cell by cell instead of only totalled.
  - 10 new Rust tests (increasing order clean, an out-of-order cell flagged, forward gaps not flagged, unexecuted cells counted without breaking ordering, the max-not-previous case, an empty notebook, a missing `execution_count` key treated as unexecuted, markdown cells correctly not consuming an index so `execution_counts` stays aligned with `cell_ranges`, and two end-to-end through `analyze_notebook` including the invalid-JSON path). **682 Rust tests total (672 previous + 10)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, the 18 `test_static_analysis.py` tests still pass (the Python change is two relay keys, no new logic — no new Python tests, per this repo's Rust-only policy). Verified live in a real browser against a hand-written 5-cell notebook (1 markdown + 4 code, counts `[1] [5] [2] null`): the markdown cell correctly consumed no index, cell 3 rendered red as out-of-order, cell 4 grey as unexecuted, and the summary read "3 ejecutada(s), 1 sin ejecutar, 1 fuera de orden".
- [ ] Model orchestration for Sythrall's own ML/DL detection (Phase 18) — beyond detecting that a project uses PyTorch/TensorFlow/sklearn, actually helping run or evaluate a model within the same session, once a concrete use case (not speculative) makes it worth the infrastructure.

`AIProvider` and model orchestration remain not started — same honesty as before: nothing there is worth building before a real consumer needs it. Notebook analysis was the exception because it needed none of that infrastructure to begin with.

### 🟡 Phase 28 — Enterprise Intelligence *(the persistence/auth layer the Enterprise pitch actually needs, promoted out of distribution)*

Was a single bullet inside the old "Sythrall Platform" phase (now Phase 29) — promoted to its own phase because the Enterprise scenario this whole 24–30 arc is built around (a company running SQL Server + Excel + legacy C/C++, activating only the Connectors/Extensions it needs) can't be sustained by a stateless single-session tool. Persistence and auth aren't "one more distribution feature" for that scenario, they're load-bearing:

- [x] **Enterprise persistence, first slice: local analysis history + metric comparison (2026-09-02)** — the bullet's full scope (PostgreSQL + Delta Lake, real JWT authentication, a public API with rate limiting) assumes a hosted, multi-tenant SaaS product with real user accounts. Checked before writing anything: Sythrall has neither today — the only JWT that exists (`routers/auth.py`) is a loopback-only session token for the embedded terminal, `sub: "local"` hardcoded, explicitly documented as "no real login yet." Building Postgres/Delta Lake/real auth/a public API before that multi-user consumer exists would be exactly the speculative-infrastructure trap this project avoids everywhere else (same reasoning as `AIProvider`, Phase 27). Scoped down to what's real and useful *today*, confirmed with the user before writing code: SQLite, no auth, local-only — a snapshot of Project Health (the same `health`/`summary` shape `/static/parse-project` already computes) saved per project, so 2 runs can be compared over time. This directly closes the `_lastAnalyzedAt` gap `panels/dashboard.ts` had been carrying as an open comment since the Dashboard was built ("no hay persistencia server-side de corridas pasadas... ver Project Health Trend, todavía sin implementar").
  - New `services/history_store.py` — plain stdlib `sqlite3` (no new dependency, no ORM), one `snapshots` table (`project_id`, `created_at`, `health_json`, `summary_json`). The database file lives at the repo root (`data/history.db`), **outside** `apps/api/` — the exact same reason `UPLOADS_DIR` (`shared.py`) already lives there: uvicorn `--reload` watches `apps/api/`, and writing a file inside it mid-request triggers a reload in the middle of that same request (a real bug this project already hit once with uploads, not repeated here).
  - `routers/static_analysis.py::parse_project`'s ~100-line health/summary aggregation was extracted into a reusable `compute_project_analysis(project_id, files)` — pure refactor, identical behavior — so the new history endpoint reuses the *exact* scoring logic instead of a second formula that could drift out of sync.
  - New `routers/history.py`: `POST /history/{project_id}/snapshot` (re-runs the analysis, saves it), `GET /history/{project_id}` (list, newest first), `GET /history/compare/{from_id}/{to_id}` (score + summary deltas between 2 snapshots), `DELETE /history/snapshot/{id}`. All 3 sync SQLite calls wrapped in `run_in_threadpool` — same recurring "don't block the event loop with sync I/O" discipline this project has hit and fixed multiple times before.
  - Frontend: a new "Historial de análisis" section in the Dashboard (`panels/dashboard.ts`) — a "Guardar snapshot actual" button (explicit save only, never automatic on every analysis, so the list doesn't fill with identical entries from clicking "Actualizar" repeatedly), a compact list of saved snapshots with delete, and a from/to dropdown comparison showing each of the 4 health scores' delta plus a "what changed" list from the summary counts. Reuses `renderHealthCards`'s existing `.st-stat`/`healthTier()` conventions rather than inventing new styling.
  - New Vite proxy entry (`/history`) and `.gitignore` entry (`data/`) — same pattern as every other new router/local-data-directory this project has added.
  - 15 new Python tests (7 for `history_store.py` — save/get/list-ordering/list-filtering/delete, isolated per-test via a `tmp_path`-monkeypatched `DB_PATH`; 8 for the router — using the real upload flow to get a real project, same convention `test_upload.py` already established) — **316 backend tests total (301 previous + 15)**, zero regressions from the `parse_project` refactor, verified live end-to-end in a real browser: uploaded a real project, saved 2 snapshots, compared them, confirmed the diff view renders correctly.
- [x] **Team dashboard with aggregated metrics — shipped as cross-*project*, explicitly NOT cross-*user* (2026-09-02)** — named here rather than in Phase 29 since it depends on the same persistence layer, not just UI work. The half that's real today is the project half: the persistence slice above already stores a Project Health snapshot per project, so aggregating the newest one of each into a single "where should I look first" view needs no new infrastructure at all. The *user* half stays unbuilt for the same reason the rest of this phase does — Sythrall has no user accounts of any kind, so a `GROUP BY user_id` would group by one invented constant value and add a fake column, not information. `routers/history.py` says so in the endpoint's own docstring, naming exactly where that `GROUP BY` goes once real accounts exist.
  - New `history_store.project_rollup()` — for every project with at least one snapshot: its newest, its previous (or `None`), and the total count. Two queries rather than one on purpose: the first selects **only** `id`/`project_id` (never the 2 JSON blobs per row), groups in Python, and keeps the 2 newest ids per project; the second fetches full rows for just those. Deliberately no window functions (`ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY ...)` would do it in one query but requires SQLite ≥ 3.25, and which SQLite the stdlib's `sqlite3` carries depends on how each machine's Python was built — not worth tying the history to for one cheap extra query).
  - New `GET /history/overview` — per project: name, whether it still exists on disk, snapshot count, the 4 health scores, an `overall` average, and a per-category delta vs. that project's own previous snapshot (`null` with only one). Plus a cross-project aggregate: average per category (a **plain** average, not weighted by project size — weighting by LOC would hide exactly the case this view exists to surface: a small project with broken security next to a large healthy one) and summed totals. Ordered worst-`overall`-first, the useful order for "where do I look," not alphabetical or chronological.
