  - **The endpoint must be declared before `GET /{project_id}`** — both are single-segment routes and FastAPI resolves in declaration order, so declared after, `/history/overview` would be swallowed as "the snapshots of a project named `overview`" and quietly return `[]`. Pinned by a test that fails (a list, not a dict) if the routes are ever reordered.
  - A project deleted from disk keeps its history: the row stays, marked `exists: false` with a `null` name, struck through in the UI rather than silently vanishing — a past score of a project that's gone is still a real measurement. Confirmed live, not just unit-tested: the orphaned snapshots of the previous slice's own test project rendered exactly that way during browser verification.
  - Frontend: a new "Panorama entre proyectos" card (`panels/dashboard.ts` + `.dash-ov-*` in `main.css`) rendered in three places — under a fully analyzed project, under the "not analyzed yet" state, and in the empty hero (no active project at all), which is precisely when "how are all my projects doing?" is the most useful question on screen. Clicking a row activates that project and re-runs its analysis. This is the **only** view in the Dashboard that isn't about the active project, so it goes last and carries its own explicit label — the same remedy `_renderAdHocSummary` already used for that exact ambiguity risk, now written into the file's header comment as the one documented exception.
  - **A real bug in the previous slice, found by this work and fixed**: `list_snapshots` ordered by `created_at DESC` alone. `created_at` comes from `datetime.now(UTC)`, and Windows' system clock granularity means two snapshots saved in the same tick get the *identical* timestamp — SQLite then returns them in arbitrary order. It surfaced as the previous slice's own ordering test failing intermittently (it passed when first shipped, purely on timing luck). Fixed with `id DESC` as the tiebreaker in both `list_snapshots` and the new `project_rollup` (the latter had it from the start), plus a deterministic regression test that freezes the clock to force the tie instead of waiting for luck to reproduce it.
  - 12 new Python tests (6 for `project_rollup`, 6 for the overview endpoint including the route-ordering pin and the deleted-project case) — **328 backend tests total (316 previous + 12)**, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, verified live in a real browser: uploaded 2 real projects through the actual UI, saved 3 snapshots between them, confirmed the panorama renders in both the project view and the empty hero with correct worst-first ordering, correct averages, the struck-through deleted project, correct light/dark theming, that clicking a row really does switch the active project (nav-rail label and all), and that the card disappears entirely once no snapshots remain — zero console errors throughout.

- [x] **Snapshots record what the engine could analyse, so a comparison can tell "your code got worse" apart from "Sythrall learned to see something"** (2026-09-05). Direct follow-through on the last four rows: each of them made a language start emitting findings where it previously returned a hardcoded `[]`, and each one **lowered the score of every project in that language without a line of code changing**. Compared against a snapshot saved before, that reads as a regression that never happened — and nothing in the product said otherwise. The persistence layer from Phase 28's first slice is where that belongs.
  - **The manifests were stale in the same way everything else was.** `plugin.rs`'s `features` lists were the natural place to record capability, and they were wrong: `python` did not declare `security_findings` despite having taint tracking since Phase 21, `c`/`cpp` did not declare `modernization` with 12 shipped patterns, and `notebook` *did* declare `security_findings` — so the manifests contradicted each other. All eleven lists brought in line with what the engine actually emits.
  - **A test that keeps them honest, in both directions.** `los_manifests_declaran_los_hallazgos_que_el_motor_produce` scans a real fixture per language and asserts (1) nothing the engine produces goes undeclared — the direction that catches drift when a new analysis lands and the manifest stays behind — and (2) nothing declared fails to appear, because a fixture written to trigger every declared feature turns an undeliverable promise into a failure. This is the third test in the family, after the LANGUAGES one and the KEYS one.
  - `plugin_registry.analysis_capabilities()` derives a sorted `["c:modernization", "python:security_findings", …]` from the manifests the sidecar already publishes — **no second table in Python**, because a parallel list of "which features count" is exactly the thing that goes stale in silence. All features are kept, not just finding types: a new parsing capability moves `summary` counts too.
  - `history_store` gains `capabilities_json`, added with `ALTER TABLE` rather than by recreating the table — existing databases hold real snapshots, and a past measurement is still a measurement. Old rows read back as `None`, not `[]`: **"unknown" and "produced nothing" are different**, and conflating them would either raise the warning when nothing changed or stay silent when something did.
  - `GET /history/compare` returns `capabilities_diff` with `known`/`comparable`/`added`/`removed` and a sentence saying what it means. The Dashboard renders it **above** the score deltas and only when there is something to warn about — reading the numbers without knowing the engine changed leads to the wrong conclusion, so the warning has to arrive before the number, and a permanent green "all fine" banner becomes invisible.
  - 5 new Python tests (including one that builds a pre-migration database by hand and asserts the migration does not drop the snapshot that was already there) and 1 new Rust test. **864 Rust tests total (863 previous + 1)**, **334 backend tests total (329 previous + 5)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean.

Real Postgres/Delta Lake, real user accounts/JWT authentication, and a public rate-limited API remain not started, no timeline — deliberately deferred until an actual multi-tenant consumer exists to justify them, same discipline as the rest of this document.

### 🟡 Phase 29 — Sythrall Platform *(renamed from the old Phase 25 — how the product reaches people, minus the bullet that moved to Phase 28)*

Everything here is orthogonal to the theory phases above — engineering/distribution work that doesn't depend on Phases 13–24 landing first:

- [x] **Native Toolchain (Zig), cross-compilation slice shipped — this is the real first Zig artifact in the repo, not just a plan for one** — `scripts/cross-compile.sh`/`.ps1`, wired the same day the philosophy update above opened Zig as a real candidate rather than leave that a promise. Uses `cargo-zigbuild` (Zig as the C linker/toolchain for a cross target) to build `complexity-engine`/`terminal-server` for a platform other than the one they're compiled on — verified end to end on this dev machine, not just documented: Windows → `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` produced two real ELF 64-bit Linux binaries (`file` confirms `ELF 64-bit LSB pie executable, x86-64 ... for GNU/Linux`), not a copied Windows binary. The script installs `cargo-zigbuild` and the target automatically if missing; Zig itself is a prerequisite it checks for but doesn't install. Standalone desktop packaging (PyInstaller/Nuitka + Tauri) and Zig as the language for a real low-level *component* (not just this toolchain role) remain unstarted — same benchmark-first rule for the latter, no profiled candidate yet (see the language philosophy table above, which now reflects an honest attempt to find one, not just an open door).
- [ ] **Cython & WASM integration** — auto-detect Cython candidates from Big-O analysis (O(n²)+ functions), generate `.pyx` stubs from Python function signatures, compile in Docker (MSVC/GCC), side-by-side Python-vs-Cython benchmark display, estimated speedup in the hover provider, WASM compilation path via Emscripten
- [ ] **Execution Path Simulator** — animated circuit-board view of Sythrall's own analysis pipeline (`Input → Parser → AST → Dependency Resolver → Metrics → Report`), step-by-step trace with timing per stage, exportable as animated SVG
- [ ] VS Code extension, LSP server (the natural client for Phases 13–19's facts once there's a standard protocol serving them), ApexVision integration (`/analyze/image` with OpenCV + YOLOv11) *(Jupyter Notebook analysis moved to Phase 27, team dashboard moved to Phase 28 — both fit their new homes better than distribution)*
- [x] GitHub Action for CI/CD — `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (typecheck/lint/build/test on every push/PR) + `release.yml` (tag → GitHub Release with CHANGELOG notes + frontend build artifact)

### 🔴 Phase 30 — Sythrall Ecosystem *(the real marketplace — third parties publishing extensions Sythrall itself didn't write)*

Develops in full what Phase 24 used to file under "deferred, no timeline: public registry/marketplace, sandboxed/WASM third-party execution, a multi-language plugin SDK" — that line still means exactly what it said (not started, no commitment), just with the fuller shape the user's own mockup gave it:

```
Sythrall Extensions
Search extensions...

┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ SQL Server Intelligence         │
│ Database analysis & migration   │
│ ✓ Verified          [Install]   │
└─────────────────────────────────┘
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ C++ Modernizer                  │
│ Legacy → Modern C++ / Rust      │
│ ✓ Verified          [Install]   │
└─────────────────────────────────┘
```

- [ ] **Third-party publishing** — a developer, not Sythrall itself, ships an extension (the user's own examples: a company-specific SQL migration extension, a Fortran scientific analyzer, a CUDA performance analyzer, a COBOL modernization engine). Sythrall provides the SDK, the API surface (Phase 24's manifest/capability interface, generalized beyond Sythrall's own 6 built-in language plugins), a sandbox for anything requesting `execute_code`, and a validation/review step before an extension can carry the "✓ Verified" badge.
- [ ] **Dependency/extension breaking-change monitoring** — continuous, not one-time: when an installed extension or a library it depends on ships a new version, Sythrall flags what in the project is affected, the risk level, and whether a regenerated regression test would catch it — same "evidence, not a bare warning" discipline as every finding shape in this document (`libXYZ 4.0 changed calculate()` → affected files → risk → recommendation, the user's own example, kept verbatim).
- [ ] Explicitly deferred within this already-deferred phase: a public registry open to arbitrary uploads, and a formal third-party trust/revenue model — neither has a reason to exist before a second real extension beyond Sythrall's own 6 built-in language plugins actually gets built by someone else.

Not started, no timeline — same reasoning the "deferred" bullet this phase replaces already gave: sandboxing and a trust model are real infrastructure commitments that only make sense once real third-party demand shows up, not before.

### 🟡 Phase 31 — Sythrall CSS Engine *(CSS as an analyzed language, and eventually a compiler — third-party tools as benchmarks, never as foundations)*

- [x] **Atomic CSS generation, with the before/after comparison the bullet demanded** (2026-10-03). The condition was *"generating is only worth it once measuring can prove what was gained"*; the analyzer already existed, so this is the other half.
  - **Measured against this repo's own CSS**: 3448 declarations → 1396 atoms (2.47 ratio). `main.css`, 103 KB → 39 KB of CSS. The most reused is `display: flex` ×109.
  - **And the number that qualifies it, right beside it: 662 of `main.css`'s 926 atoms (71%) are single-use** — they save no line and add a class to the markup. The compression comes from a heavily repeated minority.
  - **CSS shrinks, HTML grows, and that side is not measured.** The note says so; `bytes_after` is the CSS cost, not the balance. **It rewrites nothing**: it returns the sheet and the mapping to read and compare.
  - `@media` context and `!important` are part of an atom's identity and are not collapsed. Output is deterministic and the generated sheet re-parses, with a test.
  - 1493 Rust tests (+9), 345 backend tests, verified in a real browser (11/11).

- [x] **`Cargo.toml` and `pyproject.toml` in the manifest analyzer** (2026-10-02). It closes the multi-language dependency graph; the module's doc said this was to be decided "when there is a reason".
  - **On the `toml` crate**: it is a FORMAT parser, like `serde_json`/`tree-sitter`/`object` already in the tree. The project's rule is not to rebuild someone else's *analysis*, not to avoid reading standard formats.
  - **And the finding that justifies it: Cargo inverts npm's and pip's default.** `serde = "1.2.3"` in Cargo means `^1.2.3`; pinning requires `=1.2.3`. Reusing the existing classifier reported **26 of 26 dependencies as "exact"** on this repo's own `Cargo.toml` when none of them are. With a Cargo-specific classifier: 0 exact, 26 ranged.
  - String or table specs; `path`/`git`/`workspace` are not mistaken for "any version"; `pyproject.toml` reads both PEP 621 (a list) and Poetry (a table), which coexist; Poetry's `python` does not count as a dependency.
  - Verified against the repo's real manifests. 1477 Rust tests (+20), Clippy clean, 338 backend tests.

Opened 2026-09-02 from a direct conversation about the project's own philosophy, stated by the user as: **"third-party tools are benchmarks and references, not architectural dependencies."** That is not a new principle here — it is the one that replaced `radon` with an own Rust sidecar (Phase 11) and that measured own Zig/Fortran kernels against numpy/pandas/scikit-learn (Phase 26, where numpy legitimately won one of the three pieces). This phase names it and applies it to the one area where Sythrall had nothing at all.

A distinction worth keeping explicit, because it cuts the "avoid dependencies" idea in the right place: **React/Tailwind/Next ship to the user's browser. Vite does not** — its output is plain JS/CSS, zero runtime footprint. Replacing a build tool means writing a dev server with HMR, an ES module resolver, a TS transpiler, a CSS pipeline, code splitting, minification and source maps, and the measurable payoff is build seconds, not product performance. React, Tailwind, Next and "Python as the core" are already resolved in this codebase; what was genuinely open was CSS, a component layer, and a CLI. Of those, CSS is the only one with clear product value today, so it goes first.

- [x] **CSS as the 9th analyzed language — the analyzer, not the generator** (2026-09-02). The 8 language plugins never included `.css`, so a stylesheet opened in the editor produced exactly zero findings. New `services/complexity/src/cssparse.rs`: **its own hand-written tokenizer, no new dependency** — same call as `asmparse.rs`, and pulling in a CSS crate would have been precisely the architectural dependency this phase exists to avoid. It is not a spec-complete CSS parser and does not claim to be; it recognizes rules, selectors, declarations and at-rules with their line numbers, which is what the checks need.
  - **Everything it reports is provable from the file alone, and that boundary is the design.** A property declared twice with the same value inside one rule is dead by the cascade; two rules with the same selector in the same at-rule context are redundant; specificity is arithmetic. Three checks, all `High` confidence: `duplicate_declaration`, `duplicate_selector`, `high_specificity` (≥2 ids or ≥4 chained classes, the "specificity graph" convention). Plus a measured summary: rules, selectors, declarations, at-rules, `!important` count, bytes, max specificity.
  - **Two false positives designed out from the start.** A repeated property with a *different* value (`display: block; display: flex`) is a legitimate legacy fallback, so only an identical value is flagged. And a selector repeated inside a *different* `@media` is exactly how a responsive override is written, so `at_context` is compared — without that, the duplicate check would have been useless on any real stylesheet.
  - **Deliberately not reported: "unused CSS."** That needs the project's HTML/JS and still cannot be proven, because classes are built at runtime. Measured first-hand on this very codebase during the Phase 1–10 cleanup: a naive unused-class detector over `main.css` produced 34 candidates, of which **33 were false positives** (`.t-flake8`, `.sev-error`, `.rank-A`, `.src-be`, `.fstate-ok` — all generated as template strings). One was genuinely dead. That signal needs its own design and its own hedge; it does not get smuggled in here as if it were the same kind of claim, and the engine's own `note` says so.
  - **A real bug in the parser, found by pointing Sythrall at its own `main.css`** — the best possible argument for the self-analysis idea. The file's first line is `@import url('...css2?family=DM+Mono:wght@400;500&...')`, and that `;` *inside the URL string* terminated the at-rule early: the rest of the file accumulated into the buffer and came out as a single 200-character "selector" containing half the stylesheet. The initial test suite missed it because no test had a semicolon inside a string. Fixed by tracking quote state in both the tokenizer and the comment stripper, with three regression tests (the real Google Fonts import, a `{` inside a string, a `/*` inside a URL).
  - Wired like every other language: `POST /parse/css` (`main.rs`), 9th entry in `plugin.rs::builtin_manifests()` and in `plugin_registry.py`'s fallback, `parse_css_rust` + `_parse_css` (`static_parser.py`), `.css` in `PARSEABLE_EXTENSIONS`, badge and a Static-panel section. **Its result shape is deliberately different** from the other 8 — CSS has no functions, classes or algorithmic complexity, so returning `functions: []` and a Big-O table would be faking a shape that does not apply; it returns `css_rules`/`css_findings`/its own summary instead, the same way `memory_layout` and `modernization` exist only for C/C++.
  - 25 new Rust tests. **717 Rust tests total (692 previous + 25)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **328 backend tests** (two manifest-count assertions updated from 8 to 9 — no behavior change). Verified live in a real browser by analyzing Sythrall's own `main.css`: **8 findings, all real**, including `.nav-rail.collapsed .nav-rail-logo` defined at line 290 with `gap: 4px` and again at line 523 with `gap: 0` — a genuine conflict in the project's own stylesheet, confirmed by reading both lines.
- [x] **Unused-CSS detection, with the hedge as the design rather than a footnote** (2026-09-02). New `services/complexity/src/cssusage.rs`, in its own module because it's a different *kind* of analysis from `cssparse.rs`: that one proves things by looking at one file, this one crosses files and **cannot prove anything**. Both facts are stated in the report itself.
  - **The measurement that justified the whole design.** During the Phase 1–10 cleanup, a naive unused-class detector over this repo's own `main.css` returned 34 candidates of which **33 were false positives** — `.t-flake8`, `.sev-error`, `.rank-A`, `.src-be`, `.fstate-ok`, every one of them built at runtime as `` `t-${tool}` ``. That number is why the module is shaped the way it is instead of shipping a "67% unused" figure.
  - **Two design decisions follow from it.** (1) The "referenced" side is deliberately *permissive*: any token anywhere in the project that matches the class name counts as a reference, even a variable that happens to share the name. A false "referenced" harms nobody; a false "unused" invites deleting CSS that is in use — the error leans to the safe side on purpose. (2) Runtime-construction sites are reported as **first-class context, listed before the unreferenced classes**: if the project builds class names at runtime, the whole list loses reliability, not just some entries, because there's no way to know which names it generates. Rendering them in that order is deliberate — the other way around, someone reads the list as a deletion list and the caveat sits below where nobody looks.
  - Two dynamic patterns detected, both from the real cases in this repo: a `class=`/`className=` attribute whose value contains `${`, and `classList.add/remove/toggle/replace` with a non-quoted argument. A quoted literal is *not* flagged — the permissive side already counts it as a reference.
  - Reuses `scanner.rs`'s directory walker (promoted to `pub(crate)`, with the reason written down) so both analyses look at exactly the same file set under the same filtering rules, instead of drifting apart over time. New `POST /analyze/css-usage` (same request shape as `/scan/project`) and `POST /static/css-usage` on the Python side; the section loads *after* the project analysis renders, since walking the whole tree takes longer than the per-file parse.
  - **A real lesson from running it live, now baked into the report.** Pointed at `apps/web/src`, it reported **105** unreferenced classes; pointed at `apps/web` — which includes `index.html`, one level up — it reported **31**, and all three control classes I knew were in use (`url-bar`, `nav-rail-logo`, `logo-ring`) correctly disappeared. The result is worth exactly as much as the file set scanned, so the note now carries the scanned/CSS file counts and says outright that markup outside the analyzed directory inflates the list. The 31 that remain are precisely the runtime-built ones, sitting next to the 36 construction sites that explain them.
  - **The section only appears when analyzing a project, never when analyzing loose files** — cross-file CSS analysis is inherently project-level, and mixing it into the loose-file view would reintroduce exactly the source ambiguity the panels already avoid.
  - 15 new Rust tests. **770 Rust tests total (755 previous + 15)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions. Verified live in a real browser on a 3-file project: 1 runtime-construction site listed first, then `.huerfana`/`.t-flake8`/`.t-pylint` as unreferenced, with `.btn` (used in `index.html`) correctly absent, and the full note underneath.

- [x] **HTML as the 10th analyzed language — accessibility and document structure** (2026-09-02). `cssusage.rs` already *read* HTML, but only to harvest class-name tokens: the file went in as text and came out as a set of strings. This analyzes it.
  - **Why accessibility rather than "HTML validation".** Validating HTML against the full spec is a conformance validator's job, and a good free one exists (the W3C's). What a code analyzer can add that the validator doesn't is the class of defect that **breaks real use of the page and is invisible looking at it**: an image with no alternative text, a repeated `id`, a field with no label. None of that is a syntax error — the HTML is perfectly valid and the page looks fine.
  - Seven checks, all `High` confidence because **all of them are provable by reading the file**: `img_without_alt`, `duplicate_id`, `missing_lang`, `heading_level_skip`, `control_without_label`, `link_without_href`, `positive_tabindex`.
  - **The distinction that avoids this family's most common false positive**: `alt=""` is correct and deliberate — it's how a decorative image is marked so a screen reader *skips* it. Several linters flag it anyway, which trains people to ignore the tool. Only the total absence of the attribute is reported.
  - Same care on labels: an `<input>` wrapped in `<label>Text <input></label>` is correctly labeled with no `for` and no `id`, so the tokenizer tracks `<label>` depth. A test pins the closing side too — after `</label>`, the next input must be reported again, which a naive depth counter would get wrong forever after the first label.
  - Hand-written tokenizer, no new dependency (same call as `cssparse.rs`/`asmparse.rs`). It handles the cases that actually break naive scanners: comments, `<script>`/`<style>` raw-text regions (without which `if (a < b)` inside a script parses as the start of an element — pinned by a test), quoted and unquoted attributes, and `<!DOCTYPE>`.
  - Wired like the other languages: `POST /parse/html`, 10th entry in `plugin.rs::builtin_manifests()` and the Python fallback, `parse_html_rust` + `_parse_html`, `.html` in `PARSEABLE_EXTENSIONS`, badge, and a Static-panel section. Its result shape is its own, like CSS's — a document has no functions or Big-O.
  - 27 new Rust tests. **797 Rust tests total (770 previous + 27)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** (three manifest-count assertions updated). Verified live in a real browser against **Sythrall's own `index.html`**: 14 findings, and every one I checked by hand is real — line 132 is `<input id="url-main" placeholder="…">` (a placeholder is not an accessible name and disappears on typing), lines 178–180 are the three file inputs, and line 228 is the file `<select>`. Worth stating plainly: this is the same `index.html` that got an accessibility pass during the Phase 1–10 cleanup, and the engine still found 14 real issues in it.

- [x] **The project scanner only ever knew 3 of the 10 languages — a silent gap found while looking for the next feature, not reported by anything** (2026-09-02). `scanner.rs::parse_one_inner` dispatched Python, C/C++ and JS/TS, and everything else fell through to `unsupported`. Fortran (Phase 20), Assembly (Phase 19), Notebook (Phase 27), CSS, HTML and the dependency manifests all worked **one file at a time** and silently produced nothing in a whole-project scan — six plugins shipped over months, each of them wired into `static_parser.py` and none of them into the scanner that project analysis actually uses.
  - Six new dispatch arms, each returning the same JSON shape its `static_parser.py` handler already produced, so the Python aggregation needed no special-casing. Manifests get their own path: they're claimed by **filename**, so `ScanRequest` gained a `filenames` field (`#[serde(default)]`, so an older caller keeps working) and the walker accepts a file by exact name as well as by extension. Including `.json` wholesale instead would have dragged every JSON file in the project along just to reach `package.json`.
  - **A test that fails the next time someone forgets.** `el_escaneo_de_proyecto_cubre_los_10_lenguajes_y_los_manifests` writes one file per language into a temp directory, scans it, and asserts every language is present, that none degraded to `unsupported`, and that each one's own findings actually arrive (`css_rules`, `html_findings`, `manifest_findings`) — not just that the language name is right. That's the check whose absence let this happen six times in a row. A second test pins that `tsconfig.json` is *not* treated as a dependency manifest.
  - **Four finding types were dying at the file level too.** CSS, HTML, manifest and optimal-control findings were computed per file and never aggregated, so a project view showed none of them. They're now flattened with their source `file`, counted in the summary, and rendered in their own project section — same treatment `security_findings` has always had.
  - **They deliberately do NOT enter the Project Health score, and the reason is written where the code is.** The four dimensions' formulas are calibrated over function/class counts; folding accessibility or dependency findings into them would silently change the meaning of a number people compare against previous runs — and in a pure-frontend project, where `quality_penalty`'s denominator is tiny, it would distort it completely. They show as their own counts. Whether they should feed the score is a product decision, not a side effect of this pass.
  - 2 new Rust tests. **799 Rust tests total (797 previous + 2)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** with no regressions. Verified live in a real browser on a 5-file project (`.py`, `.css`, `.html`, `.f90`, `package.json`): all five parsed with their correct language and **zero errors** — before this change four of the five came back as `unsupported` — and the project view showed 5 findings that had been invisible: a duplicate CSS selector, three accessibility issues, and `lodash: "*"`.

- [ ] Atomic CSS generation, and the before/after comparison it would enable. Not started, and deliberately after the analyzer: generating is only worth it once the measuring can prove what it bought.
- [x] **Dependency Intelligence, first slice — and the platform's first Format Extension** (2026-09-02). Phase 24 named five plugin categories and only one ever had content: the Language plugins. "Format Extensions — understand a file format the built-in parsers don't cover" was defined and empty. This fills it, and acts on the principle stated in the conversation that opened this phase: *avoiding third-party dependencies isn't enough, you have to analyze them.*
  - **Dispatch by exact FILENAME, not extension — the structural change that made the category possible.** `package.json` is a `.json` like any other; resolving by extension would send every JSON file in a project to the manifest analyzer. `PluginManifest` gains a `filenames` field, `plugin_registry.py` a matching `filename_map()`, and `parse_file` checks exact name *before* extension. Two new invariant tests pin it: every plugin must claim at least one of the two, and no filename may be claimed by two plugins. `PluginCategory` gains its second variant, `Format`, after having had exactly one since it was written.
  - New `services/complexity/src/manifest.rs`, covering `package.json` (JSON, via the `serde_json` already in the tree) and `requirements.txt` (plain text). Three checks, all `High` confidence because **all of them are provable by reading the manifest**: `unbounded_dependency` (a spec that admits any future version — `*`, `latest`, or a `>=` with no upper bound), `external_source_dependency` (a git/URL/file source: no version resolution, no publication history), and `duplicate_dependency` (declared in two sections — `Medium` severity when the specs differ, since the file then doesn't say which version is installed, `Low` when they match). Plus a measured summary: total, production vs. development, and the distribution across exact / bounded-range / unbounded.
  - The distinction that makes the first check useful: **`>=1.2` is unbounded, `>=1.2,<2.0` is not**. Without that, either every range gets flagged (noise) or none does (useless). Same reasoning as the `@media` context check in `cssparse.rs`.
  - **Deliberately no CVE or latest-version lookup.** That needs network access, and `network` is a `Permission` Phase 24 declared that no plugin exercises. When that path exists, this is its natural first consumer — and the manifest will have to ask for the permission, which will be a visible change right there. Until then the module doesn't pretend to have data it doesn't have, and its `note` says so, along with the other thing it can't claim: that a dependency is unused (that needs cross-referencing the project's imports).
  - Line numbers come from searching the raw text for the key, since `serde_json` doesn't preserve positions — a finding without a line is far less useful in an editor, and the search is cheap.
  - **`Cargo.toml`/`pyproject.toml` are out, for a stated reason**: they'd need a TOML parser, and adding a crate without a benchmark to justify it contradicts this project's own rule — as does hand-writing a TOML parser to read three sections. It gets decided when there's a reason, not before. **Project-scan pickup is also deliberately not wired**: `parse_project_files` filters by extension, so picking manifests up in a whole-project scan would mean either adding `.json`/`.txt` to the parseable set (dragging in every JSON and text file) or teaching the Rust scanner about filenames. Single-file analysis works today; that plumbing is a separate decision, named rather than half-done.
  - 21 new Rust tests. **755 Rust tests total (734 previous + 21)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **329 backend tests** (three manifest-count assertions updated, and one new test pinning that `.json` resolves by name and not by extension). Verified live in a real browser against a hand-written `package.json`: exactly **4 findings** — `lodash: "*"`, a `git+https` source, `typescript` declared twice with conflicting specs, and `vitest: ">=1.0"` — with `express: "^4.18.0"` and `left-pad: "1.3.0"` both correctly silent.


### 🟡 Phase 32 — Sythrall Mathematical Analysis *(turn the program into mathematical structures and reason over them, not just look at it)*

- [x] **Genetic programming, through the door that actually holds: a multi-objective search over the compilation CONFIGURATION** (2026-10-02). Closes the fifth and last reinstated topic.
  - **And it corrects what the bullet claimed**: it said the hard part — validating equivalence — was already solved by Phase 26. It is not. Phase 26 validates the kernels Sythrall itself writes; for an arbitrary user function, proving a mutated variant is equivalent is undecidable.
  - **So the source is not touched; the configuration is searched**: same code, different options. Equivalence stops being a problem and the search is still genuinely multi-objective, which is what the bullet named with NSGA-II.
  - **Measured trade-off**: `-Os` gives 14 instructions and no vectorization, `-O3 +avx2` gives 195 with 2 packed. Those two are the front; the other four configurations are strictly dominated.
  - An exact tie is not dominance, and a configuration that failed to compile does not compete. The note states, in plain view, that **fewer instructions is not faster** — measuring time is the benchmark kernels' job, and they compile AND run.
  - 1484 Rust tests (+7), 345 backend tests, verified in a real browser (8/8).

- [x] **The QUALITY score saturated inside the range of real code** (2026-10-02). Of the five reinstated topics, the one the genetic-programming bullet named as *"the honest output of `quality_penalty`"* — solved the simple way rather than with NSGA-II, because the measured problem was not combining objectives but a broken scale.
  - The formula hit 0 at a density of 1/3 smells per unit and told you nothing above that. **Measured over 47 `site-packages` packages: median 0.14, max 0.77, and 6 of 47 (13%) at 0** — numpy 8/100, pandas 23/100, scikit-learn 0/100.
  - The mapping changes, not the penalty: `100·e^(−p/100)`, same slope at the origin (good code barely moves) and resolution where there used to be only zeros. **Saturation 13% → 2%**; the only one left is scipy, at 8 naming smells per function.
  - **And the stored snapshots**: `health` now carries `scoring_version` and `compare` returns a `scoring_diff` warning when two measurements used different formulas. Sibling of `_capabilities_diff`, the same class of lie.
  - 345 backend tests (+7), ruff clean.

Comes from a user contribution (2026-09-05) mapping ~20 theories against what Sythrall could analyse. A good part of it **was already built**, and is listed here so nobody rebuilds it: Big-O/Ω/Θ (`bigo.rs` + `space.rs`, time and space), project-level graphs (`graph.rs`: import graph, centrality, cycles), partial Boolean algebra (De Morgan in `smells.rs`), partial formal grammars (`classifiers.rs`), linear algebra and control theory (`control.rs`: LQR/Riccati + Gibbs selection), partial numerical analysis (`fparse.rs`), and partial data flow (`security.rs`'s taint tracking, 9 CWEs).

**Six claims that must NOT be repeated.** They come from the source material and are either too broad or outright dated; they are written down here so they never leak into a finding's message, where they would be a false statement with Sythrall's name on it. The first 3 came from the first batch of contributions, the last 3 from the second:

1. **"The Hilbert curve is mathematically optimal"** — it is optimal under *specific* metrics (number of clusters per range query). There are metrics and dimensionalities where it does not win. It is not a universal optimum.
2. **"GPUs constantly use the Morton curve"** — they use swizzled/tiled layouts that are Morton-*like*: vendor-proprietary, undocumented, and different across architectures. "Inspired by", yes; "use the Morton curve", no.
3. **"In high dimension distance loses meaning"** — holds for i.i.d. uniform data. Real data has far lower intrinsic dimension, which is exactly why LSH and approximate methods work at all. If the premise were literal, none of them would.
4. **"SFINAE is C++'s compile-time introspection mechanism"** — it *was*. Since C++20, **concepts** replace it in most cases, with readable error messages instead of an overload silently disappearing. A SFINAE analyser written today analyses the legacy form, and the finding has to say so: "this uses SFINAE; in C++20 it would be expressed as a `concept`" is useful, "SFINAE is *the* mechanism" is dated.
5. **"The CAP theorem forces a choice between consistency and availability"** — that is the year-2000 framing. The current refinement is **PACELC**: *if* there is a partition (P) you choose between A and C, but **if there is not** (E, *else*) you still choose between latency (L) and consistency (C). The trade-off is always there, not only during a network failure. A finding that only talks about CAP describes half the problem.
6. **"Reconstructing a genome is a Shortest Common Superstring problem"** — that is the textbook framing, and real assemblers do **not** use it: they use **de Bruijn graphs**, precisely because SCS is NP-hard. Claiming "this code implements SCS" would describe the toy version of the problem, not what an actual assembler does.

**Rice's theorem is the most valuable thing in the whole contribution, and it is not a feature: it is the name for something Sythrall already does in every module.** Rice proves that *every non-trivial semantic property of a program is undecidable*. That is: it is mathematically impossible for a static analyser to answer with certainty "does this code have a security flaw?", "is this CSS class dead?", "do these two programs do the same thing?". This is not a limitation of Sythrall or of the industry: it is a limit of the computable universe.

That changes how every hedge in this repo should be read. When `cssusage.rs` says "no static reference found, this does NOT prove it is dead", or `csecurity.rs` reports CWE-78 at `Medium` confidence because a non-literal argument *could* be a fixed internal string, or `modernization.rs` accepts that `use_after_free` is a line-order heuristic — **that is not timidity or lack of effort. It is the only mathematically available answer.** The alternative (claiming certainty) would not be more ambitious: it would be false.

- [x] **A `decidability` field on every finding — the concrete deliverable that falls out of Rice** (2026-09-24). Every `StructuralSmell` the engine emits now carries `decidability` (`decidable`/`approximate`/`unclassified`) and, when classified, a one-line `decidability_reason` saying *why* that side. The frontend renders it as a badge beside the finding, with the reason in the tooltip.
  - **Decidability is a property of the `kind`, not of the instance**, and that changed the whole design. The bullet said it "would unify the vocabulary of ~20 modules", which suggests touching all 20. It did not: one central table in `decidibilidad.rs` holding the 43 kinds and their side is enough, because two different `division_by_zero` findings never disagree on whether they are decidable.
  - **The first attempt did add two fields to the struct, and broke 41 construction sites** to repeat at each one a fact already determined by `kind`. It was reverted to a hand-written `impl Serialize` that derives both at serialization time: **no emitting module changed a single line**, and the field still travels in the JSON. The test `la_decidibilidad_aparece_al_serializar_sin_tocar_al_emisor` pins exactly that.
  - **The case that proves the distinction is not cosmetic**: `division_by_zero` is `decidable` ("the divisor's whole range is zero") and `possible_division_by_zero` is `approximate` ("zero is IN the range, not the whole range") — **both come out of the same interval analysis**. What changes is not the power of the analysis but what it managed to prove. Test: `el_mismo_analisis_da_los_dos_lados_segun_lo_que_pruebe`.
  - **A `kind` outside the table comes out `unclassified`, not silent**, and with no invented reason. Same criterion by which `Nucleo` has no `Default`: a gap is visible instead of passing for decidable without being it.
  - Verified against the live engine over HTTP and in a real browser: `division_by_zero`/`unreachable_code`/`deep_nesting` come out `decidable` and `unbounded_convergence_loop` comes out `approximate`, with the badge rendered, the tooltip populated and the colour following the theme variable. 1210 Rust tests, 334 Python tests, clippy clean.

- [x] **Control-Flow Graph (`cfg.rs`) — the structure this engine was missing, and the first finding only it can produce** (2026-09-05). Chosen as this phase's first step for a concrete, checkable reason rather than preference: **three modules in the crate say outright that they stop because there is no CFG** (`asmparse.rs` twice, `callingconv.rs` once), and two already-shipped findings report `medium` confidence for the same reason — `use_after_free` and `unchecked_malloc_dereference` are LINE-ORDER heuristics, and a `free` in one branch followed by a use in the other fools them. A CFG is what can turn those `medium`s into provable. No other item on the list has that effect on what already exists.
  - **Statement-level graph, not basic blocks**, stated in the module rather than left to be assumed: grouping statements into basic blocks is compression, not correctness — reachability, paths and cycles are identical, and per-statement every finding points at a real line without mapping back from a range.
  - Models Python's full structured flow: sequence, `if`/`else`, `while`/`for` with their back edge and `else`, `try`/`except`/`finally`, `with`, `match`, and the 4 terminators (`return`, `raise`, `break`, `continue`). Edges carry a `label` (`true`/`false`/`loop`/`except`/`finally`/`case`) because without it an `if` with two exits is indistinguishable from a sequence with two successors.
  - **First finding: `unreachable_code`**, emitted as a `StructuralSmell` so no new plumbing was invented. Three cases no textual heuristic gets right, each with its own test: code after a `return` **inside an `if` with no `else`** is NOT flagged (the false branch carries on — a textual check would flag it); code after a `while True:` with no `break` **is** flagged (there is no `return` involved at all, only the graph sees it); and a `finally` body is reachable even when the `try` exits via `return`.
  - A contiguous run of dead lines is **one** finding, not one per line: ten lines after a `return` are a single problem, and listing them all would drown the rest of the report.
  - `while True:`/`while 1:` are recognised as deliberate infinite loops; `while x or True` is **not**, because evaluating that expression is abstract interpretation and asserting it from here would be inventing.
  - 13 new Rust tests.

- [x] **Spatial Locality Analysis, first slice: the stride of a nested traversal (`locality.rs`)** (2026-09-05). The item the user singled out as the strongest in his contribution, and **built respecting the trap this same ROADMAP wrote down before starting**: for a FULL traversal, row-major is already optimal — Morton/Hilbert only pay off for range queries and tiled access. So this module **never suggests Morton or Hilbert**, and a test enforces that. What it suggests is interchanging the two loops, which is the real fix and costs one line.
  - **Why it is provable rather than an opinion.** C and C++ store `int a[N][M]` in row-major order: `a[i][j]` and `a[i][j+1]` are contiguous, `a[i][j]` and `a[i+1][j]` are `M` elements apart. In a `for i { for j { … } }` nest the fastest-varying variable is `j`, so the access has stride 1 **only if `j` is in the last subscript position**. If it is in the first, every iteration jumps a whole row and each cache line fetched is used once. That is read off the tree.
  - **Confidence is decided by reading the declaration, not assumed.** `int a[N][M]` is genuinely contiguous (nested `array_declarator`) → `High`. `int **a` is not — its rows can be scattered across the heap, and interchanging the loops helps less there → `Medium`, with the message explaining that difference rather than reporting both with the same certainty.
  - **A grammar difference between C and C++ that the C++ test found, not an assumption.** `tree-sitter-c` exposes the index in an `index` field holding one node; `tree-sitter-cpp` exposes `indices`, a `subscript_argument_list` — because C++23 allows `a[i, j]` with several indices in one bracket. The first version supported only the C form and **found nothing in C++ without failing**, which is the worst possible failure mode. Both are supported now, so C++23's multi-index syntax is analysed like the classic one, with its own test.
  - Deliberately out, each with a reason: indices that are not a bare variable (`a[i+1][j]` has a different stride, asserting anything would be inventing), 1D accesses, and un-nested loops (with no nest there is no alternative order to propose). **Fortran is left for the next slice for a concrete reason**, even though it is the most interesting case — it is column-major, so the same nest is optimal there and terrible in C: in tree-sitter-fortran `A(I,J)` is indistinguishable from a function call without tracking declarations, and that piece has to be built first.
  - Joins the same "other project findings" stream as CSS/HTML/manifests/control, with the same treatment: flattened with its source file, counted in the summary, and **not part of the Project Health score**.
  - 12 new Rust tests. **900 Rust tests total (888 previous + 12)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Spatial Locality Analysis, second slice: Fortran** (2026-09-25). `locality_findings_fortran` in `locality.rs`, with the `row_major_traversal` finding — the exact mirror of the first slice's `column_major_traversal`.
  - **The blocker the bullet named is resolved, and the previous pass resolved it.** `A(I,J)` really is indistinguishable from a function call in tree-sitter-fortran: both parse as `call_expression`. The only thing separating them is whether `A` was declared with a shape, which `tipos::arreglos_declarados_fortran` answers — built while doing type theory. A name with no array declaration produces **no finding**, pinned by a test.
  - **Why Fortran was the interesting case, measured rather than asserted.** Fortran stores column-major: `a(i,j)` and `a(i+1,j)` are neighbours. So the fastest-varying index has to be the FIRST — exactly the opposite rule to C's. Measured against the live engine with three files: the nest `do i / do j / a(i,j)` **is flagged in Fortran**, the reversed nest **is not**, and the SAME nest written in C **is not either**, because there it is the optimum. That is the full round trip, not a unit test.
  - Hence the message warns about porting: translating a loop nest between the two languages without flipping it is a classic performance bug, and the engine says so in the finding.
  - **The one way a Fortran array is NOT contiguous** is distinguished: an assumed-shape dummy argument (`real :: a(:,:)`) can receive a slice (`x(1:n:2, :)`). That case drops to `Medium` confidence and the message names the `contiguous` attribute that guarantees it. An array with an explicit shape is genuinely contiguous — Fortran has no equivalent of C's `T **`, which is what forced lower confidence in the first slice.
  - Same limits as the C slice, for the same reasons: indices that are not a bare variable (`a(i+1,j)` has a different stride), rank-1 arrays, and lone `do`s with no nesting.
  - **A pre-existing gap that came to light and was closed**: locality findings were computed, relayed, and **only shown in the PROJECT view**. In a single file the finding existed and was invisible — and had been since the first slice, meaning it was invisible for C too. It now renders in the single-file result, with the language's storage order in the heading.
  - 11 new Rust tests plus 5 for the array tracking — **1323 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, verified in a real browser with a `.f90` and a `.c` showing the two opposite rules.
- [x] **Propositional logic beyond De Morgan** (2026-09-06). Delivered whole, and split into two halves where neither covers the other.
  - **The half that needs values lives in `intervals.rs`, and comes from descending into the operands.** In `if x > 10 and x < 5:` neither comparison is determined on its own — the first is what proves the second. Judging operand by operand with the environment narrowed by the PRECEDING ones (exactly what Python's short-circuiting guarantees), the finding falls out by itself, points at the line of the impossible comparison, and the message that already existed works verbatim.
  - **The same machinery yields redundancy, the bullet's second request.** In `x > 10 and x > 5` the second is always TRUE once the first holds: it does not kill the guard, it just adds nothing. That is a different finding (`redundant_condition`), not the same one, because the consequence differs — a `Rol` (guard / conjunct of an `and` / alternative of an `or`) now decides what it means for a condition to be determined. A conjunct that is always false kills the whole guard; the same conjunct always true merely doesn't contribute.
  - **`estrechar` learned `and` and `or`, which improves everything before it.** A true conjunction says something about EVERY operand, and so does a false disjunction by De Morgan (`¬(A ∨ B) ≡ ¬A ∧ ¬B`); the other two cases say nothing about any particular operand and narrow nothing. With that, `if x >= 0 and x <= 10:` leaves `x` as `[0, 10]` inside — and the division by zero below now shows up, which it did not before. It has its test, and its negative: `if x > 0 or c:` does **not** prove `x > 0`, because it could have been `c`.
  - **The structural half is a new module, `proposicional.rs`, and needs to know nothing about values.** `a and not a` is false whether `a` is an object, a list, or anything else. Four findings: contradiction, tautology, duplicated operand, and absorption (`A ∨ (A ∧ B)` ≡ `A`, and its dual).
  - **Purity is not a detail, it is THE condition.** `f() and not f()` is **not** always false: the two calls can return different things. So only expressions without calls, walrus, `await`/`yield` or comprehensions are looked at. One concession is declared in the doc rather than hidden: an attribute access counts as pure, even though an effectful `@property` could break it — that is pathological, and excluding it would cost `self.activo and not self.activo`, which is exactly the shape that shows up.
  - **Operand equality is textual on purpose**: same source text ignoring whitespace. `a == b` against `b == a` is lost even though they are equivalent; a false equality is never claimed, which is the error that matters. And being textual it works the same on `self.cfg.modo[0]` as on `x`, with no per-node-kind table.
  - **NaN breaks the symmetry, and the module respects it.** `x < y` and `x >= y` are complementary… except with NaN, where **both** are false. So `x < y and x >= y` is still a contradiction, but `x < y or x >= y` is **not** a tautology. Ordering comparisons count only for contradiction; total ones (`==`/`!=`, `is`/`is not`, `in`/`not in`, and `not A` against `A`) for both. Confusing them would report as "always true" a condition that a NaN makes false — it has its own test in both directions.
  - The 5 new `kind`s ride the existing `StructuralSmell` shape, so they reach the project scan on their own (`scanner.rs` already reuses `rich::analyze_rich` for Python) with no new plumbing.
  - 18 new Rust tests (12 in `proposicional.rs`, 6 in `intervals.rs`). **977 Rust tests total (959 previous + 18)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Queueing theory: the absence of backpressure** (2026-09-06). New module, `colas.rs`, with three findings and two exclusions that are worth as much as the findings.
  - **What is provable and what is not is the whole point here.** That a queue has no bound is READ from the call: `Queue()` with no `maxsize` is a fact. That the queue will grow is a different matter — that is Little's law (`L = λW`): if λ exceeds μ the length grows without ceiling, if λ < μ it settles. **Nothing in the source says what λ and μ are**; they depend on the workload. So the missing bound is what gets reported, never a leak, and the message says so outright.
  - **And the bound does not buy correctness, it buys WHERE it fails.** With `maxsize`, a producer that outruns its consumer blocks at the queue itself, next to the cause; without it, the same overload surfaces much later as an OOM somewhere unrelated. That is backpressure: turning an invisible overload into a local, visible failure. That is what the finding explains, rather than saying "an argument is missing".
  - **`unbounded_queue`** — `queue.Queue`/`LifoQueue`/`PriorityQueue`, `asyncio.Queue`, `multiprocessing.Queue` with no `maxsize`, or with `maxsize` 0 or negative (which in all three families means infinite). The message **distinguishes the default from the explicit choice**, with its test: forgetting it is not the same as setting it to 0. A non-literal bound (`maxsize=cfg.size`) **is** a bound and is not reported. And `SimpleQueue` gets its own message because it has no `maxsize` even as an option: the fix is not an argument, it is a different class.
  - **`unbounded_cache`** — `@cache`, `@functools.cache` and `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`: a queue of results that never evicts. Correct while the key space is small and closed; a leak the moment a key can come from input the caller does not control. **A bare `@lru_cache` is NOT reported**: it is already `maxsize=128`, i.e. bounded.
  - **`unbounded_task_spawn`** — `Thread`/`create_task`/`ensure_future` inside a loop with nothing capping them. Two silencers with their own tests: a `.join()` in the same loop sequentialises it (never more than one alive, so reporting it would be false), and a semaphore visible in the function silences the analysis without trying to prove it actually wraps the spawn — the safe side of the error.
  - **Two deliberate exclusions, because including them would be a false claim.** `ThreadPoolExecutor()` with no `max_workers` is **not** an unbounded pool: the default is `min(32, cpu+4)`, bounded — a naive detector would lump it in with the queues ("the parameter is missing") and be saying something untrue. And `collections.deque()` with no `maxlen` does not qualify either: using it as a list is the normal case, it is only a leak as a buffer, and **nothing in the shape separates the two uses**. Both have their negative test.
  - Unlike every other smell producer, this module is called **once over the whole module** instead of once per function: it needs to see decorators, which live on the definition rather than in the body. It carries its own traversal tracking the scope name, so the panel still shows the function that holds the queue.
  - 16 new Rust tests. **993 Rust tests total (977 previous + 16)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Number theory / cryptography** (2026-09-06). New module, `cripto.rs`, with the bullet's three findings. **It does not repeat `security.rs`, it completes it**: that one covers CWE-327, which primitive was chosen (`md5`, `sha1`, `DES`); this one covers the ones chosen correctly and used wrongly — where the bits came from, how they are compared, and how many there are.
  - **The underlying problem, and why the analysis looks different in each case.** `random`'s Mersenne Twister is not weak for being non-uniform — it is extremely uniform — but for being **predictable**: 624 consecutive outputs reconstruct the internal state and yield every value that follows. For a simulation that is irrelevant; for a session token it is the entire vulnerability. **The difference is not in the code, it is in the purpose, and purpose does not live in the AST.**
  - **Hence the differing confidences, and not out of generic caution.** **Key size is `High`**: `key_size=1024` is a literal against a published minimum — read, not estimated. **Comparing a digest is `High` when the shape proves it** (`hmac.new(...).hexdigest() == signature`): the call says what it is, no guessing required. And **anything resting on a variable NAME is `Medium`**, with the message saying so: `token = random.choice(...)` is almost certainly a bug and `x = random.choice(...)` cannot be judged.
  - **`CWE-338` (Insecure Randomness)** — a `random` function whose result lands in an unambiguously cryptographic name (`token`, `password`, `salt`, `nonce`, `api_key`, `csrf`, `otp`…), or inside a function whose name declares it (`generate_token`).
  - **`CWE-208` (Observable Timing Discrepancy)** — comparing a secret with `==`. Python's `==` on bytes/str stops at the first differing byte, so **how long the comparison takes leaks how many leading bytes the attacker got right**: the secret is recovered byte by byte instead of having to be guessed whole. Comparing against a **literal** is not reported — the value is already in the source, so timing reveals nothing new.
  - **`CWE-326` (Inadequate Key Size)** — RSA/DSA below 2048 bits (NIST SP 800-57 withdrew 1024 in 2013), across both APIs (`cryptography` with `key_size=`, pycryptodome positionally); and `os.urandom`/`secrets.token_*` asking for fewer than 16 bytes. A non-literal size (`RSA.generate(cfg.bits)`) is not judged.
  - **Two exclusions a naive detector would swallow, each with its test.** **`random.SystemRandom` is NOT insecure** — it is backed by `os.urandom`, and lumping it in with `random.*` would be exactly the mistake this module exists to avoid. And **the bare word `key` is not on the sensitive-name list**: `cache_key`, `sort_key` and `dict_key` are everywhere and have nothing to do with cryptography; `api_key`, `secret_key` and `private_key` are on it, because those are unambiguous.
  - It emits `SecurityFinding`, the existing shape, so it reaches the panel and the project scan with no new plumbing — the panel already renders `category` raw, so the three new categories show up without touching the frontend.
  - 16 new Rust tests. **1009 Rust tests total (993 previous + 16)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Calculus / convergence** (2026-09-06). New module, `numerico.rs`. **The common thread is that floating-point arithmetic is not the arithmetic of the reals**, and all three findings are places where code written for the reals breaks against IEEE-754.
  - **`unbounded_convergence_loop`** — a `while abs(err) > tol:` with no cap can run forever for **two independent reasons, only one of which is about the mathematics**: the iteration may not be a contraction, so the error never shrinks — or the tolerance may be below what the format can represent at this magnitude, in which case the condition is unsatisfiable however correct the method is. Two neighbouring doubles are ~2.2e-16 apart near 1.0 but ~1.2e-10 apart near 1e6, so `tol=1e-12` on values around a million is asking for something that does not exist. What the message offers is turning a hang into a return-without-convergence, which is a failure you can see.
  - It recognises as a cap a counter the body increments that appears in the condition or in a `break` guard, or a name that says it is one (`max_iter`, `n_iter`, `limit`…) — all three with tests. And it covers **`while True:` with `if abs(err) < tol: break`**, the most common way to write it, where the loop condition says nothing on its own.
  - **`catastrophic_cancellation_step`** — here is the intuition almost everyone has backwards: in `(f(x+h) - f(x)) / h`, **smaller is NOT more accurate**. Truncation error falls like `h` but rounding error grows like `ε/h`; the sum is minimised around `√ε ≈ 1.49e-8` for float64. Below that the answer gets **worse**, and once `h` is small enough that `x + h == x` the subtraction returns exactly 0 and the derivative comes out as 0. The message offers the two real ways up: a central difference (optimal near `ε^(1/3) ≈ 6e-6`) or complex-step differentiation (`imag(f(x + ih)) / h`), which has no subtraction and therefore no cancellation.
  - **The reporting threshold is NOT the optimum, and that distinction lives in the code.** Near the minimum the error curve is flat: `h = 1e-8` is just below `√ε` and is in practice the right choice, the one everybody writes — reporting it would be pedantry. The threshold sits a decade lower (`1e-9`), where rounding genuinely dominates. It has its test, and it is exactly what the first version got wrong.
  - **`float_equality_comparison`** — a finding **beyond what the bullet asked for**, included because it is the same family and shows up everywhere: `0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3` is False in every IEEE-754 language. **`0.0` is deliberately excluded**: zero is exactly representable, and comparing against it is usually a deliberate divisor or sentinel check rather than an accident.
  - 15 new Rust tests. **1024 Rust tests total (1009 previous + 15)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Automata / state machines** (2026-09-06). New module, `automata.rs`. **Mechanical identification was half the work**: "this looks like a state machine" is not analysable, so two concrete shapes are required.
  - **A transition table** is a literal dict of dicts where **at least one inner value is also an outer key**. That overlap — states pointing at states — is what separates it from any other nested dict: a translation dictionary (`{"es": {"hello": "hola"}}`) does not satisfy it and is left alone. It has its negative test.
  - **A state variable** is a `self.state`/`estado`/`status`/`phase`/`mode` with **at least two distinct literals assigned**. Fewer than two is not a machine, it is a flag — also with a test. The name comparison is **exact** (after stripping leading `_`), not substring: `state_history` does not qualify.
  - **The table's two findings do not carry the same weight, and that is said out loud.** `undefined_state_transition` **rests on no assumption**: every key is right there, so a destination that is not a key makes `TABLE[state][event]` raise KeyError as soon as the machine lands. `unreachable_state` **does rest on one** — that the first declared key is the initial state — and the message says so rather than hiding it, along with the legitimate exception (the state being entered from outside, restored from storage).
  - **With the variable, the comparison is between what is written and what is read**, and each direction means something different: a value assigned that nothing tests for is a state the machine can enter with nothing reacting (`unhandled_state`); a value tested for that nothing assigns is a dead branch (`unreachable_state`). A `case _:` or a trailing `else` silences the first, because there it IS being handled generically — both detected explicitly, with tests.
  - **A class's scope is the whole class, not the method**, which is why the module carries its own traversal instead of hanging off everyone else's "once per function": `__init__` writes the initial state and `step()` reads it, so analysing them separately would see nothing at all. It has its test, which also checks that the reported scope is the class.
  - It recognises all three ways of writing a state: string, integer and enum member (`Estado.IDLE`), the last by the uppercase convention — without it there would be no way to tell it from `self.counter`. And `state in ("a", "b")` counts as reading both.
  - 16 new Rust tests. **1040 Rust tests total (1024 previous + 16)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Type theory for low-level languages** (2026-09-25). New `tipos.rs` module with **7 findings**: 4 for C/C++ (signed/unsigned mixing, implicit precision loss, int↔pointer, plain `char` signedness) and 3 for Fortran (missing `implicit none`, single-precision literal in a double variable, integer division assigned to a real). Measured before writing: of the 8 planted defects, **the engine detected 0** — empty territory, exactly as the bullet said.
  - **The first thing needed was declaration tracking**, because none of these findings is visible in an isolated expression: whether `i < n` mixes sign depends on how `i` and `n` were declared, not on the comparison. **And that is precisely the piece the ROADMAP declares missing for spatial-locality analysis in Fortran** (*"in tree-sitter-fortran `A(I,J)` is indistinguishable from a function call"*): it is now built.
  - **All seven are decidable, and not by accident**: a type system IS a decidable approximation of semantics, so anything answered inside it is proven by reading. They all enter the `decidibilidad.rs` table, and when the type cannot be resolved —a symbolic Fortran `kind` with no definition— the finding is **not emitted** rather than emitted as approximate.
  - **The C case that matters most**: a signed-versus-unsigned comparison converts **the signed one towards unsigned**, not the other way round, so a negative value does not become "smaller": it becomes enormous. It is the classic infinite loop, and the message names the concrete flag (`-Wsign-compare`) and says the fix is not a cast that silences the warning.
  - **The `char` one is the textbook bug**: the standard does NOT say whether `char` is signed —it is on x86, it is not on ARM— and there are **three** distinct types, not two. Where it is unsigned, `while ((c = getchar()) != EOF)` never ends. That is why `getchar()` returns `int`. `EOF` is recognised by name, because it is a macro and its value is not in the analysed file.
  - **`float f = 0.1;` does not store 0.1**: a decimal literal with no suffix is `double` in C, so there is a double conversion. And float-to-int is explained differently because it fails differently — it truncates towards zero, and out of range it is undefined behaviour. `intptr_t`/`uintptr_t` are not flagged: they are the correct form.
  - **The Fortran one worth most is the literal**: `double precision :: x; x = 0.1` does not store 0.1 — `0.1` is a default real, i.e. single precision, and ~8 digits are lost before the assignment. **Symbolic `kind`s are resolved**, because the real idiom of modern Fortran is not `real(kind=8)` (which is not portable) but `integer, parameter :: dp = kind(1.0d0)`: without resolving that, the finding would be missed exactly in the well-written code. A `kind` the file does not define is not guessed.
  - **Three grammar defects found by tests, all of the same family** this crate already knows. (1) **The declarator of a bare parameter is an `identifier`, not a `*_declarator`**: in `int f(unsigned n)`, `n` never entered the type map, so sign mixing never fired. (2) **`-1` is not a unary expression in tree-sitter-c**: the sign lives INSIDE the literal, and the `EOF` case —written with the same logic— already passed while this one failed. (3) **`program` and `subroutine` are also the keyword's own node kind**, so a program without `implicit none` produced THREE identical findings; the fourth time anonymous tokens have bitten here, after `unsafe`, `constexpr` and `.and.`/`.or.`.
  - An existing `fparse` test claimed too much (`structural_smells.is_empty()` to prove two nested `DO`s do not reach the threshold) and its fixture does not declare `implicit none` either. **The new finding is correct about that code**, so the assertion was narrowed to what the test actually proves rather than silencing the finding.
  - 32 new tests — **1308 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (0/8) and after (7/7), and verified in a real browser with a `.c` and a `.f90`, with the decidability badge populated on all seven.
- [x] **Fortran was the only one of the six languages with no cyclomatic complexity — and the panel printed `undefined`** (2026-09-22). The audit that began with C/C++ and continued with JS/TS was completed by looking at the remaining languages. Fortran's Big-O **was already correct** (it counts `do_loop` nodes from the tree, since Phase 20, and the three measured cases — `DO WHILE`, two sequential `DO`s, two nested — all come out right). What was missing was something else:

  ```text
  fortran    complexity present = False
  assembly   complexity present = True
  rust       complexity present = True
  python     complexity present = True
  c          complexity present = True
  js         complexity present = True
  ```

  - **And the panel reads it unchecked** (`${f.complexity}`), so the CC column of the Big-O table printed a literal `undefined`. **Verified in the browser before fixing it**, not deduced from the code: `celdasCC: ["undefined"]`. Same class of bug as the missing `type` field on Rust's `use`s — a field the frontend assumes and the engine does not send.
  - **Two fixes, not one.** Fortran now computes its complexity from the tree; and the panel stopped assuming the field arrives: `complexity?: number` with a grey `—` when absent. A dash is different from a zero — zero would be a claim.
  - **`.AND.` and `.OR.` are ANONYMOUS tokens** in the grammar, invisible to a named-node walk. It is exactly the same trap as Rust's `impl`-level `unsafe`, and the third time it has come up: the kind has to be asked of the `logical_expression`'s direct children.
  - **An `ELSE IF` in Fortran IS its own node** (`elseif_clause`), unlike Python, where an `elif` is an `If` nested inside `orelse` — the same construct, two opposite representations, each demanding its own handling.
  - `CASE DEFAULT` does not count, and is told apart by having a `default` child, not by its kind. Third variant of the same detail: in C it is a `case_statement` without a `value` field, in JS its own `switch_default`, in Fortran a child token.
  - **The downstream effect is larger than the table's `undefined`, and it points the wrong way.** Project aggregation reads `fn.get("complexity", 1)`, so **every Fortran function counted as CC = 1**. That sank `avg_complexity` for any project containing Fortran, and since `complexity_score = 100 - (avg_complexity - 5) * 8`, the project came out **cleaner than it was**. A stray `undefined` is visible; an inflated score is not. The two paths also disagreed: per-FILE analysis showed `undefined` while PROJECT analysis showed `1`, for the same function.
  - 5 new tests. **1140 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Container operation cost in JS — and the Python rule that would have been FALSE here** (2026-09-22). Completes the trio with `amortizado.rs` (Python) and `ccosto.rs` (C++). Measured against the sidecar before touching anything, **4 of 7 were wrong**:

  ```text
  `unshift` in a loop            real O(n²)   said O(n)   FALSE
  `splice` in a loop             real O(n²)   said O(n)   FALSE
  `includes` in a loop           real O(n²)   said O(n)   FALSE
  `out = [...out, x]` in a loop  real O(n²)   said O(n)   FALSE
  `push` in a loop               real O(n)    said O(n)   ok
  `Set.has` in a loop            real O(n)    said O(n)   ok
  `s += t` in a loop             real O(n)    said O(n)   ok
  ```

  - **What makes this slice interesting is the last row.** In Python, `s = s + t` inside a loop is O(n²) and `amortizado.rs` flags it. **In JavaScript it is not**: V8, SpiderMonkey and JSC use *cons-strings* — concatenation builds a tree node in O(1) and flattening is lazy. **The same code shape has a different complexity because the runtime changes, not the syntax.** Copying the Python rule would have been inventing a finding, so a test demands it does NOT fire, in both forms (`s += t` and `s = s + t`).
  - **JS has no type declarations to lean on the way C++ does, but it has the assigned value.** `const out = []` says `out` is an array; `new Set()` says it is not. That covers most real code, and where it does not — a receiver of unknown kind — **nothing is asserted**.
  - **The `Set` is tracked precisely in order NOT to flag it.** And there is a stronger reason than avoiding a false positive: *"use a Set"* is the concrete recommendation of the `includes` finding, and it is only honest if the engine can tell them apart. An analyser that flagged both alike would be recommending something it cannot verify itself.
  - **`x = [...x, y]` and `x = x.concat(y)` need no tracking**: the variable appearing on both sides IS the proof that it is rebuilt whole. Same shape as `x = x + [y]` in Python.
  - What is not flagged — `push`/`pop`, amortized O(1) — sits in a constant with the reason written, as in the other two modules.
  - 10 new tests. **1135 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **And then JS/TS function EXTRACTION, which was wrong in five more ways** (2026-09-22). The entry below noted that `jsts.rs` still found functions with regex and that porting it was another slice. This is it. Measured against the sidecar before touching anything:

  ```text
  class methods           expects [cargar, guardar]   finds []                 FINDS NOTHING
  object literal methods  expects [accion]            finds []                 FINDS NOTHING
  function in a comment   expects [real]              finds [fantasma, real]   INVENTS ONE
  two `handler` scopes    expects [handler, handler]  finds [a, handler, b]    LOSES ONE
  `loc` with a `"}"`      expects 5 lines             says 2 (lines 1-3)       CUTS THE FUNCTION
  ```

  - **The first is the worst and is not an edge case: an entire `class` returned ZERO functions.** Modern JS/TS is full of classes, so the engine was not failing in a corner — it was blind to half the language. And since it returned an empty list rather than an error, it looked healthy.
  - **`estimate_func_loc` counted braces over the text.** A `"}"` inside a string closed the function early: a 5-line function reported `loc` 2 and `end_line` 3. That dragged the `long_function` smell and everything computed over the truncated body along with it.
  - **Deduplication by NAME** made two `handler`s in different scopes into one — and the survivor was the outer function, which was not even called that.
  - **The tree solves all five by construction, not by patching.** A comment is a node of another kind; so is a string; a function's range is data on the node, not a brace count; and functions are distinct nodes even when they share a name.
  - **Names come from four places and that has to be known**: `function f(){}` and `metodo(){}` carry it in their `name` field; an arrow or an anonymous `function` has no name of its own and takes it from **whoever receives it** — the `variable_declarator` in `const suma = (a,b) => …`, the `pair` in `{ flecha: (w) => w }`. If nobody named receives it, it says `<anonymous>` instead of inventing one.
  - **The regex extractor is kept as a fallback**, for files the grammar cannot parse. It has all five defects and its doc says so: it exists because returning something imperfect beats returning an empty list, not because it is correct.
  - **Still out, for the same reason as before**: container operation cost in JS (`shift`/`unshift`/`splice` O(n), `push` amortized O(1)). With no type declaration there is nothing to lean on the way `ccosto.rs` does.
  - 8 new tests. **1125 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **JS/TS was the last language measuring over text — and it was wrong in both directions too** (2026-09-22). After C/C++, `jsts.rs` remained: it not only extracts functions with regex, it then measures over the body's TEXT. Measured against the live sidecar before touching anything:

  ```text
  two SEQUENTIAL loops                 real O(n)   said O(n²)      FALSE
  a loop with `Math.floor` inside      real O(n)   said O(log n)   FALSE
  a comment naming the keywords        real O(1)   said O(n²)      FALSE

  a comment naming the keywords        real CC 1   said CC 4       OVER
  a string naming the keywords         real CC 1   said CC 4       OVER
  `if(x)` with no space                real CC 2   said CC 1       UNDER
  ```

  - **The third one shows it best**: a function with **not a single loop**, carrying only a comment saying *"if we need to loop for each item while draining"*, was reported as `O(n²)`.
  - **`Math.floor` as a binary-split signal is worse than C's `contains("mid")`.** In C you had to name a variable `midpoint` to trip it; in JS, `Math.floor` is the normal way to truncate anything — prices, indices, percentages. Any loop that rounded something became `O(log n)`. Halving is now read from the tree (`/ 2`, `>> 1`, `>>> 1` and their compound forms) and **only counts inside a loop**, with the genuine binary-search test as the counterpart.
  - **Two new grammars**: `tree-sitter-javascript` and `tree-sitter-typescript`. With this, **no language measures over text any more**.
  - **What the tree solves that regex could not**: a nested function has its own `n` and its own complexity. The walk does not descend into inner functions, so a `forEach(function inner(){ for(...) })` no longer adds a loop to the outer function — with text that was impossible to separate.
  - **Grammar detail**: `switch_default` is its own kind here, unlike C where `default:` is a `case_statement` without a value. And `?.` is `optional_chain`, not a `binary_expression`, so it does not count as a decision — correctly: reaching for something that may be absent is not a branch in the program. `??` does count.
  - **Scope, stated so as not to oversell it**: this module **measures, it does not extract**. `jsts.rs` still finds functions with regex and `jscosto` gives it the metrics of the tree function containing that line (the innermost one, by containment rather than exact match, to tolerate arrows and decorators). Porting extraction itself to the tree is another slice — the one that would also fix `estimate_func_loc`, which cuts bodies by line count. If the tree cannot locate a function, it degrades to the old measurement rather than inventing a number, and that is written in the code.
  - **What does not go in either, and why**: container operation cost in JS (`shift`/`unshift`/`splice` are O(n), `push` is amortized O(1)). JS has no type declaration to lean on the way `ccosto.rs` does, so it needs its own work and is not improvised here.
  - 13 new tests. **1118 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
