- [x] **An `elif` chain is not nesting — the Python false positive that was still open** (2026-09-22). It closes the symmetry: the C/C++ side has had this pinned by a test since `cparse.rs` started reading the tree, and the Python half was still wrong. Measured against the sidecar before touching it: an `if/elif/elif/elif/elif/else` — **completely flat code**, no increasing indentation — was reported as `deep_nesting`, "5 levels of nested blocks".
  - **The cause is that Python has no `elif` node.** `if / elif / elif` is encoded as an `If` whose `orelse` holds another `If`, whose `orelse` holds another. The walk descended into `orelse` with the depth already incremented, so every `elif` added a level.
  - **The discriminator is the COLUMN, and it is exact, not heuristic.** An `elif` starts in the same column as the `if` it hangs off; an `else:` with an `if` inside starts further indented. **The two forms produce exactly the same AST** and differ only in that — which is why `max_nesting_depth` now takes the `LineIndex` it did not need before, and why there is no way to do it without one.
  - **The test that keeps the fix from being a silencer**: `un_else_con_un_if_adentro_si_es_anidamiento` compares both forms side by side and demands 1 against 2. Without it, "fixing" the false positive could have switched off the legitimate case the detector exists to find.
  - It is also pinned that a `for`'s `else` still counts as a block: the fix looks only at `If`s, but an `orelse` is not always an `elif`.
  - **Why it matters more than it looks**: a long `elif` chain is the idiomatic way to classify by ranges, and it shows up in perfectly readable code. The detector was penalising exactly the shape it tells you to use instead of nesting.
  - 3 new tests. **1105 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **C/C++ cyclomatic complexity moves to the tree too — and it was wrong in BOTH directions** (2026-09-22). The entry below closed Big-O and noted that CC was still counting text with the same technique. Measured against the live sidecar, **6 of 9 cases were wrong**, and the pattern is the interesting part:

  ```text
  a comment naming the keywords    real 1   said 4   OVER-count
  a string naming the keywords     real 1   said 3   OVER-count
  `if(x)` with no space            real 2   said 1   UNDER-count
  `a&&b` with no spaces            real 2   said 1   UNDER-count
  a ternary operator               real 2   said 1   UNDER-count
  a C++ `catch`                    real 2   said 1   UNDER-count
  ```

  - **Over-counting and under-counting are two different problems.** The first marks simple code as complex: noise, annoying but visible. The second lets complex code through unmarked: **silence**, which is worse because nobody notices. A `// if we need to loop for each item while…` added 3 decision points; an `if(x)` without a space added none.
  - **Reading the tree fixes both at once, and comments and strings stop counting without needing to be excluded**: they are nodes of another kind and are never asked. No exclusion list to maintain.
  - **The ternary was not counted at all**, and `complexity.rs` does count its Python equivalent (`IfExp`). So the two halves of the engine were measuring different things under the same name. C/C++ decision points are now the direct translation of what Python already counts: `if` (and each `else if`, which in the tree is another nested `if_statement`), the three loops, each `case` with a value, the ternary, each `&&`/`||`, and each `catch`.
  - **`default:` is a `case_statement` in the grammar but is NOT a decision**: it is the branch left when none was taken. It is told apart by having no `value` field, and a test pins that 3 `case` + 1 `default` gives CC 4, not 5.
  - **A downstream effect, stated because it is user-visible**: CC feeds the Maintainability Index (`avg_complexity`) and the WASM hint threshold (`WASM_CC_THRESHOLD = 5`). **MI numbers for C/C++ files will move**, and hot paths that crossed the threshold on keywords inside comments stop crossing it. Both are corrections rather than regressions, but they change what the panel shows for the same file.
  - With this, `cparse.rs` stops reading the body text entirely: `body_src` and `lines` were deleted because nothing consumes them any more.
  - 7 new tests. **1102 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **C/C++ Big-O moves from text to the tree — three defects, not one** (2026-09-22). The slice the previous entry left open, and it turned out larger than advertised: `infer_big_o_c` was not an AST heuristic, it was **string matching over the function's text** (`body.matches(" for ").count()`, `body.contains("mid")`). Measured against the live sidecar before touching anything, **5 of 8 cases were wrong**:

  ```text
  two SEQUENTIAL loops                 real O(n)        said O(n²)      FALSE
  two NESTED loops                     real O(n²)       said O(n²)      ok
  a variable named `midpoint`          real O(n)        said O(log n)   FALSE
  a call to `humidity(i)`              real O(n)        said O(log n)   FALSE
  vector::push_back in a loop          real O(n)        said O(n)       ok
  vector::insert(begin) in a loop      real O(n²)       said O(n)       FALSE
  map::insert in a loop                real O(n log n)  said O(n)       FALSE
  a genuine binary search              real O(log n)    said O(log n)   ok
  ```

  - **Defect 1 — counting `" for "` counts OCCURRENCES, not NESTING.** Two loops one after the other walk n each: O(n). They were reported O(n²). The worst of the three, because the pattern is everyday and the error hits correct code.
  - **Defect 2 — `contains("mid")` matches inside any identifier.** `midpoint`, `middle`, and the clearest one: **`humidity`**. Any loop touching one of those words became `O(log n)`. Halving is now read from the tree (`/ 2`, `>> 1`, `/= 2`, `>>= 1`) and **only counts inside a loop** — a bare `/ 2` reduces no range. A test confirms a genuine binary search still yields `O(log n)`: without it, fixing the false positive could have broken the case the heuristic existed to recognise.
  - **Defect 3 — the cost of the operation inside**, the same one `amortizado.rs` fixed on the Python side.
  - **And C++ needs something Python did not: declaration tracking.** `x.insert(…)` says nothing on its own — in a `std::vector` it is O(n), in a `std::map` O(log n), in a `std::unordered_map` O(1) amortized. **The same text, three different costs.** `ccosto.rs` builds a `variable → container family` map from the file's declarations and parameters, and **an unknown-typed receiver fires nothing**: without the type there is nothing to assert, and staying quiet is the correct answer, not a limitation to apologise for.
  - **A substring trap inside the fix for a substring trap**: `unordered_map` contains `map`. Asked in the naive order, a hash table would be classified as a tree and its `find` would go from O(1) to O(log n). The unordered case is asked first, and a test pins it.
  - **What was NOT touched, said so it does not read as an omission**: C/C++ cyclomatic complexity still counts `" if "`/`" for "` over the text, with the same technique replaced here. I did not change it in this slice — it has its own tests pinning current behaviour, and mixing the two would have made a regression impossible to attribute — but it is the same class of thing and deserves the same audit.
  - 14 new tests (6 in `ccosto`, 8 in `cparse`), and `infer_big_o_c` deleted. **1095 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, and verified in a real browser: all 6 cases in the fixture come out correct in the Big-O table, zero console errors.

- [x] **Amortized analysis — and the measured defect that brought it** (2026-09-22). It does not add a topic: it **corrects a false claim the product was making**. Measured against the live backend before a line was written:

  ```text
  for x in items: out.append(x)     real O(n)     said O(n)    correct
  for x in items: out = out + [x]   real O(n²)    said O(n)    FALSE
  for x in items: out.insert(0, x)  real O(n²)    said O(n)    FALSE
  ```

  The reason returned was `"One loop — walks the n elements once"`: **`bigo.rs` counted syntactic nesting and never looked at what the operation inside costs.** The same happened in C++ (`out.insert(out.begin(), x)`), which is a different code path.
  - **And it is not fixed by saying "container operations cost O(n)".** `append` is also O(n) in its worst case — when it reallocates and copies — but it is **O(1) amortized**: the realloc doubles capacity, so the copying cost is spread across the n insertions before it. Counting it as O(n) would turn the CORRECT case into a false positive. **The amortized/worst-case distinction is the only thing separating the two groups**, which is why the topic and the defect are the same thing.
  - **The exclusion that matters most, because almost nobody sees it**: `x += [y]` is an in-place `list.extend`, amortized O(k); `x = x + [y]` builds a new list and copies, O(n). **They read one character apart and differ asymptotically.** There is a test for each, and the one asserting `+=` is NOT flagged is worth as much as the one asserting `= x +` is.
  - **`amortizado.rs`** counts as hidden cost what is genuinely O(n) per call: `.insert(i, x)`, `.pop(i)` with an explicit index (no argument, or `-1`, is O(1) and does not count), `.remove(x)`, and `x = x + …`. What it does NOT flag — `append`/`extend`/`add`/`update` — sits in a constant with the reason written down, so it reads as a decision rather than an omission.
  - **Big-O now uses EFFECTIVE depth**: syntactic nesting, or the level of `n` a linear operation reaches, whichever is greater. A bare `insert` becomes O(n) (it was O(1)); inside one loop, O(n²); inside two, O(n³).
  - **Two cases where the adjustment is NOT applied, and why**: with `binary` the loop runs log n times, so log n × O(n) is O(n log n), not O(n²) — raising the depth would overstate it; with recursion the Master Theorem does the accounting. In both, the finding still fires — the operation still costs O(n) per call — but Big-O is left alone, because the arithmetic cannot be defended there.
  - **`linear_op_in_loop` is the other half of `quadratic_list_membership`**: that one covers linear SEARCH inside a loop (`x in list`), this one covers linear MUTATION. Same orange, same kind of defect — the code is quadratic and does not look it.
  - **Scope, stated so as not to oversell it**: this is **Python**. The same defect exists in C/C++ and remains open — `cparse.rs` has its own tree-sitter Big-O path and does not share `rich.rs`'s; porting the cost table there is the next slice. It is measured and written down here so it is not rediscovered. **CLOSED on 2026-09-25**, see the bullet below.

- [x] **The next slice: hidden cost in C/C++** (2026-09-25). Measured first over seven C++ functions with known costs: **three came out wrong and two carried the wrong name**.
  - **`ccosto.rs` already had half of it done**, and that changed the work: the container-family table (`Contiguo`/`Ordenado`/`Hash`/`Enlazado`) already gave a correct O(n²) for `v.insert(…)` and `v.erase(…)` inside a loop. What was missing was not the table but the shapes that are **not method calls**.
  - **The finding that explains why half the table was dead**: there is a row saying `find` on a contiguous container is O(n), but **`std::vector` has no `.find()` member** — searching a vector is written `std::find(v.begin(), v.end(), x)`, a FREE function. That row could only ever fire for `std::string::find`. Measured: a `std::find` inside a loop reported O(n) instead of O(n²).
  - **`strlen` in a `for`'s GUARD is C's classic O(n²)**, and the worst one to spot because it is not in the body: it is re-evaluated every iteration. It joins `strcat`/`strcpy`/`strcmp`/`memset`/`memcpy`.
  - **The counterpart that avoids the false positive**: `std::binary_search`/`lower_bound`/`upper_bound`/`equal_range` are O(log n) and are NOT flagged — flagging them would turn the correct algorithm into a finding, the same criterion by which `push_back` was already exempt.
  - **And the pair the Python bullet highlights, ported exactly**: `s = s + x` builds a NEW string with the whole content and copies it (O(n) per pass, O(n²) in a loop); `s += x` appends into the buffer the string already has and is amortized. **They read one character apart and differ asymptotically**, just like `x = x + [y]` versus `x += [y]`. The string must mention itself on the right —`s = a + b` in a loop does not grow— and the string map is kept separate from the family map because `Contiguo` lumps `vector` with `string`, and `v = v + w` does not compile.
  - **A user-visible defect 1323 tests could not see**: in `std::string f(int)` the FIRST child of `function_definition` is the return type, and its kind is `qualified_identifier` — the same as a qualified name's. `func_name` walked children in order and returned `std::string` as the name, so **every C++ function returning a `std::` type showed in the panel under its return type's name**, and two of them in one file shared it. The grammar already distinguished the two —the type is in the `type` field, the name descends through `declarator`—: following the field instead of the order is the whole fix. A method defined outside its class (`void S::metodo()`) does keep its qualified name, pinned by a test.
  - And a second grammar stumble, found by a test I wrote expecting it to pass: **`reference_declarator` does not expose its interior as a FIELD**, unlike `pointer_declarator`, so `int &ref(int)` came out `<anonymous>`.
  - 11 new tests — **1334 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (3 of 7 wrong, 2 misnamed) and after (7 of 7 right), and verified in a real browser in the Big-O table.
  - **A mistake of my own, corrected**: the first nested-function test asserted that an `insert` inside a `def g()` was reported at level 1. That is wrong: `rich.rs::collect_functions` runs the checks ONCE PER FUNCTION, so `g` gets its own pass and descending from the outer loop would count it twice. The test now asserts the opposite, which is correct.
  - 12 new Rust tests. **1081 total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and verified in a real browser: `correcto` stays O(n), both quadratic ones became O(n²), zero console errors.

- [x] **Probabilistic structure sizing: the concentration bound, read off the parameters** (2026-09-22). The first of the five reinstated topics to be built, and it confirms the argument: **`datastructures.rs` has detected Bloom Filters since Phase 14 — it only reported the KIND and no parameters.** What was missing was not a new detector, it was looking at the numbers already there.
  - **The exact fact, derived from a single parameter.** At optimal load, a Bloom Filter's false-positive rate is **exactly `2^-k`**, where `k` is the number of hash functions — and it **does not depend on the size of the bit array**. With k=3 the rate **cannot go below 12.5%** however many bits you give it. A bigger array buys *capacity* (how many elements fit before you pass the optimum), not accuracy.
  - **Why this is the concentration bound and not a curiosity**: at the optimum half the bits are set, so probing k bits gives a false positive with probability `(1/2)^k`. Same class of arithmetic as `w = ⌈e/ε⌉` in a Count-Min Sketch — the bound instantiated as code, which is exactly what I had claimed "does not appear in code".
  - **The practical difference is the note's register**: it goes from *"has a tunable false-positive rate"* — true and barely actionable — to *"this one has a 12.5% floor and more bits will not improve it"*. With `m` also written down it says where that optimum sits: `n* = m·ln2/k`.
  - **Parameters are looked for in both forms they are written**: `__init__` defaults (`def __init__(self, size=1000, num_hashes=3)`) and attributes (`self.num_hashes = 3`).
  - **And if they are not literals, no number is invented**: the note stays general. A test pins this — asserting a floor that was not measured would be exactly the error this project avoids everywhere else.
  - 6 new tests. **1164 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [ ] **Five topics I dismissed wrongly, and why the criterion was wrong** *(raised by the user, 2026-09-21)*. I measured all five against "does this justify core engine work right now?" instead of "is there something mechanical here?". **With the marketplace (Phase 30), the first question is not the filter**: where an analysis lives — core or extension — is a later decision, not the entry criterion. The entry criterion is the one already written for languages: it enters when it introduces a property Sythrall can analyse and verify usefully.
  - **Concentration inequalities (Chernoff/Markov/Chebyshev) — they appear in code as SIZING ARITHMETIC.** I said "there is nothing to detect", and that is false: a Bloom filter is sized with `m = -n·ln(p)/(ln2)²` and `k = (m/n)·ln2`; a Count-Min Sketch with `w = ⌈e/ε⌉`, `d = ⌈ln(1/δ)⌉`. That *is* a concentration bound written as code. And **`datastructures.rs` already detects the Bloom filter** — what is missing is looking at the parameters. Writing `log10` where `ln` belongs, or swapping `m` for `n`, yields a structure that **works** and has a different error rate than promised: silent, and no linter looks at it. The only undetectable thing is "this proof uses Chernoff", which was the only thing I had in mind.
  - **Curry-Howard — it is the exact dual of the Trust Surface Phase 34 just shipped.** The trust surface measures where the compiler **stops checking**. Curry-Howard gives the vocabulary for the other side: where the program **had a proof and threw it away**. A type is a proposition; widening the type discards the proof — `.unwrap()`, `as`, `transmute`, `any`, `# type: ignore`, `@ts-ignore`, a newtype collapsed to its primitive. All of that is read from the AST and falls on the same *reviewed vs unreviewed* axis. It is Phase 34's natural Line D, not a separate topic.
  - **Attribute grammars — wrong on two counts.** As a *target*: nobody analyses `.g4`/`.y`/`grammar.js` — left recursion, ambiguity, shadowed attributes — and Sythrall is **made of** tree-sitter grammars, so it eats its own cooking. And as *architecture*: if third parties are going to write Analysis Extensions, the declarative way to say "attach this attribute to this node kind and propagate it upward" **is** an attribute grammar. That is not an internal refactor: it is the shape of **Phase 30**'s extension API.
  - **Genetic programming — the expensive half has been built for a year.** I said "Sythrall optimises nothing", which is exactly the "not built yet, therefore not valuable" framing. **Phase 26** already has 6 kernels and two regressions (`fit_exponent`, `fit_exponential_base`): the ability to measure empirically whether a candidate implementation is faster **and still correct**. Evolving ASTs against that validator is superoptimisation, and the hard part — validating — is already solved. NSGA-II / Pareto front is the same family and was already accepted as the honest replacement for `quality_penalty`.
  - **SSA — "not needed" was too strong.** φ-nodes give def-use chains for free, which turns `intervals.rs` from a **dense** analysis (re-evaluating the environment at every program point) into a **sparse** one (propagating along def-use edges). That is an asymptotic difference, not a stylistic one. And the **INPUT** IR left open when the output contract was closed is plausibly SSA-shaped: not a new topic, but the shape of an item that already exists.

- [x] **Operations research** (2026-09-25). The bullet said it was the weakest item and **therefore last, not excluded** — and measuring it showed it was hiding a false claim by the engine, not a gap.
  - **Measured before writing anything**: two functions iterating `itertools.permutations` —a brute-force assignment and a travelling salesman— were reported as **O(n²)**. They are O(n!·n). For `n = 13`, `n!` is already 6.2 billion tuples, and the engine was telling the user "quadratic". That is among the worst claims a complexity analyser can make.
  - **The cause is that nesting counts measure the loop's SHAPE, not how many times it runs.** `for p in permutations(xs)` is a single `for`, so it adds depth 1 exactly like `for x in xs`. What changes is the iterable's cardinality, and that **is written in the call** — which is why the finding is decidable.
  - **And the finding says two OPPOSITE things depending on the problem, which is what makes it honest.** The same code is a **fixable mistake** if what is minimised is a sum of per-assignment costs —the Hungarian algorithm (Kuhn–Munkres, 1955) gives **the same optimum** in O(n³), already shipped as `scipy.optimize.linear_sum_assignment`; it is not a faster approximation, it is the same answer— and an **inherent cost** if it is the travelling salesman, which is NP-hard with no known exact polynomial algorithm (Held–Karp gets to O(n²·2ⁿ), better than `n!` but still exponential).
  - **Telling them apart from the AST is not decidable, so it is not guessed.** The assignment problem's shape is recognised —indexing a matrix as `[i][perm[i]]`— and **only then is Hungarian named**. Without that shape the message states the cost and presents both exits without choosing for the user. There is a test for each side.
  - **Deliberately not classified**, with tests: `product(a, b)` with no `repeat` is `|a|·|b|` and does not explode on its own; `combinations(xs, k)` with a non-literal `k` is polynomial for fixed `k` and exponential for `k = n/2`, so with no value there is nothing to claim. A normal `for` still reports what it always did — the correction introduces no false positives.
  - The bullet also said it "is already a complexity finding more than an OR one". **True, and that is how it landed**: it lives in the Big-O and in the structural findings, not in a separate operations-research engine.
  - 14 new tests — **1343 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (2 of 2 wrong, O(n²) for O(n!)) and after (2 of 2 right), and verified in a real browser with the two distinct messages.


#### Contributions sorted by what kind of thing they are *(2026-09-05, second batch)*

What follows is deliberately not in one bucket. A useful roadmap distinguishes **a principle** from **a new engine** from **a classifier row** from **something that already has a home** — filing them all as "new phases" is what turns a plan into a wish list.

**Genuinely new engines:**

- [x] **False Sharing (`Cache-Line Analysis`)** (2026-09-23) — the one this very bullet called *"the most practical of the batch"*. New module, `falsesharing.rs`. Two adjacent atomic fields in a struct will almost certainly land on the **same cache line**: when two threads write them, every write from one core invalidates the other's copy. The program stays **correct** — they are atomic, there is no data race — but the parallelism turns into coherence traffic and can end up **slower than single-threaded**.
  - **It asserts `potential`, and the limit was set by the user when proposing it.** All the AST can show is that nothing separates them, which is **necessary, not sufficient**: if one thread writes both, nothing goes wrong. Proving it needs the compiler's real layout, a running program and profiling. The message says so explicitly, and a test demands that it does.
  - **The three mitigations are recognised, and suppress the finding**: `alignas(...)` on the field (including C++17's portable `std::hardware_destructive_interference_size`), a padding field between them, or simply not being adjacent. **Recognising them matters as much as detecting the problem**: flagging an already-fixed struct would be asking for a change that is done, and the user would stop reading the finding.
  - **`es_relleno` requires shape AND name** (an array whose name says `pad`/`padding`/`cacheline`), not either alone: by name only, an unrelated `int padding_ms` would silence the finding; by shape only, any real `char buffer[N]` would too.
  - **`volatile` is included even though in C++ it is NOT a concurrency tool** — it neither orders nor makes operations atomic. Real code uses it with that mistaken intent, and the cache-line problem is the same, plus a worse one.
  - **Two details from the way there, both found by a failing test**: `volatile` **does not live in the `field_declaration`'s `type` field** but in a sibling `type_qualifier`, so looking inside the type never finds it. And tree-sitter's **cursor lifetime** bit for the **fourth time** in this crate: a child iterator returned as a tail expression outlives the local cursor.
  - It emits `StructuralSmell`, the channel that already travels — no new result key and no new `scanner.rs` arm, same criterion as `plantillas::template_smells`.
  - 10 new tests. **1195 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and measured against the live engine: **8 of 8**, including all three mitigations.
- [x] **Abstract interpretation, first slice: integer interval domain (`intervals.rs`)** (2026-09-05). The step that changes *what Sythrall is*, not what it reports. Every other engine recognises SHAPES — a `malloc` with no `free`, a `printf` with a non-literal format, an index in the wrong position; this one reasons about VALUES: it keeps an approximation of what each variable can hold at each point and derives from it things no shape reveals.
  - **The case that sums it up**, invisible to any syntactic pattern because both conditions are perfectly ordinary on their own:
    ```python
    if x > 100:
        if x < 50:      # impossible
    ```
    What gives it away is that inside the first branch `x ∈ [101, +∞)`, and that interval does not meet `x < 50`. It runs over an ABSTRACTION of the values, not over the program.
  - **Complements the CFG rather than repeating it.** `cfg.rs` finds unreachable code from the STRUCTURE of the graph (after a `return`, after a `while True` with no `break`). This finds it from the VALUES: the branch exists in the graph, but its guard can never be true. Two different questions with the same consequence.
  - **Four scope limits, each with its reason written down and its test.** *Integers only*: floats have rounding, NaN and infinities, and applying integer rules to them would produce false claims. *No loops*: analysing one properly needs **widening** (a converging fixed point), so any variable a loop assigns becomes ⊤ and nothing is claimed — the correct conservative answer, not an oversight. *Structural walk, not over the CFG yet*: for structured loop-free code, walking the AST with a per-branch environment is equivalent to propagating over the graph; the CFG is needed for the fixed point, i.e. for the next slice. *Only locally determined variables*: a parameter or a function return is ⊤, and with no certainty there is no finding.
  - **Two domain details a test covers because they are easy to get wrong.** Multiplication computes all **4 corners** instead of assuming `lo·lo` is the minimum: with `a = 10`, `a * -1` is `-10`, and assuming otherwise would yield a false claim. And `x != k` **narrows nothing**, because it leaves a hole an interval cannot represent — inventing an interval there would be worse than staying quiet.
  - Leaving an `if`, the two branch environments are **joined**: an `x` that is 1 or 100 becomes `[1, 100]`, so `x > 50` is undetermined (and not reported) while `x > 1000` is determined (and is). Both have a test, because the second is the value of the analysis and the first is what stops it being noise.
  - Emitted as a `StructuralSmell` (`condition_always_true`/`condition_always_false`), same treatment as `unreachable_code`: zero new plumbing, 2 frontend labels.
  - 14 new Rust tests. **914 Rust tests total (900 previous + 14)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Division by zero from the interval domain — the canonical demonstration of what a value analysis is for** (2026-09-05). No widening needed: the previous slice's domain already suffices, and this turns that infrastructure into a finding a user recognises instantly.
  - **Two cases, deliberately not conflated.** `division_by_zero`: the divisor is exactly `[0, 0]`, so the division **always** fails — proven. `possible_division_by_zero`: the divisor is a bounded interval that **contains** 0.
  - **The second is the more useful of the two, and it is what justifies the whole module.** The typical case is `if x >= 0: … a / x`: the guard checks the sign but not the zero, so inside the branch `x ∈ [0, +∞)` and the division fails for a perfectly reachable value. **No syntactic shape sees that** — the expression is ordinary, the guard exists and looks right. It is the RANGE that gives it away. With `if x > 0:` the interval is `[1, +∞)` and nothing is reported, which is the other half of the test.
  - **The rule that stops this being noise**: if the divisor is ⊤, **nothing is reported**. An unknown interval "containing" zero is a property of *not knowing*, not evidence of a bug; without that filter every division in the file would be a finding. It has its own test.
  - **A design mistake of my own that the test exposed and that was worth fixing properly.** The first version required a variable to be already bounded before recognising a comparison, which blocked narrowing on parameters — exactly the useful case. **Narrowing and judging are different things**: judging whether a condition is determined needs the prior value (⊤ determines nothing), but narrowing does not: `if x >= 0:` bounds `x` inside the branch even if it was a free parameter before. Separating the two recovered the finding entirely.
  - Covers `/`, `//` and `%` — all three raise `ZeroDivisionError` in Python.
  - 7 new Rust tests. **921 Rust tests total (914 previous + 7)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Abstract interpretation, second slice: fixed point with widening, and `range()` bounds** (2026-09-05). Replaces the first slice's "anything a loop touches becomes ⊤", which was the correct conservative answer but also the one that left almost all real code outside the analysis.
  - **Widening (∇) is what makes the analysis terminate.** If `n` grows from `[0,0]` to `[0,1]` to `[0,2]` on each turn, iterating to a fixed point never converges. The operator cuts that off: **when a bound moves outward it is sent straight to infinity** instead of being followed step by step. Precision is given up on purpose — `[0, +∞)` instead of the exact `[0, 99]` — in exchange for termination. It is abstract interpretation's central trade-off, and there is a test whose assertion is literally *that it finishes*.
  - **And the bound that does not move survives, which is where the value comes from.** `n = 0` with `n = n + 1` inside a loop used to be ⊤ (nothing to say) and is now `[0, +∞)`: the floor stays, so `n < 0` is provably impossible. That is the test separating this slice from the previous one.
  - **Bounded `range()`, the most common Python loop and fully provable.** `range(10)` runs over `[0, 9]`, not one more. With an unknown argument (`range(n)`) it is `[0, +∞)`, and **that is still sound**: if `n <= 0` the body never runs, and if it runs the index is never negative — the floor at 0 is real information, not an assumption. With a negative step (`range(10, 0, -1)`) nothing is claimed, because the bounds stop being obvious.
  - **Leaving a `while`, the condition is false** — if the loop ended by exhausting it. `while i < 10:` leaves `i >= 10` afterwards, which proves a later `i < 5` impossible. **With a `break` in the body that does not hold**: the loop may have exited with the condition still true, and narrowing there would assert something unproven. It has its test, and the `break` detector ignores those belonging to nested loops.
  - **Nothing is reported during the fixed-point iteration.** Intermediate states are half-way approximations: a finding emitted there could be false *and* repeated once per turn. Reporting happens once, in a final pass over the stabilised state — with a test checking that a division by zero inside a loop appears **once** and not six times.
  - A test from the previous slice had a stale name (`…pasa_a_desconocida`) because the value is no longer ⊤ but `[0, +∞)`: renamed to what it actually asserts, that the condition is not *determined*.
  - 9 new Rust tests. **930 Rust tests total (921 previous + 9)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Abstract interpretation, third slice: exit states (`break`/`continue`/`return`) and array bounds** (2026-09-06). What was missing for code with jumps in the middle of a loop, which is nearly all real code.
  - **Every block now returns three things instead of mutating an environment**: the environment it falls out of, and the ones it leaves through `break` and `continue`. It is exactly the shape of `cfg.rs::Salidas`, but carrying environments instead of nodes — that is, propagation over the flow graph, without building a second graph or duplicating the one that exists.
  - **A `return` kills its path, and that is where the precision comes from.** `if x != 0: return` followed by `a / x` now proves a division by zero: the true branch never arrives, so what follows can only come from the false one, where `x` was already proven equal to 0. Joining both branches — the previous behaviour — gave ⊤ and lost the finding entirely.
  - **Each `break` contributes the environment it actually had, instead of switching the reasoning off.** The previous slice scanned the body and, on finding a `break`, stopped narrowing by the negated condition. Now the `break` carries its state: in a `while i < 10` with `if i > 7: break`, the exit is `[8, 9] ∪ [10, +∞) = [8, +∞)`, enough to prove a later `i < 5` impossible — before it stayed at `[0, +∞)` with nothing to claim. And a nested loop's `break` is consumed by **that** loop, with no need for the scan that previously had to skip them by hand.
  - **Array bounds, the second finding the domain unlocks.** It leans on both halves at once: the sequence's length (from the literal that built it) and the index's range (from `range(...)`, a constant, or a guard). `xs = [1, 2, 3]` with `xs[5]` is **proven**; with `for i in range(5)` it is **possible**; with `for i in range(len(xs))` — the correct idiom — it says **nothing**, which is the half that matters.  Negatives count the way Python counts them, from the end: the valid range is `[-n, n-1]`.
  - **The length lives in the SAME environment as the integers**, under a key containing a space (`len xs`) that no Python identifier can have. Widening, branch joining and narrowing therefore treat it like any other value, with not one line of code to hold it up. As a bonus, `len(...)` is never negative: even with an unknown sequence it gives `[0, +∞)` rather than ⊤.
  - **And the length is forgotten the moment anything could change it.** The rule counts rather than matches shapes: occurrences of the name are counted against occurrences in the only two positions that provably do not mutate — a subscript base, and a `len(...)` argument; if any are left over, the length is dropped. `xs.append(1)`, `f(xs)` and `del xs[0]` all land there; `xs[0] = 9` does not, because writing an element does not grow the list. Deliberately conservative: losing a length costs a finding, inventing one costs a false claim.
  - **The slice closes by documenting what will NOT live here.** Integer *overflow* does not exist in Python (`int` is arbitrary-precision): there is nothing to prove, and that finding belongs to a C/C++ analyser, over a different tree and with the type widths at hand. *Nullability* is genuinely missing, but it does not fit in an interval — it needs its own lattice — and is left as its own slice rather than forced in here.
  - The module doc still said "no loops" and "anything a loop assigns becomes ⊤" from before widening: corrected — it was documentation that lied.
  - 11 new Rust tests. **941 Rust tests total (930 previous + 11)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Abstract interpretation, fourth slice: nullability, and the first analysis that crosses functions** (2026-09-06).
  - **The lattice has its own meaning but not its own implementation.** "Is `None`", "is not `None`" and "could be either" order EXACTLY like intervals over `{0, 1}`: `[0,0] ⊑ [0,1] ⊒ [1,1]`. That is not an encoding trick, it is an isomorphism — which is why branch joining, widening and narrowing come out correct by construction, with no second implementation of each.
  - **The fourth possibility is the key being absent, and it is what decides whether this is an analysis or a noise generator.** "I know nothing about this variable" (an ordinary parameter) is NOT "it can be `None`" (a `d.get(k)`): the first must stay silent and the second must report. They are read by requiring the exact interval, so anything else — including the ⊤ a widening leaves behind — means "unknown" and reports nothing. It has its test.
  - **The case that justifies the whole slice crosses functions**: one that returns a value on one branch and `None` on another, and a caller that uses the result without checking. No syntactic shape gives it away — both functions, on their own, are perfectly normal.
  - **And the most common way to return `None` has no `return` to read**: the `for` that searches, finds nothing, and falls off the bottom. That question is asked of **`cfg.rs::cae_por_abajo`**, new, which is literally "does the suite have any normal exit?" — something the graph constructor already computed and `build` threw away. **No fourth termination analyser was written**: the existing one was exposed, with its own test of the seven ways to end (`return`, fall-through, `if` without `else`, `if/else` where both return, a `for` that may not run, `raise`, `while True` with no `break`).
  - **Three deliberate exclusions from the interprocedural map**, each with its test: a **generator** returns a generator no matter how many bare `return`s it has; a **coroutine without `await`** returns a coroutine object, never `None` (with `await` it does count); and a **procedure** that never returns a real value is not registered, because `r = log(...)` is not the case this looks for and registering it would flood every call in the file. Calls must also be by **bare name**: `obj.buscar()` is not provably this file's `buscar`.
  - **Guards, which are the half that avoids the noise.** `if x is None: return`, `if x is not None:`, `assert x is not None` and `if not x:` all narrow — and so does short-circuiting **inside the same expression**: `x and x.upper()`, `x is not None and x.upper()`, `x.upper() if x else ""`. Reporting on the very idiom people use to avoid the error would be the worst possible false positive. The guard applies to the whole expression rather than only what follows the operator: that can silence a `v.x and v is not None` which really is a bug, but a false negative costs one finding while a false positive costs trust in all the others.
  - **Sources of nullability**: `x = None`; a parameter with a `None` default or annotated `Optional[T]` / `T | None` / `Union[T, None]` (the author's own claim, not an inference); `d.get(k)` **without** a default (with one it stays silent); `re.match`/`re.search`/`re.fullmatch`; `shutil.which`; `next(it, None)`. **A `pattern.match(s)` on a compiled pattern is NOT included**: `.match` on an arbitrary object is not provably `re`'s, and guessing by name is exactly the kind of claim this module does not make.
  - Only **the first dereference of each variable** is reported — the first one stops execution, the rest are its consequence. Same criterion `cfg.rs` uses when reporting the first statement of each dead run.
  - Along the way, `x: Optional[int] = None` started being analysed at all: `AnnAssign` fell into the default case and was never looked at, for nullability or for intervals.
  - 18 new Rust tests (17 in `intervals.rs`, 1 in `cfg.rs`). **959 Rust tests total (941 previous + 18)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Benchmark intermittency: the old note was wrong on all three things it claimed** (2026-09-22). It said the intermittent tests were `agg_bench`/`bfs_bench`, that they measured **wall time**, and that the cause was **`cargo test`'s own parallelism stealing their CPU**. Measured, none of the three:
  - **The intermittent one is `fortran_bench::validate_matmul_cubic`**, not those two. 18 full-suite parallel runs: 4 failures, all from that test.
  - **The kernel uses `CPU_TIME`**, which is PROCESSOR time, not wall time — CPU contention should barely affect it, which is why the old explanation could not have been right.
  - **The cause is CLOCK RESOLUTION.** gfortran's `CPU_TIME` on Windows advances in ~15.625 ms steps. The kernel measured from n=300, which on this machine is **1 or 2 ticks**: the same run returned 0.0156 s or 0.0312 s, a **100% difference**. Being the leftmost point of the log-log fit, it tilted the whole line.
  - **And the real failures confirm it in the direction nobody was looking**: they were `4.51` and `4.49`, **above** the 4.0 ceiling, not below. When n=300 landed on 1 tick instead of 2, the line steepened. Chasing "their CPU is being stolen" would have led to lowering the floor of the range — exactly the side that was not failing.
  - **The fix, measured before choosing it** (5 runs of 3 repeats each):

    ```text
    [300,450,600,800]  exponents 3.06 … 4.45   stdev 0.507   4/5 in range
    [450,600,800]      exponents 3.11 … 3.48   stdev 0.132   5/5 in range
    ```

    **Dropping one point cut the deviation 4×**, and it costs nothing: the one removed was the cheapest of the four, so the remaining three take the same time as the previous four. No coverage lost, just noise.
  - **Verified afterwards**: 12 full-suite parallel runs, 12 clean. Before, 4 failures in 18.
  - **A net so it cannot come back silently**: `benchsupport::descartar_bajo_resolucion` drops any point below 8 ticks of the coarsest clock, and if fewer than 2 survive, `fit_exponent` returns `None` — *"could not measure"* is the honest answer, not an exponent pulled from quantization noise.
  - **What was NOT touched, stated so it does not read as coverage**: the other nine benchmarks use far larger sizes (hundreds of thousands to billions of elements) and showed no failures across 30 runs, but **I did not measure them one by one**. The filter is available to them; wiring it in requires measuring first, as was done here.
  - 3 new tests. **1143 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean.
- [x] **Python's cyclomatic complexity was not radon's either — and fixing it closed the MI to exact parity** (2026-09-22). The second half of the previous finding, and the one that completes it. `complexity.rs` claimed *"replaces `radon.complexity.cc_visit`"* and *"same criteria as radon"*. Measured over 118 real functions: **32 differed (27%), and Sythrall ALWAYS undercounted** — never over, range −33 to −1.
  - **I had audited C/C++, JS/TS and Fortran CC assuming Python's was the correct one** to align them against. It was not. Reading radon's `ComplexityVisitor.generic_visit` surfaced five missing rules:

    ```text
    comprehension `for`      radon: len(ifs) + 1   was: only the `if` filters
    `for`/`while` `else`     radon: +1             was: 0
    `try` `else`             radon: +1             was: 0
    `assert`                 radon: +1             was: 0
    `match`                  subtracts 1 ONLY if there is a `case _`   always subtracted 1
    ```

  - **The dominant one is the first: every comprehension without a filter counted for nothing**, and in real Python code they are the majority. One backend function reported **4 where radon says 13**.
  - **The `match` case is this phase's fourth variant of the same pattern**: the wildcard is not a decision but the branch that remains — just like a `switch`'s `default` in C (a `case_statement` with no `value` field), in JS (its own `switch_default`) and in Fortran (a `default` child token). Four languages, four representations of one concept, each demanding its own handling.
  - **Measured result: 134 functions compared, 0 differences.** Exact parity.
  - **And it closes the MI, because that uses TOTAL complexity.** Fixing one without the other would have left the number wrong anyway. MI delta against radon: **23.85 → 6.34**.
  - **The final confirmation is exact**: substituting radon's Halstead volume into Sythrall's computation makes the MI **identical to theirs to the cent** (53.58, 51.79 and 93.79 across three files). So the formula and all four inputs are now exactly radon's, and **all** the residual is the Halstead count — where radon looks only at expression operators (87 tokens in a 271-line file) while Sythrall also counts `def`, calls and assignments, which is closer to the original definition. That one is a decision, not a bug.
  - **1182 Rust tests** with no regressions, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **The Maintainability Index was not radon's, although the doc claimed it was — four measured divergences** (2026-09-22). The same method that found the 36 Big-O and complexity defects, now applied to a **number the product shows the user**. The module doc said, in so many words, *"replaces `radon.metrics.mi_visit(content, multi=True)`"* and *"the same formula radon uses"*. Measured against **radon 6.0.1 installed in the project's own venv**, over 10 real backend files: **average delta of −23.85 points, maximum −69**.
  - **Decomposing the formula term by term** to attribute each divergence:

    ```text
    comments as a 0–1 ratio instead of a PERCENTAGE IN RADIANS   −9 to −19
    `sloc` (physical) instead of `lloc` (logical)                −2.6 to −3.6
    AVERAGE complexity instead of TOTAL                          +5 to +12
    constant 2.4 instead of 2.46                                 minor
    ```

  - **The dominant one is a missing call.** Radon does `math.radians(comments)` — multiplying by π/180 — over a **0–100 percentage**. Here a **0–1 ratio was passed unconverted**, so the comment term rewarded less than half of what it should.
  - **Deducing it was not enough.** I had predicted that using the average instead of the total would raise the MI, and it does — but the net **falls**, because the comment term weighs twice as much the other way. Without measuring, the fix would have gone in the wrong direction.
  - **And the four inputs radon needs were already computed by `raw::analyze`** (`lloc`, `comments`, `multi`, `sloc`): they simply were not used.
  - **Measured result: average delta from 23.85 to 10.69**, and ~5.6 excluding one case that is not comparable (below).
  - **A radon detail worth knowing so the comparison is not misread**: if the Halstead volume is 0, `mi_compute` returns **100.0 without computing anything**. That 100 is not a perfectly maintainable file: it is its degenerate case, and it happens with files of pure imports and decorators. `main.py` is exactly that, which is why it shows −61.
  - **What still differs, and why that is fine**: the **Halstead volume** is counted here from the AST rather than by re-tokenizing the text, and comes out considerably higher than radon's (8995 against 498 in one measured file). It is the dominant residual of the ~5 points that remain, and it is **a documented decision, not a bug**. Same for total complexity, which here sums functions while radon also includes module level.
  - **1182 Rust tests** with no regressions, Clippy clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Real SSA: dominators, dominance frontiers, φ-nodes and renaming (`ssa.rs`)** (2026-09-29). The second of the five reinstated topics to close, and it also closes the limit the bullet above had written down: *"it misses a variable that gets overwritten before being read"*. The new finding is `overwritten_store`.
  - **SSA turns the hard question into a lookup.** If every write creates a version and every read points at the one that reaches it, *"this write is not read"* becomes *"this version has no uses"*. φ-nodes are what make that work across branches and loops. Minimal placement by dominance frontier (Cytron et al.), dominators by Cooper–Harvey–Kennedy over the CFG that already existed, traversal with an explicit stack.
  - **Clean partition with `dead_store`**: that one covers *"the name is never read"*, this one *"it is read, but not this version"*. No duplicates.
  - **Measured against 3,869 third-party files: the first version produced 375 findings and 199 of them were false. 375 → 176**, in six classes, each with a regression test taken from the real code that exposed it: a `for` target is not an unconditional write; a closure reads its captures when called, not when defined; the entry node has a predecessor nobody draws (`while True` as the first statement); a `finally` can read what was set before the `try`; a write inside a `with` is conditional because an `__exit__` may suppress the exception — **and there I am correcting a criterion of my own that was wrong**; and a CFG bug predating this pass, the `else` of a `try` being reachable from its handlers.
  - **A bonus false positive the engine was already shipping**: `walk.rs` did not visit the type of an `except` nor the guard of a `case`, so a name read only there did not count as read and **`dead_store` accused it**. Fixed in the shared walker.
  - **0 findings across the repo's own 63 Python files**, verified not to be a mute pipeline by injecting a known case through the same endpoint. 1422 Rust tests (+29), Clippy clean, 334 backend tests with no regressions.
  - **What stays open**, stated up front: nothing inside the body of a `try` with handlers is reported, because the CFG draws the edge to the `except` from the `try` node rather than from each statement inside. Modelling the exception edge per statement is the CFG's job and is the next slice.
- [x] **Dead stores — the first real slice of def-use, and the pragmatic answer to "do we need SSA?"** (2026-09-22). `dead_code` has existed for phases but covers **only imports**: a local variable assigned and never read was seen by nobody.
  - **Why this is SSA's first slice and not a loose smell.** What an SSA form gives for free are **def-use chains** — who writes and who reads each value. This module builds the half that suffices for a concrete finding, straight off the AST. If an analysis later needs the full chains, that is where SSA earns its place; forcing it now would mean paying for a large refactor before having the case that demands it.
  - **The approximation is deliberately conservative.** It does not ask *"is this store read afterwards?"* — that needs order and flow, and with loops and branches it gets subtle fast — but **"is this name read ANYWHERE in the function?"**. If not, the store is dead for certain, whatever the flow.
  - **And the trade-off is written into the finding's own message**: a store overwritten before being read (`x = expensive(); x = 1; use(x)`) is also dead and **this does not see it**. Missing findings rather than inventing them.
  - **The read/write distinction is not heuristic**: Python's AST marks every `Name` with its context (`Load`/`Store`/`Del`). It is the same data the interpreter uses to decide what to do with that name.
  - **Five exclusions, each with a reason rather than generic caution**: `_` and `_`-prefixed names (the convention already says the discard is deliberate); `global`/`nonlocal` (the read may be in another function); tuple unpacking (you cannot avoid receiving the half you do not use); attributes and subscripts (not local variables); and **`eval`/`exec`/`locals()`, where NOTHING from that function is reported** — there any name can be read dynamically, and without that guard the analysis would assert something it cannot know.
  - **It runs PER FUNCTION, not at module level**: at module level a variable assigned and not read may be exported and read from another file.
  - 11 new tests. **1182 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and measured against the live engine: 7 of 7, including all five exclusions.

- [x] **Attribute grammars, first slice: tree-sitter's `grammar.js`** (2026-09-26). One of the five topics the user had reinstated, with both halves the bullet named: *"nobody analyses `.g4`/`.y`/`grammar.js`"*, and *"Sythrall is made of tree-sitter grammars, so it eats its own dog food"*. This slice makes the second literal — the analyser runs on **the very grammars the engine uses to parse**.
  - Measured before: on `tree-sitter-rust/grammar.js` (1690 lines) the engine reported **191 "functions"** —the `$ => …` arrows— and nothing else. It did not know it was a grammar.
  - **No new parser was needed**: `grammar.js` is JavaScript and the engine already parses it. What was missing was reading the structure that JavaScript describes. A two-substring text filter runs before the parser so every `.js` in a project is not parsed twice.
  - **The fact a grammar analyser must know, and what makes this one not ANTLR's**: left recursion **is NOT an error in tree-sitter**. Its parser is GLR, so `expr: $ => seq($.expr, '+', $.expr)` is legal and common. In recursive descent it is an infinite loop, and in ANTLR indirect left recursion is fatal. **It is deliberately not reported**, with a test, and written down so it does not get "fixed" later.
  - **And the most honest outcome of the pass: the detector's first version produced 101 findings across the four real grammars, and ALL of them were false.** Running it on its own dog food exposed that immediately, and there turned out to be three distinct causes: (1) **`externals`** are tokens produced by an external C scanner, defined even though they are not in `rules`; (2) several grammars **generate rules with a function** spread in with `...`, so the set of defined rules cannot be read from the text — there it stays quiet instead of accusing; (3) the second argument of **`alias(rule, name)` INVENTS a node name**, it does not reference a rule. With all three handled: **101 → 1**.
  - **That 1 is true, and it is a finding about a widely used grammar**: in `tree-sitter-rust-0.23.3` the rule `comment` is defined at line 1583 and there is **not a single reference to `$.comment`** in the file — `extras` uses `$.line_comment`/`$.block_comment` directly. It breaks nothing; it bloats the parse table and confuses whoever reads the grammar.
  - What is measured and reported: name, total rules, **hidden rules** (those starting with `_` produce no node in the tree — exactly the kind of surprise this crate hit several times staring at `kind()`), **declared conflicts** (each one is an ambiguity the author declares so tree-sitter uses GLR there), supertypes, external tokens, and the start rule, which in tree-sitter is **the first in `rules`** rather than declared separately — hence it is excluded by hand from "unreferenced", or every grammar's heart would be flagged.
  - The `grammar` key appears **only** if the file is a grammar: an ordinary `.js` does not carry it, rather than carrying it zeroed. Absent is not the same as empty.
  - 17 new tests — **1393 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the four real grammars the engine uses and verified in a real browser on Rust's `grammar.js`.

- [ ] **Program semantics** — the frame that ties the above to Rice: moving from "what syntax does this have?" to "what behaviour does this represent?". More an organisation of what will already exist (CFG + data flow + abstract interpretation) than a separate engine.

**Fits an engine that already exists, needs no new one:**

- [x] **Context-switch instructions in Assembly** (2026-09-24). `PUSHA`/`POPA`/`PUSHAD`/`POPAD` (x86) and `STM`/`LDM` with their eight addressing modes (ARM) are now recognised as context save/restore blocks, with their own `context_switch` category and a `ContextSwitchBlock` per block saying how many registers it moves and which. It was mechanical, as the bullet said — but **measuring before writing turned up eight false claims around it**, and three were not gaps but lies.
  - **The substantive correction: `PUSHA` does not save "all" registers.** It leaves out EFLAGS (that is `PUSHF`), the segment registers and all x87/SSE/AVX state. Believing otherwise is a real bug in an interrupt handler, so the message says so outright. Add that `POPA` **discards the saved stack pointer** instead of loading it, and that **none of the four exists in x86-64** — they were removed from 64-bit mode.
  - **That last fact is a claim about the file, and is used as one rather than as an accusation.** Finding a `PUSHA` proves the file is 16- or 32-bit code, which corrects two texts that were left lying: `callingconv.rs` explained the missing frame pointer by citing `-fomit-frame-pointer`, *"the GCC/Clang default for x86-64"*, and the panel title read *"Assembly x86-64"*. **No finding is emitted**: a 32-bit `.s` is perfectly valid, and this very repo compiles kernels with `-m32`.
  - **ARM was being reported as `intel` syntax**, which is not one of the two options available — false, not incomplete. `syntax` now has a third value, `arm`, in the same field rather than a new one: the field answers *"how do I read these operands?"*, and ARM is a third answer to that question, not a separate axis.
  - **And ARM's prologue existed; the engine said it did not.** A procedure starting with `stmfd sp!, {r4-r11, lr}` came out as *"no standard prologue"*, justified with a GCC flag for x86-64. ARM does build a prologue, just with one instruction instead of two. The case that shows it best: **`ldmfd sp!, {r4-r11, pc}` restores AND returns at once**, because writing the program counter is a branch — which is why the symmetric save pushes `lr` and not `pc`.
  - **The bug measuring found, which already had the right comment written:** `split_operands` tracked delimiter depth *for exactly this* —its doc said the comma in `(%rbp,%rax,4)` does not separate operands— but did not know about `{}`, because the module was born x86-only. `{r4-r11, lr}` came out split into `{r4-r11` and `lr}`: two broken operands, neither of which is a register.
  - **ARM's registers were invisible**: `r0`-`r7` are not in the x86 list (there the numbered ones start at `r8`), so an ARM function's arguments were not seen and `r11` showed up only by colliding with an x86-64 register. Measured: from **2 registers, one of them spurious, to 13 correct ones**. Ranges are expanded — `{r4-r11, lr}` is **nine** registers, not two — and a range that cannot be read is kept raw rather than invented.
  - **Deliberately not recognised**: `stm`/`ldm` with a condition code (`stmfdeq`), which would need a second table of 15 suffixes combinable with the 8 modes for a rare case; it falls to `other`, as it already did. And a file with no ARM evidence at all is assumed x86, which is what already happened — the difference is that it is now written down instead of being a tacit assumption.
  - 25 new tests — **1236 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before and after, and verified in a real browser with an ARM file and a 32-bit x86 interrupt handler.

**Classifier rows, not engines:** `classifiers.rs` and `datastructures.rs` already do exactly this — recognise that the code *is* a known thing and say so with its hedge. These go there, at near-zero cost, rather than as phases of their own:

- [x] **Quantum algorithms and post-quantum cryptography** (2026-09-24). 11 quantum SDKs and 8 PQC libraries are recognised under their own categories (`cuantico`, `pqc`), plus the shape of Shor and Grover at function level (`quantum_algorithm`, the fourth SHAPE detector after sorting/search/recursion).
  - **The clarification that is needed most, and is almost always told wrong**: Grover does **not** break symmetric cryptography. It gives only a **quadratic** speedup on unstructured search, so AES-256 drops to ~128 bits of effective security, which is still ample. Shor does break what rests on factoring or discrete log — RSA, Diffie-Hellman, elliptic curves. That is why the urgent migration is the asymmetric one, and why *"quantum breaks all cryptography"* is false.
  - **And Grover's ceiling is proven**: Bennett, Bernstein, Brassard and Vazirani showed √N is optimal for unstructured search, so the quadratic speedup is the end, not a starting point. Overlooked detail: **iterating too many times walks past the solution**, because the amplitude comes back down.
  - **The NIST renames of August 2024**, which is the actionable fact: Kyber → **ML-KEM (FIPS 203)**, Dilithium → **ML-DSA (FIPS 204)**, SPHINCS+ → **SLH-DSA (FIPS 205)**. Not presented as cosmetic: the standardised version differs from the original proposal, so an old implementation may **not** interoperate with one that says ML-KEM.
  - **And the two known breaks, both CLASSICAL**: SIKE/SIDH fell in 2022 to the Castryck–Decru attack —about an hour on a single core, no quantum computer involved— while it was a round-4 candidate; Rainbow fell the same year to Beullens. It is the most concrete reminder that "quantum-resistant" does not mean "secure": it only means Shor does not apply.
  - **What is NOT done**: claiming code is insecure for using RSA or ECC. Migrating has a cost and a schedule, and the engine does not know what threat this project faces. What is stated is what is verifiable: which scheme is broken today, and what each one is called now.
  - **Two false positives avoided, both found by tests**: `falcon` was deliberately left **out** of the library table, because it collides head-on with the web framework of the same name and an `import falcon` cannot tell them apart — labelling a REST API as cryptography would be worse than missing the scheme. And scheme lookup scans **import lines only**, because scanning the whole file made a matplotlib `cmap='rainbow'` fire a high-severity finding about a broken scheme.
  - A third defect was found by the browser, not a test: the `high` severity I had invented **is not in the engine's vocabulary** (`error`/`warning`/`info`), so `_ml_score` did not count it and the SIKE finding appeared next to a 100/100 score. Corrected to `error`; the score now drops to 85. **Stated and left alone**: with the current scale a single `error` leaves the score at 85, which the panel still labels "Excelente" (`>=80`) — that is a pre-existing property of the thresholds, true of any ML finding, not something this pass introduced.
- [x] **Persistent and retroactive structures** (2026-09-24). The three levels the bullet asked to distinguish are reported separately: `Persistent Structure (partial)`, `Persistent Structure (full)` and `Retroactive Structure`, as one more row in `datastructures.rs` alongside the 10 structures it already detected. **Frontend cost: zero** — the `data_structure` badge and its tooltip already existed, exactly as the ROADMAP predicted when it grouped these rows.
  - **The most valuable part of the note is a disambiguation, not a detection**: here *persistent* does NOT mean "saved to disk". The word collides head-on with database persistence, and someone reading "persistent structure" in a repo guesses wrong half the time. It means old versions stay readable after a modification (Driscoll, Sarnak, Sleator and Tarjan, 1986).
  - **And it does not cost a full copy**: only the root-to-leaf path that changes is copied and the rest is shared, so a persistent tree costs O(log n) extra space per update, not O(n) — which is why persistence is cheap on trees and expensive on arrays.
  - **The signal separating partial from full is mechanical, not naming**: if a WRITE method takes a version as an argument, you can modify from the past and history branches into a tree; if not, only the latest is extended and history is a line.
  - **Retroactivity is NOT full persistence, and conflating them is the easy mistake.** With full persistence you read the past and branch, but each branch is a separate timeline and the present is unaffected. Retroactive means inserting an operation *in the past* and having **every later version change**: the present is recomputed. Demaine, Iacono and Langerman (2007) showed there is no general transformation that gives it for free. That is why it is recognised **by name only and never inferred**: changing the past has no signature distinguishable from writing into an old version, and claiming it without the code saying so would be guessing.
  - Two rejections pinned by tests, because they are what keeps this from being noise: **a name without a shape is not enough** (`ImmutableConfig` with neither signal is a naming convention, not a structure) and **returning a new instance alone is not enough either** (a chained builder does exactly that). And the detection runs **last** on purpose: a Trie written immutably is still reported as a Trie — persistence is how it is updated, not what structure it is.
- [x] **Neural rendering** (2026-09-24). 8 libraries recognised (Nerfstudio, gsplat, tiny-cuda-nn, nvdiffrast, PyTorch3D, Kaolin, Mitsuba 3, threestudio) under their own `render` category, plus a note separating the two families the term lumps together.
  - **"Neural rendering" is a loose umbrella, and the difference is not one of degree: it is whether there is a network in the render path at all.** NeRF (2020) represents the scene as a **continuous function** —an MLP queried once per sample along each ray— so painting one pixel costs many network evaluations: hence the slowness. 3D Gaussian Splatting (2023) replaces the MLP with an **explicit** set of gaussians rasterised directly, and in its original form has **no network** where rendering happens (it uses spherical-harmonic coefficients). That is why it reaches real time. Both are grouped under the same name and only one has a network in the render loop.
  - The middle path is stated too: instant-ngp/tiny-cuda-nn keep the network, but a multiresolution hash encoding shrinks it enough to train in seconds.
  - **Its own category rather than `dl`**, with its own panel section ("Otras librerías detectadas — no son de machine learning"), for the same reason as the quantum SDKs: the panel is called ML/DL because of its origin, not its scope, and listing a gaussian rasteriser under "deep learning" would mislabel it just to avoid adding a category.
- [x] **DNA assembly — de Bruijn graphs** (2026-09-24). What correction 6 said to recognise is now recognised: the graph construction (`data_structure: "de Bruijn Graph"`) and the walk that solves it (`graph_traversal: "Eulerian Path"`), each in the field that actually fits — the graph is a structure, the walk is a traversal. Both fields already rendered: **zero frontend changes**.
  - **The fact that makes this worth reporting**: reconstructing a sequence from fragments, stated head-on, is the Shortest Common Superstring, which is NP-hard. The de Bruijn graph changes the formulation —nodes = (k-1)-mers, edges = k-mers— and then assembly is finding a path that uses **every edge** once, an Eulerian path, solvable in **O(V+E)**. The exact contrast is the Hamiltonian path (visit every **node** once), which is NP-complete. **The same problem, two different graphs, and the difference between linear and intractable.**
  - **And it is not free, which the note says**: the Eulerian path returns *a* sequence consistent with the k-mers, not necessarily the original. Any repeat longer than `k` collapses into a single node, which is exactly where real assemblers break — hence choosing `k` is a trade-off.
  - **Detection does not lean on names.** What is required is the structure: the **two** slices peeling opposite ends of the same k-mer (`kmer[:-1]` and `kmer[1:]`), the move that builds the prefix→suffix edge. One slice is not enough, and a name without the shape is not either — both pinned by tests.
  - **A de Bruijn graph is NOT a bioinformatics structure**, and the note says so when nothing in the code suggests otherwise: it is a general k-mer construction (de Bruijn, 1946, for cyclic sequences) that shows up identically in shift registers. Claiming the domain from having seen the shape would be the same mistake correction 6 flagged in the other direction.
  - **A defect found by a test I wrote expecting it to pass**: the assembly framing did not see `for lectura in lecturas` because **neither a `for` target nor parameters are in `assigned_names`** (it only looks at `Stmt::Assign`). Widening the keyword list would not have helped: the name never reached the comparison. Loop variable names are now collected.
  - The note also **corrects the Big-O the general heuristic reports**: the two nested loops of the construction do not both range over the same `n` —the outer goes over reads, the inner over positions within ONE read— so the work is linear in total bases, not quadratic.

**Reframed, because it is not a mathematical problem:**

- [ ] **Distributed systems / CAP** — the user proposed a `Distributed Systems Analyzer` (CAP, consensus, quorum, replication, partitions). The theorem itself is not analysable: you cannot "prove CAP" of an arbitrary program. What you **can** do is recognise framework and configuration usage (Kafka, Redis, replicas, timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, idempotency) and report which trade-off that configuration implies. That is a **Connectors/Format Extensions (Phase 24)** problem — reading `docker-compose`, manifests, config — more than a mathematical engine, which is where it belongs. **See correction 5**: the framing to use is PACELC, not bare CAP.

**Narrow but real:**

- [x] **SFINAE and C++ metaprogramming** (2026-09-23). New module, `sfinae.rs`, and **the bullet carried a sharp requirement that was honoured**: SFINAE is the legacy form and C++20's *concepts* replace it, so the finding had to say that rather than present SFINAE as the current mechanism.
  - **What separates the two forms is not style.** `template<typename T, typename = std::enable_if_t<...>>` hides the condition in a parameter nobody uses; `template<std::integral T>` puts it in the signature. **SFINAE does not express the constraint: it hides it.** It works through a side effect of the language — *Substitution Failure Is Not An Error* — so a type that does not satisfy it makes the overload **vanish silently**, and the caller's error reads `no matching function`, never saying what was missing. A `requires` clause or a named concept states the condition, and the compiler quotes it back when it fails.
  - **And the finding does NOT say the code is wrong, which is what makes it usable.** Targeting C++17 or earlier, `enable_if` is the only way to write it, and **the engine cannot know which standard the project targets**: that lives in the build system, not in the file. Same limit `pymodern.rs` already declares about the target Python version. That is why it is informational blue rather than warning yellow, and a test demands the message says **both things**: the concrete alternative and when it does not apply.
  - **`decltype` in the return type is NOT SFINAE**: it is type deduction. It is counted separately precisely so the constraint count is not inflated with something that constrains nothing.
  - **A grammar stumble**: a `template_declaration` **has no declarator of its own** — the function it declares is its CHILD, not its ancestor, and the constraint lives in its parameter list, a sibling of the function. Walking up to it asking for the name returned `<plantilla>`.
  - **The metrics live inside `TemplateComplexity`** (via `#[serde(flatten)]`) rather than in a new key: it is the same question the rest of that struct answers — what this file does at compile time — and it shares the panel. The findings travel the usual `StructuralSmell` channel.
  - 8 new tests. **1203 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and measured against the live engine: **6 of 6**, with all four metrics reaching the panel.

### 🟡 Phase 33 — Product Shell *(make Sythrall feel like a finished product rather than `localhost:5173` with an interface on top)*

Born from a shell review the user did over a real screenshot, with their own priority table. The diagnosis that orders it all: **what it most lacks is not more things, but removing development elements so every element has a clear purpose for the user.** The bullets keep their numbering so it is not lost.

- [x] **1 and 2 — `:8420 :3000 :5173` and the `GET https://api.com/health` bar out of the topbar** (2026-09-10). They were not technically wrong: they were in the wrong place. The three ports expose infrastructure detail (API, sidecar, dev server) and the URL bar is an endpoint tester — together they made the first thing anyone sees on opening Sythrall a development console.
  - **They were not deleted, they were moved** (bullet 7 of the same table, "keep diagnostics/API in Services"): they live in **Services → APIs**, which is what they are about. The port shortcuts kept a `Local` label saying what they are, and still work — verified in the browser by clicking `:8420` and confirming the endpoint is added.
  - **As a bonus an entire breakpoint could be deleted.** The `@media (max-width: 1650px)` existed precisely because the topbar did not fit with the URL bar and the 3 shortcuts inside; its comment said "without this breakpoint the ▶ Analyse button ends up off screen". With the elements moved, the topbar fits on its own: measured with Playwright, **0 px of overflow at 1280 and at 1024**. The 1100/680/480 rules that hid them were removed too, along with the `display:none` that would now have broken the bar in its new panel.
- [x] **5 — The topbar status is no longer a fixed `SIN ANÁLISIS`** (2026-09-10). That text covered two situations resolved differently: *no project* (open one) and *project not analysed* (press Analyse). Now the **text is the state** — `SIN PROYECTO` / `NO ANALIZADO` / `ANALIZANDO…` / `ANALIZADO · hace 12 min` — and the **colour is still health**, the signal that already existed and was worth keeping: an analysis with errors still shows red.
  - `AppState` gains `lastAnalysisAt`: without a timestamp, yesterday's analysis and one from a minute ago look identical. The "12 min ago" repaints on the 60s heartbeat that already existed, not a new timer.
  - The pill refreshes where the state **actually** changes (opening a project, starting and finishing an analysis), not only at boot. The first version did not, and stayed stuck on `SIN PROYECTO` with an analysed project — caught by the browser verification, not by the types.
- [x] **4 — The right panel stops saying "Waiting..." five times** (2026-09-10). With nothing started, the 5 stages took half the panel to convey nothing. An execution diagram with no execution is not an empty diagram: it is a view that does not apply yet, and now it says so in two lines. As soon as there is a project the real stages return.
- [x] **3 — The Dashboard with a project open but unanalysed** (2026-09-10). It was a `<div class="empty">` with a loose sentence: the same visual treatment as "there is nothing", when there IS something — a project, with a name, waiting for a single action. It now uses the same project header as the analysed Dashboard, explains what the analysis gives you, and makes **Analyse project** the primary button.

**What is still open from that same table, in its order:**

- [x] **6 — "Analyse" now asks what to run** (2026-09-10). And on the way a bigger hole than the dialog turned up.
  - **The ▶ Analyse button did not analyse the project.** It ran endpoint checks, lint and logs — but not the project analysis, which is what produces the Dashboard's Quality/Security/Complexity/Architecture. With a project open, pressing the app's main button produced **none** of the metrics the app presents as its main result: you had to go to the Dashboard or Static and press a different button. **Two actions called "analyse" doing different things.** The project analysis is now the first stage on the list and the button does what its name promises.
  - **Four real stages, not a decorative list**: project analysis, code problems (lint), endpoints and logs. Each already existed separately; what was missing was deciding them together.
  - **What does not apply is shown greyed out with the reason, not hidden.** With no endpoints loaded, "Endpoints" appears grey with *"No endpoints loaded"* under it. Hiding the option would leave the user wondering why it is not there; showing it disabled with the reason answers that by itself. And if **nothing** applies, no empty dialog opens: a notice says what is needed.
  - The choice is remembered between runs (`localStorage`), and **progress is split across the chosen stages** — with only one ticked, a bar that parks at 20% and jumps to 100% lies about what is left.
  - **A correction to what this very bullet said**: it did not depend on "the backend accepting a partial analysis". The four stages were already independent calls from the frontend; the backend was not touched at all. The assumption was mine and it was wrong.
  - **A bug introduced by the previous change, caught by the verification**: `updateGlobalPill()` ran before `state.running = false`, so the status stayed stuck on `ANALIZANDO…` when it finished. It did not matter before because the pill did not look at that flag; now that it does, the order matters.
  - Verified in the browser: with no project it opens no dialog (it warns), with a project it shows the 4 stages with 2 greyed out and their reason, and running **only** "Project analysis" leaves the Dashboard populated (FILES/LOC/FINDINGS) with the status at `ANALIZADO · recién`. No console errors.
- [x] **Command palette (`Ctrl`/`⌘`+`K`)** (2026-09-29) — **not from the user's numbered table**: it comes from their later reframing of what "enterprise" means (*"the old one was click click, i.e. basic; with enterprise it takes people a while to learn to use something like that"*) — interaction depth, not looks.
  - **Measured first**: Sythrall had scattered shortcuts in the explorer, the terminal and the user menu, and **no global keyboard navigation** at all. The 11 panels, the loaded files and the saved projects were reachable only by pointing and clicking.
  - **Indexes navigation + actions + live entities**, and the third is what makes it a palette rather than a menu in a different shape: files from `state.files` and projects from `/api/upload/projects`. Subsequence matching (`dsh` → Dashboard), not substring.
  - **`loadProject()` had to be exported**: it was private, so the first version of the project command merely switched tabs — a command that pretends to open something is exactly the kind of decoration this phase exists to remove.
