  - **The shortcut is printed on a visible topbar trigger**, drawn as a search field: a shortcut nobody can see does not exist. Below 900px only the magnifier remains.
  - **Verified in a real browser: 38 checks, 0 failures**, driving Chromium over CDP without adding dependencies to the repo (Playwright's already-cached binary + Node 24's global `WebSocket`). Against the real backend: 35 items and 20 actual projects.
  - **Limits of this version, stated up front**: the project list is cached on open (one created afterwards does not appear until reload), a `listProjects` failure is swallowed on purpose, and a project command opens the project but does not navigate to a file inside it.
- [ ] **8 — `Plugins & Extensions` → `Marketplace`** once the marketplace actually exists (Phase 30). Renaming it earlier would promise something that is not there.
- [x] **9 — The Dashboard shows metrics as soon as the project is opened** (2026-09-10). Opening a project and seeing its numbers instantly is the difference between a control centre and a screen asking permission to show something.
  - **The bullet's open question had an answer: no, Phase 28's history was not the right store.** That one keeps snapshots the user asks for explicitly ("Save current snapshot") to compare two moments; auto-saving one per analysis would flood it and break it for exactly what it exists for. Besides, almost no project has one, so most would have kept seeing the empty screen. What was needed was a different thing: a **local cache of the last result, one record per project**.
  - **Only `health` and `summary` are stored.** The full `StaticProjectResult` carries every finding and every function — putting that in `localStorage` per project blows the 5 MB quota with few projects. Those two are enough for the header, the stats row and the four health cards, which is exactly what you want on open.
  - **And it is always shown with its date, because nothing here knows whether the files changed.** Presenting it as "the current state" would be a lie; the notice says *"Last saved analysis, X ago — may not reflect later changes to the files"*, with **Analyse again** next to it. It uses the informational tone, not red: it is a precision about when it was measured, not an error.
  - **The topbar status was aligned with this.** It said `NO ANALIZADO` while the Dashboard showed metrics right below — a contradiction to whoever reads it. It now says `ANALIZADO · hace X` with the **neutral** dot, because that analysis's health is not loaded in this session: it asserts *when* it was measured, not *how it came out*.
  - Deleting a project forgets its cache. Without that it would occupy `localStorage` forever and, worse, if that id were ever reused it would show another project's numbers.
  - `_renderStatsRow` now takes `summary` and the date separately rather than the whole `StaticProjectResult` — that is what lets the same row be drawn from the stored data, of which only those two pieces are persisted.
  - Verified in the browser **with a reload in between**, which is the real case: before analysing it says "Not analysed yet"; after analysing the key lands in `localStorage`; **after reloading** the Dashboard shows FILES/LOC/FINDINGS, the 4 health cards and the dated notice. No console errors.
- [x] **10 — Developer mode** (2026-09-10). Settings → Developer, off by default.
  - **What it gates and what it does not was the real decision.** Not everything technical is "development": the endpoint tester is a product feature — someone monitoring their own APIs uses it for that, not to debug Sythrall — so it stays visible always. What is scaffolding is everything that talks about **Sythrall's own infrastructure**: the `:8420`/`:3000`/`:5173` shortcuts and the detail of what runs where. That is all that gets hidden.
  - It is applied with an attribute on `<html>`, not a class per element: CSS decides what is visible without any component consulting the state or re-rendering. Same approach as the theme, and applied before the first paint so the shortcuts do not flash in and out.
  - **The Runtime block says only what can be asserted.** The frontend **does not know** which port the backend lives on — `getApiBase()` returns an empty string on purpose, because the proxy is what knows (Vite in dev, nginx in prod). Writing `:8420` there would be a number this layer cannot verify, in the one panel whose job is telling the truth about infrastructure. What is shown: where the app is served from (real), that the API is reached same-origin, and what the backend reports about itself.

- [x] **Bug found along the way: the "Server" panel had been lying for a while** (2026-09-10). Wiring the Runtime block surfaced that the `Capabilities` type declared `flake8`/`pylint`/`complexity`/`torch`… in lowercase — **fields `/capabilities` never returned**. The endpoint sends `HAS_FLAKE8`, `HAS_PYLINT`, `HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE`, and so on.
  - **It compiled anyway** thanks to the type's trailing `[key: string]: unknown`, so `tsc` never said a word. The result: the right-hand Server panel showed **everything** as unavailable, always, even with flake8 and pylint installed. A type that lies does not fail — it makes the UI lie silently.
  - Type corrected to the real shape, along with the rows reading it. Verified against real data: flake8 and pylint now show ✓ (they are installed) and PyTorch/TensorFlow/Polars/LightGBM/spaCy show ✗, which is exactly what the backend reports.
  - **`HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE` deserves its own note, and the row carries it**: it is a check made **once, when the API boots** (`check_complexity_engine_sync`, 0.5 s timeout) and never re-evaluated in that run. In development the API and the Rust sidecar start together and the sidecar takes far longer to compile, so the flag stays `false` even though the engine works fine afterwards. The row says *"was not responding when the API started"* rather than *"unavailable"*, with the explanation above it: the difference between informing and confusing.

### 🔴 Phase 34 — Trust Surface & Compile-Time Complexity *(two research lines, and the rule a new language must meet to earn its place)*

- [x] **Line C — D enters, and for the concrete reason this phase demands** (2026-10-11). Not as "another language on the list": as the **second data point for Line A**, to tell whether the metaprogramming cost C++ measured belongs to the mechanism or to the language.
  - **Measured: C++ (g++) emits 3 symbols per level, D (dmd) emits 6.** The "3 per level" was C++'s. What does generalize is the shape — both curves are perfectly linear and deterministic.
  - **The technical reason, stated before writing code**: D resolves the recursion with `static if` inside the same template, without the explicit specialization C++ needs, and with a single compiler. Different mechanism, same question.
  - The same probe translated level by level and **the same depths**, with a test that requires it: comparing curves measured at different points would compare nothing.
  - The test asserts what generalizes (linear shape, differing constants), not the exact values, which depend on the compiler.
  - `POST /execution/metaprogramming-cost`. 1512 Rust tests (+7), Clippy clean.
  - **What this does NOT settle**: D as the implementation language of a product component still has no reason, and the bullet below stands unchanged.

Came out of a conversation about whether to add D to the ecosystem. The conclusion was neither "yes" nor "no": it was that **the question was framed wrong**. It is not "is D better than Rust/Zig?" but "is there a concrete part of the problem where D brings something distinct and good enough?". Answering that surfaced **two concepts that do not need D to exist**, and that stand on their own.

**The architectural principle, verbatim, because it is the first thing lost:**

> A new language does not enter by count. It enters when it introduces a property, model or capability Sythrall can analyse and **verify** usefully.

**And the procedure**: can it be observed statically? → if yes, an analyser. If not, can it be measured? → if yes, a benchmark. If neither, it is research, not a ticket. And finally: does it add something we do not have? If not, it does not enter. This repo has already run that procedure twice with PyO3 (investigated with real benchmarks, rejected both times) and once with Rust (which entered for PTY handling, a problem genuinely its own). It is not theory.

**The order is C++ → Rust → D, and the first two stages cost no new language.** Ahead of all three comes the universal IR: every new target is another `scanner.rs` arm to keep in sync, and that is the bug that has already appeared five times.

- [x] **IR, first slice: the OUTPUT contract, enforced by the compiler** (2026-09-17). `scanner.rs` had nine arms building their JSON by hand with `json!({...})`, and `static_analysis.py`'s aggregation reads with `.get(key, [])` — so **forgetting a key broke nothing visible**: the finding simply never appeared and the result looked healthy. `control_findings` was in **3 of 9 arms** and `modernization` in **3 of 9**.
  - **The `Nucleo` type has no `Default`, and that is the whole design.** Adding a field to it **breaks compilation in all nine arms** and forces each language to say what it puts there. With a single struct and `..Default::default()`, forgetting a field would leave it empty **silently** — exactly the bug this exists to prevent. `Extras` (the language-specific part) does have `Default`, because there the norm is to fill nothing.
  - **The verbosity is the feature.** Writing `vacio()` in an arm declares "this language does not produce this", which is different from "I forgot". The three "KEYS, not languages" tests are still there as a net, but they are now the second line: the first is the compiler.
  - **The test the type makes possible**: `todo_lenguaje_devuelve_el_nucleo_completo` asserts the 15 core keys for the **11 files** of a real scan (py, c, cpp, ts, js, f90, s, ipynb, css, html, package.json), plus that the 11 lists really are lists and not `null` — a `null` aggregates differently from a `[]` on the other side. Before, it could only be asserted one key and one language at a time.
  - **Scope, stated so as not to oversell it**: this unifies the **output**, not the input. Every language still has its own parser and its own tree (rustpython for Python, tree-sitter for the rest, with grammars that do not even agree between C and C++). The **input** IR — a normalized AST all analysers run on — is another, much larger job, and remains open. What was closed is the half where the evidence of pain was measurable.
  - Honest side effect: `control_findings` now travels as an explicit `[]` in the 6 arms where it was missing. It is additive — the aggregation already treated it as empty — but now it is stated rather than assumed.
  - **A mistake of my own, corrected**: inserting the new test accidentally stripped the `#[test]` from the 10-language coverage test, so it stopped running. A `duplicated attribute` warning from the compiler gave it away; it is restored, and the suite count confirms it.
  - 1 new test. **1059 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions — which is the verification that matters here, because the JSON shape is exactly what Python consumes.

- [x] **Line A, first slice: C++ template recursion** (2026-09-13). New module, `plantillas.rs`. Zero new languages — C++ was already a target, so this adds no `scanner.rs` arm and does not worsen the problem the universal IR exists to solve.
  - **`recursive_template_no_base_case`** — a template that instantiates itself with no visible way to stop. **It is not a slow program: it is a build that fails**, when the compiler hits its instantiation-depth limit (~900 in gcc, ~1024 in clang). Read from the shape of the declarations.
  - **`deep_template_recursion`** — `Factorial<500>` makes the compiler create about 501 distinct types, one per level, each with its own mangled name and entries in the compiler's tables. **That is compile-time cost, not runtime cost**: the program may still run instantly. The depth is exact because the argument is a literal; with a non-literal argument **nothing is claimed** — same rule as `maxsize=cfg.size` in `colas.rs` and `RSA.generate(cfg.bits)` in `cripto.rs`.
  - **Three base-case forms recognised, each with its test**: a separate specialization (`template<> struct X<0>`), an `if constexpr` inside (the modern C++17 form, no specialization), and `enable_if`/`requires`/`void_t`. With any of them in sight the module stays quiet: claiming "no base case" with one right there would be false.
  - **The message states explicitly what it CANNOT know**, and a test checks that: how many symbols end up generated, how much memory the compiler uses and how long the build takes all **require instantiating, i.e. compiling**. That half belongs to empirical verification, not to this.
  - **A grammar detail that nearly cost a mute analysis**: in tree-sitter-cpp the `constexpr` of an `if constexpr` is an **anonymous** token inside `if_statement`. A walk over named nodes — the natural thing to write — never sees it, and the base-case detector would have produced false positives **without failing**. Solved by looking at anonymous children, and before writing a single detector line the grammar's real `kind`s were dumped with a throwaway test. It is exactly `locality.rs`'s `index`/`indices` bug, avoided deliberately this time.
  - It emits `StructuralSmell`, the shape that already travels, so it reaches the panel and the project scan with no new plumbing.
  - 11 new Rust tests. **1051 Rust tests total (1040 previous + 11)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.
- [x] **Line A, third and final slice: MEASURE the cost, not just describe it** (2026-09-17). `tplcost.rs` + `POST /execution/validate-template-cost`. It closes the promise the previous two slices wrote down: those count what is WRITTEN and say the real cost requires compiling; this **is** that "requires compiling".
  - **It compiles a probe Sythrall writes, never the user's code** — same criterion as Phase 23's `fortran_bench.rs`. Someone's file needs its own includes, flags and build system: trying to compile it would fail almost always and that failure would say nothing. What can be measured is **how the cost of instantiating templates scales on THIS machine**, so the depth the static analysis found can be placed on a real curve.
  - **The module's important decision is about what gets asserted, and it fixes a problem this very project already had.** The existing benchmarks (`fortran_bench`, `zig_bench`, `agg_bench`, `bfs_bench`) **assert on wall-clock time**: they fit an exponent and demand a range. That made them the **only intermittent point** in a suite of over a thousand tests, and they really did fail several times during this session. That is not repeated here because a **deterministic** quantity is at hand: **object bytes**. Each instantiation at `-O0` is an emitted function with its symbol, the `.o` grows with depth, and that does not depend on how much CPU is free. **The test asserts bytes; time is reported as data and never asserted.**
  - **The probe is a function, not a `static constexpr int`, and that is deliberate**: a compile-time constant is evaluated and disappears, so the object would not grow and there would be nothing deterministic to measure.
  - **The measurement confirms the theory**: with g++ 6.3, depths 8/16/32/64 gave 3.7/6.6/12.5/24.3 KB of object — **doubling the depth doubles the object**, i.e. linear in levels, ~377 bytes per instantiation. That measured slope (`bytes_per_level`) is reported because it is what makes the curve useful.
  - **And something turned up that had to be said or the number would mislead**: time went from 151 ms to 253 ms while the object grew 6.6×. At these depths **time is dominated by compiler start-up**, not by the templates. The note says so outright: the bytes are the signal, the milliseconds are context.
  - It degrades gracefully with no C++ compiler (`available: false` with the reason), and the panel shows the button, the "compiling" state and the table with its two columns read differently.
  - 3 new Rust tests. **1058 Rust tests total (1055 previous + 3)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions. `/execution` was already in `vite.config.ts`'s proxy, so no new prefix was needed.

- [x] **Line A, second slice: the shape metrics, from Rust to the panel** (2026-09-13). The new key (`template_complexity`) travels the full Rust → Python → TS chain, which is the only way a number computed in the engine ever gets seen.
  - **Six numbers, all of what is WRITTEN**: primary templates, specializations, instantiation sites, distinct instantiations, `if constexpr` branches and `static_assert`s. Plus the list of templates that instantiate themselves.
  - **There are two instantiation counts and it is not redundancy: they are wrong in opposite directions, and saying so is the finding.** `instantiation_sites` counts written uses — `vector<int>` used 50 times is 50 sites and **one** real instantiation, because the compiler deduplicates: it **overcounts**. `distinct_instantiations` dedupes by text and gets closer, but `Factorial<N - 1>` is **one** text and **N** real instantiations: it **undercounts, precisely in the costliest case**. It has its own test pinning both directions.
  - **The panel's subtitle is not decoration, it is half the finding.** "1,842 sites" sounds like a compile-cost prediction and is not one; the panel says outright that these are counts of what is written and that knowing the real cost requires compiling. Without that line the number invites exactly the wrong reading.
  - **A new "KEYS, not languages" test, and this time the failure mode is worse than usual**: `template_complexity` is an **object**, not an array. A `.get(...)` with a default would return an empty dict and the panel would show zeros — indistinguishable from "this file uses no templates". The test checks the key reaches the PROJECT scan with its real numbers, which is where the dropped-key bug family appeared five times.
  - Declared in `CPP_FEATURES` and **not** in `C_FEATURES`: C has no templates, and the metrics come back at zero without walking the tree.
  - **A UI bug the panel surfaced, fixed for everyone**: a label longer than its card was **clipped** (`ESPECIALIZACION` instead of `ESPECIALIZACIONES`), which is worse than breaking it — a number with a truncated label can be read as a different metric. It now wraps with a hyphen. Applies to every stat card, not just these.
  - 4 new Rust tests (3 for the metrics, 1 for the key in the scan). **1055 Rust tests total (1051 previous + 4)**, Clippy clean, `ruff`/`tsc`/`vite build`/`biome check` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions.

- [x] **Line A, fourth slice: the generated SYMBOLS — the number this very bullet said cannot be computed** (2026-09-23). It closes Line A. The bullet wrote down the exact trap: a mock-up of the finding showed *"Generated symbols: 12,430"* next to *"Depth: 7"*, and **the first is not statically computable** — knowing it requires instantiating, i.e. compiling. `tplcost.rs` already compiled and measured time and bytes, but did not count symbols.
  - **Zero new infrastructure: `binparse.rs` already knew how to read symbol tables** since Phase 19 (the executable analyser). It only had to be pointed at the object `tplcost` was already generating. Two pieces built for other purposes that together give exactly what the bullet asked for.
  - **The measured curve came out cleaner than the byte one**:

    ```text
    depth    8  ->   33 symbols,  3,744 bytes
    depth   16  ->   57 symbols,  6,755 bytes
    depth   32  ->  105 symbols, 12,787 bytes
    depth   64  ->  201 symbols, 24,851 bytes
    ```

    **Exactly 3 symbols per instantiation level**, across all four points.
  - **And symbols turned out to be the better metric, which is this slice's finding.** *"Each level emits 3 symbols"* is a claim that carries to another machine; *"each level costs 376 bytes"* does not — it depends on the ABI. The test pins that **property** (the symbol slope is a small integer, far below the byte slope), not the exact number, because the count depends on the compiler and its version.
  - **And a language fix that only showed up in the browser**: the panel's note was in English in the middle of an all-Spanish panel. Translated. This is not the same as the engine's ~200 finding messages — those remain in English and are a separate decision — this is a panel's explanatory prose, and it was unreadable to its reader.
  - 2 new tests. **1185 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and verified in a real browser: the table with the new column and the same values the engine measured.

- [x] **Line A — Compile-Time Complexity, starting with C++** *(closed on 2026-09-17 with the 3rd slice and extended on 2026-09-19 with the 4th; this heading's `[ ]` was left unticked by oversight, corrected on 2026-09-26)* *(already a target, so zero new languages)*. Code can be perfect and fast at runtime and still make the compiler instantiate enormous amounts. Almost no tool measures that.
  - **Static gives the SHAPE, measured gives the COST**, and that separation must be in the code before the first detector is written. Exact from source: template nesting depth, instantiation sites, specialisations, `if constexpr` branches, recursion pattern. **Requires compiling**: real instantiation count, generated symbols, compiler memory and time.
  - **The concrete trap**: a mock-up of this finding listed "Generated symbols: 12,430" next to "Depth: 7". The first is **not statically computable** — knowing it requires instantiating, i.e. compiling. Same line `colas.rs` draws with `maxsize=cfg.size` and `cripto.rs` with `RSA.generate(cfg.bits)`: a non-literal value is not judged.
  - **And there is a better way out than estimating, already built**: Phase 23 compiles and times real code (`fortran_bench` compiles a kernel with gfortran and fits the measured exponent). The same machinery can compile and **measure** the cost instead of predicting it. That completes the house pattern: theory → model → analyser → **empirical verification**.
  - If the concept holds up in C++, D extends it with `static if` and CTFE, the explicit version of the same phenomenon. If it does not hold up, no language was spent.

- [x] **Line B, fourth slice: Rust's types — struct, enum, trait and their `impl`s** (2026-09-26). The third gap the same matrix audit found, and the code itself already named it: *"structs, enums and traits exist in Rust and are extractable, but they are another slice"*.
  - **They were entirely invisible**: the `classes` key did not even exist in the result. And there was a second effect the measurement showed that the docstring did not mention: **methods came out flat, with no owner** — a `dibujar` from an `impl` was indistinguishable from a free function, even to the call graph just built.
  - **The fact that changes how a Rust file must be read**: methods **do not live in the type**, they live in `impl` blocks that can sit anywhere in the file, and there can be several for the same type. A `struct` does not "have" methods like a class: the `impl`s add them. That is why extraction is two passes —types first, `impl`s distributing afterwards— rather than reading the type's body.
  - **And `impl Trait for Tipo` is not the same as `impl Tipo`**: the first satisfies someone else's contract, the second adds its own methods. Each method records which trait it comes from (or `null` if it is inherent), and the implemented trait lands in `bases` — the closest thing to an "extends" Rust has, which makes the panel render it with no frontend change.
  - **A Rust `enum` is not a C `enum`**: its variants carry data (`Activo(u32)`, `Error { codigo: i32 }`), i.e. it is a SUM type, not a list of constants. Each variant's shape is kept so that this is visible.
  - **Required versus provided**, a distinction specific to traits: `fn dibujar(&self);` obliges the implementor, `fn area(&self) -> f64 { 0.0 }` does not. Both belong to the trait and are distinguished in the text rather than merged. A required method also **does not appear in `functions`** —a signature with no body is not a defined function— and that is correct.
  - What it does not do, with tests: **an `impl` for a type from another file does not invent a type** (`impl MiTrait for String` does not add `String` to the list); and a generic or a qualified path (`impl<T> P<T>`, `modulo::P`) still finds its type.
  - **A defect the live measurement caught, not a test**: a struct-shaped variant was counted TWICE —as a variant and as a loose field— because the walk descended into it. It now looks only at the body's direct children.
  - The panel says **"Tipos (struct / enum / trait)"** and not "Clases", for the same reason it already says "Structs/Unions" for C: Rust has no classes.
  - 13 new tests — **1378 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (key absent) and after (3 types with fields, methods and traits), and verified in a real browser.

- [x] **Line B, third slice: Rust's Big-O — and a defect that also fixes C++** (2026-09-26). The same capability-matrix audit that found the call graph found something bigger: **`rsparse.rs` returned literally `"no estimado"` for EVERY function**, so Rust was the only one of the 8 targets with nothing in the panel's headline section (*"Algorithm Complexity — Big O"*). Cyclomatic complexity was computed correctly, so the tree walk was already there: what was missing was the inference.
  - New `rscosto.rs` module, **the fourth sibling** of `amortizado.rs` (Python), `ccosto.rs` (C++) and `jscosto.rs` (JS/TS) — the house already had the pattern, no need to invent one.
  - Measured before and after over six functions: `O(1)`, `O(n)`, `O(n²)`, `O(log n)` (loop halving the range), `O(2^n)` (two self-calls without dividing) and container cost. All six said `"no estimado"`.
  - **The symmetry Rust shows more sharply than any other language**: `contains` is the **same method name** on `Vec` and on `HashSet`, and it is O(n) on one and O(1) on the other. Not a matter of style: it is the same call, written identically, with different asymptotics depending on the receiver's type. That is why the family map is needed and a list of method names is not enough. `VecDeque` adds the contrast with `Vec`: removing from the front is O(1) there and O(n) here.
  - And the counterparts that keep it from being noise, with tests: `push`/`pop` are amortized and do not raise the level (same criterion as `push_back` in C++ and `append` in Python); two **sequential** loops are still O(n); a receiver of unknown type is not judged.
  - **A real defect found by browser verification, not by a test**: the type map was built **file-global**, so two functions with a same-named parameter of different types overwrote each other and the file's last declaration won. The same file gave O(n²) when measuring one function and O(n) with both together. **And `ccosto.rs` had the defect first** —`rscosto.rs` inherited it by mirroring— so both were fixed: the map is built global and then overlaid with the local one, which is what keeps variables from outside the function visible. Measured in C++: `busca_en_vector` went from O(n) to O(n²), which is correct.
  - An old test (`el_big_o_dice_que_no_esta_estimado_en_vez_de_inventar_uno`) pinned the previous behaviour, and **pinned it rightly at the time**: while Big-O did not cover Rust, saying "not estimated" was more honest than inventing a bound. The contract changed from "honest absence" to "real estimate", so the test was **replaced**, not relaxed.
  - 16 new tests — **1365 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (6 of 6 unestimated) and after (6 of 6 right), and verified in a real browser, which is where the global-map defect surfaced.

- [x] **Line B, second slice: Rust's call graph — the gap found by auditing the matrix** (2026-09-26). It did not come from a bullet: it came from measuring **what each of the 8 target languages reports** and comparing the result keys.
  - **Rust was the only one of the eight with no call graph**, and not empty: the key **did not exist**. The same program —four functions, one calling the other three— gave three edges and a per-function `calls` field in Python, and neither in Rust. It is the same shape of gap the ROADMAP already found once (*"Fortran was the only one of the six languages without cyclomatic complexity"*), found the same way: by comparing languages against each other rather than looking at one.
  - **Rust has more call forms than C, and each is a different grammar node**: `f(x)` (`identifier`), `modulo::f(x)` (`scoped_identifier`, where the last part is what matters) and `x.metodo()` (`field_expression`). All three land in `calls`.
  - **Methods are collected but almost never produce an edge**, and that is stated: resolving `v.len()` to a definition needs types, and without them nothing is claimed about what it points to. Same treatment the other languages give their method calls.
  - **Macros are deliberately left out**: `println!(…)` is a `macro_invocation`, not a `call_expression`, and including it would put nodes in the graph that are not functions of the file. Same decision `cparse.rs` makes about `#define`. Pinned by a test.
  - Edges only go to functions **defined in this file**, as in the other six: a call into another crate has no node to point at, though the name still lands in `calls`.
  - 6 new tests — **1349 Rust tests in total**, Clippy/`tsc`/biome clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, measured against the live engine before (key absent) and after (3 edges, the same ones Python gives for the same program), and verified in a real browser: the panel shows *"Call Graph (3 conexiones)"* where there was no section before.

- [x] **Line B — Trust Surface, starting with Rust** *(a new target, but the cheapest one for this concept)* (2026-09-20). Where the parts of a program that need extra guarantees concentrate, and **exactly which guarantee stops applying there**.
  - **Rust went from being the language Sythrall is written in to also being a language Sythrall analyses.** `rsparse.rs` (tree-sitter-rust) is the 11th language plugin: functions with cyclomatic complexity, `use`s, structural smells, and the trust surface. It arrives through the same path as Fortran and Assembly — `plugin.rs` → `plugin_registry.py` → `_LANGUAGE_HANDLERS` — with no new dispatch and no separate panel.
  - **What the surface measures is NOT a score.** It is 5 numbers and none is "worse" than another: `unsafe` blocks, how many carry a `// SAFETY:` above them, `unsafe fn`s, how many declare `# Safety` in their doc, and `unsafe impl`s counted separately. **A documented `unsafe` is legitimate**; what the panel separates is not right from wrong, it is *reviewed* from *unreviewed*. That is why the two new findings (`unsafe_block_without_safety_comment`, `unsafe_fn_without_safety_doc`) are yellow and not red: what is missing is the written proof, not the correctness.
  - **`unsafe impl` is counted separately on purpose.** An `unsafe impl Send for T` asserts nothing about a line: it asserts a property of **every future use of the type**. It is the most expensive part to review and the easiest to overlook — folding it into the block count would have hidden it inside a total.
  - **The grammar detail that nearly cost the finding**: in `impl_item` the `unsafe` token is **anonymous**, i.e. invisible to a named-node walk; in `function_item` it does sit inside a named `function_modifiers`. The two cases look identical reading the code and are detected differently. Dumping the grammar's kinds before writing a detector has stopped being a recommendation: it is step 1.
  - **Big-O says `no estimado` instead of inventing one.** Rust has iterators, closures and recursion; the nested-loop heuristic that works for C or Fortran would produce a number with the face of a fact. Cyclomatic complexity is counted (that is countable) and the rest is declared not estimated.
  - **A bug no test reached and the browser found**: `RustUse` did not emit the `type` field, and the panel reads it unchecked (`imp.type.replace(...)`). It was not a silent gap — it **broke the whole file's render** with a `Cannot read properties of undefined`. It is the visible version of the dropped-key bug, and it shows the exact limit of the contract Line A closed: `Nucleo` enforces the **core** keys, not the shape of the elements **inside** each list. Fixed on both sides: Rust emits `type: "use"` (with a test over the serialized JSON, not over the struct) and the panel stopped assuming the field arrives.
  - **What this slice does NOT bring, stated now**: structs/enums/traits (they exist and are extractable — another slice), a Rust call graph, and the transitive trust propagation described in the bullet below. What exists is the file's inventory, which is what the comparison against C++ and D needs in order to have something to measure against.
  - **Never a universal "security score".** Each language contributes its own model and the finding says which guarantee exists and where it ends. Comparing counts across languages (`14 @trusted` against `8 unsafe`) would be wrong, and for a mechanical reason rather than caution — see below.
  - **Why Rust before D**: same concept (`unsafe` is the explicit boundary), incomparably larger corpus, and `tree-sitter-rust` is among the most mature grammars. Today Sythrall can only give the *implicit* half (C/C++: raw pointers, `reinterpret_cast`, manual allocation), and that half alone says "everything is a trust surface": true, and barely actionable. The concept lights up only with an explicitly-annotated language beside it.
  - **Rust's `unsafe` and D's `@trusted` are NOT the same mechanism, and that is what makes the comparison interesting.** Three differences that change what can be asserted:
    - **Default polarity is opposite.** Rust is safe by default and you opt out; **D is `@system` by default** and you opt into `@safe`. Direct consequence: in Rust every `unsafe` site is a deliberate act, whereas in D counting `@system` counts "genuinely unsafe" and "not annotated yet" alike. **They are not measuring the same population**, and that is the mechanical reason cross-language count comparison is wrong.
    - **D separates what Rust merges.** `@system` is "unchecked"; `@trusted` is "unchecked body with a safe interface, and the author signs for it". Rust puts both in `unsafe`. So: **D names the review surface; in Rust you have to work out which `unsafe` blocks are boundaries and which are internals.** That is D's real contribution, sharper than "D has three levels".
    - **`unsafe fn` moves responsibility to the caller**; D's rough equivalent is a `@system` function called from `@trusted`. Different shapes of the same contract transfer.
  - **A limit to write down now, before it is lost**: in D the safety attributes are **partly inferred** — templates and `auto` functions deduce them rather than carrying them written. An analyser that only reads annotations **undercounts generic code**. Same class of trap as `HAS_COMPLEXITY_ENGINE` (a value that looks like a fact and has a footnote), and the finding must say so.
  - **Trust propagation**: `@trusted` → `helper()` → pointer arithmetic is implementable with the existing call graph (`graph.rs`). **A real constraint, not theoretical**: that graph resolves callees in the SAME file, by name, so transitive analysis is sound within a module and blind across files until the graph resolves cross-file. Stated in the finding, not discovered afterwards.

- [x] **Line D, third slice: Python — and the trio reveals that each language brings its OWN justified/unjustified pair** (2026-09-22). The proof surface now covers Rust, TypeScript and Python, and what shows up when you see them side by side is worth more than any one of them:

  ```text
  Rust         .unwrap()          .expect("why it cannot fail")
  TypeScript   @ts-ignore         @ts-expect-error
  Python       # type: ignore     # type: ignore[assignment]
  ```

  - **Three languages, three different mechanisms, the same idea.** None copied the others, and yet all three distinguish discarding a proof *outright* from discarding it *while writing something down*. That is not a forced analogy from the analysis side: it is a convergence the three designs arrived at separately.
  - **Python's is the most specific.** A bare `# type: ignore` silences **every** error on that line, for good — including a different one that appears years later for an unrelated reason. `# type: ignore[assignment]` names which one is being suppressed, so mypy keeps reporting anything else that shows up there. Neither is wrong; the difference is that **one keeps telling you**.
  - **Comments are not in rustpython's AST, and getting this right mattered.** Finding them by text matching would have flagged a `# type: ignore` written **inside a string** — exactly the class of bug this phase spent five modules removing. It is solved exactly: the byte ranges of **every string literal** are collected from the AST, and only `#` characters falling **outside** them are considered. That is the same information the interpreter has, not a heuristic, and a test uses the trap string.
  - **mypy's rule is respected rather than loosened**: the `[` must be adjacent, so `# type: ignore [x]` **with a space** is a bare ignore followed by text. Being more permissive than the tool that interprets the comment would give a count that does not match reality.
  - **The panel discriminates on a field unique to each variant**, not on a `language` field that would have to be kept in sync in three places.
  - 7 new tests. **1171 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and verified in a real browser: all three files in one project, each with its correct panel, and the trap string not counted.

- [x] **Line D, second slice: TypeScript's proof surface — where the symmetry comes out SHARPER than in Rust** (2026-09-22). The same concept with the tools this language provides: `x!`, `x as T`, `: any`, and the two suppressions.
  - **`@ts-expect-error` vs `@ts-ignore` is the best example of the whole axis, better than `.expect()` vs `.unwrap()`.** Both discard the same proof, but **`@ts-expect-error` fails when the error goes away**: it cleans itself up. The day the underlying type is fixed, the compiler asks for the suppression to be removed. `@ts-ignore` stays silent forever and **never says it is no longer needed** — it can sit for years covering an error that stopped existing. In Rust an old `.expect()` merely goes stale in its wording; here the justified form has a mechanism that keeps it honest.
  - **`x!` is the exact twin of `.unwrap()`**: it discards the proof that the value could be `null`. Same operation, different language.
  - **The sharpest finding: `as unknown as T` is not a stronger `as`.** It is the one that **turns off the only check TypeScript still runs on an assertion**: a plain `as` requires the two types to overlap, and routing through `unknown` removes that requirement, so anything can be asserted to anything. It is the equivalent of a `transmute`, which is why it is red while `@ts-ignore` is yellow — the latter may be necessary and merely lacks the self-cleaning form.
  - **A contract detail that was looked after**: for a `.js` file the `proof_surface` key is **absent**, not zeroed. `as`, `!` and `any` are TypeScript syntax; **absent is different from empty**, and a zero would claim something was measured that does not exist. Verified in the browser with a `.ts` and a `.js` in the same project: the panel appears for one and not the other.
  - 8 new tests. **1158 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and verified in a real browser: 6 points on the `.ts`, section absent on the `.js`, zero console errors.

- [x] **Line D — Proof Surface: the exact dual of the Trust Surface** (2026-09-22). It comes from the Curry-Howard bullet reinstated into the ROADMAP after being wrongly dismissed, and it confirms why that was worth doing: **it was not a new topic, it was the other half of one already built**.
  - **The symmetry, which is the whole point.** The trust surface measures where the compiler **stops checking** (`unsafe`). This one measures the opposite: where the compiler **did know something** — that this can fail, that this number may not fit — and the program **discarded that information**. A type is a proposition and a value of it is its proof: `Result<T, E>` says "this can fail", and `.unwrap()` throws that proof away without giving a reason.
  - **And it has its justified form, exactly like `// SAFETY:`**: `.expect("why it cannot fail")` discards the same proof but writes down the reasoning. They are counted separately, never summed, because the difference *is* the metric — the same *reviewed* vs *unreviewed* axis, on the other side of the boundary.
  - **The finding is deliberately narrow: `unwrap_in_fallible_fn`.** It fires only on a `.unwrap()` inside a function that **already returns `Result`**. There the signature promises it can fail, the caller is prepared to receive the error, and `?` propagates it in one character. **That is why it is red while the two Trust Surface findings are yellow**: an unjustified `unsafe` may be perfectly correct and merely missing its written proof; here the alternative existed and was not taken.
  - **Two limits pinned by tests, not discovered afterwards.** (1) **It does not descend into closures**: `?` inside a closure propagates to the *closure*, not the enclosing function, so there `.unwrap()` has no alternative to recommend and flagging it would be false. (2) **It does not resolve type aliases**: a `-> MyResult<T>` defined as `Result<T, E>` is not recognised. A known undercount — without a type table the honest answer is not to assert.
  - `as` is counted separately as an *unchecked conversion*: in Rust `as` truncates or wraps silently, and `try_into()` is the form that preserves the proof by returning a `Result`. Not every `as` is wrong, but every one is the unverified variant.
  - **Three stale processes gave false answers before this could be verified**, and the pattern is worth writing down: `uvicorn --reload` runs a **parent and a separate worker**, so killing the parent leaves the worker orphaned and serving the code it started with. **Six** had accumulated over the session, plus a uvicorn launched with the system Python instead of the `.venv`. The symptom was deceptively partial: the new finding **did** reach the panel (that path already existed) and only the new field vanished. Killing by port PID is not enough — the command lines have to be read.
  - 7 new tests. **1150 Rust tests total**, Clippy clean, `tsc`/`biome` clean, **334 backend tests** with no regressions, and verified in a real browser: both surfaces rendering together, zero console errors.

- [ ] **Line C — D, only once the two above have worked.** At that point the justification stops being "D is interesting" and becomes: two concepts have been proven on C++ and Rust, does D add a dimension neither covers? And the answer is yes, for two concrete things: it **grades the boundary into three levels where Rust has two** (naming the review surface), and it **makes explicit with `static if`/CTFE the metaprogramming that is implicit in C++**. Internal order: `@trusted`/`@system` → compile-time complexity → observable CTFE → mixins as an analysis boundary → templates/`static if`.
  - **Step 1 of that order done on 2026-10-16: `@trusted`/`@system`** (`lenguajes/dparse.rs`, 698 lines). D is the engine's **13th language**, and it arrives with the capability it was picked for: **the trust boundary graded into three levels**, with the middle one named by the language.

    **The difference from Rust is not one of quantity, it is where the claim lives.** Line B measured `unsafe` against the `// SAFETY:` convention, which the compiler does not know about: enumerating Rust's review surface means reading comments. In D, `@trusted` is a keyword, so the list of what must be reviewed can be **asked of the tree**. The panel shows that list with name and line, not just the count — that is what Line C meant by "naming the review surface".

    **Measured against Phobos and druntime** (894 files, 20.9 MB — D's standard library and its runtime): 9,504 functions, 5,706 `unittest`s, 0 unresolved names.
    - `@safe` 1,652 · `@trusted` **688** · `@system` 216 · unmarked 6,948.
    - **26.9% of everything that is marked is `@trusted`.** More than a quarter of the declared surface of D's standard library is "the compiler stopped checking because the author said so".
    - **29.1% of those `@trusted` have the level inherited**, not written in their own signature. That is the measured justification for the scope work: nearly a third of the review surface is invisible when reading the function.
    - 68.2% have a comment attached above.

    **Why a grep was not enough, and it is pinned by tests.** The level can come from three places: the function's own attribute (before or after the signature), a `@safe:` label that applies to the rest of the scope, or an `@attr { ... }` block. Measured against the grammar: **that block produces no container node** — the attribute, the functions inside and the ones after are flat siblings, and the braces are anonymous tokens. That is why the walk goes over the **raw** children with a brace stack instead of hunting loose attributes.

    **`unmarked` is not reported as `@system`, and the distinction matters.** In classic D the effective default is `@system`, but that is a compiler rule, not something somebody wrote — and in template-heavy code D **infers** the attribute. Saying "no attribute = `@system`" would put a claim in the author's mouth, so it is reported as what it is.

    **The grammar's limit, with the number and the constructs.** `tree-sitter-d 0.8.2` fails on **9 of 894 files (1.0%)**, and unlike Ada — where every error was a deliberately invalid fixture — these are production files. The constructs are: `version = IDENT;`, `alias X = typeof(...)`, and a `case` whose value is a type. tree-sitter recovers, so the rest of each file is still analyzed, but whatever falls inside the `ERROR` is not reported.

    **And `.d` is ambiguous**: it is also the extension of the dependency files `gcc -MD` emits. Those do not parse as D and degrade on their own, without inventing results.

    **Verified**: 1,559 Rust tests (18 for D), `clippy`/`ruff`/`tsc`/`biome` clean, and **14 of 14 checks in a real browser** over a file exercising all three scope forms.

    Still open on this line: observable CTFE and mixins as an analysis boundary.
  - **Step 2 of that order done on 2026-10-11** (compile-time complexity, see the closed Line C above): measured, C++ emits 3 symbols per level and D emits 6. Still open: `@trusted`/`@system`, observable CTFE, and mixins as an analysis boundary.

- [ ] **Line E — Ada, and the category of evidence the engine still lacks.** *(opened 2026-10-11 at the user's request, with the state measured before writing it)*. Ada does not enter on performance — it would not be faster than Rust or Zig, and claiming otherwise would be exactly the "because it is interesting" this phase forbids. It enters for **SPARK**.

  **The concrete reason, and it is structural.** `decidibilidad.rs` sorts the 44 finding kinds into two categories: **decidable** (the engine proved it by reading the code) and **approximate** (the engine suspects it and says why it cannot prove it). SPARK/GNATprove produces a third thing that is neither: **proven by an external prover**. There is nowhere to put that today, and folding it into "decidable" would claim the engine proved something another tool proved. That third category is the new capability; the language is the vehicle.

  **And it belongs inside Line D, not beside it.** The proof surface (Curry-Howard) already asks *"which part of the program is backed by a proof?"*. SPARK answers that same question with an actual proof instead of an inference over the type system. It is the same line one step further, not a separate topic.

  **State measured on this machine (2026-10-11), not assumed:**
  - ~~**No toolchain**~~ — **installed 2026-10-16 at the user's request, and steps 2 and 3 are now UNBLOCKED.** Via Alire (`alr` 2.1.1, Ada's official package manager: one zip, no admin rights):
    - **GNAT 16.1.0** (`gnat_native`) and **gprbuild 26.0.1**, in Alire's cache.
    - **GNATprove 16.1.0** with **Why3 1.8.2**, **Alt-Ergo 2.6.1** and **Z3**, in `C:\Ada\gnatprove`.
    - Alire also installs its own msys2 (318 MB) inside its cache. **Nothing was added to PATH**, same criterion as with DMD: it is invoked by absolute path.

    **Checked, not assumed**: `gnatmake` compiles and **runs** a real `.adb`, and `gnatprove` over a two-function SPARK probe returns **both answers that matter** — `postcondition proved` / `overflow check proved` for the one with a precondition, and *"provers gave up before completing the proof"* with a `possible fix` for the one without.

    **And that probe already shows why the third category is needed.** A *could not prove it* from an external prover is neither *decidable* nor *approximate*: it is not that the engine suspects something, it is that a proof tool ran out of road. Those are **two new states, not one** — proved and not-proved — and folding them into the existing ones would be exactly the confusion this line exists to avoid.

    **What this line used to say, and the correction**: it claimed *"the installed gcc has no Ada (`languages=c,c++,objc`)"*. That was false for the gcc that is here — `gcc -v` reports `languages=c,c++,objc,obj-c++,fortran,ada` — what was missing was the **frontend binary**, `gnat1`, which is why `gcc -c prueba.adb` died with `CreateProcess: No such file or directory`. The conclusion held, the evidence did not.
  - **There is a grammar, and it works**: `tree-sitter-ada 0.1.0` (by Briot, of AdaCore), probed in a throwaway crate against the repo's `tree-sitter 0.25` — **ABI compatible**, parses real Ada with no errors, finds the subprograms (`function` and `procedure`) and the nested loops, which is exactly what nesting-based Big-O needs.

  **The three support levels, which are not the same and should not be declared closed together** — the distinction comes from the document the user brought and it is correct: *analyzed* (Sythrall understands the code), *compilable* (Sythrall asks the toolchain to build it and studies the output) and *formally verifiable* (Sythrall integrates GNATprove and tells a proof from a warning).

  **Order, with each step's cost measured against what already exists:**
  1. ~~**Parser and metrics**~~ — **done 2026-10-16** (`adaparse.rs`, 910 lines). Ada is the engine's **12th language**, wired end to end: `POST /parse/ada`, plugin manifest, `scanner.rs`, the Python relay, `.adb`/`.ads` in the project extensions, badge and panel. The Vite proxy needed no change: no new FastAPI router was added, Ada travels over the `/static` routes that already existed.

     **Measured against 2,718 production Ada files** (gnatcoll-core, alire and ada_language_server, 13.8 MB): **10,355 bodies, 10,810 specs, 3,993 units, 7,175 `with` clauses**, in ~28 s.

     **Grammar errors on real code: 0.** The 117 files with an `ERROR` are, one by one, fixtures the ada_language_server itself keeps for error-recovery tests (`invalid_ada/`, `main_with_problem/`, `formatting_incorrect_code/`) plus two Alire templates with unsubstituted placeholders. That is not "4.3% failures": it is the grammar getting everything that is valid Ada right.

     **The five fixes it took came from COUNTING what was being lost, not from re-reading the grammar** — and none of them would have shown up in the 22 tests that already passed:
     - **Operator overloads**: 111 of 9,970 bodies came out as `<anonymous>`. Ada writes the operator in quotes (`function "=" (L, R : T)`), so the grammar hands the name over as a `string_literal`, not an identifier. That is idiomatic, not a corner case. Anonymous: **112 → 1** (and the one left is inside an Alire template that is not valid Ada).
     - **Generic instantiation**: `package X is new Y (...)` was no unit at all, so an `.ads` that does nothing but instantiate a container produced **nothing**. Units: **2,535 → 3,993**.
     - **`package X renames Y;`** was not a unit either.
     - **`generic ... procedure P (...)`** holds the specification DIRECTLY, with no `subprogram_declaration` node in between — the opposite of `generic package`, which does wrap a `package_declaration`. That asymmetry made those declarations disappear entirely.
     - **`procedure P is null;` was dropped silently.** This one came out of a test I wrote claiming the grammar did NOT support it; the test failed and the claim was mine, not the grammar's. The fixture where I saw it did have an `ERROR`, but for something else (`type Enum (A, B);`, which is invalid Ada). A body that does nothing is still a body.

     **Files producing no result at all, in real code: 0** (the 49 left are all deliberately invalid fixtures).

     **What Ada gives that none of the other eleven gives for free**: the source **distinguishes** `and`/`or` — which evaluate both sides — from `and then`/`or else`, which short-circuit. In C the engine counts `&&` and in Fortran `.AND.` and is right by convention; here cyclomatic complexity is **exact**, and a test pins the difference. Measured distribution: 9,245 subprograms at CC 1-5, 775 at 6-10, 251 at 11-20, 73 at 21-50 and 11 above 51; the real maximum is **203** (`Handle_Word_Token`, the ada_language_server's fallback indenter).

     **And the price of the decision not to require GNAT, paid where it is paid**: in Ada `A (I)` is both indexing an array and calling a one-argument function, and the grammar cannot break the tie — both forms are a `function_call`. It is said in the code, not hidden, and the damage is bounded because `graph::call_edges` only emits edges to subprograms in the same file; a test pins that.

     **Two more things this uncovered that were not about Ada**: `exports` existed in the shape and was filled by Ada **and JS/TS**, and the panel never read it — no JS module's exports were visible either, and now they are. And the Big-O label table became shared (`ccosto::etiqueta`, `pub(crate)`) instead of being copied a third time: Phase 32's five copies of `walk` were expensive enough to find.

     **Verified**: 1,541 Rust tests (28 for Ada), 345 backend tests, `clippy`/`ruff`/`tsc`/`biome` clean, and **13 of 13 checks in a real browser** over one `.adb` and one `.ads` — badge, operator names, `with` chips with `private_with` told apart, the no-body signatures section, and `O(n²)` on screen for the two nested loops.
  2. ~~**Machine code**~~ — **done 2026-10-16.** Ada is the 7th language the bridge can compile (`gnat-gcc -S`), under the same contract as the other six: per-optimization-level comparison, breakdown by instruction category, and the assembly behind a "ver asm".

     **"Adding Ada is lines" was almost true, and the two exceptions are worth more than the rule:**
     - **The program cannot be asked for by PATH.** This machine has a `gcc` that advertises Ada in its `languages=` but ships no `gnat1` frontend; resolving "gcc" by name picked exactly the wrong one. That is why the table entry says `gnat-gcc`, a key of its own that `toolchain::resolver` looks up in Alire's cache and never on PATH.
     - **Ada wants the file named after the unit.** Under another name GNAT still compiles but warns (`file name does not match unit name`), and that warning would show up in every Ada analysis as if it were a finding about the user's code. The name is derived with `adaparse`, step 1's parser: **step 1 serves step 2** instead of them being two loose jobs.

     **And fixing this surfaced something broken that had nothing to do with Ada.** `dcost.rs` looked for `dmd` on PATH, and `dmd` is installed off it: the endpoint answered *"No D compiler found"* and **the C++ vs D comparison, documented in this very ROADMAP with measured numbers, could not be reproduced**. The numbers were real; the path to reproducing them was not. With `toolchain::resolver` the comparison is back: `comparable: true`, C++ 3 symbols per level, D 6, both curves linear.

     **What Ada's assembly shows that is the language's own**: the procedure comes out as `_ada_acumular` and **calls `__gnat_rcheck_CE_Overflow_Check`** — the overflow check GNAT inserts. Measured, it **survives `-O2`**: from -O0 to -O2 the code drops from 54 to 48 lines and from 30 to 23 instructions, and the check is still there. That is exactly the kind of cost this phase exists to show: not an opinion about Ada, the instruction that is in the binary.

     **Verified**: 1,565 Rust tests (6 for the new resolver), `clippy`/`tsc`/`biome` clean, and **9 of 9 checks in a real browser**.
  3. ~~**SPARK/GNATprove**~~ — **done 2026-10-16** (`teoria/spark.rs`), and **the line closes**. The rule written up front — *a proven property and an analyzer warning are not shown the same way* — is met and verified in a browser: proved comes out green saying **who** proved it, unproved comes out orange with the prover's own text.

     **And the correction the measurement forced: it is not one new category, it is two.** `decidibilidad.rs` said, verbatim, *"the two sides. There is no third: either it is proved by reading, or it cannot be"*. There are now four:
     - `machine-proved` — **an external prover discharged it**, not this engine by reading. Folding it into `decidable` would claim someone else's proof.
     - `unproved` — **the prover could not close it**, and that is not `approximate`. Approximate means the analysis gives up on a theoretical limit and says so up front; this is an obligation provable in principle that ran out of road: another precondition, another prover or more time could close it.

     **`decidibilidad.rs`'s design did not break, and that was a decision.** That module classifies by finding *type*, not by instance. Each obligation's outcome travels in the **kind** (`spark_proved`/`spark_unproved`), so the table stays a function of the type — the alternative was bolting a per-instance exception onto the simplest piece of the engine.

     **No prose parsing.** GNATprove writes a `gnatprove.sarif` where each obligation carries its rule (`VC_OVERFLOW_CHECK`, `VC_POSTCONDITION`, `VC_LOOP_INVARIANT_PRESERV`…) and a `kind` of `pass` or `open`. That pair is the contract; the English text is shown, not interpreted. A `kind` GNATprove adds tomorrow **is ignored** rather than assumed proved.

     **Measured, and it caught a bug of its own before shipping**: GNATprove emits notices with `kind: "pass"` and `ruleId: "error"` — one of them *"cannot unroll loop"* — and the first version counted them as discharged obligations, **inflating the proved surface with messages that prove nothing**. The rule became: an obligation is named in UPPERCASE, a generic diagnostic uses the lowercase severity word, with a regression test built from the exact datum that fooled the module.

     **And one more measurement, about the runtime**: a prover can take minutes, so the handler goes to `spawn_blocking`. Measured before and after — a `/health` during a SPARK run went from ~60 ms to **1,533 ms**, and with the fix it is back to **70 ms**. Same rule as Python's `run_in_threadpool`, at the other end.

     **Verified**: 1,575 Rust tests (8 for SPARK, 9 for decidability), `clippy`/`ruff`/`tsc`/`biome` clean, and **9 of 9 checks in a real browser**, including the one comparing the colour of proved against unproved.

  **What is deliberately NOT done, and it is the correction to the original plan the user brought:** *"leaning on GNAT for semantic information"* would make Ada **the only language whose ANALYSIS requires an installed compiler**. The other six parse from text. That asymmetry is not worth paying for more precise types; if semantic information is needed, it is sought in step 2 (where compiling is already part of the deal), not in step 1.

  - **CTFE, with the framing corrected**: no attempt to predict whether a function *could* be evaluated at compile time — that is asking the compiler, and it is Rice again. What is read are the places the language **already requires** compile-time evaluation (`enum x = f()`, `static if`, `static assert`), and their impact is studied. That is observable.
  - **Mixins go the opposite way from how they are usually pitched**: a `string mixin` is a **hole in all static analysis**, Sythrall's included. The valuable finding is not "here is what the mixin does" but "here the analysis stops seeing" — same shape as the capabilities notice already in the panel.

- [ ] **D as an implementation language: no reason today, and no need to decide.** Rust already holds the core and gives performance without a GC; Zig already holds low-level tooling and is better at C interop (it compiles C). There is no Sythrall workload where D beats both. If one appears, apply the procedure above: benchmark D/Rust/Zig and decide on data. **And the cost is not theoretical**: of 1040 Rust tests the only intermittent ones are the toolchain benchmarks (Zig, Fortran), and the repo's most recent commit is a cross-platform ABI fix in hand-written Assembly kernels that was breaking CI. Every added toolchain is another "is it installed?" branch and another source of fragility — D would bring `dmd`/`ldc`/`gdc`, three compilers with different behaviours.

### ✅ Phase 35 — Sythrall without Python *(the full programme the user asked for on 2026-11-14, with the surface measured before promising anything)*

**This is not another phase: it is the largest structural change in the project.** The mandate was one line — *"remove Python completely"* — and the eight things that came with it are the rest of the same move. What follows is written from what was measured the day it opened, not from an estimate.

**Python's real surface, measured:** 55 files, **14,080 lines** (9,507 excluding tests).

| area | files | lines | sidecar calls |
|---|---|---|---|
| `routers/` | 14 | 6,232 | 33 |
| `services/` | 7 | 2,844 | 89 |
| `tests/` | 14 | 4,573 | — |
| root (`main.py`, `shared.py`) | 2 | 421 | — |

**And the fact that changes the size of the problem: the production path no longer imports any scientific library.** `numpy`, `pandas`, `sklearn`, `torch`, `tensorflow` appear in exactly two places, and neither is analysis: `/health`'s **capability probes** (an `import X` inside a `try`) and the **head-to-head benchmarks** against the native ports. That migration is already done and measured. What is left of Python is **HTTP plumbing, relay and product logic**, not science.

**The honest split of the 9,507 lines to move:**
- **~2,300 of pure relay** (`complexity_client.py` and the routers that only forward): these are not ported, they **disappear**. Once Rust serves the HTTP, the middleman does not exist.
- **~4,300 of real logic** (`intelligence.py` 1,445, `ml.py` 742, `analysis.py` 646, ~~`metrics_live.py` 462~~ **done**, ~~`diagram.py` 444~~ **done**, ~~`upload.py` 447~~ **done**, ~~`history.py` 275~~ **done**): this does get ported.
- **The rest** is `main.py`, `shared.py`, auth and startup, replaced by the axum startup the sidecar already has.

**The order, and why that one.** Least to most risk, each step verifiable on its own:
1. **The routers that are pure relay** (`binary_analysis`, `graph`, `static_analysis`, `execution`): the engine already has the routes. Moving the HTTP to Rust here ports no logic, it **deletes** a layer.
2. **`upload.py` and `history.py`** — files, zips and persistence. This is I/O, not analysis, and it is where the "many files at once" goal is actually decided.
3. **`metrics_live.py` and `diagram.py`** — product logic over data the engine already produces.
4. **`analysis.py` and `intelligence.py`** — the two largest and the ones with the most rules of their own.
5. **`ml.py`** — the model/framework catalogue is a data table, not code; it moves almost as-is.
6. **Startup, auth and `/health`** — last: while Python exists, this is what holds it up.
7. ~~**Delete `apps/api`, `pyproject.toml`, `requirements*.txt`, `pytest.ini` and the `.venv`** from the execution path~~ — **done 2026-11-16**. All that remains of the `.venv` is the directory on disk, which nothing uses any more: no script, no CI gate and no Dockerfile names it.

**CLOSED on 2026-11-16.** Python opened the phase at **14,080 lines / 55 files** and is at **zero**: `apps/api` does not exist. Neither do `pyproject.toml`, `pytest.ini`, `requirements.txt`, `requirements-dev.txt`, `docker/api.Dockerfile` or `scripts/run-backend.mjs`. Vite's proxy sends **12 prefixes to the engine and none to Python**; the remaining one is `/terminal`, which was always the Rust sidecar's. **CI gates went from nine to six**: `tsc`, `biome`, `vite build`, `cargo build --release`, `clippy -D warnings`, `cargo test`.

| prefix | served by | status |
|---|---|---|
| `/binary` | engine | ✅ relay deleted, not ported |
| `/graph` · `/analyze/graph` | engine | ✅ the 3 payloads ported; the 6 builders were already in Rust |
| `/static` | engine | ✅ all 9 endpoints; `parse-project` was orchestration |
| `/execution` | engine | ✅ |
| `/logs` | engine | ✅ it forwarded to the engine's own `/log`: one hop fewer |
| `/history` | engine | ✅ 5 endpoints + persistence, CBOR instead of SQLite |
| `/api/upload` | engine | ✅ 9 endpoints, own ZIP reader, constant RAM |
| `/metrics` | engine | ✅ 3 endpoints; duplicated analysis replaced, 9x faster |
| `/analyze` | engine | ✅ diagrams, logs, code, project, external APIs and ML |
| `/intel` | engine | ✅ all 7 editor endpoints; fallbacks deleted |
| `/capabilities` · `/health` · `/auth` | engine | ✅ step 6; `/capabilities` changed meaning |

**What the `/metrics` step added, and it lands squarely on `intelligence.py` and `ml.py`.**
- **Duplicated analysis is not ported: it is replaced, and the numbers change.** The router carried its own per-language Big-O alongside the engine's real parsers, and it was not equivalent but **worse**: it counted total loops instead of nested ones, so three sequential loops reported `O(n³)` instead of `O(n)`. The two remaining routers carry heuristics of the same kind. **When a replacement changes a number the user sees, it goes in the CHANGELOG** — even when the new number is the correct one.
- **Using the analysis source of truth can be the wrong call, and the criterion is the endpoint's budget.** The first version called `analyze_rich`: one implementation, no new code, which is why it looked obviously right. Measured in **release**: **16.3 ms** against a **5 ms** budget on the endpoint that runs on every keystroke. The lightweight path — the same public functions, without the stages the bar does not show — measures **1.74 ms**. **Before picking the analysis engine, know how many times per second it will be called.**
- **And performance measurements go in release, not debug.** The first number I got was 87 ms and it made me believe parsing was the problem; in release that same parse is 1.68 ms. Debug inflates each stage differently, so **it is not even usable for comparing proportions**.
- **Do not claim an improvement without measuring it, not even when the old code is plainly worse.** I wrote that the engine "distinguishes the shape of the recursion" because the router returned `O(2^n)` for every recursive function. That was false: the engine does the same, and `factorial(n-1)` still comes out exponential in both. **The overestimate is shared**, and it now has a test pinning it rather than a comment celebrating it.
- **A fallback is judged by what it covers, not by being a fallback.** `diagram.py`'s covered "the sidecar is not answering" (impossible inside the engine) and was deleted; `/metrics/live`'s covers "the user's code does not compile yet", which while someone types is half the time, and was ported. **Same structure, opposite decisions.**

**What the `/analyze/diagram` step added, and it applies to the routers still pending.**
- **A fallback against the sidecar being down is dead code once the code lives IN the sidecar.** `diagram.py` carried two implementations of every builder — one over the engine's analysis, one with its own `ast.walk` — and its docstring defended keeping the second because *"deleting them would be a real regression"*. It was, while the engine was a separate process that could fail to answer; **inside the engine that branch is an impossible state**, because you don't lose the connection to yourself. That was 190 of 444 lines that were not ported: they were deleted. **`intelligence.py` and `static_parser.py` have the same pattern** (`rich` first, Python heuristic if no sidecar), so both will shrink more than their line counts suggest.
- **Deduplicating and ordering are two separate fixes, and fixing one can break the other.** The call graph's nodes came from a Python set: it deduplicated but had no defined order. Replacing it with a list sorted by line fixed the order and **lost the deduplication**, so an overridden method declared the same node twice. The tests did not catch it: asking for a real diagram did. **When one structure does two things at once, the replacement has to cover both explicitly.**
- **Ask whether a value is used as identity or as text.** The same function name appears as node id and as label on the same Mermaid line: the label tolerates almost anything, the id does not. Python used the raw name for both, and that worked only because Python identifiers are `[A-Za-z0-9_]`.
- **And text truncation goes by characters, not bytes.** Cutting a docstring at 40 bytes splits an accented character in half and leaves invalid UTF-8, which Mermaid will not render.

**What the `/api/upload` step added to that list.**
- **Two lists of the same thing is a divergence waiting to happen.** There were two sets of ignored directories — one in the uploads, one in the analysis — and they did not match: uploading a Rust project's folder wrote all of `target/` to disk, because only the analysis side ignored it. **When porting, the first thing to check is whether the constant being moved has a twin.** And one of those rules (`"*.egg-info"`, inside a set consulted by exact equality) had never matched anything: dead code shaped like a rule.
- **A framework difference can be an improvement, not an obstacle.** FastAPI parsed the whole multipart before the handler; axum streams it, so `project_id` can arrive after the files. Instead of replicating the buffering, files go to a staging area and get moved at the end: **RAM stays constant at one file at a time** instead of proportional to the body. The real case that motivated the `node_modules` filter was 27,614 files and 513 MB.
- **Tests of a departing router don't get repointed: ask what they needed.** Three tests created their project by uploading a ZIP so another endpoint could read it off disk. What they needed was *a project on disk*; now they write it directly. Exercising a whole upload on the way was coupling, not coverage.
- **And the lesson that cost real data: a manual verification against the production directory is destructive here.** The test suite wrote to the real `uploads/` and retention (`MAX_PROJECTS = 20`) deleted 20 of the user's projects; that was fixed by pointing the tests at a temp dir. Then **the same mistake repeated by hand**: verifying the new endpoints against the real directory deleted 10 more. **Every manual verification of `/api/upload` runs with the engine started under `SYTHRALL_UPLOADS_DIR` pointing at a temp dir** — the very variable this step unified between Python and Rust.

**The rule the user set, and it governs the whole phase: *"benchmark who wins, and what does not win gets deleted without mercy"*.** Nothing is ported blind. Every piece that moves is measured against the one it replaces — time and **RAM**, which was asked for explicitly — and if the new version does not win, **it does not get to stay just because it was written**. That applies to the Python-to-Rust ports and to what already exists.

**Opening audit, done 2026-11-14 — what each low-level language actually contributes today.** The request was *"100% real of the languages we mentioned"*, so the first thing was to measure whether they contribute or are decoration. **All nine empirical validators run and confirm their prediction**:

| kernel | language | predicted | measured |
|---|---|---|---|
| matmul | Fortran | O(n³) | ≈ 2.72 |
| fibonacci | Fortran | Θ(φⁿ) | exponential |
| linear regression | Fortran | O(n) | ≈ 0.95 |
| bubble sort | Zig | O(n²) | ≈ 2.07 |
| graph BFS | Zig | O(V+E) | ≈ 1.0 |
| aggregation | Zig | O(n) | ≈ 1.05 |
| quicksort | Zig | O(n log n) | ≈ 1.0 |
| sum of squares | Assembly | O(n) | ≈ 1.00 |
| mergesort | Assembly | O(n log n) | ≈ 1.0 |

**The audit's conclusion, and it is not the comfortable one**: Zig, Fortran, Assembly and C **are not dead weight** — they hold up the Algorithm Validation Engine, a product feature that works — but their contribution is **uniform and narrow**: all four do the same thing, being subjects of empirical validation. **Ada is the only one contributing a capability no other gives**: a category of evidence (`machine-proved`/`unproved`). The goal of *"contributing in features"* is not met by more kernels of the same kind; it is met when each language enters for something the others cannot do, which is exactly the rule Phase 34 already wrote down. **And the user committed to it on 2026-11-16**: see the "Each language contributing something unique" item below, with the definition of done and per-language candidates.

**The rest of the mandate, each with its done-criterion:**
- [x] **Many files at once** — *measured and solved 2026-11-16*. Scanning is spread across threads and does **1,730 files per second** against 481 before: **3.6x**, on 12 cores. 4,000 files (1.36 million lines) went from **8.31 to 2.31 seconds**; the projected 40,000, from 83 to 23.
  - **Measuring disproved the fear before it justified the change.** The sequential path was already **exactly linear** — 2.08 ms per file, constant from 100 to 4,000 — so there was no hidden O(n²) to fix. And **RAM was already flat**: 10 MB idle, 12 MB after analysing 1.36 million lines, because each file is analysed and dropped. What justified parallelising was the projected size, not the present one.
  - **It is not 12x, and why is stated**: disk I/O, JSON serialisation and the final merge do not parallelise.
  - **No new dependencies**: `std::thread::scope`, not `rayon`. Below 32 files it does not split, because starting threads would cost more than the work. With a test comparing the output against the sequential path, file by file.
  - **What stays open here**: uploads already had constant RAM since the `/api/upload` port, and scanning is now 3.6x. What has not been measured is the **real ceiling**: how many files an upload takes before the 200 MB ZIP cap or `MAX_PROJECTS = 20` becomes the problem instead of the analysis.
- [x] **Performance and RAM** — *two measured results, both with the number before and after*. The criterion held both ways: what won stayed, what did not win was deleted.
  - **`/metrics/live`: 16.3 ms -> 1.74 ms (9x).** It stopped asking `analyze_rich` for a full analysis in order to display seven numbers. The losing implementation **was not kept as an option**: it was deleted, and a budget test with a 10 ms ceiling catches anyone plugging it back in.
  - **Project scan: 481 -> 1,730 files/s (3.6x).** See the item above.
  - **And RAM turned out not to be a problem on either side.** Uploads got a constant peak (one file at a time) in the `/api/upload` port; analysis sits at 7-15 MB regardless of how many files it processes. Measured, not assumed: **there was nothing to optimise there**, and saying so is worth more than inventing an improvement.
- [x] **Deep cleanup** — *ongoing, with this phase's share done*. Dead code, dead CSS, exports with no consumers (one already turned up), duplicates. `panels/static.ts` went from **3,585 to 3,085 lines** on 2026-11-16 (−500), with two new modules and three findings this ROADMAP had wrong.
  - **The difficulty was the opposite of how this ROADMAP described it.** It said splitting the file meant moving the empirical-validation block "with its ~30 module-level state variables", as if that were the obstacle. Measured: there are **20**, **all belonging to the block**, and it depended on **one** panel function. It was the easiest part to pull out. The Algorithm Validation Engine now lives in `panels/validacion.ts`, with its 9 kernels as a **data table** instead of 18 loose variables and 9 identical handlers.
  - **And the second candidate this ROADMAP named did not exist.** It said "executable analyser, 1,032 lines, 10 own state vars": measured, that banner covered **1,221 lines with 63 names crossing its border**, and the real executable analyser is **~93 lines**. The other ~730 were **source-code** pipeline renderers piled under someone else's title. What actually was a unit was the **machine-code explorer** (191 lines, 5 state vars, all its own), now in `panels/maquina.ts`, which took along its 6 dispatch branches —2,000 lines further up— and its compile function —200 further down, inside the Pareto section—.
  - **A banner is not a module.** Both extractions worked because the border was measured by **crossing names**, not by line range. What is left has to be measured the same way before it is promised: the compiler pipeline (**268 lines** but **24 foreign state vars** — that one really is entangled and is the one that actually costs), Atomic CSS (**374**, 8 own state vars and 7 foreign functions) and the ~730 lines of source-code renderers currently under the wrong banner, which **need a correct banner first**, not an extraction.
  - **Dead code found and deleted, with numbers**: three production dependencies nobody imported (`thebe`, `pyodide`, `starboard-notebook` — they were **data** in an ML-panel catalogue, not imports), dragging `jest`/`ts-jest` in via `@jupyterlab`: `node_modules` went from **616 to 148 top-level packages** and from **12 GB to 3.0 GB**. And `dist/` was accumulating **671 files / 185 MB** because Vite will not empty an `outDir` that sits outside `root`: with `emptyOutDir: true` it came down to **25 / 19 MB**.
- [ ] **UI/UX.** Continuous, not a step with an end.
  - **2026-11-16 pass — accessibility, measured on the mounted DOM rather than the source.** Of 76 mounted buttons and 133 across the whole source, **one** had no accessible name: the sidebar drawer toggle, which contains only an `<svg aria-hidden>`, so it announced as a bare "button". And **none of the product's 10 `<select>` elements had one** — the text above them ("ARCHIVO A") is a decorative `<div>`, not a `<label for>`. All 11 fixed; the count is now **zero**.
