The button also declares `aria-expanded` and the handler updates it, because an `aria-expanded` that does not follow real state lies from the first click.
  - **Of 16 findings, 8 were my own false positives, verified one by one instead of "fixed"**: three checkboxes wrapped in a `<label>` (valid implicit labelling), three `input[type=file]` hidden by a global rule, and two `<select>` that were that word inside a CSS comment. A black band in the headless screenshot was not a layout bug either: at 900 and at 1200 window height the gap is the same 150 px of browser chrome.
  - **What this pass deliberately did NOT touch**: colour contrast, tab order and keyboard behaviour. Those are different checks and none was measured here, so nothing is claimed about them.
  - **Gap recorded during this step**: the flowchart for non-Python languages detects JS/TS functions with a pattern and, for any other extension, shows only the filename and its line count — even though the engine already parses **13 languages properly**. It was ported as-is on purpose: widening it is a product change, not part of a migration. When it happens, the parsers are already there.
- [ ] **Each language contributing something unique — COMMITTED, not a maybe.** The user confirmed it on 2026-11-16: *"si o si implementaremos 100% real los otros lenguaje"* ("we will implement the other languages 100% for real, no matter what"). It stops being an audit finding and becomes pending work with a definition of done.
  - **The honest state, measured in the Phase 35 opening audit**: Zig, Fortran, Assembly and C **are not dead weight** — they hold up the Algorithm Validation Engine, which works — but all four do **the same thing**: being subjects of empirical validation. Ada is the only one contributing a capability no other gives: a **category of evidence** (`machine-proved`/`unproved` via SPARK), which changed the model in `decidibilidad.rs`.
  - **The rule this has to satisfy**, already written by Phase 34: a language earns its place through **something the others cannot do**, not through another kernel of the same kind. Adding a tenth empirical validator does not move this item at all.
  - **Definition of done, per language**: each one has to enable a **product capability** that does not exist today and could not exist without that language. "It is used" is not enough: you have to be able to name what Sythrall knows how to do thanks to it.
  - **Candidates to evaluate, one per language** — not committed yet, they are the starting point for the design:
    - **Zig** — `comptime` is genuine compile-time evaluation. A language where you can *measure* what got resolved before running and what was left for runtime: that is a category of analysis none of the other four gives.
    - **Fortran** — array aliasing and contiguity. The standard guarantees the compiler things C cannot, and that can be **proven** over the user's code instead of assumed.
    - **Assembly** — it already measures SIMD and counts instructions. What is missing is the one thing only it can give: **real cost** (per-instruction latency/throughput, execution ports), which is the ground truth every Big-O falls short of.
    - **C** — ABI and memory layout. Padding, alignment, field order: decisions invisible in the source that change performance and only show up on compiling.
  - **What does NOT count as done**: another empirical validator, another set of bindings, or "eight languages in the capabilities table". If at the end you cannot say *"Sythrall knows X because it uses Zig, and without Zig it would not"*, the item stays open.
- [ ] **Desktop app — COMMITTED, no date.** No longer blocked: its only declared blocker was Python, and Python left in Phase 35 (a desktop app embedding a Python interpreter would have been starting backwards). Today the product is **two Rust binaries and a static bundle**, which is exactly the shape a desktop app needs.
  - **What would need deciding when it lands**: whether the shell is the system WebView (light, no embedded Chromium, but with per-platform differences) or a bundled one. The call is measurable and gets measured first, like everything else.
  - **What is already solved by accident**: the engine binds to `127.0.0.1` with configurable host and port, needs no external service, and stores everything relative to environment variables (`SYTHRALL_UPLOADS_DIR`, `SYTHRALL_HISTORY_DIR`, `SYTHRALL_LOG_DIR`). Packaging it does not require rewriting anything.
- [x] **Licence and return to open source** — *done 2026-11-14*. See the notice at the top of the READMEs: the project was born public under GPL-3.0, went private at v4.12.0, and returns to public under GPL-3.0. **And a real defect was fixed**: `LICENSE` was a summary with a link, and the GPL requires the program be accompanied by *a copy* of the licence. It now carries the complete text (674 lines).
  - **Completed 2026-11-16: three things were missing for it to be fully real.** (1) No manifest declared the licence — neither `package.json` nor `Cargo.toml` had a `license` field, so registries and dependency scanners read the project as **"no licence declared"**, i.e. proprietary by accident. (2) There was no inventory of the third-party code the binaries carry compiled in, and it turned out **none of tree-sitter's nine grammars publishes its copyright notice on crates.io** — MIT requires reproducing it when distributing, so without `THIRD-PARTY.md` that notice reached nobody. The nine notices are **not the same** (one has a different holder) and `tree-sitter-fortran` **has none at all**, which is stated rather than invented. (3) The startup notice the GPL asks for in programs with terminal interaction was missing. All three done and verified.

---

### 🔬 Research spikes — leftover ideas, not committed

- [x] ~~Rust extension (PyO3) for the static parser's hottest path~~ — **investigated twice with real benchmarks, not adopted either time**: first pass profiled `static_parser.py` on large *individual* files (250+ functions, 3000+ synthetic) and found the AST-walk consolidation already done was noise-level neutral (~160ms for realistic file sizes). Second pass (Phase 10, above) tested the other axis — thousands of *files* in one project, not one huge file — and found the parser itself still scaled linearly; the actual O(n²) cost was three ordinary Python bugs in the code *around* the parser, fixed without any new language. Rust *did* end up in the project (Phase 9's `terminal-server`), but for a genuinely Rust-shaped problem — cross-platform PTY handling — not as a speed rewrite of working Python. If a real bottleneck ever shows up in `parse_file` itself, the integration model would be an Axum sidecar (same pattern as `terminal-server`) talking HTTP to FastAPI, not PyO3 embedding — simpler to maintain solo, no native-binding build matrix.
